#+TITLE: Thinking in Common Lisp
#+SUBTITLE: A hand-held guide and reference
#+STARTUP: content
#+OPTIONS: toc:2
#+PROPERTY: header-args:lisp :results verbatim

* How to Use This Guide

** What this guide is

This guide takes you from zero to reading, writing and thinking in
Common Lisp. It is long on purpose. Read it in order the first time.
Afterwards, use it as a reference.

Two promises:

1. *No forward references.* You will never see "don't worry about this
   yet". Every piece of code uses only what has already been explained.
2. *You run everything.* Every example is code you type and run
   yourself, with its result printed underneath so you can check
   yourself as you go.

You know Python and Java. Now and then a short note will say "this is
roughly like X in Java". Treat those as landmarks, not translations.
Lisp works best when you meet it on its own terms.

** First: set up your machine

Before anything else, do these in order:

1. Appendix A: install SBCL (the Lisp itself).
2. Appendix B: make Emacs talk to SBCL and run your first expression.
3. Skim Appendix C: the Emacs keys you need.

Come back here when the test expression in Appendix B prints =3=.

** How each chapter is built

Every chapter has the same parts:

- *Explanation*, with code you run as you read.
- *Common mistakes:* the errors beginners actually make, what the error
  message looks like, and how to fix it.
- *Project step:* one new piece of the running project (see below).
- *Exercises:* answers are in Appendix D.

** Running the code

A listing looks like this:

#+begin_src lisp
(+ 1 2)
#+end_src

You run it by typing it at the REPL and pressing ~RET~:

#+begin_example
CL-USER> (+ 1 2)
3
#+end_example

Most listings in this guide carry that answer as a comment, so you can
check yourself without waiting for us:

#+begin_src lisp
(+ 1 2)   ; => 3
#+end_src

There are two ways to run a listing, and the book works with either:

- *At the REPL.* Type it, press ~RET~, read the answer. Best for
  experiments and one-liners.
- *In a =.lisp= buffer.* Type it in a file, put the cursor after the
  closing parenthesis, and press ~C-c C-c~. Emacs sends the expression
  to Lisp and shows the value in the echo area at the bottom of the
  frame. Best for code you want to keep. Chapter 1 calls this the
  edit-evaluate loop, and Appendix B sets it up.

*** Two kinds of results

A listing either *returns* a value or *prints* text, and the two are
shown differently.

The value is what Lisp hands back — what the REPL shows after you
press ~RET~. Listings that return a value show it as a =; =>= comment:

#+begin_src lisp
(* 6 7)   ; => 42
#+end_src

Printed text is output written along the way, by =print=, =princ=,
=format= or the debugger. It comes out above the value:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(print "hello")
(print "world")
#+end_src

The two printed lines appear first, then the value of the last
=print= — the string ="world"= — comes back on its own line.

When a listing's result looks "wrong", it is usually this: you expected
the printed text, and what you are looking at is the value that came
back at the end.

*** One Lisp, running

Everything you run goes into the same Lisp process. If you define
something once, later listings can use it. This means:

- Run the code in order, from top to bottom.
- If you restart Emacs or Lisp, re-run the definitions the later
  examples depend on.

*** Editing comfortably

Emacs indents Lisp for you: select a region and press ~TAB~, or put the
cursor inside a definition and press ~C-M-q~ to re-indent it. Indented
code is how Lispers read structure, so let Emacs do it.

** Writing and checking your exercise answers

Write your answer where you can run it — a scratch =.lisp= buffer, or
straight at the REPL. Exercise 1.1 asks for a function =twice=, so the
answer is:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun twice (n)
  (* n 2))
#+end_src

Then check it. Each answer in Appendix D comes with *test lines*. Run
them after your definition:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (twice 4) (twice 0) (twice -3))   ; => (8 0 -6)
#+end_src

If your result matches the one in Appendix D, you're done. Your
version doesn't need to look like the answer. There are many correct
ways to write most things.

Habits that help:

- *Type the examples, don't paste them.* Your fingers learn the shape
  of Lisp.
- *Break things on purpose.* Delete a parenthesis, misspell a name, and
  read the error. Every chapter's "Common mistakes" section shows how.
- *If an error window (the debugger) pops up,* press ~q~ to close it.
  A later chapter teaches you how to use it properly.

** The running project

Across the chapters you will build a small *text adventure*. You'll
walk between rooms, pick up items and type commands. It starts as a
few lines of data and grows a little with each chapter. Each project
step uses only that chapter's ideas and the ones before it.

* Part I: First Steps

Part I gives you the core of the language: how Lisp reads and runs
code, the basic kinds of data, variables, functions and decisions.
By the end you will write small programs and read most beginner Lisp
code without trouble.

** A note on reading results

Most listings in this guide show their expected result as a comment:

#+begin_src lisp
(+ 1 2)   ; => 3
#+end_src

A semicolon starts a comment. Lisp ignores everything from =;= to the
end of the line. The ~; =>~ comment means "this evaluates to": run the
expression, and the REPL should answer with the value shown.

** Chapter 1: The REPL and the Edit-Evaluate Loop

*** What Lisp does with your code

Lisp works in a loop of four steps:

1. *Read* the text you typed and turn it into data.
2. *Evaluate* that data, which means work out its value.
3. *Print* the value.
4. *Loop* back and wait for more.

That is the REPL. Every time you press ~RET~ on an expression — or
~C-c C-c~ on one in a file — the same thing happens: it is read,
evaluated, and the value is shown.

In Java you write a whole program, compile it, and run =main=. In
Lisp you talk to a running program, one expression at a time, and it
answers. The program grows while it runs. This is the single biggest
change in how you will work.

*** Your first expressions

Run each of these at the REPL.

#+begin_src lisp
42
#+end_src

A number evaluates to itself. You get =42=.

#+begin_src lisp
(+ 2 3)   ; => 5
#+end_src

This is a /call/. The parentheses mean "do something". The first
thing inside is the operator (=+=). The rest are the arguments (=2=
and =3=). This style, operator first, is called /prefix notation/.

#+begin_src lisp
(+ 1 2 3 4 5)   ; => 15
#+end_src

=+= accepts any number of arguments. So do =-=, =*= and many others.

#+begin_src lisp
(* 2 (+ 3 4))   ; => 14
#+end_src

Expressions nest. Lisp works out the inner one first, =(+ 3 4)= is
=7=, then the outer one, =(* 2 7)= is =14=.

There is no operator precedence to remember. In Python, =2 + 3 * 4=
depends on rules about =*= binding tighter than =+=. In Lisp you
write exactly what you mean:

#+begin_src lisp
(+ 2 (* 3 4))   ; => 14
(* (+ 2 3) 4)   ; => 20
#+end_src

When a listing contains several expressions, they all run, top to
bottom, and you see the value of the *last* one. The listing above
shows =20=.

*** Using the REPL directly

Switch to the REPL window (~C-x o~, or ~C-x b~ and type =repl=). At
the =CL-USER>= prompt, type:

#+begin_example
CL-USER> (* 6 7)
42
#+end_example

Press ~RET~ after the closing parenthesis. If you press ~RET~ before
the parentheses are balanced, the REPL waits for the rest. That isn't
an error. Finish typing and press ~RET~ again.

Use ~M-p~ to bring back what you typed before. Use the REPL for quick
experiments. Put anything you want to keep in a file.

*** Printing

=print= writes a value to the output:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(print 42)
(print (+ 1 1))
#+end_src

Text in double quotes is a /string/. Chapter 3 covers strings fully.
For now, you can print one:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(print "Hello, Lisp")
#+end_src

=print= starts a new line before the value. A string prints with its
quotes, because =print= shows values the way Lisp would read them
back.

=princ= prints for humans, without quotes and without the new line:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(princ "Hello, ")
(princ "Lisp")
#+end_src

=print= and =princ= also /return/ the value they printed. So in a
REPL you see the value twice: once printed, once as the result.
That's normal.

Remember the difference between the two kinds of result:

- The *value* of the last expression is what the REPL prints at the
  prompt, and what a =; =>= comment records.
- *Printed* output comes out above it — whatever =print= and =princ=
  wrote on the way.

*** Comments

#+begin_src lisp
;;; Three semicolons: a heading for a section of code.

;; Two semicolons: a comment on its own line,
;; about the code below it.
(+ 1 2)   ; One semicolon: a short note at the end of a line.
#+end_src

Lisp treats all three the same. The number of semicolons is a
convention that tells human readers what kind of comment it is.

*** Your first error

Run this. It has a mistake on purpose:

#+begin_src lisp
(plus 1 2)
#+end_src

A debugger window opens. The first line says something like:

#+begin_example
The function COMMON-LISP-USER::PLUS is undefined.
#+end_example

Read it carefully:

- =PLUS= is the name you used. Lisp shows names in capitals.
  Chapter 3 explains why.
- =COMMON-LISP-USER::= is the area where your names live. You can
  ignore it for now. It's always there for your own code.
- "undefined" means there is no function with that name.

Press ~q~ to close the window. Nothing is broken. The Lisp is still
running and everything you defined is still there.

Errors in Lisp don't crash your program. The program pauses and asks
you what to do. Closing the window with ~q~ means "give up on this
expression and go back to the prompt".

*** Reading parentheses

New Lispers worry about parentheses. Two facts make them easy:

1. *Emacs counts them for you.* Put the cursor right after a closing
   parenthesis and Emacs highlights the opening one.
2. *Experienced Lispers read indentation, not parentheses.* The shape
   of the code on the page shows its structure. Emacs indents it for
   you: select a region and press ~TAB~.

Closing parentheses go together at the end of the last line:

#+begin_src lisp
;; Good Lisp style
(* 2
   (+ 3
      4))

;; Not Lisp style: closing parentheses on their own lines
(* 2
   (+ 3
      4
   )
)
#+end_src

Both work. Only the first looks like Lisp. Use it.

*** Common mistakes

- *Putting the operator in the middle:* =(1 + 2)=. The error is
  "Illegal function call" or "1 is not a function name". The operator
  always comes first: =(+ 1 2)=.
- *Leaving out spaces:* =(+1 2)= is not "plus, one, two". Lisp reads
  =+1= as the number positive one, then complains that =+1= is not a
  function. Always put a space after the operator.
- *Using commas between arguments:* =(+ 1, 2)=. Lisp separates
  arguments with spaces only. A comma here gives "comma not inside a
  backquote", which is a feature you will meet in Part IV.
- *Expecting printed text in the result:* you used =print= but what
  you see is the value that came back. The text was printed above it.
- *Unbalanced parentheses:* you get an "end of file" error. One
  closing parenthesis is missing. Put the cursor after each closing
  parenthesis to find the one that has no partner.

*** Project step: the adventure begins

The project is a text adventure. The player is in a small house and
moves between rooms. In this chapter, print the opening line of the
game.

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(princ "You wake up in a small, quiet house.")
#+end_src

That's the whole step. In the next chapter, the rooms become data.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 1.1

Write an expression that calculates the number of minutes in a week
(7 days, 24 hours per day, 60 minutes per hour).

**** Exercise 1.2

Write this calculation in Lisp: =(10 - 4) * (3 + 2)=.

**** Exercise 1.3

Write this one: =100 / (2 * 5) - 3=.

**** Exercise 1.4

Print the line =Welcome, traveller.= without quotes. Which printing
function earns that, and what does the REPL show you in return?

**** Exercise 1.5

Each line below has a mistake. Say what's wrong and write the fixed
version.

#+begin_example
(5 * 3)
(+2 3)
(+ 1 (* 2 3)
(* 2, 4)
#+end_example

** Chapter 2: Expressions, Atoms and Lists

*** Everything is an expression

In Java, there are /statements/ (like =if= or =for=) and
/expressions/ (like =a + b=). Statements do things. Expressions have
values.

In Lisp, everything is an expression. Everything has a value. You
will see in Chapter 6 that even =if= gives back a value.

Lisp code is built from just two kinds of thing.

*** Atoms

An /atom/ is anything that isn't a list. Some atoms:

#+begin_src lisp
42          ; a number
3.14        ; another number
"hello"     ; a string
#+end_src

Numbers and strings evaluate to themselves.

There is a third important kind of atom: the /symbol/. A symbol is a
name, like =x=, =total=, =+= or =print=. When Lisp evaluates a
symbol, it looks up the value that name stands for. Chapter 3 covers
symbols in depth. Chapter 4 shows how to give them values.

*** Lists

A /list/ is some things inside parentheses, separated by spaces:

#+begin_example
(1 2 3)
(+ 1 2)
(a b c)
(1 (2 3) 4)
()
#+end_example

The last one is the empty list. A list can hold other lists.

Here is the key idea of Lisp: *code is written as lists.* =(+ 1 2)=
is a list of three things: the symbol =+=, the number =1= and the
number =2=. When Lisp evaluates a list, it treats the first element
as the name of something to do, and the rest as the arguments.

*** The evaluation rule

When Lisp evaluates =(f a b)=, it does this:

1. Look at the first element, =f=. It names an operator.
2. Evaluate each argument in order, left to right.
3. Call the operator with those values.

Trace it on a nested example:

#+begin_example
(+ (* 2 3) (- 10 4))
1. The operator is +.
2. Evaluate (* 2 3)  → 6
   Evaluate (- 10 4) → 6
3. Call + with 6 and 6 → 12
#+end_example

Each argument is itself evaluated by the same rule. That's why
nesting works to any depth.

This is the /normal/ rule, for /function calls/. A few special
operators follow different rules. You'll meet them one at a time,
and each time the guide will say exactly how it differs. =quote=,
below, is the first.

*** Quote: "don't evaluate this"

Try to make a list of three numbers:

#+begin_src lisp
(1 2 3)
#+end_src

Error: "illegal function call". Lisp tried to /call/ =1= as an
operator. You want the list as data, not as code.

=quote= stops evaluation. It gives back its argument untouched:

#+begin_src lisp
(quote (1 2 3))   ; => (1 2 3)
#+end_src

You will write =quote= so often that it has a short form, the
apostrophe:

#+begin_src lisp
'(1 2 3)          ; => (1 2 3)
'(+ 1 2)          ; => (+ 1 2)
#+end_src

The second one is the /list/ =(+ 1 2)=, not the number =3=. Quote
keeps code from running and gives it to you as data.

=quote= breaks the normal evaluation rule on purpose: it does *not*
evaluate its argument. That is what makes it special.

*** Building lists with =list=

=list= is an ordinary function. It evaluates its arguments and makes
a list of the results:

#+begin_src lisp
(list 1 2 3)           ; => (1 2 3)
(list 1 (+ 1 1) 3)     ; => (1 2 3)
'(1 (+ 1 1) 3)         ; => (1 (+ 1 1) 3)
#+end_src

Compare the last two. =list= computed =(+ 1 1)=. The quote didn't.

Use quote when you know the exact contents ahead of time. Use =list=
when some parts must be computed.

*** Taking lists apart

#+begin_src lisp
(first '(a b c))    ; => A
(second '(a b c))   ; => B
(third '(a b c))    ; => C
(rest '(a b c))     ; => (B C)
#+end_src

- =first= gives the first element.
- =rest= gives a list of everything after the first element.
- =second= through =tenth= also exist.

Lisp shows =a= as =A=. Chapter 3 explains why.

You'll also see =car= and =cdr= in older code. They mean the same as
=first= and =rest=. Part II explains where those names come from.

#+begin_src lisp
(first (rest '(a b c)))   ; => B
#+end_src

Read nested calls from the inside out: "the rest of =(a b c)= is
=(b c)=, and the first of that is =b=".

*** How results are printed

Results are shown the way the REPL prints them, not the way a
typesetter would. A list stays a list — =(1 2 3)=, not a table — and a
string keeps its quotes, because that is the form Lisp would read
back.

So when you compare your answer with ours, expect the readable form.
The human form — no quotes, no escaping — is what =princ= and
=format= are for.

*** Counting and indexing

#+begin_src lisp
(length '(a b c d))    ; => 4
(nth 0 '(a b c d))     ; => A
(nth 2 '(a b c d))     ; => C
#+end_src

=nth= counts from zero, like array indexes in Java. =(nth 0 ...)= is
the same as =first=. The argument order is index first, then the
list.

*** The empty list and NIL

The empty list has a second name, =nil=:

#+begin_src lisp
'()              ; => NIL
(rest '(a))      ; => NIL
(length nil)     ; => 0
#+end_src

=()= and =nil= are the same thing. Lisp always prints it as =NIL=.

Taking apart an empty list gives back =nil= instead of an error:

#+begin_src lisp
(first '())      ; => NIL
(rest '())       ; => NIL
#+end_src

=nil= also means /false/. The symbol =t= means /true/. Lisp has no
separate true/false type like Java's =boolean=. Any value that isn't
=nil= counts as true. Chapter 6 relies on this.

*** Asking questions about data

A function that answers yes or no is called a /predicate/. It returns
=t= or =nil=. Many predicate names end in =p= (for "predicate"):

#+begin_src lisp
(listp '(1 2))   ; => T     is it a list?
(listp 5)        ; => NIL
(atom 5)         ; => T     is it an atom?
(atom '(1 2))    ; => NIL
(null '())       ; => T     is it the empty list?
(null '(a))      ; => NIL
#+end_src

One surprise: =nil= is both an atom and a list.

#+begin_src lisp
(atom nil)       ; => T
(listp nil)      ; => T
#+end_src

It's an atom because it isn't built from parts. It's a list because it
is the empty list. Part II shows how lists are built, and this will
then look natural.

*** Combining and reversing lists

#+begin_src lisp
(append '(a b) '(c d))       ; => (A B C D)
(append '(1) '(2 3) '(4))    ; => (1 2 3 4)
(reverse '(1 2 3))           ; => (3 2 1)
(last '(1 2 3))              ; => (3)
#+end_src

=append= joins any number of lists into one. =reverse= gives a new
list in the opposite order. Neither changes the lists you gave it.

Look closely at =last=. It returns a list holding the last element,
not the element itself. To get the element, take its =first=:

#+begin_src lisp
(first (last '(1 2 3)))      ; => 3
#+end_src

Part II explains why =last= works this way.

*** Common mistakes

- *Forgetting the quote:* =(first (1 2 3))= gives "illegal function
  call". Lisp tried to call =1=. Write =(first '(1 2 3))=.
- *Quoting something that should be computed:* ='(1 (+ 1 1))= gives
  =(1 (+ 1 1))=, not =(1 2)=. If a part must be worked out, use
  =list=: =(list 1 (+ 1 1))=.
- *Swapping the arguments of =nth=:* =(nth '(a b c) 1)= gives an error
  saying the list "is not of type UNSIGNED-BYTE". The index comes
  first: =(nth 1 '(a b c))=.
- *Counting =nth= from one:* =(nth 1 '(a b c))= is =B=, not =A=.
  Indexes start at zero.
- *Expecting =last= to return the element:* it returns a one-element
  list. Use =(first (last ...))=.
- *Thinking =()= and =nil= are different:* they're the same object.
  Both print as =NIL=.

*** Project step: rooms as data

The house has four rooms. A quoted list holds their names:

#+begin_src lisp
'(bedroom hallway kitchen garden)
;; => (BEDROOM HALLWAY KITCHEN GARDEN)
#+end_src

The player starts in the first room:

#+begin_src lisp
(first '(bedroom hallway kitchen garden))   ; => BEDROOM
#+end_src

A room also needs to know where you can go from it. Put the room's
name first and a list of its neighbours second:

#+begin_src lisp
'(kitchen (hallway garden))
#+end_src

Now take it apart:

#+begin_src lisp
(first '(kitchen (hallway garden)))    ; => KITCHEN
(second '(kitchen (hallway garden)))   ; => (HALLWAY GARDEN)
#+end_src

The first expression returns =KITCHEN=. The second returns the list of
exits.
You've just designed a data format with nothing but lists. In Java
you'd write a =Room= class first. In Lisp you often start with plain
lists and add structure later, when you know what you need.

*** Exercises

Predict the result of each line, then run them one at a time to
check:

#+begin_example
'(+ 2 3)
(list '+ 2 3)
(+ 2 3)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 2.2

Get the symbol =C= out of ='(a b c d)= in three different ways.

**** Exercise 2.3

Get the symbol =BLUE= (not the list =(BLUE)=) out of
='(red green blue)=.

**** Exercise 2.4

Using ='(1 2 3)= and ='(4 5 6)=, build =(6 5 4 3 2 1)= in one
expression.

**** Exercise 2.5

Predict the result of each line:

#+begin_example
(listp nil)
(null '(nil))
(length '((a b) (c d e)))
(rest '(only))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 2.6

The room data ='(garden (kitchen shed))= says the garden connects to
the kitchen and the shed. Write an expression that returns =SHED=.

** Chapter 3: Numbers, Characters, Strings and Symbols

This chapter covers the atoms you'll use every day. Each section is a
small tour. Run everything, and come back to this chapter as a
reference.

*** Integers

Integers in Lisp have no size limit. Java's =int= overflows past about
two billion. Lisp just keeps going:

#+begin_src lisp
(expt 2 10)     ; => 1024
(expt 2 100)    ; => 1267650600228229401496703205376
#+end_src

=expt= raises its first argument to the power of the second. You
never need to think about =int= versus =long= versus =BigInteger=.
Lisp switches to big numbers by itself when needed.

*** Ratios: exact fractions

Here is the first real surprise:

#+begin_src lisp
(/ 6 3)     ; => 2
(/ 7 2)     ; => 7/2
(/ 1 3)     ; => 1/3
#+end_src

Dividing two integers gives an exact answer. If it isn't a whole
number, Lisp gives a /ratio/. Java would give =3= for =7 / 2= and
throw away the remainder. Lisp keeps the exact value.

You can write ratios yourself, and they reduce automatically:

#+begin_src lisp
(+ 1/3 1/6)    ; => 1/2
(* 2/3 3)      ; => 2
(/ 4 6)        ; => 2/3
#+end_src

*** Floats

A number with a decimal point is a /float/:

#+begin_src lisp
(/ 7 2.0)        ; => 3.5
(+ 1.5 2)        ; => 3.5
(float 7/2)      ; => 3.5
(float 1/3)      ; => 0.33333334
#+end_src

If any argument is a float, the result is a float. =float= converts a
number to a float.

By default, =1.5= is a /single-float/, with about 7 digits of
precision, like Java's =float=. For more precision, like Java's
=double=, write =d0= at the end:

#+begin_src lisp
(float 1/3 1d0)  ; => 0.3333333333333333d0
(* 1.5d0 2)      ; => 3.0d0
#+end_src

The second argument to =float= is an example of the precision you
want. The =d0= means "times ten to the power zero, in double
precision". For most work in this guide, integers and ratios are
enough.

*** Arithmetic you'll use

| Expression        | Result | Meaning                         |
|-------------------+--------+---------------------------------|
| =(+ 1 2 3)=       | =6=    | add                             |
| =(- 10 3)=        | =7=    | subtract                        |
| =(- 5)=           | =-5=   | negate                          |
| =(* 2 3 4)=       | =24=   | multiply                        |
| =(/ 12 4)=        | =3=    | divide                          |
| =(1+ 5)=          | =6=    | add one                         |
| =(1- 5)=          | =4=    | subtract one                    |
| =(mod 17 5)=      | =2=    | remainder (like Java's =%=)     |
| =(expt 2 8)=      | =256=  | power                           |
| =(sqrt 16)=       | =4.0=  | square root (gives a float)     |
| =(isqrt 17)=      | =4=    | whole-number square root        |
| =(abs -7)=        | =7=    | absolute value                  |
| =(max 3 9 2)=     | =9=    | largest                         |
| =(min 3 9 2)=     | =2=    | smallest                        |

=1+= is a single name, not "one plus". It's a function called =1+=.
Lisp names can contain characters like =+=, =-= and =*=.

*** Comparing numbers

#+begin_src lisp
(= 3 3)          ; => T
(= 3 3.0)        ; => T
(/= 3 4)         ; => T    "not equal"
(< 1 2)          ; => T
(< 1 2 3 4)      ; => T    each one smaller than the next
(< 1 3 2)        ; => NIL
(>= 5 5)         ; => T
#+end_src

=(< 1 2 3 4)= checks a whole chain at once. In Java you'd write
=1 < 2 && 2 < 3 && 3 < 4=.

=/== is "not equal". There is no =!== in Lisp.

=== compares numbers only. Using it on anything else is an error.

*** Number predicates

#+begin_src lisp
(numberp 5)      ; => T     is it a number?
(integerp 5)     ; => T     is it an integer?
(integerp 5.0)   ; => NIL
(zerop 0)        ; => T     is it zero?
(plusp 3)        ; => T     is it positive?
(minusp -3)      ; => T     is it negative?
(evenp 4)        ; => T
(oddp 4)         ; => NIL
#+end_src

*** Characters

A single character is written with =#\= in front:

#+begin_src lisp
#\a              ; => #\a
#\Z              ; => #\Z
#\Space          ; => #\Space
#\Newline        ; => #\Newline
#+end_src

A character and a one-letter string are different things. =#\a= is a
character. ="a"= is a string that holds one character.

#+begin_src lisp
(characterp #\a)        ; => T
(characterp "a")        ; => NIL
(char-upcase #\a)       ; => #\A
(char-downcase #\Q)     ; => #\q
(char-code #\A)         ; => 65
(code-char 97)          ; => #\a
(char= #\a #\a)         ; => T
(alpha-char-p #\a)      ; => T
(alpha-char-p #\7)      ; => NIL
(digit-char-p #\7)      ; => 7
(upper-case-p #\A)      ; => T
(char< #\a #\b)         ; => T
(char-equal #\a #\A)    ; => T
#+end_src

What each one does:

- =char-upcase= and =char-downcase= change the case of a character.
- =char-code= gives the character's number. =code-char= goes back the
  other way.
- =char== tests whether two characters are the same. Case matters:
  =#\a= and =#\A= are different.
- =char-equal= is the same test, but ignores case.
- =alpha-char-p= asks "is it a letter?"
- =digit-char-p= returns the digit's value, or =nil= if the character
  isn't a digit. A value that isn't =nil= counts as true, so it
  works as a yes/no question too.
- =char<= compares characters by alphabetical order.

Use =char== for characters, not ===. Remember that === is only for
numbers.

*** Strings

A string is text inside double quotes:

#+begin_src lisp
"Hello"              ; => "Hello"
(stringp "Hello")    ; => T
(length "Hello")     ; => 5
#+end_src

To put a double quote inside a string, write =\"=. To put a
backslash in one, write =\\=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(princ "She said \"hi\" and left.")
#+end_src

=princ= shows the text the way a human would read it. =print= would
show the backslashes, because it shows strings the way you'd type
them into Lisp.

A string can span several lines. The line breaks become part of the
text:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(princ "Line one
Line two")
#+end_src

**** Getting characters out of a string

#+begin_src lisp
(char "lisp" 0)      ; => #\l
(char "lisp" 3)      ; => #\p
#+end_src

=char= takes the string first, then the index. Indexes start at zero,
like =nth=. Notice the order is the opposite of =nth=, which takes
the index first. This is a common source of mistakes.

**** Pieces of strings

#+begin_src lisp
(subseq "hello world" 0 5)   ; => "hello"
(subseq "hello world" 6)     ; => "world"
#+end_src

=subseq= ("sub-sequence") takes a start index and an optional end
index. The end index is /not/ included, like Java's =substring=. With
no end, it goes to the end of the string.

#+begin_src lisp
(search "lo" "hello")        ; => 3
(search "xyz" "hello")       ; => NIL
#+end_src

=search= finds where the first string appears inside the second. It
returns the starting index, or =nil= if it isn't there.

**** Joining strings

#+begin_src lisp
(concatenate 'string "Hello" ", " "world")   ; => "Hello, world"
#+end_src

The first argument, ='string=, is a quoted symbol. It tells
=concatenate= what kind of result to build. =concatenate= can also
join lists:

#+begin_src lisp
(concatenate 'list '(1 2) '(3 4))   ; => (1 2 3 4)
#+end_src

For strings you'll always write ='string=. Forgetting it is one of
the most common beginner errors.

**** Changing case and trimming

#+begin_src lisp
(string-upcase "hello")            ; => "HELLO"
(string-downcase "HELLO")          ; => "hello"
(string-capitalize "hello world")  ; => "Hello World"
(string-trim " " "   hi   ")       ; => "hi"
(reverse "abc")                    ; => "cba"
#+end_src

=string-trim= removes the characters in its first argument from both
ends of the second. All of these return a /new/ string. The original
is unchanged.

=reverse= and =length= work on strings as well as lists. Lists and
strings are both /sequences/, and many functions accept either.
Part III covers sequences fully.

**** Comparing strings

#+begin_src lisp
(string= "abc" "abc")       ; => T
(string= "abc" "ABC")       ; => NIL
(string-equal "abc" "ABC")  ; => T
#+end_src

=string== cares about case. =string-equal= ignores it. This matches
the =char== and =char-equal= pair.

Alphabetical order:

#+begin_src lisp
(string< "apple" "banana")  ; => 0
(string< "banana" "apple")  ; => NIL
#+end_src

=string<= does not return =T= when the answer is yes. It returns the
index where the two strings first differ. That's =0= here, because
=a= and =b= differ at position zero. Since =0= isn't =nil=, it counts
as true. If you only need yes or no, you can treat any non-=nil=
result as yes.

**** Turning other things into strings

#+begin_src lisp
(princ-to-string 42)       ; => "42"
(princ-to-string 'kitchen) ; => "KITCHEN"
(string #\a)               ; => "a"
#+end_src

=princ-to-string= gives the text that =princ= would have printed, as
a string. =string= turns a character into a one-character string.

*** Symbols

You've used symbols since Chapter 1: =+=, =first=, =kitchen=. A symbol
is a name. It's a real object that Lisp creates the first time it
reads that name, and it's the same object every time you write that
name again.

**** Two jobs of a symbol

1. *As code*, a symbol names something: a function (=+=), or a value
   (Chapter 4 shows how).
2. *As data*, after a quote, a symbol is just itself:

#+begin_src lisp
'kitchen          ; => KITCHEN
(symbolp 'kitchen)  ; => T
(symbolp "kitchen") ; => NIL
#+end_src

Use symbols as data for fixed labels: room names, directions, colours,
states. In Java you'd use an =enum=. In Lisp you just write the name.

**** Why everything is in capitals

When Lisp reads your code, it converts symbol names to upper case.
=kitchen=, =Kitchen= and =KITCHEN= all become the same symbol,
=KITCHEN=:

#+begin_src lisp
'Kitchen               ; => KITCHEN
(symbol-name 'kitchen) ; => "KITCHEN"
#+end_src

=symbol-name= gives the name as a string. You still write code in
lower case. Lisp displays symbols in capitals. That's all there is to
it.

If you really need lower-case letters in a symbol, put the name
between vertical bars: ='|Kitchen|=. You'll rarely need this. When
you see vertical bars in a result, it usually means you made a
symbol from a lower-case string by mistake.

Strings keep their case. ="Kitchen"= stays ="Kitchen"=. Use strings
for text a human reads, and symbols for labels the program uses.

**** Is it the same symbol?

=eq= tells you whether two symbols are the same one:

#+begin_src lisp
(eq 'north 'north)   ; => T
(eq 'north 'NORTH)   ; => T
(eq 'north 'south)   ; => NIL
#+end_src

For symbols, =eq= is the right test. For the other atoms you know:

| To compare | Use          |
|------------+--------------|
| numbers    | ===          |
| characters | =char==      |
| strings    | =string==    |
| symbols    | =eq=         |

Part IV covers equality fully, including how to compare lists.

**** Keywords

A symbol that starts with a colon is a /keyword/:

#+begin_src lisp
:north              ; => :NORTH
(keywordp :north)   ; => T
#+end_src

A keyword evaluates to itself, so it never needs a quote. You'll use
keywords a lot from Chapter 5 onwards, as labels for function
arguments.

**** T and NIL are symbols

=t= and =nil= are symbols too, and like keywords they evaluate to
themselves:

#+begin_src lisp
t      ; => T
nil    ; => NIL
(symbolp t)   ; => T
#+end_src

*** Common mistakes

- *Integer division surprise:* expecting =(/ 7 2)= to give =3= or
  =3.5=. It gives =7/2=. Use =(float (/ 7 2))= for a decimal, or
  =(floor 7 2)= for the whole-number part. (=floor= shows a second
  number in the REPL, the remainder. The first number is the answer.)
- *Using === on non-numbers:* =(= "a" "a")= gives an error saying the
  value "is not a NUMBER". Use =string== for strings, =char== for
  characters, =eq= for symbols.
- *Forgetting ='string= in =concatenate=:* =(concatenate "a" "b")=
  gives an error about an unknown type or "is not a valid sequence
  type specifier". Write =(concatenate 'string "a" "b")=.
- *Mixing up =char= and =nth= argument order:* =char= takes the
  string first, =(char "abc" 0)=. =nth= takes the index first,
  =(nth 0 '(a b c))=.
- *Confusing ="a"= and =#\a=:* functions for characters won't accept a
  string. =(char-upcase "a")= is an error. Use =(char-upcase #\a)= or
  =(string-upcase "a")=.
- *Unescaped quote in a string:* ="He said "hi""= ends the string early
  and confuses Lisp. Write ="He said \"hi\""=.
- *Expecting =string<= to return =T=:* it returns an index. Any number
  means yes.
- *Expecting symbols to keep their case:* ='Kitchen= becomes
  =KITCHEN=. If you need to show it nicely, use =string-capitalize=
  on its name.

*** Project step: room descriptions

A room now has three parts: its name, a description for the player,
and its exits.

#+begin_src lisp
'(kitchen "A warm kitchen that smells of bread." (hallway garden))
#+end_src

The name is a symbol, because the program uses it as a label. The
description is a string, because the player reads it.

Build the line the game shows when you enter the kitchen:

#+begin_src lisp
(concatenate 'string
             "You are in the "
             (string-capitalize
              (symbol-name
               (first '(kitchen "A warm kitchen that smells of bread."
                        (hallway garden)))))
             ". "
             (second '(kitchen "A warm kitchen that smells of bread."
                       (hallway garden))))
;; => "You are in the Kitchen. A warm kitchen that smells of bread."
#+end_src

Read it from the inside out:

1. =(first '(kitchen ...))= gives the symbol =KITCHEN=.
2. =symbol-name= turns it into the string ="KITCHEN"=.
3. =string-capitalize= turns that into ="Kitchen"=.
4. =(second '(kitchen ...))= gives the description.
5. =concatenate= joins all four strings.

You had to type the room list twice. That's clumsy, and it's exactly
the problem Chapter 4 solves: you'll give the list a name and use the
name instead.

To show the line to the player, wrap the same expression in =princ=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(princ (concatenate 'string
                    "You are in the "
                    (string-capitalize (symbol-name 'kitchen))
                    ". "
                    "A warm kitchen that smells of bread."))
#+end_src

You should see the text without quotes:

#+begin_example
You are in the Kitchen. A warm kitchen that smells of bread.
#+end_example

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 3.1

Add 2/3 and 1/4 and get the exact answer. Then write a second
expression that gives the same answer as a decimal.

**** Exercise 3.2

Predict the result of each line, then check:

#+begin_example
(/ 10 4)
(/ 10 4.0)
(mod 10 4)
(isqrt 50)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 3.3

Use =subseq= to get ="Lisp"= out of ="I love Lisp"=.

**** Exercise 3.4

Starting from the strings ="hello"= and ="world"=, build
="HELLO, WORLD!"= in one expression.

**** Exercise 3.5

Write two expressions:

1. One that gives the numeric value of the character =#\7=.
2. One that turns =#\b= into upper case and then gives its character
   code.

**** Exercise 3.6

Predict the result of each line:

#+begin_example
(eq 'Apple 'APPLE)
(string= "Apple" "APPLE")
(string-equal "Apple" "APPLE")
(symbol-name :north)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 3.7 (project)

Given this room:

#+begin_example
'(garden "An overgrown garden." (kitchen shed))
#+end_example

Write an expression that returns the string
="You are in the Garden. An overgrown garden."=. Take the name and
the description out of the list. Don't type them in by hand.

** Chapter 4: Variables

So far, every value had to be typed out in full, sometimes twice.
A /variable/ gives a value a name, so you can use the name instead.

Lisp has two kinds of variables:

- *Global variables* exist everywhere, for as long as Lisp runs.
- *Local variables* exist only inside one piece of code.

*** Global variables with =defparameter=

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *price* 25)
#+end_src

The result is =*PRICE*=, the name of the variable you just made.
=defparameter= returns the name, not the value.

Now use it:

#+begin_src lisp
*price*                 ; => 25
(* *price* 4)           ; => 100
#+end_src

This is the evaluation rule for symbols from Chapter 2: when Lisp
evaluates a symbol, it looks up the value the name stands for. Before
this chapter, the only symbols you evaluated were =t= and =nil=, which
stand for themselves. Now you can make your own.

Compare with the quoted symbol:

#+begin_src lisp
'*price*                ; => *PRICE*
#+end_src

Quoted, it's just the name. Unquoted, it's the value.

**** The stars

The stars around =*price*= are part of the name. They're called
/earmuffs/. Every Lisp programmer writes global variables this way, so
that a reader can tell at a glance which names are global. Lisp
doesn't force you, but always do it. A later chapter shows a real
reason behind the habit, and you'll already have it.

**** Variables live in the running Lisp

The variable now exists in your Lisp session. Every later expression
can use it. If you restart Lisp (or Emacs), it's gone. Run the
=defparameter= again to bring it back.

*** Changing a variable with =setf=

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *price* 30)       ; => 30
*price*                 ; => 30
#+end_src

=setf= ("set field") stores a new value in an existing variable and
returns that value. It's Lisp's version of Java's ===.

=setf= can set several variables at once, in pairs:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *width* 0)
(defparameter *height* 0)
(setf *width* 7
      *height* 3)
(* *width* *height*)    ; => 21
#+end_src

**** Adding and subtracting: =incf= and =decf=

Changing a number by a little is common enough to have its own
operators:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *score* 0)
(incf *score*)          ; => 1
(incf *score*)          ; => 2
(incf *score* 10)       ; => 12
(decf *score* 5)        ; => 7
#+end_src

=incf= adds (1 by default), =decf= subtracts. Both change the
variable and return its new value. They're like Java's =score++= and
=score += 10=.

Run the =incf= lines a second time, without the =defparameter=, and
you get different numbers: =*score*= keeps the value it had. The
=defparameter= is what resets it to =0=. Keep this in mind for the next
section.

*** =defvar=: define once, keep the value

=defvar= also makes a global variable, with one difference. If the
variable already exists, =defvar= leaves it alone.

Run each of these once, in order:

#+begin_src lisp
(defvar *lives* 3)
*lives*                 ; => 3
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *lives* 1)
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(defvar *lives* 3)
*lives*                 ; => 1
#+end_src

The second =defvar= did nothing, because =*lives*= already had a
value. With =defparameter=, it would have gone back to =3=.

When to use which:

| Use            | When                                                      |
|----------------+-----------------------------------------------------------|
| =defparameter= | Settings and data you want reset each time you re-run the code |
| =defvar=       | State you build up while the program runs, and don't want lost if you re-run the file |

When you're not sure, use =defparameter=. Its behaviour is easier to
predict.

*** Constants with =defconstant=

#+begin_src lisp
(defconstant +days-per-week+ 7)
(* +days-per-week+ 52)  ; => 364
#+end_src

A constant can never be changed. =(setf +days-per-week+ 8)= is an
error. The plus signs around the name are the convention for
constants, like the stars for global variables.

Use constants only for values that truly never change. For anything
else, =defparameter= is more convenient.

*** Local variables with =let=

Global variables are handy, but most names in a program are only
needed for a moment. =let= makes variables that exist only inside it:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((width 7)
      (height 3))
  (* width height))     ; => 21
#+end_src

The shape of a =let=:

#+begin_example
(let ((name1 value1)        ← a list of bindings
      (name2 value2))         each binding is (name value)
  expression1               ← the body: one or more expressions
  expression2)              ← the value of the last one is the result
#+end_example

Watch the parentheses. There's one pair around the whole list of
bindings, and one pair around each binding. That's why a =let= starts
with =((=.

Linking a name to a value is called /binding/ the name. The names
=width= and =height= are /bound/ only inside the body. Outside, they
don't exist:

#+begin_src lisp
width
#+end_src

The debugger opens with "The variable WIDTH is unbound." "Unbound"
means "this name has no value here". Press ~q~.

Local variables don't get earmuffs. The stars are only for globals.

**** The body can have several expressions

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(let ((name "Ada")
      (age 36))
  (print name)
  (print age))
#+end_src

The body runs top to bottom. The two =print= calls write their lines,
and the form's value is =36= — the value of the last expression.

**** Values can be computed

The value in a binding is evaluated first, like any argument:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((total (* 25 4))
      (items (list 'pen 'ink)))
  (list total (length items)))   ; => (100 2)
#+end_src

**** Changing a local variable

=setf= and =incf= work on local variables too:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((count 0))
  (incf count)
  (incf count)
  count)                ; => 2
#+end_src

**** Using globals inside =let=

The body can use global variables as well as its own:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *tax-rate* 1/5)

(let ((price 50))
  (+ price (* price *tax-rate*)))   ; => 60
#+end_src

*** =let*=: bindings that use earlier bindings

In a =let=, every value is worked out /before/ any name is bound. So a
binding can't use a name from the same =let=:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((a 2)
      (b (* a 10)))
  b)
#+end_src

Error: "The variable A is unbound." When Lisp computed =(* a 10)=,
=a= didn't exist yet. Press ~q~.

=let*= binds the names one at a time, top to bottom. Each value can
use the names above it:

#+begin_src lisp
(let* ((a 2)
       (b (* a 10)))
  b)                    ; => 20
#+end_src

A good habit: use =let= by default and switch to =let*= when one
binding needs another. Readers then know that a =let*= has bindings
that depend on each other.

*** Nesting and shadowing

A =let= can go inside another one:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((x 1))
  (let ((y 2))
    (+ x y)))           ; => 3
#+end_src

The inner body sees both =x= and =y=.

If the inner =let= uses the same name, the inner one wins while
you're inside it. This is called /shadowing/:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((x 1))
  (list x
        (let ((x 100))
          x)
        x))             ; => (1 100 1)
#+end_src

Read it slowly. The first =x= is =1=. Inside the inner =let=, =x= is
=100=. After the inner =let= ends, =x= is =1= again. The inner
binding never changed the outer one. It only hid it for a while.

*** How to choose

- Need a value in one calculation? Use =let=.
- Need it to stay around between expressions, or for the whole program?
  Use =defparameter= or =defvar=.
- Never changes? Use =defconstant=.

Most Lisp code uses many local variables and only a few globals.

*** Common mistakes

- *=setf= on a name that was never defined:* =(setf *gold* 10)= with
  no =defparameter= first. SBCL warns "undefined variable: *GOLD*".
  It may seem to work, but the behaviour isn't reliable. Always
  create globals with =defparameter= or =defvar= first.
- *Missing parentheses in =let=:* =(let (x 5) x)= is wrong. Lisp reads
  =(x 5)= as the list of bindings, and complains that the binding =5=
  is malformed. Write =(let ((x 5)) x)=.
- *Using a name from the same =let=:* =(let ((a 1) (b (+ a 1))) b)=
  gives "The variable A is unbound". Use =let*=.
- *Using a local name outside its =let=:* the name is unbound there.
  Move the code inside the body.
- *Expecting =defvar= to reset a value:* re-running =(defvar *x* 0)=
  keeps the old value. Use =setf= to change it, or use =defparameter=.
- *Quoting a variable:* =(* '*price* 2)= gives "*PRICE* is not a
  NUMBER". The quote gives you the name, not the value. Leave the
  quote off.
- *Running code out of order:* after restarting Lisp, an expression
  that uses =*price*= fails with "unbound". Run the =defparameter=
  first.
- *Forgetting the earmuffs when using a global:* if you defined
  =*price*=, then =price= is a different name and is unbound.

*** Project step: naming the rooms

Give each room a name, so you never type it twice again:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *bedroom*
  '(bedroom "A small bedroom with an unmade bed." (hallway)))

(defparameter *hallway*
  '(hallway "A narrow hallway with creaky floorboards." (bedroom kitchen)))

(defparameter *kitchen*
  '(kitchen "A warm kitchen that smells of bread." (hallway garden)))

(defparameter *garden*
  '(garden "An overgrown garden." (kitchen)))
#+end_src

Now keep track of where the player is. A global variable holds the
current room. The player starts in the bedroom:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *current-room* *bedroom*)
#+end_src

=*current-room*= now holds the same list as =*bedroom*=.

Build the description with =let=, so each part has a clear name:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((name (first *current-room*))
      (description (second *current-room*)))
  (concatenate 'string
               "You are in the "
               (string-capitalize (symbol-name name))
               ". "
               description))
;; => "You are in the Bedroom. A small bedroom with an unmade bed."
#+end_src

Compare this with the Chapter 3 version. The room list appears
nowhere in the code. The =let= names its parts, and the last
expression puts them together.

Move the player to the hallway, then run the description again:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* *hallway*)
#+end_src

The same description code now says "You are in the Hallway." The
code didn't change. Only the data did.

You still have to copy the description each time you want to
use it. Chapter 5 turns it into a function you can call by name.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 4.1

Make two global variables: =*apples*= with the value 12 and
=*pears*= with the value 8. Write an expression for the total number
of fruit. Then change =*pears*= to 20 with =setf= and run the total
again.

**** Exercise 4.2

Predict the final value of =*mode*=, then check by running the lines
in order:

#+begin_example
(defvar *mode* 'easy)
(setf *mode* 'hard)
(defvar *mode* 'easy)
(defparameter *mode* 'normal)
(defvar *mode* 'easy)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 4.3

Using =let=, bind =width= to 8 and =height= to 5. Return a list of
the area and the perimeter.

**** Exercise 4.4

Using =let*=, bind =celsius= to 30, then =fahrenheit= computed from
it with the formula F = C × 9/5 + 32. Return a list of both values.

**** Exercise 4.5

Make a global =*steps*= starting at 0. Write one form that adds 1
three times and then adds 10, and shows the final value.

**** Exercise 4.6

Each line has a mistake. Say what's wrong and fix it.

#+begin_example
(let (total 10) total)
(let ((a 3) (b (* a a))) b)
(* '*apples* 2)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 4.7

Predict the result:

#+begin_example
(let ((x 5))
  (let ((x (* x 2)))
    (setf x (+ x 1))
    x))
#+end_example

Then predict what changes if you replace the last line with
=(list x)= placed /after/ the inner =let= instead of inside it.

**** Exercise 4.8 (project)

Set =*current-room*= to =*kitchen*=. Using =let=, write an expression
that returns a list of two things: the room's name and the number of
exits it has.

** Chapter 5: Functions

In Chapter 4 you still had to copy the description code each time
you wanted it. A /function/ gives a piece of code a name, so you can
run it again with different inputs.

*** Defining a function with =defun=

#+begin_src lisp
(defun twice (n)
  (* n 2))
#+end_src

The result is =TWICE=, the function's name. Now call it like any
built-in function:

#+begin_src lisp
(twice 21)            ; => 42
(twice (twice 5))    ; => 20
(+ (twice 3) 1)       ; => 7
#+end_src

The shape of a =defun=:

#+begin_example
(defun name (parameter1 parameter2)   ← name, then the parameter list
  expression1                         ← the body
  expression2)                        ← its last value is returned
#+end_example

When you call =(twice 21)=:

1. Lisp evaluates the argument, =21=.
2. It binds the parameter =n= to =21=, just like a =let=.
3. It runs the body, =(* n 2)=.
4. The value of the last expression, =42=, is the result.

There is no =return= statement. A function returns the value of the
last expression in its body. In Java you write =return n * 2;=. In
Lisp, the last expression /is/ the return value.

*** Parameters are local variables

A parameter works exactly like a variable made by =let=. It exists
only inside the function body:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun area (width height)
  (* width height))

(area 7 3)             ; => 21
#+end_src

Outside the function, =width= and =height= are unbound. Each call
gets its own fresh bindings, so calls never interfere with each
other.

You can use =let= and =let*= inside a body, as you would anywhere
else:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun rectangle-info (width height)
  (let ((area (* width height))
        (perimeter (* 2 (+ width height))))
    (list area perimeter)))

(rectangle-info 8 5)   ; => (40 26)
#+end_src

*** Functions with no parameters

The parameter list can be empty, but it must be there:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun dice-faces ()
  6)

(dice-faces)           ; => 6
#+end_src

To call it, put the name in parentheses with nothing after it.
Writing just =dice-faces= without parentheses asks for a /variable/
called =dice-faces=, which doesn't exist.

*** A body with several expressions

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(defun shout (text)
  (princ (string-upcase text))
  (princ "!"))

(shout "hello")
#+end_src

The body runs top to bottom. The value of the last expression is
returned. Here that's the value of the second =princ=, which is
="!"=; the printed text =HELLO!= comes out first, on its own line.

Ask for the value alone and you see just that:

#+begin_src lisp
(shout "hello")        ; => "!"
#+end_src

The text is still printed — that is what the output is for — and the
value is what comes back at the end.

Keep two ideas apart:

- *Printing* shows text to a human.
- *Returning* gives a value back to the code that called the
  function.

Most functions should return values and not print. A returned value
can be used by other code. Printed text can only be read. Print only
in the few functions whose job is to talk to the user.

*** Documentation strings

If the first thing in the body is a string, and more expressions
follow it, that string is the function's /documentation string/, or
/docstring/:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun twice (n)
  "Return N multiplied by two."
  (* n 2))
#+end_src

Lisp keeps it, and you can ask for it:

#+begin_src lisp
(documentation 'twice 'function)   ; => "Return N multiplied by two."
#+end_src

In Emacs, put the cursor on a function name and press ~C-c C-d d~ to
see its documentation. It works on built-in functions too. Try it on
=concatenate=.

By convention, parameter names in a docstring are written in
capitals, like =N= above.

*** Redefining a function

You just defined =twice= a second time. Lisp replaced the old
definition with the new one. SBCL may print a note like:

#+begin_example
WARNING: redefining COMMON-LISP-USER::TWICE in DEFUN
#+end_example

That's harmless. It's telling you what you already know.

This is how you work in Lisp. You don't restart the program to fix a
function. You change the =defun=, run it again with ~C-c C-c~, and
the running program uses the new version immediately.

*** Functions calling functions

Functions you define can call each other, and the built-in ones:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun square (n)
  (* n n))

(defun sum-of-squares (a b)
  (+ (square a) (square b)))

(sum-of-squares 3 4)   ; => 25
#+end_src

You can define them in any order, as long as each one exists by the
time it is /called/. If you define =sum-of-squares= first, SBCL may
warn "undefined function: SQUARE". That's only a reminder. Once
=square= is defined, the call works.

*** Optional parameters: =&optional=

Parameters after =&optional= can be left out when calling:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun greeting (name &optional (punctuation "."))
  (concatenate 'string "Hello, " name punctuation))

(greeting "Ada")       ; => "Hello, Ada."
(greeting "Ada" "!")   ; => "Hello, Ada!"
#+end_src

=(punctuation ".")= means "a parameter called =punctuation=, with the
default ="."= if the caller leaves it out". The shape is the same as
a =let= binding: a name and a value.

If you write a plain name with no default, it becomes =nil= when
left out:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun show-optional (a &optional b)
  (list a b))

(show-optional 1)      ; => (1 NIL)
(show-optional 1 2)    ; => (1 2)
#+end_src

Optional arguments are filled in order. To give the second one, you
must also give the first.

*** Keyword parameters: =&key=

Keyword parameters are given by name, in any order:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-label (text &key (prefix "[") (suffix "]"))
  (concatenate 'string prefix text suffix))

(make-label "ok")                      ; => "[ok]"
(make-label "ok" :prefix "<")          ; => "<ok]"
(make-label "ok" :suffix ">" :prefix "<")  ; => "<ok>"
#+end_src

When calling, you write the keyword (Chapter 3), then its value. The
keyword =:prefix= matches the parameter =prefix=.

Use =&key= when a function has several settings that callers will
rarely change. Many built-in functions work this way. You'll see
keyword arguments throughout the rest of this guide.

Use =&optional= for one or two arguments in a natural order. Use
=&key= for anything more. Don't use both in the same function: the
combination behaves in confusing ways.

*** Any number of arguments: =&rest=

=&rest= collects all remaining arguments into a list:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun collect (&rest things)
  things)

(collect)              ; => NIL
(collect 1 2 3)        ; => (1 2 3)
#+end_src

You can put normal parameters before it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun tag (label &rest items)
  (list label (length items) items))

(tag 'fruit 'apple 'pear)   ; => (FRUIT 2 (APPLE PEAR))
#+end_src

This is how =+= and =list= accept any number of arguments.

*** The parameter list, in full

#+begin_example
(defun name (required1 required2
             &optional (opt1 default1)
             &rest more
             &key (key1 default1))
  body)
#+end_example

You will rarely need more than one of =&optional=, =&rest= and =&key=
at a time. The required parameters always come first.

*** Naming functions

- Use lower case with hyphens: =sum-of-squares=, not =sumOfSquares=.
  Lisp would turn =sumOfSquares= into =SUMOFSQUARES= anyway.
- A function that answers yes or no ends in =p=: =emptyp=,
  =room-dark-p=. Use =-p= when the name already has a hyphen, and
  plain =p= when it's a single word.
- Don't reuse the names of built-in functions such as =list=, =first=
  or =room=. SBCL stops you with "Lock on package COMMON-LISP
  violated". Pick another name. (If it happens, choose the restart
  that says to ignore the lock only if you know what it means;
  otherwise press ~q~.)

*** Common mistakes

- *Wrong number of arguments:* =(area 7)= when =area= needs two. The
  debugger says "invalid number of arguments: 1". Check the parameter
  list. Press ~C-c C-d d~ on the name if you forget it.
- *Forgetting the empty parameter list:* =(defun dice-faces 6)=. Lisp
  reads =6= as the parameter list and complains. Write
  =(defun dice-faces () 6)=.
- *Calling without parentheses:* writing =dice-faces= instead of
  =(dice-faces)=. The error is "The variable DICE-FACES is unbound".
- *Quoting the parameter:* =(defun twice (n) (* 'n 2))= gives "N is
  not a NUMBER". Inside the body, use =n= without a quote.
- *A typo in the body:* =(defun area (width height) (* widht height))=.
  SBCL warns "undefined variable: WIDHT" when you define it. Read
  those warnings: they catch the bug before you call the function.
- *Printing instead of returning:* a function that ends with =princ=
  returns the printed value, which is usually not what the caller
  needs. Let the function return the value, and print it at the place
  that talks to the user.
- *A docstring that is the whole body:* =(defun name () "Ada")=
  returns ="Ada"=. A string counts as a docstring only when more
  expressions follow it.
- *Forgetting the keyword when calling:* =(make-label "ok" "<")= gives
  an error about an "odd number of &KEY arguments". Write
  =(make-label "ok" :prefix "<")=.

*** Project step: room functions

Run the Chapter 4 project code first, so the room variables exist.

Each room is a list of name, description and exits. Instead of
remembering which position holds what, give each part a function:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun room-name (place)
  "Return the name symbol of PLACE."
  (first place))

(defun room-description (place)
  "Return the description string of PLACE."
  (second place))

(defun room-exits (place)
  "Return the list of room names PLACE connects to."
  (third place))
#+end_src

The parameter is called =place= because =room= is the name of a
built-in function.

#+begin_src lisp
(room-exits *hallway*)     ; => (BEDROOM KITCHEN)
#+end_src

These small functions are called /accessors/. If you later change how
a room is stored, you only change these three functions. The rest of
the game keeps working.

Now the description, written once:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun describe-room (place)
  "Return the text shown when the player is in PLACE."
  (concatenate 'string
               "You are in the "
               (string-capitalize (symbol-name (room-name place)))
               ". "
               (room-description place)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(describe-room *kitchen*)
;; => "You are in the Kitchen. A warm kitchen that smells of bread."
#+end_src

The player mostly wants to know about the room they're standing in.
A function with no parameters handles that:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun look ()
  "Describe the room the player is in."
  (describe-room *current-room*))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* *bedroom*)
(look)
;; => "You are in the Bedroom. A small bedroom with an unmade bed."
#+end_src

=look= reads the global =*current-room*= each time it runs. Change the
variable and =look= follows:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* *garden*)
(look)
;; => "You are in the Garden. An overgrown garden."
#+end_src

The player still moves by you typing =setf=, and nothing stops them
from walking from the bedroom straight into the garden. Chapter 6
adds the decisions a real =go= command needs.

itself.

**** Exercise 5.2

Write a function =average3= that takes three numbers and returns
their average. Give it a docstring.

**** Exercise 5.3

Write a function =greet= that takes a name and returns
="Hello, NAME!"= with the name in capitals. =(greet "ada")= should
give ="Hello, ADA!"=.

**** Exercise 5.4

Write a function =power-of= that takes a base and an optional
exponent, which defaults to 2. =(power-of 5)= gives =25=,
=(power-of 2 10)= gives =1024=.

**** Exercise 5.5

Write a function =make-greeting= that takes a name and two keyword
parameters: =greeting= (default ="Hello"=) and =punctuation=
(default ="!"=). =(make-greeting "Ada" :greeting "Welcome")= should
give ="Welcome, Ada!"=.

**** Exercise 5.6

Write a function =count-args= that accepts any number of arguments
and returns how many it got.

**** Exercise 5.7

Each line has a mistake. Say what's wrong and fix it.

#+begin_example
(defun add (a b) (+ a b c))
(defun (twice n) (* 2 n))
(add 1)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 5.8 (project)

Write two functions using the room accessors:

1. =exit-count=, which takes a room and returns how many exits it
   has.
2. =room-title=, which takes a room and returns its name as a
   capitalised string, such as ="Kitchen"=.

** Chapter 6: Conditionals

Programs need to make choices: is the exit open, is the score high
enough, did the player type a real command. This chapter covers every
way Lisp makes a choice.

*** True and false, again

From Chapter 2:

- =nil= means false.
- Every other value means true. =t= is the usual way to write
  "true", but =0=, =""= and ='(a)= are all true as well.

This is different from Python, where =0= and =""= are false. In
Lisp, only =nil= (the empty list) is false.

*** =if=

#+begin_src lisp
(if (> 5 3)
    "bigger"
    "not bigger")      ; => "bigger"
#+end_src

The shape:

#+begin_example
(if test
    then-expression     ← evaluated when test is true
    else-expression)    ← evaluated when test is nil
#+end_example

=if= is a special operator. It does *not* evaluate all its arguments
like a function would. It evaluates the test first, then *only one*
of the other two. You can see this:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(if t
    (print "then")
    (print "else"))
#+end_src

Only ="then"= is printed. The else part never runs.

=if= returns the value of whichever branch ran. That makes it an
expression, like Java's =test ? a : b=, and you can use it anywhere
a value goes:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun describe-number (n)
  (concatenate 'string
               "The number is "
               (if (evenp n) "even" "odd")))

(describe-number 7)    ; => "The number is odd"
#+end_src

The else part can be left out. Then =if= returns =nil= when the test
fails:

#+begin_src lisp
(if (> 1 5) "yes")     ; => NIL
#+end_src

*** =progn=: several expressions where one is expected

Each branch of =if= is a single expression. To do two things in a
branch, wrap them in =progn=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(if (> 5 3)
    (progn
      (print "5 is bigger")
      (print "doing a second thing"))
    (print "not bigger"))
#+end_src

=progn= runs its expressions in order and returns the value of the
last one. You've seen this behaviour before: the body of =let= and
=defun= works the same way. =progn= gives you that behaviour anywhere.

*** =when= and =unless=

When there is no else part, =when= is clearer than =if=, and its body
can hold several expressions without =progn=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(when (> 5 3)
  (print "5 is bigger")
  (print "no progn needed"))
#+end_src

=unless= is the opposite: it runs its body when the test is =nil=.

#+begin_src lisp
(unless (> 1 5)
  "1 is not bigger than 5")   ; => "1 is not bigger than 5"
#+end_src

Both return =nil= when the body doesn't run.

How to choose:

| Use      | When                                          |
|----------+-----------------------------------------------|
| =if=     | You need both a "then" and an "else"           |
| =when=   | Only a "then", especially with several steps   |
| =unless= | Only something to do when the test fails       |

*** =not=, =and=, =or=

#+begin_src lisp
(not nil)              ; => T
(not 5)                ; => NIL
(and (> 5 3) (< 1 2))  ; => T
(or (> 1 5) (< 1 2))   ; => T
#+end_src

=not= turns true into =nil= and =nil= into =t=. =and= and =or= take
any number of arguments.

Two things make them more useful than Java's =&&= and =||=.

**** They stop early

=and= stops at the first =nil=. =or= stops at the first true value.
The rest isn't evaluated:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(or t (print "never printed"))
#+end_src

Nothing is printed. This lets you guard a test:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun safe-divide-check (a b)
  (and (/= b 0) (> (/ a b) 1)))

(safe-divide-check 10 0)   ; => NIL  (no division by zero)
(safe-divide-check 10 2)   ; => T
#+end_src

When =b= is =0=, =and= stops before dividing.

**** They return real values

=or= returns the first true value itself, not just =t=. =and=
returns the last value if all are true:

#+begin_src lisp
(or nil 5 10)          ; => 5
(or nil nil)           ; => NIL
(and 1 2 3)            ; => 3
(and 1 nil 3)          ; => NIL
#+end_src

A common use of =or= is a fallback value:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun display-name (name)
  (or name "stranger"))

(display-name "Ada")   ; => "Ada"
(display-name nil)     ; => "stranger"
#+end_src

*** =cond=: many choices

When there are more than two choices, nested =if= gets hard to read.
=cond= lists the choices one after another:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun temperature-word (degrees)
  (cond ((< degrees 0)  "freezing")
        ((< degrees 15) "cold")
        ((< degrees 25) "mild")
        (t              "hot")))

(temperature-word -5)   ; => "freezing"
(temperature-word 20)   ; => "mild"
(temperature-word 31)   ; => "hot"
#+end_src

The shape:

#+begin_example
(cond (test1 expression ...)   ← a clause: a test, then a body
      (test2 expression ...)
      (t     expression ...))  ← t is always true: the "otherwise"
#+end_example

=cond= tries each test from the top. At the first test that is true,
it runs that clause's body and returns its last value. It ignores
every clause after it. That's why the order matters: =20= is also
less than =25=, but the ="cold"= test failed first and the ="mild"=
test matched.

The final =(t ...)= clause catches everything else. If no clause
matches and there's no =t= clause, =cond= returns =nil=.

A clause body can hold several expressions, like =when=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(let ((n 7))
  (cond ((evenp n)
         (print "even")
         (print "divisible by two"))
        (t
         (print "odd"))))
#+end_src

Note the doubled parentheses at the start of each clause: one pair
opens the clause, the next opens the test.

*** =case=: choosing by exact value

When every test is "is this value equal to X?", =case= is shorter:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun day-number (day)
  (case day
    (monday 1)
    (tuesday 2)
    (wednesday 3)
    (otherwise 0)))

(day-number 'tuesday)  ; => 2
(day-number 'sunday)   ; => 0
#+end_src

=case= evaluates =day= once, then compares it to the key at the start
of each clause. The keys are *not evaluated*, so you don't quote them:
write =monday=, not ='monday=. =otherwise= catches everything else.
(=t= works too, but =otherwise= is clearer.)

A clause can match several keys if you put them in a list:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun weekend-p (day)
  (case day
    ((saturday sunday) t)
    (otherwise nil)))

(weekend-p 'sunday)    ; => T
(weekend-p 'monday)    ; => NIL
#+end_src

=case= compares with the same test as =eq= for symbols, and it also
works for numbers and characters:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun vowel-p (letter)
  (case (char-downcase letter)
    ((#\a #\e #\i #\o #\u) t)
    (otherwise nil)))

(vowel-p #\E)          ; => T
#+end_src

=case= does *not* work with strings. ="yes"= and ="yes"= are two
separate string objects, and =case= doesn't look inside them. For
strings, use =cond= with =string==.

*** =member=: is it in the list?

One more function belongs here, because it's the most common test in
the project:

#+begin_src lisp
(member 'b '(a b c))   ; => (B C)
(member 'z '(a b c))   ; => NIL
#+end_src

=member= looks for an element in a list. If it finds it, it returns
the rest of the list starting from that element. If not, it returns
=nil=. Since any non-=nil= value is true, you can use it as a yes/no
test:

#+begin_src lisp
(if (member 'garden '(hallway garden))
    "you can go there"
    "no way through")  ; => "you can go there"
#+end_src

Like =case=, =member= works for symbols, numbers and characters, but
not for strings. Part IV shows how to make it work with strings.

*** Choosing the right tool

| Situation                                  | Use              |
|--------------------------------------------+------------------|
| Two choices                                | =if=             |
| Do something only when true                | =when=           |
| Do something only when false               | =unless=         |
| Three or more choices with different tests | =cond=           |
| Three or more choices by exact value       | =case=           |
| Combine tests                              | =and=, =or=, =not= |
| A default value                            | =or=             |

*** Common mistakes

- *Several expressions in an =if= branch:*
  =(if test (print "a") (print "b") (print "c"))= is an error: "too
  many elements". =if= takes exactly a test and up to two branches.
  Use =progn=, or =when= if there is no else part.
- *Treating =0= or =""= as false:* =(if 0 "yes" "no")= gives ="yes"=.
  Use =(zerop n)= or =(string= s "")= to test for them.
- *Forgetting the extra parentheses in =cond=:*
  =(cond (< x 0) "negative")= reads =<= as the test, which is a
  variable that doesn't exist. Each clause needs its own pair:
  =(cond ((< x 0) "negative"))=.
- *Putting a general test before a specific one in =cond=:* the first
  matching clause wins. Test =(< n 0)= before =(< n 15)=, not after.
- *Quoting =case= keys:* =(case day ('monday 1))=. The key becomes
  the list =(quote monday)=, and SBCL warns about a duplicate key.
  Write =(case day (monday 1))=.
- *Using =case= with strings:* =(case "yes" ("yes" 1))= never
  matches. Use =cond= with =string==.
- *Forgetting the default:* a =cond= with no =t= clause, or a =case=
  with no =otherwise=, quietly returns =nil= when nothing matches.
  Add a default that returns something meaningful.
- *Expecting =member= to return =T=:* it returns part of the list.
  That's still true. Don't compare its result with =t=.

*** Project step: moving between rooms

The player should only walk through real exits. To move, the game
needs two things: a way to get from a room's /name/ to its /list/,
and a check that the name is one of the current room's exits.

First, the lookup. =case= matches the name to the right variable:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun find-room (name)
  "Return the room whose name is NAME, or NIL if there is none."
  (case name
    (bedroom *bedroom*)
    (hallway *hallway*)
    (kitchen *kitchen*)
    (garden *garden*)
    (otherwise nil)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(room-description (find-room 'hallway))
;; => "A narrow hallway with creaky floorboards."
#+end_src

Now the move. =cond= handles the three possible situations:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun go-to (name)
  "Move the player to the room called NAME, if it is an exit."
  (cond ((eq name (room-name *current-room*))
         "You are already there.")
        ((member name (room-exits *current-room*))
         (setf *current-room* (find-room name))
         (look))
        (t
         "You can't go there from here.")))
#+end_src

The second clause has two expressions: it changes the room, then
returns the new description.

Play a few moves:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* *bedroom*)
(list (go-to 'garden)
      (go-to 'hallway)
      (go-to 'kitchen)
      (go-to 'kitchen))
#+end_src

The result is a list of four answers:

#+begin_example
("You can't go there from here."
 "You are in the Hallway. A narrow hallway with creaky floorboards."
 "You are in the Kitchen. A warm kitchen that smells of bread."
 "You are already there.")
#+end_example

The bedroom has no exit to the garden, so the first move fails. Each
later move starts from where the last one left the player.

The game now has rules. In Part II you'll learn how lists are really
built, and the rooms will move out of separate variables into one
list the game can search.

**** Exercise 6.1

Write a function =sign= that returns the symbol =negative=, =zero= or
=positive= for a number.

**** Exercise 6.2

Predict the result of each line, then check:

#+begin_example
(if nil 1 2)
(if 0 1 2)
(when (> 1 2) 'yes)
(unless (> 1 2) 'yes)
(if (member 3 '(1 2 3)) 'found 'missing)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 6.3

Write =my-abs=, which returns the absolute value of a number, using
=if= and without using =abs=.

**** Exercise 6.4

Write =grade=, which takes a score from 0 to 100 and returns the
symbol =a= for 90 and above, =b= for 80 to 89, =c= for 70 to 79 and
=f= for anything lower.

**** Exercise 6.5

Write =day-type= using =case=. It returns =weekend= for =saturday=
and =sunday=, =weekday= for =monday= to =friday=, and =unknown= for
anything else.

**** Exercise 6.6

Each listing has a mistake. Run it, read the error or the wrong
result, and fix it.

#+begin_example
(let ((x 5))
  (if (> x 0)
      (print "positive")
      (print "big")
      (print "negative")))

(cond (> 5 0) 'yes)

(let ((answer "yes"))
  (case answer
    ("yes" 1)
    (otherwise 0)))
#+end_example

In the first listing, the intent is: when =x= is positive, print both
="positive"= and ="big"=. Otherwise print ="negative"=.

In the third listing, the intent is to return =1= when =answer= is
="yes"=.

**** Exercise 6.7

Predict the result of each line:

#+begin_example
(or nil "" 5)
(and 1 0 nil)
(or (member 'x '(a b)) 'none)
(and 'a 'b)
(not 0)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 6.8

Write =fizzbuzz-word=, which takes a number and returns:

- ="FizzBuzz"= if it divides by both 3 and 5,
- ="Fizz"= if it divides by 3,
- ="Buzz"= if it divides by 5,
- otherwise, the number as a string, such as ="7"=.

Hint: a number divides by 3 when =(mod n 3)= is zero.

**** Exercise 6.9 (project)

Write =can-go-p=, which takes a room name and returns exactly =T= if
it is an exit of the current room, and =NIL= if not. It must not
return a piece of the exit list the way =member= does.

**** Exercise 6.10 (project)

The garden door is locked. Make a global variable =*garden-unlocked*=
that starts as =nil=. Then write a new =go-to= that answers
="The garden door is locked."= when the player tries to enter the
garden while it is still locked. Every other move must work as
before.

** Part I review

You now know the core of Lisp:

- Code is made of atoms and lists. A list is evaluated by calling its
  first element with the values of the rest. =quote= stops
  evaluation.
- The everyday atoms are numbers (with exact ratios), characters,
  strings and symbols.
- =defparameter= and =defvar= make global variables. =let= and =let*=
  make local ones. =setf=, =incf= and =decf= change them.
- =defun= makes functions. The last expression is the return value.
  =&optional=, =&key= and =&rest= make parameter lists flexible.
- =if=, =when=, =unless=, =cond= and =case= make choices. Only =nil=
  is false.

The adventure has rooms, descriptions, a =look= command and moves
that follow the exits. Before moving on, scroll back through the
project steps from Chapter 1 to Chapter 6 and read the code without
running it. If you can say what each line does, you're ready for
Part II.

* Part II: Thinking in Lists

Part I used lists as boxes of things. Part II opens the box. You'll
see what a list is made of, then learn the two ways Lisp programmers
work through lists: recursion, and functions that take other
functions. By the end of Part II, lists will feel like the natural
shape for almost any data.

** Chapter 7: Cons Cells and List Structure

*** =cons=: adding to the front

#+begin_src lisp
(cons 1 '(2 3))        ; => (1 2 3)
(cons 'a '(b c))       ; => (A B C)
(cons 'a nil)          ; => (A)
#+end_src

=cons= takes a value and a list, and returns a new list with the
value added at the front. The original list is not changed.

That sounds like a small convenience. It's actually the whole story.
Every list is built from =cons=, and only from =cons=.

*** The cons cell

=cons= makes one small object called a /cons cell/, or just a /cons/.
A cons has exactly two parts:

- the *car*, its first part,
- the *cdr* (say "could-er"), its second part.

Draw a cons as a box with two halves:

#+begin_example
(cons 'a 'b)

+---+---+
| A | B |
+---+---+
 car cdr
#+end_example

#+begin_src lisp
(car (cons 'a 'b))     ; => A
(cdr (cons 'a 'b))     ; => B
#+end_src

The names come from the IBM 704 computer that the first Lisp ran on,
in 1958: "Contents of the Address part of the Register" and "Contents
of the Decrement part of the Register". They mean nothing today, but
you'll see them everywhere in Lisp code, so learn to read them.

*** A list is a chain of conses

Here is how =(1 2 3)= is really stored:

#+begin_example
+---+---+   +---+---+   +---+---+
| 1 | *-+-->| 2 | *-+-->| 3 |NIL|
+---+---+   +---+---+   +---+---+
#+end_example

Three conses. Each car holds an element. Each cdr points to the next
cons. The last cdr is =nil=, which marks the end.

You can build it yourself:

#+begin_src lisp
(cons 1 (cons 2 (cons 3 nil)))   ; => (1 2 3)
#+end_src

Read it from the inside out: =(cons 3 nil)= is =(3)=, consing =2= on
the front gives =(2 3)=, and consing =1= gives =(1 2 3)=.

If you know Java's =LinkedList=: a cons is a node, the car is the
value, and the cdr is the =next= pointer. Lisp just shows you the
nodes directly.

This explains several things from Part I:

- *=first= is =car= and =rest= is =cdr=.* =first= takes the car of the
  first cons. =rest= takes its cdr, which is the rest of the chain.
  The two pairs of names do exactly the same thing. =first= and
  =rest= read better when you think of a list. =car= and =cdr= read
  better when you think of a single cons.
- *=nil= is both an atom and a list.* =nil= isn't a cons, so it's an
  atom. It's what every chain ends with, so it's the empty list.
- *=last= returns a list.* =last= gives you the last /cons/ of the
  chain. A cons whose cdr is =nil= prints as a one-element list, so
  =(last '(1 2 3))= shows =(3)=.

*** Lists inside lists

A car can point to another list:

#+begin_example
((A B) C)

+---+---+   +---+---+
| * | *-+-->| C |NIL|
+-+-+---+   +---+---+
  |
  v
+---+---+   +---+---+
| A | *-+-->| B |NIL|
+---+---+   +---+---+
#+end_example

The top row is the outer list, with two elements. The first element
is itself a chain of two conses.

#+begin_src lisp
(cons (list 'a 'b) '(c))   ; => ((A B) C)
#+end_src

*** Dotted pairs

What if the cdr of a cons isn't a list?

#+begin_src lisp
(cons 1 2)             ; => (1 . 2)
#+end_src

Lisp prints a dot between the car and the cdr. This is called a
/dotted pair/. It's just one cons with two values in it.

Compare these carefully:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (cons 1 2)       ; one cons: car 1, cdr 2
      (list 1 2)       ; two conses: car 1, then car 2, then NIL
      (cons 1 '(2)))   ; same as (list 1 2)
;; => ((1 . 2) (1 2) (1 2))
#+end_src

A chain that ends in something other than =nil= is called an
/improper list/:

#+begin_src lisp
(cons 1 (cons 2 3))    ; => (1 2 . 3)
#+end_src

A chain that ends in =nil= is a /proper list/. Every list you've used
so far was proper. Most list functions expect proper lists.
=(length '(1 2 . 3))= is an error.

Dotted pairs are useful on their own, as a cheap way to keep two
values together. You'll use them later in this chapter.

You can also /type/ a dotted pair. Put spaces around the dot:

#+begin_src lisp
'(key . bedroom)       ; => (KEY . BEDROOM)
