(cdr '(key . bedroom)) ; => BEDROOM
#+end_src

*** Testing for conses

#+begin_src lisp
(consp '(1 2))         ; => T
(consp '(1 . 2))       ; => T
(consp nil)            ; => NIL
(listp nil)            ; => T
#+end_src

=consp= asks "is it a cons?". =listp= asks "is it a cons or =nil=?".
The only difference is =nil=: the empty list is a list, but it isn't a
cons. Chapter 8 depends on this difference.

*** Shortcuts: =cadr=, =cddr= and friends

Taking the car of the cdr is so common that it has its own name:

#+begin_src lisp
(car (cdr '(a b c)))   ; => B
(cadr '(a b c))        ; => B
#+end_src

The letters between =c= and =r= say what to do, read from *right to
left*. =cadr= is "cdr, then car". Some more:

| Name    | Means              | On =(a b c d)= | Same as  |
|---------+--------------------+----------------+----------|
| =cadr=  | car of cdr         | =B=            | =second= |
| =cddr=  | cdr of cdr         | =(C D)=        |          |
| =caddr= | car of cdr of cdr  | =C=            | =third=  |
| =caar=  | car of car         | (error here)   |          |
| =cdar=  | cdr of car         | (error here)   |          |

=caar= and =cdar= need a list whose first element is itself a cons,
such as =((a . 1) (b . 2))=: =caar= gives =A=, =cdar= gives =1=.

Use =second= and =third= when you mean list positions. Use the =c...r=
names when you're working with conses, or when reading older code.
Combinations go up to four letters, such as =cadddr=, but anything
beyond three is hard to read.

=nthcdr= skips a number of conses:

#+begin_src lisp
(nthcdr 2 '(a b c d))  ; => (C D)
#+end_src

*** Sharing structure

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *tail* '(2 3))
(defparameter *whole* (cons 1 *tail*))
*whole*                ; => (1 2 3)
#+end_src

=cons= didn't copy =*tail*=. The new cons points straight at it:

#+begin_example
*whole*      *tail*
   |            |
   v            v
+---+---+   +---+---+   +---+---+
| 1 | *-+-->| 2 | *-+-->| 3 |NIL|
+---+---+   +---+---+   +---+---+
#+end_example

That's why adding to the front of a list is fast, no matter how long
the list is. It takes one new cons.

Adding to the *end* is different. =append= has to copy every cons of
the lists before the last one, because their final cdrs must change
to point onward. On a long list that's slow. Lisp programmers build
lists from the front and, when they need the other order, call
=reverse= once at the end.

Sharing is safe as long as nothing changes the shared conses.
Everything in this guide so far makes new conses instead of changing
old ones, so you can share freely.

*** =push= and =pop=: a list in a variable

=push= conses a value onto the front of the list stored in a
variable, and stores the new list back in the variable:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *stack* nil)
(push 'a *stack*)      ; => (A)
(push 'b *stack*)      ; => (B A)
(push 'c *stack*)      ; => (C B A)
*stack*                ; => (C B A)
#+end_src

=(push 'a *stack*)= does the same as
=(setf *stack* (cons 'a *stack*))=. It's shorter and says what you
mean.

=pop= does the opposite. It returns the first element and stores the
rest back in the variable:

#+begin_src lisp
(pop *stack*)          ; => C
*stack*                ; => (B A)
#+end_src

Popping an empty list returns =nil= and leaves the variable as =nil=.

The names come from a /stack/ of plates: you add to the top and take
from the top. Java's =Deque= has =push= and =pop= methods that work
the same way.

**** Building a list in order

=push= adds to the front, so the list comes out backwards. Build it
with =push=, then =reverse= it once at the end:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((result nil))
  (push 1 result)
  (push 2 result)
  (push 3 result)
  (reverse result))    ; => (1 2 3)
#+end_src

=push= works on local variables too. This "push, then reverse"
pattern is one you'll use often from Chapter 8 onwards.

**** =push= changes the variable, not the list

=push= never changes an existing cons. It makes a new one and points
the variable at it. Other variables that held the old list still
hold the old list:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *original* '(1 2))
(defparameter *copy* *original*)
(push 0 *original*)
(list *original* *copy*)   ; => ((0 1 2) (1 2))
#+end_src

=*copy*= didn't change, because nothing touched the conses it points
to.

*** Association lists

A list of dotted pairs makes a simple lookup table. Each pair holds a
/key/ in its car and a /value/ in its cdr:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *colours*
  '((apple . red)
    (banana . yellow)
    (grape . purple)))
#+end_src

This is called an /association list/, or /alist/. =assoc= finds the
pair for a key:

#+begin_src lisp
(assoc 'banana *colours*)        ; => (BANANA . YELLOW)
(cdr (assoc 'banana *colours*))  ; => YELLOW
(assoc 'kiwi *colours*)          ; => NIL
#+end_src

=assoc= returns the whole pair, not the value. Take its =cdr= to get
the value.

When the key is missing, =assoc= returns =nil=, and =(cdr nil)= is
also =nil=. So =(cdr (assoc 'kiwi *colours*))= is =nil=, not an error.
Combine it with =or= for a default:

#+begin_src lisp
(or (cdr (assoc 'kiwi *colours*)) 'unknown)   ; => UNKNOWN
#+end_src

In Java you'd use a =HashMap= for this. For a few dozen entries an
alist is simpler, and you can read it at a glance. Part III covers
hash tables, for when you have many entries.

**** Newer entries hide older ones

=assoc= returns the *first* pair whose key matches. So pushing a new
pair onto the front changes the answer, without removing the old
pair:

#+begin_src lisp
(push '(apple . green) *colours*)
(cdr (assoc 'apple *colours*))   ; => GREEN
*colours*
;; => ((APPLE . GREEN) (APPLE . RED) (BANANA . YELLOW) (GRAPE . PURPLE))
#+end_src

The old =(APPLE . RED)= is still there, hidden behind the new one.
This is a cheap way to update an alist, and the project uses it below.

**** The value can be anything

The cdr of a pair can be a list. Look at what happens then:

#+begin_src lisp
(cons 'kitchen '("A warm kitchen." (hallway garden)))
;; => (KITCHEN "A warm kitchen." (HALLWAY GARDEN))
#+end_src

A pair whose cdr is a list prints as an ordinary list. The other way
round: any list is a pair whose car is its first element. So a list
of lists is also an alist, with each list's first element as its key:

#+begin_src lisp
(assoc 'garden
       '((kitchen "A warm kitchen." (hallway garden))
         (garden "An overgrown garden." (kitchen))))
;; => (GARDEN "An overgrown garden." (KITCHEN))
#+end_src

Your rooms already have this shape. The project step uses it.

**** What =assoc= can compare

=assoc= compares keys the same way =member= does. It works for
symbols, numbers and characters, but not for strings. Use symbols as
keys. Part IV shows how to make it work with strings.

*** Common mistakes

- *Using =cons= to join two lists:* =(cons '(1 2) '(3 4))= gives
  =((1 2) 3 4)=. =cons= adds /one/ element to the front, and here the
  element is the list =(1 2)=. To join lists, use =append=.
- *Putting the list first:* =(cons '(2 3) 1)= gives =((2 3) . 1)=.
  The element comes first, the list second: =(cons 1 '(2 3))=.
- *Making a dotted pair by accident:* =(cons 1 2)= is =(1 . 2)=, not
  =(1 2)=. For a two-element list, write =(list 1 2)=.
- *Expecting =cons= or =push= to add at the end:* both add at the
  front. Build from the front and =reverse= at the end.
- *Using =push= without a variable:* =(push 'x '(a b))= is an error,
  because =push= needs somewhere to store the new list. Use =cons=,
  or put the list in a variable first.
- *Reading =cadr= left to right:* the letters run right to left.
  =cadr= is "cdr first, then car", which is =second=.
- *Expecting =assoc= to return the value:* it returns the pair. Use
  =(cdr (assoc key alist))=.
- *String keys in an alist:* =(assoc "b" '(("a" . 1) ("b" . 2)))=
  returns =nil=. Use symbols as keys.
- *Calling =length= on an improper list:* =(length '(1 2 . 3))= gives
  an error saying a value is not of type =LIST=. Check how the list
  was built: somewhere a =cons= got a non-list as its second
  argument.

*** Project step: all rooms in one list

Your rooms are lists whose first element is the room name. Put them
all in one list and you have an alist of rooms. This also adds a new
room, the cellar, below the kitchen:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *rooms*
  '((bedroom "A small bedroom with an unmade bed." (hallway))
    (hallway "A narrow hallway with creaky floorboards." (bedroom kitchen))
    (kitchen "A warm kitchen that smells of bread." (hallway garden cellar))
    (garden "An overgrown garden." (kitchen))
    (cellar "A damp cellar. Something drips in the dark." (kitchen))))
#+end_src

Now =find-room= becomes one line. Run this to replace the Chapter 6
version:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun find-room (name)
  "Return the room whose name is NAME, or NIL if there is none."
  (assoc name *rooms*))
#+end_src

Your =go-to= from Chapter 6 calls =find-room=, so it now uses the
new list without any change. This is what the accessors and small
functions from Chapter 5 were for: you changed how rooms are stored,
and only one function had to change.

There's one catch. =*current-room*= still holds the list from the old
=*bedroom*= variable, whose exits don't know about the cellar. Point
it at the room from the new list:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'bedroom))
(list (go-to 'hallway)
      (go-to 'kitchen)
      (go-to 'cellar))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
("You are in the Hallway. A narrow hallway with creaky floorboards."
 "You are in the Kitchen. A warm kitchen that smells of bread."
 "You are in the Cellar. A damp cellar. Something drips in the dark.")
#+end_example

If you kept the locked-garden =go-to= from Exercise 6.10, moves into
the garden still need =*garden-unlocked*= to be =t=. Everything else
works the same.

The old variables =*bedroom*=, =*hallway*= and so on still exist, but
the game no longer uses them. From now on, rooms live in =*rooms*=.

**** Items

A game needs things to pick up. A second alist says where each item
is. The key is the item, the value is the name of the room it's in:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))

(defun item-location (item)
  "Return the name of the room ITEM is in, or PLAYER if it is carried."
  (cdr (assoc item *item-locations*)))

(defun item-name (item)
  "Return ITEM as a lower-case string, for messages."
  (string-downcase (symbol-name item)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(item-location 'key)   ; => KITCHEN
(item-name 'key)       ; => "key"
#+end_src

When the player takes an item, its location becomes the symbol
=player=. Instead of changing the old pair, push a new one on the
front. =assoc= finds the newest pair first, so the old one is hidden:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun take (item)
  "Pick up ITEM if it is in the current room."
  (cond ((eq (item-location item) 'player)
         "You already have that.")
        ((eq (item-location item) (room-name *current-room*))
         (push (cons item 'player) *item-locations*)
         (concatenate 'string "You take the " (item-name item) "."))
        (t
         "You don't see that here.")))
#+end_src

Try it in the kitchen:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(list (take 'lamp)
      (take 'key)
      (take 'key)
      (item-location 'key))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
("You don't see that here." "You take the key." "You already have that." PLAYER)
#+end_example

The lamp is in the cellar, so the first =take= fails. The key is in
the kitchen, so the second works. After that, the key is carried.

Run it a second time and the first =take 'key= says "You
already have that.", because the key is still marked as carried. To
start over, re-run the =defparameter= for =*item-locations*=.

The game can't yet list the items in a room, because that means
looking at every pair in the alist. Chapter 8 teaches you how to walk
through a whole list.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 7.1

Build each list using only =cons= and =nil=:

1. =(1 2 3)=
2. =(A (B C))=

Then draw the box diagram for =(A (B C))=, in the style of this
chapter.

**** Exercise 7.2

Predict the result of each line, then check:

#+begin_example
(cons 'a 'b)
(cons '(a) '(b))
(cons nil nil)
(cdr '(a))
(car '((x y) z))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 7.3

Write three expressions:

1. Get =C= from ='(a b c d)= with =caddr=.
2. Get =(C D)= from ='(a b c d)= with =cddr=.
3. Get =1= from ='((a . 1) (b . 2))= with =cdar=.

**** Exercise 7.4

Write a function =swap-pair= that takes a dotted pair and returns a
new pair with the car and cdr swapped. =(swap-pair '(1 . 2))= gives
=(2 . 1)=.

**** Exercise 7.5

Predict the value =pop= returns and the final value of =*s*=:

#+begin_example
(defparameter *s* '(x))
(push 'y *s*)
(push 'z *s*)
(pop *s*)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 7.6

Make an alist =*capitals*= that maps =tunisia= to =tunis=, =france=
to =paris= and =japan= to =tokyo=. Write a function =capital-of= that
returns the capital of a country, or =unknown= if the country isn't
in the list.

**** Exercise 7.7

Each line has a mistake. Say what's wrong and fix it.

#+begin_example
(cons '(1 2) '(3 4))                        ; wanted (1 2 3 4)
(cdr (assoc "b" '(("a" . 1) ("b" . 2))))    ; wanted 2
(push 'x '(a b))                            ; wanted (X A B)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 7.8 (project)

Write =drop=, which takes an item. If the player carries it, the item
moves to the current room and =drop= returns a message such as
="You drop the key."=. If not, it returns ="You don't have that."=.

**** Exercise 7.9 (project)

Add a shed reached from the garden. Without retyping =*rooms*=, push
two new rooms onto it:

- a new garden, the same as the old one but with exits
  =(kitchen shed)=,
- a shed with the description ="A wooden shed full of old tools."=
  and the exit =(garden)=.

Then put the player in the garden and walk into the shed. Why does
the new garden replace the old one?

** Chapter 8: Recursion

Most of the work a program does is walk through a collection of
things and do something to each one. In Java you write a =for= loop.
In Lisp, the natural first tool is a function that calls itself.

*** The idea in one sentence

A recursive function solves a big case by calling itself on a
smaller case, until the case is so small that the answer is obvious.

*** A list is either empty or it isn't

Look again at how a list is built (Chapter 7). Every list is one of
two things:

- the empty list, =nil=, or
- a cons: one element, with a shorter list behind it.

The rule gives you a recipe. A function that walks a list asks one
question: is this list empty?

- *If it is,* return the answer for the empty case. This is the /base
  case/, and it stops the recursion.
- *If it isn't,* do something with =(first lst)=, and call yourself on
  =(rest lst)=, which is one cons shorter.

The recursion always moves toward the empty list, so it always stops.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun all-positive-p (numbers)
  "Return T if every number in NUMBERS is greater than zero."
  (cond ((null numbers) t)
        ((plusp (first numbers)) (all-positive-p (rest numbers)))
        (t nil)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(list (all-positive-p '(1 2 3))
      (all-positive-p '(1 -2 3))
      (all-positive-p '()))
;; => (T NIL T)
#+end_src

Read the three clauses with the two questions in mind:

- =(null numbers)=: the list is empty. Every number in an empty list
  is positive, so the answer is =t=. This is the base case.
- =(plusp (first numbers))=: the first number is fine, so the answer
  depends on the rest. Call yourself on =(rest numbers)=.
- otherwise: the first number isn't positive, so the answer is =nil=,
  without looking further.

If you forget the first clause, the function never stops. That's the
mistake to watch for, and it's in the "Common mistakes" section below.

*** Building an answer as you come back

The previous example returned an answer found somewhere in the list.
Often you want to build a /new list/. Use =cons= to put something on
the front of the answer for the rest:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun double-all (numbers)
  "Return a list of each number in NUMBERS, doubled."
  (if (null numbers)
      nil
      (cons (* 2 (first numbers))
            (double-all (rest numbers)))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(double-all '(1 2 3))     ; => (2 4 6)
(double-all '())          ; => NIL
#+end_src

The shape is worth naming: *handle the first element, then trust the
recursive call for the rest.*

For =(double-all '(1 2 3))=, Lisp can't finish the =cons= until it
knows =(double-all '(2 3))=. So the calls pile up:

#+begin_example
(double-all (1 2 3))  needs (double-all (2 3))
(double-all (2 3))    needs (double-all (3))
(double-all (3))      needs (double-all ())
(double-all ())       → NIL, and now each call can finish
(double-all (3))      → (6)
(double-all (2 3))    → (4 6)
(double-all (1 2 3))  → (2 4 6)
#+end_example

The base case answers first, and the answers come back up the chain.
The =cons= calls happen on the way /out/.

*** Watching it happen with =trace=

When recursion is hard to follow, ask Lisp to show you every call:

#+begin_src lisp
(trace double-all)
(double-all '(1 2 3))
(untrace double-all)
#+end_src

Only the final value comes back; the trace lines are printed as they
happen, in the *REPL window*, so switch to it (~C-x o~) to read them.
They look like this:

#+begin_example
0: (DOUBLE-ALL (1 2 3))
  1: (DOUBLE-ALL (2 3))
    2: (DOUBLE-ALL (3))
      3: (DOUBLE-ALL NIL)
      3: DOUBLE-ALL returned NIL
    2: DOUBLE-ALL returned (6)
  1: DOUBLE-ALL returned (4 6)
0: DOUBLE-ALL returned (2 4 6)
#+end_example

The number at the start of a line is the depth. Every call indents
one step further and returns one step earlier. You can see the base
case at the bottom, and the answers coming back up.

=untrace= turns tracing off, and so does restarting Lisp. Trace one
function at a time; if you trace something that calls another traced
function, the output becomes a wall of lines.

This is the single most useful tool for learning recursion. Whenever
a recursive function surprises you, trace it.

*** The accumulator pattern

Building the answer on the way out is elegant, but the calls pile up,
and each one adds a step of work on the way back. There's a second
way: carry the answer /with/ you as an extra parameter, and add to it
on the way in.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun double-all-into (numbers result)
  "Cons each number in NUMBERS, doubled, onto RESULT."
  (if (null numbers)
      result
      (double-all-into (rest numbers)
                       (cons (* 2 (first numbers)) result))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(double-all-into '(1 2 3) nil)   ; => (6 4 2)
#+end_src

=(6 4 2)= is backwards. Consing onto the front while walking forwards
does that: 1 goes on first, then 2 lands in front of it, then 3. Fix
it with one =reverse= at the end:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun double-all (numbers)
  "Return a list of each number in NUMBERS, doubled."
  (reverse (double-all-into numbers nil)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(double-all '(1 2 3))            ; => (2 4 6)
#+end_src

The parameter =result= is called an /accumulator/. It holds the
answer so far. Each call adds one thing to it and hands it on. The
base case doesn't build anything: it just returns what has been
accumulated.

Because the =cons= happens on the way in, nothing is left waiting on
the way out. Nothing piles up. A call like this, where the recursive
call is the very last thing the function does, is in /tail position/,
and Lisp can turn it into a plain jump. The section "How deep can you
go" comes back to this.

Which pattern to use:

| Pattern                     | Use when                                                |
|-----------------------------+----------------------------------------------------------|
| Build with =cons= on the way out | The result has the same order as the input, or the order doesn't matter |
| Accumulator, then =reverse= | You are walking a long list, or the result is naturally built backwards |

When in doubt, use the first. Reach for the accumulator when a list
is long or when the recursion would otherwise go deep.

*** Recursion on numbers

Lists aren't the only thing that can be smaller. Any number can be
one smaller than itself, and the base case is whatever number stops
the process.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun countdown (n)
  "Return a list of the numbers from N down to 1."
  (if (zerop n)
      nil
      (cons n (countdown (1- n)))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(countdown 5)      ; => (5 4 3 2 1)
(countdown 0)      ; => NIL
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(defun factorial (n)
  "Return N!, the product of the numbers from 1 to N."
  (if (zerop n)
      1
      (* n (factorial (1- n)))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(list (factorial 0) (factorial 5) (factorial 30))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
(1 120 265252859812191058636308480000000)
#+end_example

The last value is exact, because Lisp integers have no size limit
(Chapter 3). In Java you'd need =BigInteger= for that.

Notice the base cases: =nil= for building a list, =1= for a product.
A sum would use =0=, and a yes/no question would use =t= or =nil=.
*The base case returns the answer for "nothing", and that answer must
have the right type.* Writing =nil= where a number is expected is one
of the four mistakes at the end of this chapter.

*** Recursion on nested lists

A list whose elements are themselves lists is a /tree/. Chapter 7
drew one. Trees are the natural home of recursion, because a tree is
either an atom, or a cons with two smaller trees inside it.

Here is a function that counts every atom in a tree:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-atoms (tree)
  "Return the number of atoms in TREE, at any depth."
  (cond ((null tree) 0)
        ((consp tree) (+ (count-atoms (car tree))
                         (count-atoms (cdr tree))))
        (t 1)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(list (count-atoms '(a (b (c)) d))
      (count-atoms 5)
      (count-atoms nil))
;; => (4 1 0)
#+end_src

Three cases, and this time the recursion appears twice:

- =nil= is empty, so it holds no atoms. (It's a list, so it counts as
  zero, not one.)
- A cons is a tree of two halves, its car and its cdr, so add the
  counts of both. This is where a list and a tree differ: for a plain
  list, you only recurse on the =rest=.
- Anything else is an atom, so it counts as one.

Recursing on both halves ends, because each half contains strictly
fewer conses than the whole.

*** Mutual recursion

A function can call another, which calls the first one back. Two
functions that take turns are /mutually recursive/:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun my-even-p (n)
  "Return T when N is even, by counting down."
  (if (zerop n)
      t
      (my-odd-p (1- n))))

(defun my-odd-p (n)
  "Return T when N is odd, by counting down."
  (if (zerop n)
      nil
      (my-even-p (1- n))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(list (my-even-p 4) (my-odd-p 4) (my-even-p 0))
;; => (T NIL T)
#+end_src

SBCL may warn "undefined function: MY-ODD-P" while you run the first
=defun=, because =my-odd-p= doesn't exist yet. As Chapter 5 says,
that's a note, not a problem. Once both are defined, the calls work.

They take turns right down to zero: =my-even-p 4= calls =my-odd-p 3=,
which calls =my-even-p 2=, and so on. Whoever reaches zero decides.

*** How deep can you go

When a call is in tail position, Lisp can throw away the current
frame and jump. On a stock SBCL:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-down-to-zero (n)
  "Return the symbol DONE after counting from N down to zero."
  (if (zerop n)
      'done
      (count-down-to-zero (1- n))))

(count-down-to-zero 1000000)   ; => DONE
#+end_src

A million calls, and no memory used up. The recursive call is the
last thing =count-down-to-zero= does, so no frame is kept.

Now the other side. When the call is *not* in tail position, every
frame stays alive until the answer comes back. =(countdown
100000)= builds a hundred thousand conses and needs a hundred
thousand frames at once. SBCL has a fixed control stack, and when it
runs out you get:

#+begin_example
Control stack exhausted (no more space for function call frames).
#+end_example

The debugger opens. Press ~q~ to close it, or use the "abort"
restart if the REPL asks, and everything else keeps working. Nothing
is corrupted.

Two rules of thumb:

- A recursion that builds a result with =cons= (or =+=, or =append=)
  needs one frame per element. Fine for hundreds and thousands, not
  for millions.
- A tail recursion with an accumulator needs no frames at all. Use
  it for the long walks.

SBCL does this tail-call optimisation by default. Other Lisp
implementations may not, and compiler settings can switch it off.
Treat it as a useful property of your current setup, not as something
to design around. When you meet =loop= in Part III, you'll see how
Lisp programmers write the long walks without recursion at all.

*** How to write a recursive function

A recipe that works almost every time:

1. *Say what the function does in one sentence,* including what it
   returns for the empty list, or for zero, or for the smallest
   input. That sentence is your base case.
2. *Write the base case first.* Test it immediately, before writing
   anything else.
3. *Shrink the input.* For a list, that's =(rest lst)=. For a
   number, =(1- n)=. For a tree, the car and the cdr.
4. *Decide what to do with the smaller answer:* return it as is,
   wrap it with =cons=, add something to it, or pick between it and
   the current element.
5. *Test with an empty input, one element, then two.*
6. *If it surprises you, trace it.*

Step 3 is where the thinking happens. The function is correct when
the smaller input is always genuinely smaller, and when the base
case is reached for every input the function will ever get.

*** Common mistakes

- *No base case:* =(defun first-of (lst) (first-of lst))= never
  stops. SBCL prints "Control stack exhausted" and opens the
  debugger. Press ~q~. Check that your =cond= or =if= has a clause
  for the empty list, and that it comes first.
- *A base case that is never reached:* counting down with =(1- n)=
  and testing =(zerop n)= works for =0= and positive numbers.
  =(my-even-p -4)= counts down forever. Check the base case against
  negative arguments, or add a clause that turns them around first.
- *Forgetting to use the recursive result:* =(defun my-length (lst)
  (if (null lst) 0 (my-length (rest lst))))= always returns =0=.
  The call was made, its answer was thrown away. It needs
  =(+ 1 (my-length (rest lst)))=.
- *Recursing on the input instead of the smaller input:*
  =(sum-list lst)= calling =(sum-list lst)= again. The "Control
  stack exhausted" message catches it. Look at the argument of the
  recursive call: it must be =(rest lst)=, =(1- n)=, or another
  smaller thing.
- *A base case of the wrong type:* =(defun sum-list (lst) (if (null
  lst) nil (+ (first lst) (sum-list (rest lst)))))= fails with "The
  value NIL is not a NUMBER" as soon as the recursion reaches the
  end. The base case for a sum is =0=.
- *Rebuilding a list in the wrong order, over and over:* using an
  accumulator and forgetting the =reverse=, then adding a second
  =reverse= inside the loop. Build backwards once, then reverse
  once.
- *Tracing everything:* =(trace)= on a function that's called in a
  tight loop floods the REPL. Trace one function, and =untrace= it
  when you're done.
- *Recursing where a built-in already does the job:* after Part III
  you'll reach for =loop= or =mapcar= for the common walks.
  Recursion earns its place on trees, on divide-and-conquer
  problems, and on anything where the shape of the data is itself
  recursive.

*** Project step: what's lying around

The game can move the player and take items, but the player can't see
what's there. Write that now: walk the item alist and collect the
items whose location matches.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun items-in (pairs place)
  "Return the items in the alist PAIRS whose place is PLACE."
  (cond ((null pairs) nil)
        ((eq (cdar pairs) place)
         (cons (caar pairs) (items-in (cdr pairs) place)))
        (t
         (items-in (cdr pairs) place))))
#+end_src

With the chapter's idea in mind, read the three clauses:

- empty alist: nothing found, so =nil=.
- the first pair's value is this place: keep its item, and look
  through the rest.
- otherwise: skip this pair, and look through the rest.

Every call passes =(cdr pairs)=, one pair shorter, so it stops at
=nil=.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun items-here (place)
  "Return the items lying in PLACE."
  (items-in *item-locations* place))
#+end_src

Reset the items so this chapter starts from a known state:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(list (items-here 'kitchen)
      (items-here 'cellar)
      (items-here 'player)
      (items-here 'garden))
;; => ((KEY) (LAMP) NIL NIL)
#+end_src

The third one is empty because the player is carrying nothing — and
that's the same function doing the work. Carried items are marked
with the symbol =player=, so asking for the items in =player= asks
for the inventory.

Now turn that list of symbols into words a player can read:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun item-names (items)
  "Return a string listing ITEMS by name, separated by commas."
  (cond ((null items) "")
        ((null (cdr items)) (item-name (first items)))
        (t
         (concatenate 'string
                      (item-name (first items))
                      ", "
                      (item-names (rest items))))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(list (item-names nil)
      (item-names '(key))
      (item-names '(key lamp)))
;; => ("" "key" "key, lamp")
#+end_src

The three clauses do three different jobs: nothing, exactly one, or
the first one plus the rest. The middle clause exists so you don't
get a stray =", "= at the end or a missing one in the middle.
Exercise 8.11 makes it read "key and lamp".

Now let =look= mention what's there:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun look ()
  "Describe the room the player is in, and what is lying there."
  (let ((here (items-here (room-name *current-room*))))
    (if (null here)
        (describe-room *current-room*)
        (concatenate 'string
                     (describe-room *current-room*)
                     " You can see: "
                     (item-names here)
                     "."))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(princ (look))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
You are in the Kitchen. A warm kitchen that smells of bread. You can see: key.
#+end_example

And an inventory command, built from the same pieces:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun inventory ()
  "Describe what the player is carrying."
  (let ((carried (items-here 'player)))
    (if (null carried)
        "You are carrying nothing."
        (concatenate 'string
                     "You are carrying: "
                     (item-names carried)
                     "."))))
#+end_src

Check it against =take=:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (take 'key) (inventory))
;; => ("You take the key." "You are carrying: key.")
#+end_src

Both functions work with no extra bookkeeping. =take= marks an item
with the symbol =player=, and =items-here= finds everything marked
that way. The inventory is just another search through the same
alist, using the same recursive walk.

That's the shape of a Lisp program: a few small functions that each
walk a list or pick it apart, composed until they do something that
looks like a game.

**** A bug: pairs that are hidden but not gone

Reset the items, take the key, and ask what's in the kitchen:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(list (take 'key) (items-here 'kitchen))
;; => ("You take the key." (KEY))
#+end_src

The key is in the player's hands, yet the kitchen still has it. The
alist now holds two pairs for the key:

#+begin_example
((KEY . PLAYER) (LAMP . CELLAR) (KEY . KITCHEN) (LETTER . BEDROOM))
#+end_example

=assoc= stops at the first pair, so =item-location= gives the right
answer. =items-in= walks /every/ pair, so it finds the old one too.
The trick from Chapter 7 is safe only for code that looks things up
with =assoc=. Code that walks the whole list sees everything.

The fix: keep exactly one pair per item. A recursive function builds
a new alist in which the item's pair is replaced:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun set-location (pairs item place)
  "Return a copy of the alist PAIRS with ITEM moved to PLACE."
  (cond ((null pairs) nil)
        ((eq (caar pairs) item)
         (cons (cons item place)
               (set-location (cdr pairs) item place)))
        (t
         (cons (car pairs)
               (set-location (cdr pairs) item place)))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(set-location '((lamp . cellar) (key . kitchen)) 'key 'player)
;; => ((LAMP . CELLAR) (KEY . PLAYER))
#+end_src

It's the same three-clause walk as =items-in=: stop at the end,
replace the matching pair, copy every other pair. The old list is
untouched.

Now =take= stores the new list instead of pushing a pair:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun take (item)
  "Pick up ITEM if it is in the current room."
  (cond ((eq (item-location item) 'player)
         "You already have that.")
        ((eq (item-location item) (room-name *current-room*))
         (setf *item-locations*
               (set-location *item-locations* item 'player))
         (concatenate 'string "You take the " (item-name item) "."))
        (t
         "You don't see that here.")))
#+end_src

Run the test again:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(list (take 'key) (items-here 'kitchen) (inventory))
;; => ("You take the key." NIL "You are carrying: key.")
#+end_src

If you wrote =drop= in Exercise 7.8, redefine it the same way:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun drop (item)
  "Put down ITEM in the current room, if the player carries it."
  (if (eq (item-location item) 'player)
      (progn
        (setf *item-locations*
              (set-location *item-locations* item
                            (room-name *current-room*)))
        (concatenate 'string "You drop the " (item-name item) "."))
      "You don't have that."))
#+end_src

The lesson is worth keeping. A shortcut that relies on how one
function behaves, here "=assoc= finds the first pair", breaks as soon
as other code reads the same data in a different way.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 8.1

Write =my-length=, which returns the number of elements in a list,
without using =length=.

**** Exercise 8.2

Write =my-sum=, which adds up the numbers in a list, without using
=+= on the whole list at once. It should return =0= for an empty
list.

**** Exercise 8.3

Predict the result of each line, then check:

#+begin_example
(count-atoms '(a (b) c))
(countdown 3)
(factorial 4)
(items-in '((lamp . cellar) (key . kitchen)) 'kitchen)
(double-all-into '(1 2) nil)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 8.4

Write =my-reverse= using the accumulator pattern: a helper that
takes the list and the answer so far, and a one-line =my-reverse=
that calls it.

**** Exercise 8.5

Write =my-find=, which takes an item and a list and returns the item
when it is in the list, and =nil= when it isn't. The item can be
anywhere in the list.

**** Exercise 8.6

Write =count-symbol=, which takes a symbol and a tree and returns how
many times the symbol appears in the tree, at any depth.

**** Exercise 8.7

Write =flatten=, which turns a nested list into a flat one:
=(flatten '(a (b (c)) d))= gives =(a b c d)=. It should also handle
an empty list, giving =nil=.

**** Exercise 8.8

Each function below is wrong. Say what's wrong without running it,
then fix it and check.

#+begin_example
(defun bad-length (lst)
  (if (null lst)
      0
      (bad-length (rest lst))))

(defun bad-sum (lst)
  (if (null lst)
      nil
      (+ (first lst) (bad-sum (rest lst)))))

(defun bad-countdown (n)
  (if (zerop n)
      nil
      (cons n (bad-countdown (1+ n)))))
#+end_example

Careful with the third one: fix it, but don't call the broken version
with a large number. It only stops when the control stack runs out.

**** Exercise 8.9

Write =count-conses=, which returns how many cons cells a tree is
made of. =(count-conses '(a b))= gives =2=, and an atom gives =0=.

**** Exercise 8.10 (project)

Write =item-report=, which takes the item alist and returns a list of
strings, one per item, saying where it is. Items the player carries
should read differently from items lying in a room:

#+begin_example
("coin is carried"
 "lamp is in the cellar"
 "key is in the kitchen"
 "letter is in the bedroom")
#+end_example

**** Exercise 8.11 (project)

Rewrite =item-names= so that two items read ="key and lamp"= and
three read ="key, lamp and coin"=.

#+begin_example
(item-names nil)              ; => ""
(item-names '(key))           ; => "key"
(item-names '(key lamp))      ; => "key and lamp"
(item-names '(key lamp coin)) ; => "key, lamp and coin"
#+end_example

**** Exercise 8.12 (project)

Write =loot=, which picks up everything lying in the current room. It
returns ="There is nothing here to take."= when the room is empty,
and a message naming what was taken otherwise. Write a recursive
helper that calls =take= on each item in turn.

** Chapter 9: Functions as Values

Until now, a function was something you called. In Lisp a function
is also a value, like a number or a list. You can store it in a
variable, put it in a list, pass it to another function, and return
it from a function. This chapter shows how, and why it's one of the
most useful ideas in the language.

*** Getting hold of a function: =#'=

#+begin_src lisp
(defun twice (n)
  "Return N multiplied by two."
  (* n 2))

#'twice
#+end_src

The result looks like this:

#+begin_example
#<FUNCTION TWICE>
#+end_example

=#'twice= means "the function named =twice=", the function itself,
not a call to it. The =#<...>= notation is how Lisp prints objects
that can't be typed back in as text.

=#'= is short for the special operator =function=, the same way ='= is
short for =quote=:

#+begin_src lisp
(function twice)         ; => #<FUNCTION TWICE>
(functionp #'twice)      ; => T
(functionp 'twice)       ; => NIL
#+end_src

The last line matters. ='twice= is a symbol, the /name/ of the
function. =#'twice= is the function. Two different things, which the
end of this chapter puts to use.

*** Two kinds of names

Why not just write =twice=, the way Python does? Because in Common
Lisp, a symbol can name a function and a variable /at the same time/,
and the two never collide:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter twice 100)

(list twice (twice 5))  ; => (100 10)
#+end_src

The first =twice= is in a normal argument position, so Lisp looks up
the /variable/. The second is first in a list, so Lisp looks up the
/function/. Each name has two separate slots: one for its value as a
variable, one for its function.

That's why you need =#'=: it says "the function slot, please" in a
place where Lisp would otherwise read the variable slot.

The same rule lets you use =list= as a variable name without losing
the =list= function:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((list '(1 2 3)))
  (list list (length list)))   ; => ((1 2 3) 3)
#+end_src

You'll see parameters called =list= in other people's code. It works,
but it's confusing to read, so this guide uses =lst=.

Clean up the variable before going on, so it doesn't surprise you
later. =makunbound= removes a variable's value:

#+begin_src lisp
(makunbound 'twice)
#+end_src

*** Calling a function you're given: =funcall=

A function stored in a variable can't be called by putting the
variable first in a list. That would look in the function slot, which
is empty. =funcall= calls whatever function you hand it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *operation* #'twice)

(funcall *operation* 21)    ; => 42
(funcall #'+ 1 2 3)         ; => 6
#+end_src

=funcall= takes the function first, then the arguments, exactly as you
would write them in a normal call.

=funcall= also accepts a symbol. It looks up the function the symbol
names at the moment of the call:

#+begin_src lisp
(funcall 'twice 4)         ; => 8
#+end_src

*** Arguments from a list: =apply=

Sometimes the arguments are already in a list. =apply= spreads the
list out into separate arguments:

#+begin_src lisp
(apply #'+ '(1 2 3 4))      ; => 10
(apply #'max '(3 9 2))      ; => 9
#+end_src

=(apply #'+ '(1 2 3 4))= is the same as =(+ 1 2 3 4)=.

You can give some arguments directly before the list. The last
argument must always be a list:

#+begin_src lisp
(apply #'+ 1 2 '(3 4))      ; => 10
(apply #'list 'a '(b c))    ; => (A B C)
#+end_src

How to choose:

| Use       | When                                          |
|-----------+-----------------------------------------------|
| A normal call | You know the function when you write the code |
| =funcall= | The function is in a variable, the arguments are separate |
| =apply=   | The function is in a variable, the arguments are in a list |

*** Writing functions that take functions

Here is a function whose first parameter is itself a function:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun apply-twice (f x)
  "Call F on X, then call F on the result."
  (funcall f (funcall f x)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(apply-twice #'twice 5)    ; => 20
(apply-twice #'reverse '(1 2 3))   ; => (1 2 3)
(apply-twice #'rest '(a b c d))    ; => (C D)
#+end_src

Inside =apply-twice=, =f= is an ordinary variable holding a function,
so the body calls it with =funcall=. The caller decides /what/
happens. =apply-twice= decides /how often/.

A function that takes or returns functions is called a /higher-order
function/. Java has these too, through interfaces like =Function= and
method references like =Math::abs=. In Lisp they need no special
machinery.

*** =mapcar=: do it to every element

The most common higher-order function walks a list, calls a function
on each element, and collects the results:

#+begin_src lisp
(mapcar #'twice '(1 2 3))         ; => (2 4 6)
(mapcar #'string-upcase '("a" "b")) ; => ("A" "B")
(mapcar #'first '((a 1) (b 2) (c 3))) ; => (A B C)
#+end_src

Compare the first line with =double-all= from Chapter 8. That
recursive function, with its base case and its =cons=, did exactly
what =mapcar= does with =#'twice=. =mapcar= is that pattern, written
once, for any function.

With several lists, =mapcar= takes one element from each and passes
them together:

#+begin_src lisp
(mapcar #'+ '(1 2 3) '(10 20 30))  ; => (11 22 33)
(mapcar #'list '(a b) '(1 2))      ; => ((A 1) (B 2))
#+end_src

The function must accept as many arguments as there are lists. If the
lists have different lengths, =mapcar= stops at the end of the
shortest:

#+begin_src lisp
(mapcar #'+ '(1 2 3) '(10 20))     ; => (11 22)
#+end_src

Chapter 10 covers the rest of this family: functions that keep or
remove elements, find one, or combine them all into one value.

*** =lambda=: functions without names

Often the function you want to pass is tiny and used only once.
Giving it a name with =defun= would be clutter. =lambda= makes a
function on the spot:

#+begin_src lisp
(mapcar (lambda (n) (* n n)) '(1 2 3 4))   ; => (1 4 9 16)
#+end_src

=(lambda (n) (* n n))= is a function with one parameter =n= that
returns =n= squared. It has the same shape as a =defun=, without the
name:

#+begin_example
(defun square (n) (* n n))   ← a named function
(lambda (n) (* n n))         ← the same function, unnamed
#+end_example

A =lambda= evaluates to a function object, so you don't need =#'=
in front of it. You'll see =#'(lambda ...)= in older code. It means
the same thing.

#+begin_src lisp
(funcall (lambda (a b) (+ a b)) 3 4)       ; => 7
((lambda (a b) (+ a b)) 3 4)               ; => 7
#+end_src

The second line puts the =lambda= itself first in the list. That's
allowed, and it's the one case where something other than a name can
come first. You'll rarely write it, but you'll recognise it.

Run a =lambda= on its own and you see something like:

#+begin_example
#<FUNCTION (LAMBDA (A B)) {5383DB5B}>
#+end_example

The number in braces is a memory address. Yours will differ.

Everything from Chapter 5 works in a =lambda=: several body
expressions, =&optional=, =&key=, =&rest=.

#+begin_src lisp
(mapcar (lambda (name &optional (greeting "Hi"))
          (concatenate 'string greeting ", " name))
        '("Ada" "Alan"))
;; => ("Hi, Ada" "Hi, Alan")
#+end_src

*** A =lambda= can use the variables around it

A =lambda= written inside a =let= or a function body can use the
variables visible there:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun add-to-all (amount numbers)
  "Return NUMBERS with AMOUNT added to each one."
  (mapcar (lambda (n) (+ n amount)) numbers))

(add-to-all 10 '(1 2 3))    ; => (11 12 13)
#+end_src

The =lambda= has one parameter, =n=. It also uses =amount=, which
isn't its own parameter. It belongs to =add-to-all=. The =lambda= can
see it because it was written inside =add-to-all=, where =amount= is
in scope.

A function that remembers variables from the place where it was
written is called a /closure/. In Lisp every =lambda= is one, so you
never have to ask for it.

*** Functions that return functions

A function can build a =lambda= and hand it back:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-adder (amount)
  "Return a function that adds AMOUNT to its argument."
  (lambda (n) (+ n amount)))

(defparameter *add-five* (make-adder 5))

(funcall *add-five* 10)             ; => 15
(mapcar (make-adder 100) '(1 2 3))  ; => (101 102 103)
#+end_src

=make-adder= has already returned when =*add-five*= is called, yet
the function still knows that =amount= is =5=. The closure keeps the
variable alive for as long as the function exists. Each call to
=make-adder= creates a fresh =amount=, so =(make-adder 5)= and
=(make-adder 100)= don't interfere.

*** A closure can change its variables

The variable a closure remembers is a real variable, not a frozen
copy of its value. The closure can change it with =setf= or =incf=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-counter ()
  "Return a function that counts up by one each time it is called."
  (let ((count 0))
    (lambda () (incf count))))

(defparameter *counter-a* (make-counter))
(defparameter *counter-b* (make-counter))

(list (funcall *counter-a*)
      (funcall *counter-a*)
      (funcall *counter-a*)
      (funcall *counter-b*))
;; => (1 2 3 1)
#+end_src

Each counter has its own =count=, created by its own call to
=make-counter=. Nothing outside the closure can read or change that
variable. It is private state, the job a private field does in a Java
object, with no class around it.

*** Symbol or function?

Near the start of this chapter, ='twice= and =#'twice= turned out to
be different things. Both work with =funcall=, but they behave
differently when you redefine the function.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *saved* #'twice)   ; the function object itself

(defun twice (n)                 ; a new, wrong definition
  (* n 3))

(list (funcall *saved* 5)         ; the old function, kept in the variable
      (funcall 'twice 5))        ; whatever TWICE means right now
;; => (10 15)
#+end_src

SBCL may print a warning that =twice= is being redefined. That's
expected.

=#'twice= grabs the function as it is at that moment. Redefining
=twice= later creates a new function, and =*saved*= still holds the
old one. ='twice= is only a name, so =funcall= looks it up at each
call and always finds the current definition.

Put the right definition back:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun twice (n)
  "Return N multiplied by two."
  (* n 2))
#+end_src

In everyday code, write =#'=. It's clearer and slightly faster. When
you store functions in a table that should follow your edits while you
work at the REPL, store their names. The project step below does
exactly that.

*** =mapc=: when you only want the effect

=mapcar= collects results into a new list. If you call a function only
for what it does, such as printing, the collected list is waste.
=mapc= calls the function on each element the same way, and returns
the original list instead of building a new one:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(mapc #'print '(1 2 3))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
1
2
3
#+end_example

*** Common mistakes

**** Forgetting =#'=

#+begin_example
(mapcar twice '(1 2 3))
;; error: The variable TWICE is unbound.
#+end_example

Without =#'=, =twice= in an argument position is read as a variable.
Write =#'twice=.

**** Calling a parameter directly

#+begin_example
(defun call-it (f)
  (f 10))
;; warning: undefined function: F
#+end_example

=(f 10)= looks for a /function/ named =f=, and =f= is a variable. A
function held in a variable is called with =(funcall f 10)=.

**** Quoting a =lambda=

#+begin_example
(mapcar '(lambda (x) (* x x)) '(1 2))
;; error: The value (LAMBDA (X) (* X X)) is not of type (OR FUNCTION SYMBOL)
#+end_example

With a quote, the =lambda= is just a list of three elements, not a
function. Write the =lambda= without a quote, or with =#'=.

**** =apply= without a list at the end

#+begin_example
(apply #'+ 1 2)
;; error: The value 2 is not of type LIST
#+end_example

The last argument of =apply= must be a list. Use =funcall= when the
arguments are separate.

**** Expecting =mapcar= to change the list

=mapcar= returns a new list and leaves the original alone. If you want
a variable to hold the result, store it:
=(setf *numbers* (mapcar #'twice *numbers*))=.

**** The wrong number of arguments

#+begin_example
(mapcar #'cons '(a b c))
;; error: invalid number of arguments: 1
#+end_example

=mapcar= with one list calls the function with one argument. =cons=
needs two, so it needs two lists.

*** Project step: a command table

So far you play the game by calling functions: =(take 'key)=,
=(inventory)=. A real game reads commands such as =(take key)= and
decides which function to run. Functions as values make that a short
piece of code.

Start with a table, an alist that maps each command word to the name
of the function that handles it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *commands*
  '((look . look)
    (inventory . inventory)
    (take . take)
    (go . go-to)
    (loot . loot))
  "Map each command word to the function that handles it.")
#+end_src

If you wrote =drop= in Exercise 7.8, add =(drop . drop)= to the list.

The values are symbols, not =#'look= and so on. =run-command= looks
up the current definition every time, so when you fix a bug in =take=
(as Chapter 8 did), the table uses the fixed version without being
rebuilt.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun run-command (command)
  "Run COMMAND, a list such as (TAKE KEY), and return the reply."
  (let ((entry (assoc (first command) *commands*)))
    (if (null entry)
        "I don't know how to do that."
        (apply (cdr entry) (rest command)))))
#+end_src

=(first command)= is the verb, =(rest command)= is the list of its
arguments, and =apply= spreads that list into the call. =(take key)=
becomes =(take 'key)=, and =(look)= becomes =(look)= with no
arguments. Like =funcall=, =apply= accepts a symbol and calls the
function it names.

The words in the command don't need quotes inside, because the whole
command is quoted once:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))

(list (run-command '(take key))
      (run-command '(inventory))
      (run-command '(dance)))
;; => ("You take the key." "You are carrying: key." "I don't know how to do that.")
#+end_src

A whole session is a list of commands, and =mapcar= runs each one:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun play (commands)
  "Run each command in COMMANDS and return the list of replies."
  (mapcar #'run-command commands))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))

(play '((take key) (take key) (inventory)))
;; => ("You take the key." "You already have that." "You are carrying: key.")
#+end_src

=mapcar= calls =run-command= on the commands in order, from first to
last, so the second =take= sees the effect of the first.

A command with the wrong number of words, such as =(look kitchen)=,
calls =look= with an argument it doesn't accept, and you land in the
debugger. Press =q= to leave it (Appendix C). Later chapters show how
to catch that kind of error.

Adding a command now means writing a function and adding one pair to
=*commands*=. =run-command= never changes.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 9.1

Predict the result of each line, then check:

#+begin_example
(funcall #'list 1 2)
(apply #'* 2 '(3 4))
(mapcar #'length '((a) (b c) ()))
(mapcar (lambda (x) (cons x x)) '(a b))
(apply-twice (lambda (s) (concatenate 'string s "!")) "hi")
#+end_example

**** Exercise 9.2

Write =my-mapcar=, which takes a function and one list and does what
=mapcar= does, using recursion and =funcall=.

**** Exercise 9.3

Use =mapcar= and a =lambda= to turn ='("ada" "alan" "grace")= into
='("Hello, Ada" "Hello, Alan" "Hello, Grace")=.

**** Exercise 9.4

Write =make-multiplier=, which takes a number and returns a function
that multiplies its argument by that number.

**** Exercise 9.5

Write =compose=, which takes two functions =f= and =g= and returns a
function of one argument that calls =g= first, then =f= on the result.
=(funcall (compose #'twice #'1+) 5)= should give =12=.

**** Exercise 9.6

Each line below is wrong. Say what's wrong, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(mapcar twice '(1 2))

(defun call-with-ten (f)
  (f 10))

(funcall '(lambda (x) (+ x 1)) 5)

(apply #'max 3 7)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 9.7

Write =make-stepper=, which takes a start and a step and returns a
function. Its first call returns the start, and each later call
returns the previous value plus the step:

#+begin_example
(let ((s (make-stepper 10 5)))
  (list (funcall s) (funcall s) (funcall s)))
;; => (10 15 20)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 9.8

Use =mapcar= to build the alist =((A . 1) (B . 2) (C . 3))= from the
lists =(a b c)= and =(1 2 3)=, in one short expression.

**** Exercise 9.9

=*table-a*= and =*table-b*= below both point at =shout=. Predict what
each call returns after =shout= is redefined, then check:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun shout (s) (string-upcase s))

(defparameter *table-a* (list (cons 'shout #'shout)))
(defparameter *table-b* (list (cons 'shout 'shout)))

(defun shout (s) (concatenate 'string (string-upcase s) "!"))

(list (funcall (cdr (assoc 'shout *table-a*)) "hey")
      (funcall (cdr (assoc 'shout *table-b*)) "hey"))
#+end_src

**** Exercise 9.10 (project)

Add short forms to the command table: =l= for =look= and =i= for
=inventory=. Don't write any new functions.

**** Exercise 9.11 (project)

Write a =help= command that lists every command word in the table,
and add it to =*commands*=. Reuse =item-names= from Exercise 8.11,
which already turns a list of symbols into a readable string. The
reply should look like:

#+begin_example
"Commands: help, look, inventory, take, go and loot."
#+end_example

** Chapter 10: Working with Sequences

Chapter 8 wrote every list walk by hand. Chapter 9 showed that
=mapcar= is one of those walks, written once for any function. Common
Lisp has a whole family of these: functions that keep elements,
remove them, find one, count them, combine them or sort them. Once you
know the family, most of the recursive functions from Chapter 8 shrink
to one line.

These functions work on /sequences/. A sequence is an ordered
collection that you can walk from start to end. Lists are sequences,
and so are strings, which are sequences of characters. The same
function works on both:

#+begin_src lisp
(length '(a b c))     ; => 3
(length "abc")        ; => 3
(reverse "abc")       ; => "cba"
#+end_src

=mapcar= is the exception: it works on lists only. This chapter shows
=map=, its sequence version.

*** Keeping and removing: =remove-if= and =remove-if-not=

=remove-if= takes a predicate and a sequence, and returns a new
sequence without the elements the predicate says yes to:

#+begin_src lisp
(remove-if #'evenp '(1 2 3 4 5))                ; => (1 3 5)
(remove-if (lambda (n) (> n 10)) '(5 12 8 20))  ; => (5 8)
#+end_src

=remove-if-not= does the opposite: it keeps only the elements the
predicate says yes to.

#+begin_src lisp
(remove-if-not #'evenp '(1 2 3 4 5))            ; => (2 4)
#+end_src

The name says what gets removed: =remove-if= removes the matches,
=remove-if-not= removes the rest. Other languages call the second one
"filter".

On a string, the result is a string. =digit-char-p= returns a true
value for a digit character such as =#\7=, and =nil= otherwise:

#+begin_src lisp
(remove-if #'digit-char-p "a1b2c3")             ; => "abc"
#+end_src

When you only want to remove one particular value, =remove= is
shorter:

#+begin_src lisp
(remove 'b '(a b c b))      ; => (A C)
(remove #\space "a b c")    ; => "abc"
#+end_src

None of these change the sequence you give them. They return a new
one:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *numbers* (list 1 2 3 4))
(list (remove-if #'evenp *numbers*) *numbers*)
;; => ((1 3) (1 2 3 4))
#+end_src

**** =complement=

The Common Lisp standard marks the =-if-not= functions as deprecated,
meaning "may be removed one day". Every implementation still has them
and you'll see them everywhere, so use them freely. The alternative is
=complement=, which takes a predicate and returns a new predicate that
answers the opposite:

#+begin_src lisp
(funcall (complement #'evenp) 3)              ; => T
(remove-if (complement #'evenp) '(1 2 3 4))   ; => (2 4)
#+end_src

=complement= is a function that returns a function, like =make-adder=
in Chapter 9.

*** Finding: =find=, =find-if=, =position=, =member-if=

=find= returns the first element equal to the one you ask for, and
=find-if= returns the first one the predicate says yes to. Both return
=nil= when nothing matches:

#+begin_src lisp
(find 3 '(1 2 3 4))              ; => 3
(find-if #'evenp '(1 3 4 5 6))   ; => 4
(find-if #'evenp '(1 3))         ; => NIL
#+end_src

=position= and =position-if= return /where/ the match is, counting
from zero like =nth=:

#+begin_src lisp
(position 'c '(a b c))             ; => 2
(position-if #'digit-char-p "abc7") ; => 3
(position 'z '(a b c))             ; => NIL
#+end_src

=find= has one blind spot. If the element you're looking for is
=nil=, you can't tell "found =nil=" from "found nothing":

#+begin_src lisp
(find nil '(a nil b))     ; => NIL, but it was there
(position nil '(a nil b)) ; => 1, no doubt
#+end_src

=member= from Chapter 6 has an =-if= version too. It returns the rest
of the list from the first match:

#+begin_src lisp
(member-if #'evenp '(1 3 4 5))   ; => (4 5)
#+end_src

*** Counting: =count= and =count-if=

#+begin_src lisp
(count 'a '(a b a c))            ; => 2
(count-if #'evenp '(1 2 3 4))    ; => 2
(count #\s "mississippi")        ; => 4
#+end_src

*** Asking about every element: =every= and =some=

=every= is true when the predicate says yes to every element. =some=
is true when it says yes to at least one. =plusp= is true for numbers
above zero:

#+begin_src lisp
(every #'plusp '(1 2 3))     ; => T
(every #'plusp '(1 -2 3))    ; => NIL
(some #'evenp '(1 3 4))      ; => T
(some #'evenp '(1 3 5))      ; => NIL
#+end_src

Compare the first line with =all-positive-p= from Chapter 8. That
whole function is =(every #'plusp lst)=.

=every= on an empty list is =T=: no element says no. =some= on an
empty list is =NIL=: no element says yes.

=some= returns the first true value the predicate gave, the same way
=or= returns the first true value (Chapter 6). =digit-char-p= returns
the digit's value, so:

#+begin_src lisp
(some #'digit-char-p "ab7c")   ; => 7
#+end_src

Like =mapcar=, both accept several sequences and pass one element from
each. They stop at the end of the shortest:

#+begin_src lisp
(every #'< '(1 2 3) '(2 3 4))  ; => T, because 1<2, 2<3 and 3<4
#+end_src

=notany= and =notevery= are the negated versions: =(notany #'evenp
lst)= means "no element is even".

*** The keyword arguments they share

Most of these functions accept the same keyword arguments (=&key=,
Chapter 5). Learn them once and they work everywhere.

**** =:key=: look at part of each element

=:key= takes a function. Each element goes through that function
before it is compared or tested:

#+begin_src lisp
(find 'b '((a 1) (b 2) (c 3)) :key #'first)      ; => (B 2)
(remove-if #'evenp '((a 1) (b 2)) :key #'second)  ; => ((A 1))
(count 'kitchen '((key . kitchen) (lamp . cellar) (box . kitchen))
       :key #'cdr)                                ; => 2
#+end_src

The result is still the whole element, =(B 2)=, not just the part that
was compared. =(find 'b alist :key #'car)= does the same job as
=(assoc 'b alist)=.

**** =:test=: which equality to use

By default these functions compare with =eql=, which (like =eq=) says
two strings are different unless they are the very same object. That's
the string-key problem from Chapter 7 again:

#+begin_src lisp
(find "b" '("a" "b"))                  ; => NIL
(find "b" '("a" "b") :test #'string=)  ; => "b"
#+end_src

=:test= takes the function that decides whether two things count as
equal. The next section lists the choices.

**** =:start=, =:end=, =:from-end= and =:count=

=:start= and =:end= limit the work to part of the sequence. =:from-end
t= searches from the end. =:count= limits how many elements are
removed:

#+begin_src lisp
(position #\a "banana" :start 2)      ; => 3
(position #\a "banana" :from-end t)   ; => 5
(remove 'a '(a b a c a) :count 2)     ; => (B C A)
#+end_src

Positions are always counted from the start of the whole sequence,
even with =:start= or =:from-end=.

*** Four kinds of equality

You've used =eq= for symbols and =string== for strings. Common Lisp
has four general equality functions. Each one is looser than the one
above it:

| Function | True when                                                   |
|----------+-------------------------------------------------------------|
| =eq=     | Both are the very same object. Use it for symbols.          |
| =eql=    | Like =eq=, and also equal numbers of the same type, and equal characters. The default for =find=, =member=, =assoc= and the rest. |
| =equal=  | Like =eql=, and also lists and strings with the same contents. Case matters. |
| =equalp= | Like =equal=, ignoring case in strings, and comparing numbers by value. |

#+begin_src lisp
(list (eql #\a #\a)               ; T
      (eql 1 1.0)                 ; NIL, an integer and a float
      (equalp 1 1.0)              ; T
      (eql (list 1 2) (list 1 2)) ; NIL, two separate lists
      (equal (list 1 2) (list 1 2)) ; T, same contents
      (equal "abc" "ABC")         ; NIL
      (equalp "abc" "ABC"))       ; T
#+end_src

Don't use =eq= on numbers or characters. Whether two equal numbers are
the same object is up to the implementation, so =eq= may give either
answer. =eql= is always safe for them.

For lists of lists or strings, =:test #'equal= is usually what you
want:

#+begin_src lisp
(member '(b 2) '((a 1) (b 2)) :test #'equal)   ; => ((B 2))
#+end_src

*** =map=: =mapcar= for any sequence

=map= works like =mapcar= on strings as well as lists. Its first
argument says what kind of sequence to build, the same way
=concatenate= does (Chapter 3). =char-upcase= turns one character into
upper case, and =char-code= gives a character's number:

#+begin_src lisp
(map 'string #'char-upcase "abc")   ; => "ABC"
(map 'list #'char-code "AB")        ; => (65 66)
(map 'list #'+ '(1 2) '(10 20))     ; => (11 22)
#+end_src

*** =reduce=: combine everything into one value

=reduce= takes a function of two arguments and uses it to combine the
elements, from left to right:

#+begin_src lisp
(reduce #'+ '(1 2 3 4))     ; => 10
(reduce #'max '(3 9 2))     ; => 9
#+end_src

The steps for the first line:

#+begin_example
(+ 1 2)   => 3
(+ 3 3)   => 6
(+ 6 4)   => 10
#+end_example

The first argument of each call is the result so far, the second is
the next element. That's the accumulator from Chapter 8, with =reduce=
doing the walk.

**** Starting value: =:initial-value=

=:initial-value= gives =reduce= a starting point. The first call
combines it with the first element:

#+begin_src lisp
(reduce #'+ '(1 2 3) :initial-value 100)   ; => 106
#+end_src

It matters most for short lists. With one element, =reduce= returns
that element without calling the function. With no elements, it calls
the function with /no arguments/:

#+begin_src lisp
(reduce #'+ '(7))     ; => 7
(reduce #'+ '())      ; => 0, because (+) is 0
(reduce #'max '())    ; error: invalid number of arguments: 0
#+end_src

=(+)= with no arguments is =0= and =(*)= is =1=, so those two are safe.
=max= needs at least one number. When the list may be empty, give a
starting value, and =reduce= returns it unchanged:

#+begin_src lisp
(reduce #'max '() :initial-value 0)        ; => 0
#+end_src

With a starting value of =nil= and a =lambda=, =reduce= can build a
list. This is =my-reverse= from Exercise 8.4:

#+begin_src lisp
(reduce (lambda (acc x) (cons x acc))
        '(1 2 3)
        :initial-value nil)
;; => (3 2 1)
#+end_src

**** Other keywords: =:from-end= and =:key=

=:from-end t= combines from right to left. With =#'list= as the
function you can see the grouping:

#+begin_src lisp
(reduce #'list '(1 2 3))               ; => ((1 2) 3)
(reduce #'list '(1 2 3) :from-end t)   ; => (1 (2 3))
#+end_src

With =:from-end t=, the element comes first and the result so far
second, the reverse of the normal order.

=:key= works as everywhere else:

#+begin_src lisp
(reduce #'+ '((a . 1) (b . 2) (c . 3)) :key #'cdr)   ; => 6
#+end_src

*** =sort=

=sort= takes a sequence and a predicate of two arguments. The
predicate answers "should the first one come before the second?":

#+begin_src lisp
(sort (list 3 1 2) #'<)                 ; => (1 2 3)
(sort (list 3 1 2) #'>)                 ; => (3 2 1)
(sort (list "pear" "apple") #'string<)  ; => ("apple" "pear")
#+end_src

=:key= sorts by part of each element:

#+begin_src lisp
(sort (list '(b . 2) '(a . 1) '(c . 3)) #'< :key #'cdr)
;; => ((A . 1) (B . 2) (C . 3))
#+end_src

Strings are sequences too. =char<= compares two characters:

#+begin_src lisp
(sort (copy-seq "hello") #'char<)       ; => "ehllo"
#+end_src

**** =sort= destroys its argument

Every function so far in this chapter left its input alone. =sort= is
different: it reuses the conses of the list you give it and rearranges
them. Afterwards the original variable points at one cons somewhere in
the middle of the result:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *nums* (list 3 1 2))
(list (sort *nums* #'<) *nums*)
;; => ((1 2 3) (3)) in SBCL
#+end_src

=*nums*= still points at the cons holding =3=, which is now the last
cons of the sorted list. Other implementations may leave something
else behind. The rules that follow from this:

- *Always use the value =sort= returns.* To sort a variable, write
  =(setf *nums* (sort *nums* #'<))=.
- *To keep the original, sort a copy.* =copy-list= makes a new list
  with the same elements: =(sort (copy-list *nums*) #'<)=. =copy-seq=
  does the same for any sequence, strings included.
- *Never sort a quoted list.* ='(3 1 2)= written in your code is a
  constant, and changing a constant has undefined results. That's why
  the examples above use =(list 3 1 2)=.

A list fresh from =mapcar=, =remove-if= or =list= belongs to nobody
else, so sorting it directly is safe.

**** =stable-sort=

When two elements are equal for the predicate, =sort= may put them in
either order. =stable-sort= keeps their original order:

#+begin_src lisp
(stable-sort (list '(ada . 2) '(bob . 1) '(cy . 2)) #'< :key #'cdr)
;; => ((BOB . 1) (ADA . 2) (CY . 2))
#+end_src

=ada= stays ahead of =cy= because it was ahead before. =stable-sort=
destroys its argument just like =sort=.

*** =remove-duplicates= and =subseq=

=remove-duplicates= returns a sequence in which each element appears
once. By default it keeps the /last/ copy of each; =:from-end t= keeps
the first:

#+begin_src lisp
(remove-duplicates '(a b a c))              ; => (B A C)
(remove-duplicates '(a b a c) :from-end t)  ; => (A B C)
(remove-duplicates "mississippi")           ; => "msip"
#+end_src

It accepts =:test= and =:key= too. =subseq=, from Chapter 3, works on
lists as well as strings:

#+begin_src lisp
(subseq '(a b c d) 1 3)    ; => (B C)
#+end_src

Most of these functions have a destructive twin, such as =delete= for
=remove=. They save memory and come with the same rules as =sort=.
This guide uses the safe versions.

*** Choosing the right function

| You want                          | Use                          |
|-----------------------------------+------------------------------|
| A result for every element        | =mapcar=, =map=              |
| Only some elements                | =remove-if-not=, =remove-if= |
| The first matching element        | =find=, =find-if=            |
| Where it is                       | =position=, =position-if=    |
| How many match                    | =count=, =count-if=          |
| Yes or no for all or any          | =every=, =some=              |
| One value from all elements       | =reduce=                     |
| A new order                       | =sort=, =stable-sort=        |
| Each element once                 | =remove-duplicates=          |

Write a recursive function when none of these fits, for example when
you walk a tree as in =count-atoms=. These functions walk only the top
level of a sequence.

*** Common mistakes

**** Ignoring what =sort= returns

#+begin_example
(sort *nums* #'<)
*nums*    ; => (3), not the sorted list
#+end_example

Store the result: =(setf *nums* (sort *nums* #'<))=.

**** Sorting a quoted list

#+begin_example
(sort '(3 1 2) #'<)
#+end_example

It may seem to work, but it modifies a constant in your code. Write
=(sort (list 3 1 2) #'<)=, or sort a =copy-list=.

**** Mixing up =remove-if= and =remove-if-not=

=remove-if= throws away the matches. If you get exactly the elements
you wanted to keep, missing, swap to =remove-if-not=.

**** Finding strings without =:test=

#+begin_example
(find "key" '("lamp" "key"))   ; => NIL
#+end_example

The default test is =eql=. Add =:test #'string== or =:test #'equal=.

**** Confusing =:key= and =:test=

#+begin_example
(find "key" '("lamp" "key") :key #'string=)
;; error: invalid number of arguments: 1
#+end_example

=:key= takes a function of one argument that picks a part of each
element. =:test= takes a function of two arguments that compares.
=string== needs two, so it belongs in =:test=.

**** =reduce= on an empty list

=(reduce #'max '())= calls =(max)= and fails. Give an
=:initial-value= when the list can be empty.

**** Expecting =remove= to change the variable

Like =mapcar=, =remove= and friends return a new sequence. Store it
with =setf= when you need to.

*** Project step: shorter item functions

Three functions from Chapter 8 walk the item alist by hand. Each one
fits one of this chapter's patterns.

=items-in= keeps the pairs whose place matches, then takes the item
from each pair. That's =remove-if-not= followed by =mapcar=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun items-in (pairs place)
  "Return the items in the alist PAIRS whose location is PLACE."
  (mapcar #'car
          (remove-if-not (lambda (where) (eq where place))
                         pairs
                         :key #'cdr)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(items-in '((lamp . cellar) (key . kitchen) (box . kitchen)) 'kitchen)
;; => (KEY BOX)
#+end_src

=set-location= copies every pair, replacing one. That's =mapcar= with
a =lambda= that decides, pair by pair:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun set-location (pairs item place)
  "Return a copy of the alist PAIRS with ITEM moved to PLACE."
  (mapcar (lambda (pair)
            (if (eq (car pair) item)
                (cons item place)
                pair))
          pairs))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(set-location '((lamp . cellar) (key . kitchen)) 'key 'player)
;; => ((LAMP . CELLAR) (KEY . PLAYER))
#+end_src

=items-here=, =take=, =drop=, =loot= and =inventory= call these two by
name, so they use the new versions without any change.

One improvement while you're here: list the inventory in alphabetical
order. =items-here= returns a fresh list built by =mapcar=, so sorting
it directly is safe:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun inventory ()
  "Describe what the player is carrying, in alphabetical order."
  (let ((carried (items-here 'player)))
    (if (null carried)
        "You are carrying nothing."
        (concatenate 'string
                     "You are carrying: "
                     (item-names (sort carried #'string<
                                       :key #'symbol-name))
                     "."))))
#+end_src

Check it. The test moves two items straight into the player's hands
with =set-location=, so you don't have to walk around:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *item-locations* (set-location *item-locations* 'lamp 'player))
(setf *item-locations* (set-location *item-locations* 'key 'player))
(inventory)
;; => "You are carrying: key and lamp."
#+end_src

Without the =sort=, the reply would be ="key and lamp"= the other way
round, following the order of the alist. The =defparameter= alist is
quoted, but nothing here sorts or changes it: =set-location= builds a
new list each time.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 10.1

Predict the result of each line, then check:

#+begin_example
(remove-if-not #'evenp '(5 6 7 8))
(find-if #'numberp '(a b 3 c 4))
(count-if #'symbolp '(a 1 b 2 c))
(position #\n "banana")
(reduce #'* '(1 2 3 4))
(every #'symbolp '())
(remove-duplicates '(1 2 1 3 2))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 10.2

Rewrite each of these Chapter 8 functions with a one-line body, using
this chapter's functions: =my-sum= (8.2), =my-find= (8.5),
=all-positive-p= and =my-reverse= (8.4). Name them =sum2=, =find2=,
=all-positive2-p= and =reverse2= so they don't replace the originals.

**** Exercise 10.3

Use =remove-if= to drop every string shorter than four characters from
='("a" "lisp" "is" "great")=.

**** Exercise 10.4

Given:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *scores* '((ada . 90) (alan . 72) (grace . 85)))
#+end_src

Write one expression for each:

1. The alist sorted from the highest score to the lowest, leaving
   =*scores*= untouched.
2. The first pair with a score below 80.
3. The total of all scores.

**** Exercise 10.5

Write =my-remove-if= recursively, using =funcall=. It takes a
predicate and a list.

**** Exercise 10.6

Write =count-vowels=, which counts the vowels a, e, i, o and u in a
string, upper or lower case. =(count-vowels "Common Lisp")= gives =3=.
Hint: =find= can look for a character in the string ="aeiou"=.

**** Exercise 10.7

Predict =T= or =NIL= for each, then check:

#+begin_example
(eql 2 2)
(eql 2 2.0)
(equal "a" "a")
(equal '(1 (2)) '(1 (2)))
(equalp "Lisp" "LISP")
(eql (list 1) (list 1))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 10.8

Each line below is wrong. Say what's wrong, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(sort '(3 1 2) #'<)

(reduce #'max '())

(find "b" '("a" "b"))

(remove-if #'evenp *numbers*)   ; meant to remove the even numbers
                                ; from *numbers* for good
#+end_example

**** Exercise 10.9

Use =reduce= to find the longest string in ='("hi" "hello" "hey")=.

**** Exercise 10.10

Write =my-every=, which takes a predicate and a list and works like
=every=, using only =some=, =complement= and =not=.

**** Exercise 10.11 (project)

Write =empty-rooms=, which returns the names of the rooms in =*rooms*=
that have no items lying in them. Use =mapcar= and =remove-if=.

**** Exercise 10.12 (project)

Change =help= from Exercise 9.11 so that it lists the commands in
alphabetical order, each word once, even if a pair was pushed twice.

** Part II review

You can now read any list-shaped data at sight. The load-bearing
ideas: everything is an object built from cons cells (Ch 7);
recursion follows the shape of the data (Ch 8); functions are
values you can pass (Ch 9); sequences are lists with a standard
vocabulary (Ch 10); and =loop= is the do-what-I-mean iteration
(Ch 11). The project's alists — =*rooms*=, =*item-locations*= —
were this part's running example.

If any of these feel shaky, the fix is REPL time, not re-reading:
take one chapter's exercises and redo two of them from scratch.

* Part III: Core Tools

Part II taught you to think in lists: recursion, functions as values,
and the sequence functions. Part III gives you the everyday tools that
sit on top of those ideas: loops, formatted output, the data shapes
beyond lists, macros, the condition system, and the packages-and-files
layer that turns source into a program.

The chapters in this part don't depend on each other as tightly as
Part II's did, and you can skip around within it once you've read
Chapter 11. But the project steps build on the game as you left it at
the end of Part II, so the ordering is the order the game grows in.

** Chapter 11: Loops

Part II taught you to think in lists: recursion, functions as values,
and the sequence functions. This chapter opens the everyday tools with
the other way to repeat work: loops.

Recursion and =mapcar= cover a lot, but some jobs read more naturally
as "do this for each element" or "count from 1 to 10". Common Lisp has
small loops for the simple cases (=dolist=, =dotimes=) and one large,
flexible one (=loop=).

*** =dolist=: once per element

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(dolist (x '(a b c))
  (print x))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
A
B
C
#+end_example

=(dolist (x list) body...)= binds =x= to each element in turn and runs
the body. =x= is a local variable, like one made by =let=.

=dolist= returns =nil=. It's for effects, such as printing or changing
a variable, not for building a result. To return something, add a
third element, the /result form/, which is evaluated after the last
pass:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((evens nil))
  (dolist (n '(1 2 3 4 5 6) (reverse evens))
    (when (evenp n)
      (push n evens))))
;; => (2 4 6)
#+end_src

=push= puts each number at the front, so the result form reverses
the list at the end. That's the accumulator pattern from Chapter 8,
with =dolist= doing the walk.

*** =dotimes=: a number of times

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(dotimes (i 3)
  (print i))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
0
1
2
#+end_example

=(dotimes (i n) body...)= runs the body =n= times, with =i= going from
=0= up to =n= minus one. It also accepts a result form:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((total 0))
  (dotimes (i 5 total)
    (incf total i)))
;; => 10, because 0 + 1 + 2 + 3 + 4
#+end_src

*** Leaving early: =return= and =return-from=

=(return value)= leaves a =dolist= or =dotimes= at once, and the loop
returns =value=:

#+begin_src lisp
(dolist (x '(1 3 4 5 6))
  (when (evenp x)
    (return x)))
;; => 4
#+end_src

The loop stops at =4= and never looks at =5= or =6=.

=return= leaves only the innermost loop. To leave a whole function
from inside nested loops, use =(return-from name value)=, where =name=
is the function's name:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun find-pair-sum (target lst)
  "Return the first two elements of LST that add up to TARGET."
  (dolist (a lst)
    (dolist (b lst)
      (when (= (+ a b) target)
        (return-from find-pair-sum (list a b))))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(list (find-pair-sum 10 '(1 4 6))
      (find-pair-sum 100 '(1 4 6)))
;; => ((4 6) NIL)
#+end_src

A plain =return= here would leave only the inner =dolist=, and the
outer one would carry on with the next =a=. When nothing matches, both
