loops finish and return =nil=, so the function returns =nil=.

*** =do=: the old general loop

You'll meet =do= in older code. It sets up variables, steps them, and
stops when a test is true:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(print
 (do ((i 0 (1+ i)))     ; variable, start value, step
     ((= i 3) 'done)    ; stop when true, then return DONE
   (print i)))          ; the body
#+end_src

#+begin_example
0
1
2
DONE
#+end_example

Each variable is written =(name start step)=. Before each pass, =do=
checks the test. When it is true, =do= returns the value after it.
Most programmers reach for =loop= instead, so you only need to be able
to read =do=.

*** =loop=: one macro, many clauses

=loop= reads almost like English. It is built from /clauses/: words
such as =for=, =collect= and =when=, each followed by its arguments.

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(1 2 3)
      collect (* x 10))
;; => (10 20 30)
#+end_src

"For each =x= in the list, collect =x= times ten." =collect= builds the
list in order, so no =reverse= is needed. The sections below go
through the clauses one group at a time.

**** Counting with =for=

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for i from 1 to 5 collect i)        ; => (1 2 3 4 5)
(loop for i from 0 below 5 collect i)     ; => (0 1 2 3 4)
(loop for i below 3 collect i)            ; => (0 1 2)
(loop for i from 10 downto 7 collect i)   ; => (10 9 8 7)
(loop for i from 0 to 10 by 5 collect i)  ; => (0 5 10)
#+end_src

| Words      | Meaning                                  |
|------------+------------------------------------------|
| =from n=   | Start at =n= (=0= if left out)           |
| =to n=     | Stop after =n=: =n= is included          |
| =below n=  | Stop before =n=: =n= is left out         |
| =downto n= | Count down, =n= included                 |
| =by n=     | Step by =n= instead of =1=               |

**** Walking lists and strings

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(a b c) collect (list x))     ; => ((A) (B) (C))
(loop for tail on '(a b c) collect tail)      ; => ((A B C) (B C) (C))
(loop for c across "abc" collect c)           ; => (#\a #\b #\c)
#+end_src

=in= gives each element. =on= gives each tail of the list: the whole
list, then its =rest=, then the =rest= of that. =across= walks a
string, one character at a time.

=by= works on lists too. It takes the function used to move forward,
=cdr= by default:

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(a 1 b 2 c 3) by #'cddr collect x)   ; => (A B C)
#+end_src

**** Taking elements apart

The variable after =for= can be a pattern. Each element is split up to
fit it:

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for (name . score) in '((ada . 90) (alan . 72))
      collect name)
;; => (ADA ALAN)

(loop for (a b) in '((1 2) (3 4))
      collect (+ a b))
;; => (3 7)
#+end_src

The first pattern matches dotted pairs, the shape of an alist. The
second matches two-element lists. This is the cleanest way to walk an
alist.

**** Several =for= clauses

With two =for= clauses, both step together, and the loop stops when
either one runs out:

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(a b c)
      for i from 1
      collect (cons i x))
;; => ((1 . A) (2 . B) (3 . C))
#+end_src

=for i from 1= has no end, so the list decides when to stop.

**** =for= with ===: computed values

=for var = start then next= starts with one value and computes each
later value from the previous one:

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for n = 1 then (* n 2)
      repeat 5
      collect n)
;; => (1 2 4 8 16)
#+end_src

=repeat 5= stops the loop after five passes. Without =then=, the
expression after === is evaluated again on every pass:

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(1 2 3)
      for square = (* x x)
      collect square)
;; => (1 4 9)
#+end_src

**** Collecting results

| Clause         | Result                                  |
|----------------+-----------------------------------------|
| =collect x=    | A list of every =x=, in order            |
| =append x=     | The lists =x= joined into one list       |
| =sum x=        | The total                               |
| =count x=      | How many times =x= was true             |
| =maximize x=   | The largest =x=                          |
| =minimize x=   | The smallest =x=                         |

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(1 2 3) sum x)                ; => 6
(loop for x in '(1 2 3 4) count (evenp x))    ; => 2
(loop for x in '(3 9 2) maximize x)           ; => 9
(loop for x in '((a b) (c)) append x)         ; => (A B C)
#+end_src

=maximize= and =minimize= on an empty list give a result the standard
leaves open, so check for an empty list first.

**** Choosing elements: =when=, =unless=, =if=

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(1 2 3 4 5)
      when (evenp x) collect x)
;; => (2 4)
#+end_src

A =when= clause controls only the clause right after it. To control
two, join them with =and=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(print
 (loop for x in '(1 2 3 4)
       when (evenp x) collect x and do (print x)))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
2
4
(2 4)
#+end_example

=if= has an =else=. With =into=, each =collect= fills its own named
list:

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(1 2 3 4)
      if (evenp x) collect x into evens
      else collect x into odds
      finally (return (list evens odds)))
;; => ((2 4) (1 3))
#+end_src

With =into=, =loop= no longer returns the list by itself. The
=finally= clause runs once after the last pass, and =(return ...)=
inside it sets the value of the whole =loop=.

**** Running code: =do=

=do= inside a =loop= runs ordinary Lisp forms on every pass. It may be
followed by several forms:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(loop for x in '(1 2)
      do (princ "item: ")
         (princ x)
         (print 'done))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
item: 1
DONE item: 2
DONE 
#+end_example

=print= starts with a newline and ends with a space, which explains
the odd layout. A =loop= with only =do= returns =nil=.

**** Local variables: =with=

#+begin_src lisp
(loop with total = 0
      for x in '(1 2 3)
      do (incf total x)
      finally (return total))
;; => 6
#+end_src

=with= makes a variable once, before the first pass. Here =sum= would
be shorter, but =with= helps when the work doesn't fit one of the
collecting clauses.

**** Stopping: =while=, =until=, =return=

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(1 2 3 -1 4)
      while (plusp x)
      collect x)
;; => (1 2 3)

(loop for x in '(1 3 4 5)
      when (evenp x) return x)
;; => 4
#+end_src

=while= stops as soon as its test is false, =until= as soon as its
test is true. =return= as a clause leaves the loop with a value. You
can also write the Lisp form =(return x)= inside a =do=.

**** Yes-or-no questions: =always=, =never=, =thereis=

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for x in '(1 2 3) always (plusp x))               ; => T
(loop for x in '(1 2 3) never (> x 5))                  ; => T
(loop for x in '(a 7 b) thereis (and (numberp x) x))    ; => 7
#+end_src

These are =every=, =notany= and =some= from Chapter 10, as clauses.
=thereis= returns the first true value, like =some=.

**** Clause order

Put =with= and =for= clauses first, then everything else: =repeat=,
=while=, =when=, =collect=, =do= and the rest. The Common Lisp
standard requires this order, and some implementations complain if
you break it.

*** Which one to use

| Job                                        | Use                         |
|--------------------------------------------+-----------------------------|
| A result for every element                 | =mapcar=                    |
| Keep, find, count with one predicate       | Chapter 10's functions      |
| Effects for each element, such as printing | =dolist=                    |
| Something =n= times                        | =dotimes=                   |
| Counting ranges, several lists at once, filtering and collecting together, early exit with a result | =loop= |
| Trees, nested structure                    | Recursion (Chapter 8)       |

These overlap. =(mapcar #'twice lst)= and
=(loop for x in lst collect (twice x))= do the same job. Pick the one
that reads most clearly.

*** Common mistakes

**** Expecting =dolist= to return results

#+begin_example
(dolist (x '(1 2 3)) (* x 2))   ; => NIL
#+end_example

The body's values are thrown away. Use =mapcar=, or
=(loop for x in lst collect (* x 2))=.

**** =to= versus =below=

=(loop for i from 0 to 3 collect i)= gives four numbers, =(0 1 2 3)=.
To match =nth= positions in a three-element list, use =below 3=.

**** =in= on a string

#+begin_example
(loop for c in "abc" collect c)
;; error: The value "abc" is not of type LIST
#+end_example

=in= walks lists. Strings need =across=.

**** Forgetting =do=

#+begin_example
(loop for x in '(1 2 3) (print x))
;; error: (PRINT X) found where a LOOP keyword was expected
#+end_example

Plain Lisp forms inside a =loop= go after =do=.

**** =finally= without =return=

#+begin_example
(loop with total = 0
      for x in '(1 2 3)
      do (incf total x)
      finally total)    ; => NIL
#+end_example

=finally= runs its forms, but their value is thrown away. Write
=finally (return total)=.

**** =return= inside nested loops

=return= leaves only the innermost loop. To leave the function, use
=return-from= with the function's name.

**** =when= controlling one clause only

In =when (evenp x) collect x do (print x)=, the =print= runs for every
=x=, even or odd. Join the clauses with =and= when both belong to the
=when=.

*** Project step: a transcript and a shorter report

=play= from Chapter 9 returns a list of replies. A real game prints
each command and its reply as it goes. That's a job for =dolist=,
because the work is printing, not building a list.

=terpri= prints a newline. Its name is an old abbreviation of
"terminate print line".

#+begin_src lisp
(defun transcript (commands)
  "Run each command in COMMANDS, printing it and its reply."
  (dolist (command commands)
    (princ "> ")
    (princ command)
    (terpri)
    (princ (run-command command))
    (terpri)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))

(transcript '((take key) (take key) (inventory)))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
> (TAKE KEY)
You take the key.
> (TAKE KEY)
You already have that.
> (INVENTORY)
You are carrying: key.
#+end_example

=princ= prints the command list in upper case, because that's how Lisp
stores the symbols (Chapter 3). The reply strings print as written.

Next, =item-report= from Exercise 8.10 walked the alist by hand and
used =caar= and =cdar= to reach inside each pair. A destructuring
=loop= names the two halves directly:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun item-report (pairs)
  "Return one string per item in PAIRS, saying where it is."
  (loop for (item . place) in pairs
        collect (if (eq place 'player)
                    (concatenate 'string (item-name item) " is carried")
                    (concatenate 'string
                                 (item-name item)
                                 " is in the "
                                 (string-downcase (symbol-name place))))))
#+end_src

If you skipped Exercise 8.10, this definition is complete on its own.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((coin . player)
    (lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)))
(item-report *item-locations*)
;; => ("coin is carried" "lamp is in the cellar" "key is in the kitchen")
#+end_src

(loop for i below 4 sum i)

(loop for tail on '(1 2 3) collect (length tail))
(dolist (x '(1 2 3)) x)
(loop for x in '(5 8 2 9) count (> x 4))
(loop for c across "hello" count (char= c #\l))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 11.12 (project)

Change =transcript= so that it prints a header line
="You are in the kitchen."= before the commands, and a footer line
="Game over."= after them, using a =let= around the =dolist=. Then

many of them =run-command= doesn't recognise. =run-command= returns a

** Chapter 12: format

Every chapter so far has built strings with =concatenate= and
=string-downcase=. That works, but the game's messages are turning
into nested calls. =format= is Lisp's answer: one function that takes
a /control string/ with placeholders and produces text. Other
languages copied it: Python's =%= formatting and its newer format
mini-language both trace back to =format=.

This chapter is a reference as much as a lesson. Learn the first
third; the rest you can look up as you need it.

*** The shape of =format=

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(format t "Hello, world.~%")
#+end_src

#+begin_example
Hello, world.
#+end_example

=format= takes a /destination/, a control string, and any number of
arguments. The destination decides where the text goes:

| Destination      | Meaning                                  |
|------------------+------------------------------------------|
| =t=              | Print to standard output, return =nil=   |
| =nil=            | Build a string and return it             |
| A stream         | Write to that stream (Chapter 16)        |

So =t= is for printing and =nil= is for building. The game's message
functions build strings, so they use =nil=:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "You take the ~A." 'key)
;; => "You take the key."
#+end_src

*** The tilde: directives

Everything in a control string is copied out word for word, except
what starts with a tilde. A tilde begins a /directive/, a placeholder
or an instruction:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "You see ~A and ~A." 'lamp 'key)
;; => "You see LAMP and KEY."
#+end_src

=~A= means "put the next argument here, printed for people to read".
Arguments are used in order, one per placeholder.

The directive letter is case-insensitive: =~a= and =~A= are the same.
Capital letters in the directives that print words choose the case of
the output, as you'll see.

*** The essential directives

**** =~A= and =~S=

=print= and =princ= from Chapter 1 are two ways to show a value:
=princ= for people, =print= for Lisp. =~A= and =~S= are =format='s
versions of the same distinction:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~A" "hello")   ; => "hello"
(format nil "~S" "hello")   ; => "hello", with the quotes
(format nil "~A" 'key)      ; => KEY
(format nil "~S" 'key)      ; => KEY
#+end_src

For strings the difference matters, for symbols it doesn't. Use =~A=
for messages people read, =~S= when the text must be read back by
Lisp, for example in a saved file.

**** =~D=: decimal integers

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "You have ~D coins." 42)          ; => "You have 42 coins."
(format nil "~D" -7)                          ; => -7
#+end_src

It fails on anything but an integer, on purpose: =format= doesn't
guess.

**** =~F=: floats

=~-F= says how many digits to show after the decimal point:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "Distance: ~,2F km" 123.4567)   ; => "Distance: 123.46 km"
(format nil "~F" 0.5)                       ; => "0.5"
#+end_src

**** =~C=: characters

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "Press ~C to quit." #\q)   ; => "Press q to quit."
#+end_src

**** =~%=: newline, =~&=: fresh line, =~~=: tilde

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(format t "Line one.~%Line two.~%")
#+end_src

#+begin_example
Line one.
Line two.
#+end_example

=~%= prints exactly =n= newlines, where =n= is a number between the
tilde and the letter. =~&= is more forgiving: it prints a newline only
if the output isn't already at the start of one. Ending messages with
=~&= instead of =~%= is a good habit, because two messages in a row
then never leave a blank line between them.

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(format t "A.~%")
(format t "~3%")
(format t "B.~&")
(format t "C.~&")
#+end_src

#+begin_example
A.

B.
C.
#+end_example

=~~= prints one tilde, since a bare =~= would start a directive.

*** Arguments used in other ways

**** =~{~}: the next argument is a list

Some directives don't print an argument themselves, but change how
the next ones are treated. =~{= says "the next argument is a list, and
its elements are the arguments for what follows":

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~{~A~}" '(a b c))       ; => "ABC"
(format nil "~{~A, ~}" '(a b c))     ; => "A, B, C, "
#+end_src

=~{~}= walks the list, running the directives between =~{= and =~}=
once per element. That's =mapcar= inside a control string.

Because =~A= consumes from the list, you can interleave text and
values:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~{~A: ~A~%~}" '("name" "Ada" "age" "36"))
;; => "name: Ada
;;     age: 36
;;     "
#+end_src

**** =~@{~}: the arguments themselves are the list

=~@{= is the same walk, but over =format='s own arguments instead of a
list:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~{~A or ~}" 'x 'y)      ; wrong, no list argument
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~@{~A or ~}" 'x 'y)     ; => "X or Y or "
#+end_src

**** Trailing separators: =~^=

Both walk forms have a companion directive that changes the last
pass. =~^= means "stop here if the list is exhausted":

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~{~A~^, ~}" '(a b c))       ; => "A, B, C"
(format nil "~@{~A~^, ~}" 'x 'y 'z)      ; => "X, Y, Z"
#+end_src

=~^= is how you join a list with commas and no dangling separator —
the exact job =item-names= did by hand in Exercise 8.11.

**** =~T=: columns

=~T= moves to a column, like a tab:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(format t "~A~10T~A~%" 'room 'items)
(format t "~A~10T~A~%" 'kitchen 'key)
#+end_src

#+begin_example
ROOM      ITEMS
KITCHEN   KEY
#+end_example

=~10T= means "move to column 10". =~@T= means "move right by that
many spaces".

*** Width, padding and case

A number between =~= and the letter sets the /minimum width/; the
value is padded with spaces on the left:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "[~5D]" 42)    ; => "[   42]"
(format nil "[~5A]" "hi")  ; => "[   hi]"
#+end_src

The =@= modifier flips the padding to the right:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "[~5@A]" "hi")  ; => "[hi   ]"
#+end_src

The =:= modifier on =~D= adds commas:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~:D" 1000000)   ; => "1,000,000"
#+end_src

On =~A= and =~S=, the =:= modifier prints =nil= as =()= instead of
=NIL=:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~A" nil)    ; => "NIL"
(format nil "~:A" nil)   ; => "()"
#+end_src

Modifiers combine: =~:@D= signs the number with =+=:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~:@D" 42)   ; => "+42"
#+end_src

*** Conditionals inside format

=~[~]= picks one of several texts by the value of an argument. The
argument is an integer, counting from zero:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "You have ~[no coins~;one coin~:;~D coins~]." 3 3)
;; => "You have 3 coins."
#+end_src

The =:;= clause is the default, used when the number is out of range.
This one expression handles zero, one and many — the plural problem,
solved in the control string. Pass =0= or =1= to see the other
clauses.

=~:[~]= takes a true-or-false argument instead: two clauses, =nil=
gets the first, anything else the second:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "The door is ~:[closed~;open~]." t)   ; => "The door is open."
#+end_src

=~:[= can also compare against nothing at all: =~:[~A~;nothing~]= takes
no integer. When the argument is =nil=, the second clause prints:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "You see ~:[something~;nothing~]." nil)
;; => "You see nothing."
#+end_src

*** Recursive format: ~/= and named functions

There's a directive that spells out numbers in words:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~R" 42)        ; => "forty-two"
(format nil "~:R" 3)        ; => "third"
#+end_src

=~R='s related trick is more important: you can write your own
directive as a Lisp function and call it with =~/name=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun format-room (stream argument colonp atp)
  "Print ARGUMENT, a room entry, as a short line."
  (declare (ignore colonp atp))
  (format stream "~A: ~A" (first argument) (second argument)))

(format nil "~/format-room/" '(kitchen "the key is here"))
;; => "KITCHEN: the key is here"
#+end_src

A =~/= directive calls a function of four arguments: the stream,
the value, and two flags that say whether you wrote =:= and =@=.
=declare (ignore ...)= tells the compiler the unused parameters are
on purpose, which keeps it quiet. This is rare in day-to-day code,
but it explains the shape: the first argument of any =format= helper
function is the stream, and that's why =format= can take =nil= or =t=
there.

*** Building messages for the game

The game's string building so far:

#+begin_src lisp
(concatenate 'string
             (item-name item)
             " is in the "
             (string-downcase (symbol-name place)))
#+end_src

The same message with =format=:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~A is in the ~(~A~)." 'key 'kitchen)
;; => "key is in the kitchen"
#+end_src

=~(~A~)= lower-cases everything it wraps. With =:= it capitalises the
first word instead:

#+begin_src lisp
(format nil "~:(~A~) is in the ~(~A~)." 'key 'kitchen)
;; => "Key is in the kitchen"
#+end_src

The examples above are fragments; the full message is in the project
step. The important part is the pair of directive modifiers:

| Directive | Effect                              |
|-----------+-------------------------------------|
| =~A=      | As is                               |
| =~(~A~)=  | Lower-case                          |
| =~:(~A~)= | First letter of each word upper-case |
| =~@(~A~)= | First letter upper-case, rest lower |
| =~:@(~A~)=| All upper-case                      |

=~@(~A~)= is the one for starting a sentence: it gives ="Key"= from
=KEY=, with any capitals inside tamed to lower case.

*** Common mistakes

**** Extra or missing arguments

#+begin_example
(format nil "~A and ~A" 'x)
;; error: too few arguments
#+end_example

Count the placeholders and the arguments. One =~A= per argument.

**** A stray tilde

#+begin_example
(format nil "100% sure")
;; error: unknown directive %
#+end_example

=%= after a space is a directive too, roughly =~%=. Write =~~= for a
literal tilde, and be careful with =%= in text meant for people.

**** =format t= inside a builder

A function whose job is to /return/ a string must use =format nil=.
Mixing them up either prints nothing or returns =nil=:

#+begin_example
(defun describe-room (room)
  (format t "You are in the ~A." room))
;; returns NIL, prints as a side effect
#+end_example

**** =~D= on the wrong type

=~D= takes an integer. =(format nil "~D" 'key)= is an error, and
=(format nil "~D" 3.5)= is too. Use =~A= when the type varies.

**** Case directives without the closing =~)=

=~(= must be closed with =~)=, just as =~{= must be closed with =~}=.
An unclosed case directive reads the rest of the string as text to
change.

*** Project step: messages in one place

The game's message functions each build strings by hand. This step
rewrites them around =format=, and gathers the messages where you can
see and change them.

First the helper that names a list of items with commas, no "and":

#+begin_src lisp
(defun item-list-string (items)
  "Return a readable list of the names of ITEMS, comma-separated."
  (format nil "~{~(~A~)~^, ~}" (mapcar #'symbol-name items)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(list (item-list-string nil)
      (item-list-string '(key))
      (item-list-string '(key lamp))
      (item-list-string '(key lamp coin)))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("" "key" "key, lamp" "key, lamp, coin")
#+end_example

The empty list gives an empty string, which the callers guard against.
The ="key and lamp"= form needs two passes, which =~{~}= alone can't
do, so keep the =item-names= from Exercise 8.11 for the game's player
messages, and use =item-list-string= where plain commas are right: the
=help= command and =describe-all=.

Now the messages, rebuilt around =format nil=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun inventory ()
  "Describe what the player is carrying, in alphabetical order."
  (let ((carried (sort (items-here 'player)
                       #'string< :key #'symbol-name)))
    (if (null carried)
        "You are carrying nothing."
        (format nil "You are carrying: ~A." (item-names carried)))))

(defun take (item)
  "Pick up ITEM if it is in the current room."
  (cond ((eq (item-location item) 'player)
         (format nil "You already have the ~(~A~)." item))
        ((eq (item-location item) (room-name *current-room*))
         (setf *item-locations*
               (set-location *item-locations* item 'player))
         (format nil "You take the ~(~A~)." item))
        (t
         "You don't see that here.")))

(defun drop (item)
  "Put down ITEM in the current room, if the player carries it."
  (if (eq (item-location item) 'player)
      (progn
        (setf *item-locations*
              (set-location *item-locations* item
                            (room-name *current-room*)))
        (format nil "You drop the ~(~A~)." item))
      "You don't have that."))

(defun loot ()
  "Take everything lying in the current room."
  (let ((found (items-here (room-name *current-room*))))
    (if (null found)
        "There is nothing here to take."
        (progn
          (take-all found)
          (format nil "You take ~A." (item-names found))))))

(defun item-report (pairs)
  "Return one string per item in PAIRS, saying where it is."
  (loop for (item . place) in pairs
        collect (if (eq place 'player)
                    (format nil "~@(~A~) is carried" item)
                    (format nil "~@(~A~) is in the ~(~A~)"
                            item place))))
#+end_src

=take=, =drop= and =item-report= now pass symbols straight to
=format=, and the case directives do the lowering. Compare
=item-report= with the version from Chapter 11: no =symbol-name=, no
=string-downcase=, no =concatenate=. The message reads as a sentence
in the code.

Run the whole thing. This resets the items and the current room, so
run it as one piece:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))

(list (run-command '(take key))
      (run-command '(take key))
      (run-command '(inventory))
      (run-command '(take lamp))
      (run-command '(drop key)))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("You take the key."
 "You already have the key."
 "You are carrying: key."
 "You don't see that here."
 "You drop the key.")
#+end_example

One more: a single function that describes a room the way a player
sees it, the way =look= does but with the items in the sentence. The
conditional directives handle the empty room and the plurals:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun describe-room-text (room)
  "Return a sentence describing ROOM and what lies there."
  (let ((here (items-here room)))
    (format nil "You are in the ~(~A~). ~:[There is nothing here.~;You see ~{~:(~A~)~^, ~}~]~&"
            room
            here
            (mapcar #'symbol-name here))))
#+end_src

Read the control string from the start: the room's name, a period,
then =~:[= with two clauses — =nil= gets "There is nothing here.",
anything else gets "You see " followed by the item walk with commas.
=~&= ends the string so two descriptions never run together.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(list (describe-room-text 'kitchen)
      (describe-room-text 'garden))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("You are in the kitchen. You see Key."
 "You are in the garden. There is nothing here.")
#+end_example

The capital on ="Key"= comes from =~:(~A~)=. With two items, the walk
would print ="Key, Letter"= — right for a list, and the exercises
refine the "and".

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 12.1

Predict the result of each line, then check:

#+begin_example
(format nil "~A!" 'done)
(format nil "~S" "hi")
(format nil "~3D" 7)
(format nil "~,1F" 2.25)
(format nil "~A~A" 'a 'b)
(format nil "a~2%b")
#+end_example

gives ="a, b, c"=. One =format= expression.

="1: one, 2: two, 3: three"= from the list ='((1 "one") (2 "two")
(3 "three"))=, with no =loop= or =mapcar=.

in 8 with two decimals:
=(price-line 'coffee 3.5)= gives ="coffee         3.50"=.

Predict the result, then check:

#+begin_example
(format nil "You have ~[no keys~;one key~:;~D keys~]." 0)
(format nil "You have ~[no keys~;one key~:;~D keys~]." 1)
(format nil "You have ~[no keys~;one key~:;~D keys~]." 5)

**** Exercise 12.7

Each line below is wrong. Say what's wrong, then fix it.

(format nil "50% off")

#+end_example

Write =bar=, which takes a number from 0 to 10 and returns a health
bar: that many ="#"= characters, then the rest as ="-"=, in brackets.

prepared string of =#= characters, plus a second prepared string of

**** Exercise 12.9 (project)

Rewrite =help= from Exercise 10.12 to use =item-list-string=, keeping
the alphabetical order. The reply should end with a period.

**** Exercise 12.10 (project)

Improve =describe-room-text= so two items read ="You see Key and
Letter"= and three read ="You see Key, Letter and Coin"=. You may
write a helper function; the =format= call stays the same.

** Chapter 13: Structures, Hash Tables and Arrays

Lists are flexible, but they have costs. Finding an item in an alist
takes a walk through the whole list. Reaching the third field of a
room means =third= or =caddr=, and reading =(caddr room)= later
tells you nothing about what that slot means. This chapter adds three
new data shapes that fix those problems:

- *Structures*: named records with named fields, for data with a fixed
  shape, such as a room.
- *Hash tables*: look up by key in one step, however many entries.
- *Arrays*: fixed-length indexed collections, faster than lists.

*** Structures: =defstruct=

=defstruct= defines a new kind of record:

#+begin_src lisp
(shadow "ROOM")   ; ROOM is also a CL function name; take our own symbol
(defstruct room
  name
  description
  exits)
#+end_src

That one form does a lot:

- It defines a new type, =room=.
- It defines a constructor, =make-room=.
- It defines an accessor for each field: =room-name=,
  =room-description=, =room-exits=.
- It defines a predicate, =room-p=.

Each of these exists before you write a line for them. The names are
derived from the structure name: =make-room=, =room-name=, =room-p=.

Creating a record uses keyword arguments, one per field:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *bedroom*
  (make-room :name 'bedroom
             :description "A quiet room with a bed."
             :exits '(south)))
#+end_src

Fields you leave out get =nil=.

Reading a field calls the accessor:

#+begin_src lisp
(room-name *bedroom*)          ; => BEDROOM
(room-description *bedroom*)   ; => "A quiet room with a bed."
#+end_src

Changing a field uses =setf= on the accessor:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf (room-exits *bedroom*) '(north south))
(room-exits *bedroom*)         ; => (NORTH SOUTH)
#+end_src

This is the same =setf= expansion as =(setf (car ...))= from
Chapter 7: =setf= knows how to assign to any accessor.

=room-p= tells you whether a value is a room:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (room-p *bedroom*) (room-p 'bedroom))   ; => (T NIL)
#+end_src

**** Printing and comparing

=defstruct= has options. The two most useful go right after the name:

#+begin_src lisp
(defstruct (room (:print-function print-room))
  name
  description
  exits)
#+end_src

By default a structure prints unreadably, like =#S(ROOM :NAME
BEDROOM :DESCRIPTION ...)=, which is fine. A =:print-function= makes
it print your way:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun print-room (room stream depth)
  (declare (ignore depth))
  (format stream "<room ~(~A~)>" (room-name room)))

(print *bedroom*)   ; => <room bedroom>
#+end_src

The print function receives the value, the stream to print on, and
the current print depth, which you can usually ignore — hence the
=declare= line, which tells the compiler the third parameter is
deliberately unused. The body calls =format= with a stream as
destination — the third destination from Chapter 12, put to work.

Because =print-room= mentions =room-name=, and =room-name= is only
created by the =defstruct=, SBCL warns "undefined function ROOM-NAME"
when it compiles =print-room= before the structure exists. The
warning is harmless: =print-room= is only *called* when a room is
printed, which happens after =defstruct= has run. If you find the
warning noisy, put the =defstruct= first and the =print-room= second —
then re-run the =defstruct= with =:print-function= to install the
printer.

The =:type= option is a bigger change; skip it. But =:named= and the
comparison options matter less than a simpler rule: to compare two
structures field by field, use =equalp=, which (Chapter 10) compares
structures by their contents:

#+begin_src lisp
(equalp (make-room :name 'a) (make-room :name 'a))   ; => T
(eq (make-room :name 'a) (make-room :name 'a))       ; => NIL
#+end_src

=eq= asks whether they are the same object, and each =make-room=
call builds a new one.

**** Default values

A field can have a default, given as a slot option:

#+begin_src lisp
(defstruct (item (:print-function print-item))
  (name 'thing)
  (weight 1))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(item-name (make-item))   ; => THING
#+end_src

**** Why structures instead of lists

The rooms of the game are alists of fields, or lists reached with
=third= and =caddr=. Structures replace both with names:

| List version            | Structure version          |
|-------------------------+----------------------------|
| =(third room)=          | =(room-description room)=  |
| Comments to remember the order | The field names say it |
| One wrong =cadr= and data is misread | The accessor checks the type |

When data has a fixed shape that you'll never change at runtime, a
structure is the natural home for it.

*** Hash tables

A hash table stores key-value pairs and finds any pair in roughly one
step, by computing where the key lives. The alist walk of =assoc= is
linear: twice the pairs, twice the work. A hash table isn't.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *caps* (make-hash-table))

(setf (gethash 'one *caps*) 1)
(setf (gethash 'two *caps*) 2)

(gethash 'two *caps*)        ; => 2, and T
#+end_src

=make-hash-table= makes one, =gethash= reads and (with =setf=)
writes. Reading a missing key returns =nil=, but the REPL shows two
values:

#+begin_example
NIL, T      ← the value, and "was it found?"
NIL, NIL
#+end_example

=gethash= returns /two/ values: the value, and a flag saying whether
the key was there. That flag matters when =nil= is a legitimate
stored value:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf (gethash 'nothing *caps*) nil)

(multiple-value-list (gethash 'nothing *caps*))  ; => (NIL T)
(multiple-value-list (gethash 'absent *caps*))   ; => (NIL NIL)
#+end_src

=multiple-value-list= catches all the values a form returns, as a
list. Multiple values are the subject of the next section.

=gethash= also takes a default, returned when the key is absent:

#+begin_src lisp
(gethash 'absent *caps* 0)   ; => 0
#+end_src

**** Iterating: =maphash= and =loop=

=maphash= calls a function of two arguments, key and value, on every
entry. The order is unspecified:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(maphash (lambda (k v) (format t "~A -> ~D~%" k v)) *caps*)
#+end_src

=loop= can walk a hash table too:

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for k being the hash-keys of *caps* collect k)
(loop for v being the hash-values of *caps* sum v)
#+end_src

**** Removing: =remhash= and =clrhash=

#+begin_src lisp
(remhash 'one *caps*)   ; => T, it was there
(clrhash *caps*)        ; empties the whole table
#+end_src

**** Keys and tests

=make-hash-table= takes a =:test= argument. The default is =eq=,
which suits symbol keys — the game's room and item names. For string
keys you must ask for =equal=, or the same string spelled twice
counts as two different keys:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *ages* (make-hash-table :test #'equal))

(setf (gethash "ada" *ages*) 36)

(gethash "ada" *ages*)   ; => 36, T
#+end_src

Only =eq=, =eql=, =equal= and =equalp= are valid tests. Numbers as
keys need =(make-hash-table :test #'eql)=; =eq= on numbers is unsafe
(Chapter 10).

**** Hash table vs alist

|                                | Alist     | Hash table |
|--------------------------------+-----------+------------|
| Lookup                         | Walk, slow on big lists | One step |
| Insert at the front            | =push=, fast | =setf gethash=, fast |
| Keep an order                  | Yes       | No, order is unspecified |
| Readable in your source        | Yes, quoted | No, built at runtime |
| A key with two values (history)| Yes       | No, one value per key |

Use an alist for small tables you want to read in your code, a hash
table for lookups that grow, and switch when an alist starts to feel
slow. The game's tables stay alists: they are small, and being
visible in the source has been worth more than speed.

*** Arrays

An array stores elements by index. =make-array= makes one:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *a* (make-array 3 :initial-element 0))
#+end_src

Read and write with =aref=, whose indexes count from zero like
=nth='s:

#+begin_src lisp
(aref *a* 0)          ; => 0
(setf (aref *a* 1) 42)
*a*                   ; => #(0 42 0)
#+end_src

=#(...)= is how an array prints. Like =#'= and =#\=, the =#= signals
"not an ordinary list".

=length= works on arrays too. =svref= is a faster =aref= for simple
arrays such as this one, and =vector= builds one from elements:

#+begin_src lisp
(vector 'a 'b 'c)     ; => #(A B C)
#+end_src

A one-dimensional array is called a /vector/. Strings are vectors of
characters — which is why =(length "abc")= and =(aref "abc" 1)= work:

#+begin_src lisp
(aref "abc" 1)        ; => #\b
#+end_src

Arrays have a fixed size. An array of 3 stays 3; to grow, make a
bigger one and copy, or use =adjustable-array-p= territory, which
this guide leaves for later reading. Lists and vectors cover
different needs:

|                              | List      | Vector    |
|------------------------------+-----------+-----------|
| Grow at the front            | =push=, fast | No |
| Reach element =n=            | Walk, =nth= | One step, =aref= |
| Fixed or growing             | Grows     | Fixed     |
| Printed as                   | =(...)=   | =#(...)=  |

Reach =push= for collections that grow; =aref= for collections you
index by number.

*** Multiple values

=gethash= returned two values. Any function can: =values= returns
several:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun divide (a b)
  "Return the quotient and remainder of A divided by B."
  (values (floor a b) (mod a b)))

(divide 17 5)        ; REPL shows: 3, 2
#+end_src

In ordinary code, a form with multiple values behaves as its first
value. =(list (divide 17 5))= gives =(3)=. To catch the rest,
=multiple-value-bind= binds them to names:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (q r) (divide 17 5)
  (list q r))          ; => (3 2)
#+end_src

=floor= itself returns two values — the quotient and the remainder —
which is why =divide= above works without naming =mod= twice. Other
functions that return extra values: =gethash=, =floor=, =truncate=
and =round=.

Common Lisp's =values= is a quiet feature: you can ignore it
everywhere and catch it when you need it. Java needed a whole class
or an out-parameter to return two things; here it's a =values= form
and one binding form.

*** Common mistakes

**** Forgetting the =make-= name

=defstruct room= creates =make-room=, not =make-<room>= or
=new-room=. The constructor is always =make-= plus the structure
name.

**** =:test= on the wrong hash table

#+begin_example
(defparameter *t* (make-hash-table))
(setf (gethash "a" *t*) 1)
(gethash "a" *t*)     ; => NIL, NIL
#+end_example

The default test is =eq=, and two ="a"= strings are different
objects. Declare =:test #'equal= when the keys are strings.

**** Treating a hash table like an alist

=assoc= doesn't work on hash tables: =(assoc 'one *caps*)= fails,
because a hash table isn't a list. Its walk is =maphash= or the
=loop for ... being= form.

**** =aref= on a list, =nth= on an array

#+begin_example
(aref '(a b c) 1)   ; error: The value (A B C) is not of type VECTOR
(nth 1 (vector 'a 'b 'c))   ; error, a vector is not a list
#+end_example

Each shape has its own accessor.

**** Expecting hash-table order

=maphash= and the =loop= walk may present entries in any order. If
you need an order, collect the keys and sort them.

**** Ignoring the second value of =gethash=

=(gethash key table)= returning =nil= means either "the value is
=nil=" or "the key is absent". When =nil= can be stored, bind both
values with =multiple-value-bind=.

*** Project step: rooms as structures

The rooms of the game become structures. This is the biggest single
change to the project so far, and it pays off immediately: every
function that reached into a room with =second= or =third= now reads
by name.

First the structure, with a print function so rooms read well:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun print-room (room stream depth)
  (declare (ignore depth))
  (format stream "<room ~(~A~)>" (room-name room)))

(defstruct (room (:print-function print-room))
  name
  description
  exits)
#+end_src

If you defined =room= in an earlier experiment, redefine it now with
this definition, so the accessors match. SBCL warns "undefined function
ROOM-NAME" while compiling =print-room=, because the accessor doesn't
exist yet. The warning is harmless: =print-room= is only *called*
when a room is printed, which is after =defstruct= has run.

Now the five rooms, with the same topology as before, built with
=make-room=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *rooms*
  (list
   (make-room :name 'bedroom
              :description "A small bedroom with an unmade bed."
              :exits '(hallway))
   (make-room :name 'hallway
              :description "A narrow hallway with creaky floorboards."
              :exits '(bedroom kitchen))
   (make-room :name 'kitchen
              :description "A warm kitchen that smells of bread."
              :exits '(hallway garden cellar))
   (make-room :name 'garden
              :description "An overgrown garden."
              :exits '(kitchen))
   (make-room :name 'cellar
              :description "A damp cellar. Something drips in the dark."
              :exits '(kitchen))))
#+end_src

The old =*rooms*= held lists; this holds structures. Every function
that reads a room keeps working, because they all go through
=room-name=, =room-description= and =room-exits= — and those are now
the structure's accessors, generated by =defstruct=, replacing the
Chapter 5 functions of the same names.

=find-room= needs one change: =assoc= walked an alist, and =*rooms*=
is now a plain list of structures. Chapter 10's =find= with =:key=
fits exactly:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun find-room (name)
  "Return the room whose name is NAME, or NIL if there is none."
  (find name *rooms* :key #'room-name))
#+end_src

=go-to=, =look= and the rest call =find-room=, so they need nothing.
Check the whole chain:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (find-room 'kitchen)
      (room-exits (find-room 'kitchen))
      (setf *current-room* (find-room 'hallway))
      (go-to 'kitchen)
      (look))
#+end_src

Expected: the kitchen structure (printed as =<room kitchen>=), the
list of exits, then the go-to message and look description for the
kitchen.

One new ability that structures make natural: a room can hold more
than the old shape allowed. Redefine the structure with a =locked=
field defaulting to =nil=, then set it on the garden:

#+begin_src lisp
(defstruct (room (:print-function print-room))
  name
  description
  exits
  (locked nil))

(setf (room-locked (find-room 'garden)) t)

(room-locked (find-room 'garden))   ; => T
#+end_src

SBCL warns about redefining the structure; that's expected. Every
existing room still works — the new field defaults to =nil= for the
four unlocked rooms, and =setf= on the accessor sets it for the
garden. Chapter 6's hard-coded locked-garden logic can now ask the
room itself; the exercises do that.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 13.1

Define =defstruct book= with fields =title=, =author= and
=(year nil)=. Create one with =make-book=, read each field, set
=year= with =setf=, and show that =book-p= accepts your book and
rejects a symbol.

**** Exercise 13.2

Predict the result of each line, then check:

#+begin_example
(aref (vector 10 20 30) 2)
(length (vector 'a 'b))
(aref "lisp" 0)
(multiple-value-list (floor 17 5))
(multiple-value-list (gethash 'x (make-hash-table)))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 13.3

Build a hash table =*prices*= with =:test #'equal=, keyed by strings,
holding three items and prices. Then write =total=, which takes a
list of item names and adds up their prices with =maphash= — or, more
simply, with =reduce= and =gethash=.

**** Exercise 13.4

Write =hash-keys=, which returns the keys of a hash table as a list,
using =loop=. Then sort them, since hash-table order is unspecified.

**** Exercise 13.5

Each line below is wrong. Say what's wrong, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(assoc 'one *caps*)

(aref '(a b c) 1)

(defparameter *t* (make-hash-table))
(setf (gethash "a" *t*) 1)
(gethash "a" *t*)

(make-room 'kitchen "desc" '(west))   ; wrong call style
#+end_example

**** Exercise 13.6

Write =oldest=, which takes a hash table mapping name symbols to
ages and returns the name of the oldest person, using =loop for key
being the hash-keys=, with =maximize= on the values — or a =let=
that keeps the best so far.

**** Exercise 13.7

Predict =T= or =NIL=, then check:

#+begin_example
(equalp (make-room :name 'a :exits '(n)) (make-room :name 'a :exits '(n)))
(eq (make-room :name 'a) (make-room :name 'a))
(room-p (make-room))
(eql 1.0 1)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 13.8

Write =invert=, which takes a hash table and returns a new one with
keys and values swapped. Assume values are unique and symbols.

**** Exercise 13.9 (project)

Change =go-to= so that it checks =room-locked=: when the destination
has =room-locked= true, reply ="The garden is locked."= and stay
where you are. The hard-coded =eq= test on the garden's name goes
away.

**** Exercise 13.10 (project)

Write =locked-rooms=, which returns the names of all locked rooms,
using =remove-if-not= over =*rooms*=.

**** Exercise 13.11 (project)

Give rooms an =items= field, defaulting to =nil=, and write
=take-struct= and =drop-struct=, which move item symbols between the
current room's =items= field and a list =*carrying*=. Compare the
feel of this with the alist version: which functions became simpler,
and which became longer? Answer in one paragraph of prose.

** Chapter 14: Macros

You've used macros all along without the name: =when=, =unless=,
=cond=, =and=, =or=, =setf=, =incf=, =push=, =defun=, =defparameter=,
=loop=, =dotimes= are all macros. Now you learn what they are and how
to write them. This is Lisp's most famous feature: the code you write
is data, and programs can write programs.

*** Functions cannot do everything

The =if= from Chapter 6 evaluates only one of its two branches:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(if t (print 'yes) (print 'no))
#+end_src

Only =YES= prints. Now try to write =my-if= as a function:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun my-if (condition then-branch else-branch)
  (cond (condition then-branch)
    (t else-branch)))
#+end_src

Call it:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(my-if t (print 'yes) (print 'no))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
YES
NO
YES
#+end_example

Both branches print. Lisp evaluated all three arguments /before/
=my-if= ran, because that's how functions work: arguments are
evaluated first. No function can receive the two branches unrun.

=if= and =when= get around this because they are /macros/: they
receive their arguments as raw code and decide themselves which parts
to evaluate. That's the whole idea. A macro is a function from code
to code, run at compile time, whose result is the code that actually
executes.

*** The macro's point of view

A macro call hands the macro /forms/, not values. Write a macro with
=defmacro=, whose shape matches =defun=, and you can see the raw
forms by having the macro return them as data:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro last-form (form)
  form)

(macroexpand-1 '(last-form (+ 1 2)))
#+end_src

=macroexpand-1= asks a macro what it would produce, without running
the result:

#+begin_example
(+ 1 2)
#+end_example

A macro that /does/ something to its forms, building a new form:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro twice (form)
  (list 'progn form form))

(macroexpand-1 '(twice (princ "hi")))
;; => (PROGN (PRINC "hi") (PRINC "hi"))
#+end_src

=twice= receives =(princ "hi")=, builds =(progn (princ "hi")
(princ "hi"))=, and that's the code that runs. The body used ordinary
list functions — =list=, quoting — because the arguments are lists,
the data from Part II.

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(twice (princ "hi"))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
hihi
#+end_example

*** Backquote: templates for code

Building lists by hand gets clumsy fast. The /backquote/ character
== quotes a form the way ='= does, but inside it, a comma =,= unquotes
— inserts the value of an expression:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter name 'ada)

`(hello ,name)
;; => (HELLO ADA)
#+end_src

Everything stays as written, except =,name=, which is replaced by
its value. With a plain quote there are no holes at all — the whole
form is data, so =name= stays the symbol:

#+begin_src lisp
'(hello name) ; => (HELLO NAME)
#+end_src

A comma outside a backquote is not syntax at all: the reader stops
with "Comma not inside a backquote". That error is worth
recognising, because it usually means a missing backquote rather
than a stray comma.

Inside a macro, backquote is how you write the expansion as a
template, with commas marking the holes:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro twice (form)
  `(progn ,form ,form))

(macroexpand-1 '(twice (princ "hi")))
;; => (PROGN (PRINC "hi") (PRINC "hi"))
#+end_src

Same expansion, but the code reads as the code it produces.

=,@= (comma-at) splices: it inserts a /list's elements/ rather than
the list itself. It's =append= where =,= is =cons=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter extra '(c d))

`(a ,extra e)   ; => (A (C D) E)
`(a ,@extra e)  ; => (A C D E)
#+end_src

Now macros that earn their keep. =when= is close to:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro my-when (test &body body)
`(if ,test (progn ,@body) nil))
#+end_src

=&body= is =&rest= for macro bodies: same behaviour, a signal to
readers and to editors that the forms are a body of code. Check it:

#+begin_src lisp
(macroexpand-1 '(my-when (plusp 5) (princ "big") (princ "!")))
;; => (IF (PLUSP 5) (PROGN (PRINC "big") (PRINC "!")) NIL)
#+end_src

And =my-unless=, the same shape with the branches swapped:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro my-unless (test &body body)
`(if ,test nil (progn ,@body)))
#+end_src

*** A macro the functions couldn't do: =while=

Here is a control structure Common Lisp doesn't have built in — a
=while= loop that repeats until its test goes false. No function
could write it, because the body must run many times, and a function
receives its body once:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro while (test &body body)
  `(loop while ,test
    do ,@body))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(defparameter count 0)
(while (< count 3)
(print count)
(incf count))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
0
1
2
#+end_example

The expansion is ordinary =loop= code:

#+begin_src lisp
(macroexpand-1 '(while (< n 10) (princ n)))
;; => (LOOP WHILE (< N 10) DO (PRINC N))
#+end_src

You defined syntax. Users of =while= can't tell it from something
built into the language. Java gained lambdas and streams over the
years, but no Java program can add new syntax to Java; the language
grows only when its committee grows it. In Lisp, =defmacro= is part
of the language for every programmer, and much of the standard
library is itself macros written this way.

*** Expansion, not evaluation

=macroexpand-1= shows one step. =macroexpand= expands repeatedly,
until the result is no longer a macro call — useful because your
macros often expand into other macros:

#+begin_src lisp
(macroexpand '(my-when t (my-unless nil (princ "ok"))))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
(IF T (PROGN (IF NIL NIL (PROGN (PRINC "ok")))) NIL)
#+end_example

=when= and =unless= expanded into =if= and =progn= here, because
=my-when= and =my-unless= are macros too.

Two facts to keep straight:

    The macro's parameters are /forms/, so inside the macro you call
    list functions (Chapter 7) and, in the template, =,= and =,@=.

Watch the difference in this macro, which counts how many forms are
in its body:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro body-size (&body body)
  `(quote ,body))

(body-size (princ 1) (princ 2) (princ 3))
;; => ((PRINC 1) (PRINC 2) (PRINC 3))
#+end_src

The three =princ= calls never ran. The macro saw them as data and
returned them as data.

*** Variables in macros: the hygiene trap

Macros that introduce variable names can collide with the names the
caller uses. Here is a broken macro:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro swap-bad (a b)
  `(let ((temp ,a))
    (setf ,a ,b)
    (setf ,b temp)))
#+end_src

It looks right:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *x* 1)
(defparameter *y* 2)
(swap-bad *x* *y*)
(list *x* *y*) ; => (2 1)
#+end_src

But pass a variable literally named =temp=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter temp 'kept)
(swap-bad temp *x*)
temp ; => 2, not KEPT
#+end_src

The expansion was =(let ((temp temp)) ...)=, and the macro's =temp=
shadowed the caller's. The caller's variable was clobbered — a bug
that only shows up for certain callers, the worst kind.

The fix: use =gensym=, which generates a symbol guaranteed never to
have been used before:

#+begin_src lisp
(gensym) ; => #:G1234
(gensym) ; => #:G1235
#+end_src

The =#:= shows an uninterned symbol: a symbol no package owns
(packages are Chapter 16). Build the expansion so the temporary name
comes from =gensym= at expansion time:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro swap (a b)
  (let ((temp (gensym)))
    `(let ((,temp ,a))
      (setf ,a ,b)
      (setf ,b ,temp))))
#+end_src

Notice the two levels: =let= (plain) runs at expansion time and makes
=temp= name the fresh symbol; backquote with =,temp= inserts that
symbol into the template.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter temp 'kept)
(defparameter *x* 1)
(swap temp *x*)
(list temp *x*) ; => (1 KEPT)
#+end_src

Now no caller can break it. The rule: /any variable a macro
introduces in code that runs more than once should be a gensym/.
Code that runs once, such as a =let= binding evaluated a single time
in the expansion, is safe with an ordinary name.

The same trap exists for arguments evaluated repeatedly:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro square-bad (n)
  `(* ,n ,n))

(square-bad (progn (princ "evaluated ") 5))
;; prints "evaluated " twice, then 25
#+end_src

=,n= appears twice, so the form is evaluated twice. The fix binds
the form once:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro square (n)
  (let ((v (gensym)))
    `(let ((,v ,n))
      (* ,v ,v))))
#+end_src

*** Destructuring in defmacro

A macro's parameter list can take lists apart, like =loop='s
patterns in Chapter 11:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro do-pair ((a b) list &body body)
  `(dolist (pair ,list)
    (let ((,a (first pair))
        (,b (second pair)))
      ,@body)))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(do-pair (name price) '((key 5) (lamp 10))
(format t "~A costs ~A~%" name price))
#+end_src

#+begin_example
KEY costs 5
LAMP costs 10
#+end_example

The parameter =(a b)= destructures the first argument of every call,
and =,@body= splices the caller's body into the =let=.

*** When to write a macro

Macros are powerful, and power wants restraint. The test: /could a
function do it?/ If yes, write a function — functions compose better,
can be passed with =#'=, and debug more simply. Write a macro when
you need one of these:

    Control flow: evaluate some arguments and not others (=when=,
    =and=), or evaluate them more than once (=while=).

    New binding forms: introduce variables the body can use
    (=do-pair='s =a= and =b=, =with-*= style macros).

    Access to forms: you need the code itself, as =setf= needs the
    place and =trace= needs the function name.

Everything else is a function, and Chapter 9's higher-order functions
cover much of what another language would reach for macros to do.

*** Common mistakes

**** Calling the macro where you wanted the expansion

=macroexpand= takes a /form/, quoted:

#+begin_example
(macroexpand (my-when t 1))
;; expands the literal call, then macroexpand receives 1
#+end_example

Write =(macroexpand '(my-when t 1))=, with a quote.

**** Forgetting the comma

#+begin_example
(defmacro double-expr (x)
`(+ x x)) ; wrong: uses the symbol X

(macroexpand-1 '(double-expr (+ 2 3)))
;; => (+ X X) — the argument vanished
#+end_example

The comma in =(+ ,x ,x)= is what inserts the caller's form.

**** The reverse: comma outside backquote

#+begin_example
(defmacro bad (x)
(+ ,x 1))
;; reader error: comma is not inside a backquote
#+end_example

A comma only means "insert" inside a backquoted form.

**** Splicing a non-list

#+begin_example
(defmacro wrap (form)
`(progn ,@form))

(wrap (princ "hi"))
;; works: (princ "hi") is a form

(wrap 5)
;; error: 5 is not a list, and ,@ needs one
#+end_example

=,@= requires a list. When the argument may be one form or many,
decide in the macro body.

**** A gensym that evaluates at the wrong time

#+begin_example
(defmacro swap-broken (a b)
`(let ((,(gensym) ,a))
(setf ,a ,b)
(setf ,b ,(gensym))))
#+end_example

Two different =gensym= calls run at /two different moments/, and the
second =gensym= here runs when the expanded code runs, not when the
macro expands. Compute the fresh symbol once, in a plain =let=
around the backquote, and bind it in the expansion.

**** Writing a macro where a function fits

If your macro just computes a value from its arguments' values,
delete it and write a function. =defmacro= is for syntax, and the
project step shows the difference on the game.

*** Project step: an undo macro and a command with syntax

The game gets two things from this chapter: an /undo/ command, and a
macro that cleans up a repeated pattern.

Undo needs a history of item alists. Each move pushes a copy of
=item-locations= onto a stack; undo pops and restores:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter history nil)

(defun snapshot ()
  "Record the current item locations for undo."
  (push (copy-alist item-locations) history))

(defun undo ()
  "Restore the item locations from before the last move."
  (if (null history)
    "Nothing to undo."
    (progn
      (setf item-locations (pop history))
      "Undone.")))
#+end_src

=copy-alist= copies both levels of the alist, so the snapshot
survives later changes to the live one (Chapter 7's sharing
structure). Then every mutating command saves state first. The
pattern "snapshot, then act" repeats in =take=, =drop= and =go-to= —
a macro's job:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro with-undo (&body body)
  `(progn
    (snapshot)
    ,@body))
#+end_src

Redefine =take= to use it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun take (item)
  "Pick up ITEM if it is in the current room."
  (cond ((eq (item-location item) 'player)
      (format nil "You already have the ~(~A~)." item))
    ((eq (item-location item) (room-name current-room))
      (with-undo
        (setf item-locations
          (set-location item-locations item 'player)))
      (format nil "You take the ~(~A~)." item))
    (t
      "You don't see that here.")))
#+end_src

Here =with-undo= is a macro only because of where the =snapshot=
sits: it must run /before/ the caller's forms, interleaved with them,
and the caller's forms must splice into the same =progn=. A function
=(with-undo (setf ...))= couldn't do that — it would receive the
=setf= already evaluated.

Now the command table. Add undo to it (if Exercise 14.7 has you add
=drop= too, do that after):

#+begin_src lisp
(push '(undo . undo) commands)
#+end_src

Check the undo. This resets everything, so run it whole:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter item-locations
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf history nil)
(setf current-room (find-room 'kitchen))

(list (run-command '(take key))
  (run-command '(undo))
  (items-in item-locations 'kitchen)
  (run-command '(take key)))
#+end_src

Expected: the take message, ="Undone."=, =(KEY)= back in the kitchen
alist, and the take message again. The undo restored not just the
items but the /possibility/: the second =take= succeeds because the
first one's effect was erased.

Second, a macro that reads better at the call site. Several game
replies depend on the player carrying something:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun open-chest ()
  "Open the chest if the player carries the key."
  (if (member 'key (items-here 'player))
    "The chest opens."
    "The chest is locked."))
#+end_src

One of these is fine. When a dozen checks pile up, a macro names the
concept:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro if-carrying (item then &body else)
  `(if (member ,item (items-here 'player))
    ,then
    (progn ,@else)))

(defun open-chest ()
  "Open the chest if the player carries the key."
  (if-carrying 'key
    "The chest opens."
    "The chest is locked."))
#+end_src

Note =then= is a single form (no =&body=), and =else= is a body. The
indentation convention — the lone then on its own line, the else
body indented under it — is what makes the call read like =if=.

Both macros expand into code you could have written by hand; check
with =macroexpand= to see it. That's the healthy shape of most macros
in real programs: small, readable expansions of repetitive patterns,
not clever machinery.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 14.1

Predict each expansion, then check with =macroexpand-1=:

#+begin_example
(twice (princ 'x))
(my-when (> 2 1) (princ 'a))
(swap p q)
(square (+ 1 2))
#+end_example

Then run each form on suitable variables and say what happens and why.

**** Exercise 14.2

Write =my-or2=, a two-argument =or=: evaluate the first; if it is
true, return it without evaluating the second; otherwise evaluate and
return the second. No function can do this — say why in one comment
line in your test code.

**** Exercise 14.3

Write =repeat=, a macro =(repeat n &body body)= that runs the body =n=
times, where =n= is a form evaluated once. =(repeat (+ 1 2) (princ
'x))= prints =XXX= — and prints ="evaluated"= only once if =n= is
=(progn (princ "evaluated ") 3)=.

**** Exercise 14.4

Each macro below is broken. Say what breaks, for which caller, then
fix it with =gensym= or a different shape.

#+begin_example
(defmacro first-of (lst)
`(let ((head ,lst))
(first head)))

(defmacro inc-twice (x)
`(incf ,x ,x))

(defmacro unless-bad (test &body body)
`(if ,test nil ,body))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 14.5

Write =benchmark=, a macro =(benchmark form)= that returns the
result of =form=, but prints a line before and after, so the
expansion is =(progn (princ "start") <form-run> (princ "end") ...)=
with the result threaded through. Then explain in one comment why
=benchmark= must be a macro and not a function. (Real timing needs
=get-internal-run-time=, which is beyond this chapter.)

**** Exercise 14.6

Write =for-each=, a macro =(for-each (x lst) &body body)= that works
like =dolist=. Check its expansion with =macroexpand-1=, then use it
to print the rooms' names.

**** Exercise 14.7 (project)

Add =(drop . drop)= and =(undo . undo)= to =commands=, and write a
=rewind= command that undoes repeatedly while =history= is
non-empty, returning how many steps it rolled back. Use =loop with=.

**** Exercise 14.8 (project)

Write =if-room=, a macro =(if-room name then &body else)= that
evaluates =then= with the variable =it= bound to the room found by
=find-room=, or the =else= body when no room has that name:

#+begin_example
(if-room kitchen
(format nil "~A is found" (room-name it))
"No such room.")
#+end_example

This is Lisp's =if-let= pattern, and it shows a macro introducing a
binding — impossible for a function.

** Chapter 15: Conditions and the Debugger

You've landed in the debugger often enough by now: a wrong argument,
a missing function, =sort= on a quoted list. In most languages an
error means the program dies and you read a stack trace. Common Lisp
does something different: an error is not the end of the program but
an event other code can respond to, and the debugger is the place
where /you/ are the handler. This chapter covers both sides.

*** Signalling: =error=, =warn=, =signal=

=error= announces that something has gone wrong and unwinds the
stack looking for a handler:

#+begin_src lisp
(error "That room doesn't exist.")
;; Enters the debugger
#+end_src

The string is a /format control/ — =error= calls =format= on it
(Chapter 12), so directives work, and following arguments feed them:

#+begin_src lisp
(error "Can't take ~A: not here." 'key)
#+end_src

Never build the message with =concatenate= before calling =error=.
Pass the parts and let =format= do it. That keeps the message
machine-readable as well as human-readable, which handlers below
depend on.

=warn= is =error='s softer cousin. It prints a warning and continues;
the stack is not unwound and the program carries on:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(warn "Low on fuel: ~D litres left." 2)
(princ 'still-running)
#+end_src

#+begin_example
WARNING: Low on fuel: 2 litres left.
STILL-RUNNING
#+end_example

=signal= is the quietest: it announces an event and, if nobody
handles it, returns =nil= instead of entering the debugger. It is for
events that may or may not be anyone's business. Most code needs only
=error= and =warn=.

*** Conditions are objects

A /condition/ is the object that represents the problem. Every error
signal creates one. The one you've met most often is =simple-error=,
and its relatives cover the common cases: =type-error= when a value
has the wrong type, =unbound-variable= when a name has no value,
=undefined-function= when a function isn't defined, =division-by-zero=.

You can see one directly with =make-condition=, and read its fields
without ever signalling it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *c* (make-condition 'type-error
                                  :datum "abc"
                                  :expected-type 'integer))

(type-error-datum *c*)            ; => "abc"
(type-error-expected-type *c*)    ; => INTEGER

(princ *c*)    ; prints something like:
               ; The value "abc" is not of type INTEGER.
#+end_src

Each condition type has accessors for its facts: =type-error= knows
the bad value and the type wanted. Handlers read these instead of
parsing text — the reason =error= takes a format control rather than
a finished string.

New condition types are defined with =define-condition=, which reads
like =defstruct= with a report instead of a print function:

#+begin_src lisp
(define-condition room-not-found (error)
  ((name :initarg :name :reader room-not-found-name))
  (:report (lambda (condition stream)
             (format stream "No room called ~(~A~)."
                     (room-not-found-name condition)))))

(make-condition 'room-not-found :name 'attic)
#+end_src

The parent class, =(error)=, decides how the condition is treated:
inheriting from =error= makes it an error, inheriting from =warning=
makes it a warning, inheriting from =condition= alone makes it a
plain event. =:report= builds the message the debugger shows.
=:initarg= and =:reader= are Chapter 13's slots again — conditions
are CLOS objects under the hood, and Chapter 17 makes that explicit.

Signalling your own type:

#+begin_src lisp
(error 'room-not-found :name 'attic)
#+end_src

*** Handling: =handler-case=

=handler-case= runs a body with a rescue plan for each condition
type. Its shape:

#+begin_src lisp
(handler-case
    body-form
  (condition-type (c) recovery-form...))
#+end_src

If =body-form= runs cleanly, its value is the result. If it signals a
matching condition, the stack unwinds to this point and the recovery
forms run instead:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun parse-count (text)
  "Return the integer in TEXT, or NIL."
  (handler-case (parse-integer text)
    (error (c) (declare (ignore c)) nil)))

(list (parse-count "42")
      (parse-count "  7 ")
      (parse-count "nope"))
;; => (42 7 NIL)
#+end_src

=parse-integer= is new here. It converts the digits in a string to a
number, and /signals an error/ when there are none — it refuses to
guess. It also returns two values: the number and where it stopped
reading (Chapter 13's multiple values).

Compare this with other languages' try/catch. The differences that
matter:

- The /condition type/ decides which clause runs, and the clause can
  be as specific as your own condition type. Catching =error= catches
  everything, which is rarely what you want — write specific clauses.
- The stack has already unwound when the handler runs: the recovery
  code runs where =handler-case= sits, not where the error happened.
  That limits what a handler can do — it can substitute a value, but
  it cannot "fix things and continue from the middle". The next
  section is the feature that fills that gap.
- The condition object =c= is bound for the recovery forms, so the
  handler can ask questions: =(type-error-datum c)= and so on.

Several clauses, most specific first:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun try-integer-divide (a b)
  "Return A divided by B, or a message when that can't work."
  (handler-case (/ a b)
    (division-by-zero () "Can't divide by zero.")
    (type-error (c) (format nil "Bad input: ~A." (type-error-datum c)))))

(list (try-integer-divide 10 2)
      (try-integer-divide 10 0)
      (try-integer-divide 10 'x))
#+end_src

Expected: =(5 "Can't divide by zero." "Bad input: X.")=

The clause with no parameter, =(division-by-zero () ...)=, is
normal: the condition object is there if you want it, not if you
don't. The clause form =(:no-error ...)= runs when the body
/succeeded=, which is handy for functions that report either a
result or a failure:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun safe-parse-or-log (text)
  (handler-case (parse-integer text)
    (error (c) (format t "~&parse failed: ~A~%" c) nil)
    (:no-error (n) (format t "~&parsed ~D~%" n) n)))
#+end_src

=:no-error='s variable list gets the body's /values/ — a
multiple-value safe exit.

*** Restarts: repair instead of rescue

=handler-case= substitutes an answer. A /restart/ offers the caller
a /choice of ways to continue/, and this is what makes Lisp's system
different. When the debugger opens, what you've been seeing is a
menu of restarts — =ABORT=, and usually others such as
=USE-VALUE= or =STORE-VALUE=.

You can define your own with =restart-case=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun read-temperature (text)
  "Return the temperature in TEXT, or 20 when unreadable."
  (restart-case (parse-integer text)
    (use-default ()
      :report "Use the default temperature of 20."
      20)))
#+end_src

If =parse-integer= signals, no handler catches it here — but the
=restart-case= has registered a way out. In the debugger, the
=USE-DEFAULT= restart appears in the menu, and choosing it returns
=20= — execution resumes /as if= that value had been produced.

Invoking restarts programmatically is =invoke-restart=, but the
everyday tool is =find-restart= with =invoke-restart= inside a
handler, or better: associating them. The full pattern — a low-level
function that signals, a middle layer that offers restarts, a
high-level function that chooses — is the /condition system idiom/:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun move-towards (name)
  "Change room, signalling ROOM-NOT-FOUND on a bad name."
  (let ((room (find-room name)))
    (when (null room)
      (error 'room-not-found :name name))
    (setf *current-room* room)
    (look)))

(defun move-safely (name)
  "Like MOVE-TOWARDS, but offers recovery choices."
  (restart-case (move-towards name)
    (substitute-room (other)
      :report (lambda (stream)
                (format stream "Go to ~(~A~) instead." other))
      (move-towards other))
    (stay-put ()
      :report "Stay where you are."
      "You stay put.")))
#+end_src

That =:report= lambda is overcomplicated, and shows why restarts
belong with simpler arguments: the report format string is the
main readable part, and the lambda hides it. A cleaner version:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun move-safely (name)
  "Like MOVE-TOWARDS, but offers recovery choices."
  (restart-case (move-towards name)
    (stay-put ()
      :report "Stay where you are."
      "You stay put.")))

(defun move-or-stay (name)
  "Move to NAME, or stay when no such room exists."
  (handler-case (move-towards name)
    (room-not-found ()
      (invoke-restart 'stay-put))))
#+end_src

Read the layers: =move-towards= knows nothing about recovery; it
only announces facts. =move-safely= offers choices. =move-or-stay=
decides. Any layer can be swapped without touching the others — the
same separation Java's exception hierarchy aims at, but with the
choice of /continuing/ built in, not just unwinding.

=(invoke-restart 'stay-put)= only works when a restart of that name
is in scope — inside the =restart-case=. From outside, the debugger
menu offers the restarts interactively; that's the same mechanism
with you at the controls.

*** Interactive restarts: the debugger as REPL

When an unhandled error opens the debugger in SLIME or SLY
(Appendix C), you see:

- The condition and its message.
- The menu of restarts, numbered.
- The stack of calls, each inspectable.

The restarts you'll actually use:

| Restart       | Meaning                                       |
|---------------+-----------------------------------------------|
| =ABORT=       | Give up this attempt, back to the REPL (=q=)   |
| =USE-VALUE v= | Pretend the failing expression produced =v=    |
| =STORE-VALUE v= | Like =USE-VALUE=, for places rather than values |
| =RETRY=       | Run the failing form again                     |

=USE-VALUE= is the one worth knowing by name. When a lookup fails
deep in a long computation, you don't restart the computation: you
supply the missing value and continue. In your own code, you can
offer exactly that restart with =restart-case= and
=:report "Use a replacement value."=, and the debugger menu grows a
choice tailored to the problem.

*** =ignore-errors= and =unwind-protect=

Two small forms cover the everyday cases.

=ignore-errors= returns =nil= when its body signals any error, and
the body's value otherwise. It's =handler-case= with one catch-all
clause and no access to the condition:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (ignore-errors (/ 1 0))
      (ignore-errors (/ 10 2)))
;; => (NIL 5)
#+end_src

It's quick and blunt. For anything where you'd like to know /what/
went wrong, use =handler-case=.

=unwind-protect= isn't about errors at all, but about cleanup: its
second form runs no matter how the first form exits — normally, by
error, by a jump:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *log* nil)

(defun careful-change (f)
  (unwind-protect
      (progn (push 'start *log*)
             (funcall f)
             (push 'end *log*))
    (push 'cleanup *log*)))

(careful-change (lambda () (error "boom")))
(reverse *log*)   ; => (START CLEANUP)
#+end_src

The =end= never ran, because =error= unwound the stack, but =cleanup=
did. Any resource you open — a file (Chapter 16), a socket, a
temporary state — should be closed in an =unwind-protect=. The
=with-open-file= macro you'll meet next chapter has exactly that
inside it.

*** Common mistakes

**** Catching =error= when you mean something specific

=(handler-case body (error (c) ...))= recovers from /everything/,
including the typos and bugs you wanted to see. Catch the narrowest
condition type that covers the case.

**** Building the message before signalling

#+begin_example
(error (concatenate 'string "Can't take " (symbol-name item) "."))
#+end_example

Pass a format control: =(error "Can't take ~A." item)=. The
condition keeps the pieces, and handlers can read them.

**** Invoking a restart that isn't in scope

=(invoke-restart 'stay-put)= outside the =restart-case= that defined
it gives "The restart STAY-PUT is not active". The restart lives
while the =restart-case= form runs; the handler must sit inside that
dynamic extent.

**** Confusing =warn= with =error=

=warn= continues; =error= unwinds. If your program keeps running
after something you thought was fatal, you signalled a warning.

**** Forgetting that the stack has unwound

A =handler-case= handler cannot "go back" into the failed code. It
chooses a value from where it stands. Continuation-from-the-middle is
the restarts' job, offered at the failing site.

*** Project step: failsafe commands

The command runner from Chapter 9 crashes the session on a malformed
command: =(look kitchen)= calls =look= with an argument, =(take)=
calls =take= with none, =(go)= likewise. This step makes =run-command=
answer politely instead, and adds a =count-letters= command built on
the condition system.

First, a safe runner. The current =run-command= (Chapter 9) looks up
the command and applies it; the failure happens inside =apply=.
Separate that into two pieces so the error-catching layer has a
smaller target:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun call-command (command)
  "Call the function that handles COMMAND."
  (let ((entry (assoc (first command) *commands*)))
    (if (null entry)
        "I don't know how to do that."
        (apply (cdr entry) (rest command)))))

(defun run-command (command)
  "Run COMMAND, a list such as (TAKE KEY), and return the reply."
  (restart-case (handler-case (call-command command)
                  (program-error ()
                    "Wrong number of arguments for that command."))
    (retry-with (fixed)
      :report "Run a corrected command instead."
      (run-command fixed))))
#+end_src

=program-error= covers wrong argument counts. The =retry-with=
restart isn't used by the handler — it's there for you: when this
fires in the debugger, you can supply a corrected command and the
session continues. That's a debugging affordance, built in four
lines.

Check the graceful replies:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (run-command '(look kitchen))
      (run-command '(take))
      (run-command '(dance)))
#+end_src

Expected: the wrong-arguments message twice, and the unknown-command
message. No debugger.

Second, a command that counts the letters in an item's name and
demonstrates =restart-case= with a real recovery:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun letter-count (item)
  "Return the number of letters in ITEM's name, or offer zero."
  (restart-case
      (let ((entry (assoc item *item-locations*)))
        (when (null entry)
          (error 'room-not-found :name item))
        (format nil "~(~A~) has ~D letters."
                item (length (symbol-name (first entry)))))
    (guess-zero ()
      :report "Treat it as zero letters."
      (format nil "~(~A~) has 0 letters." item))))

(defun count-letters (command)
  "Handle (COUNT-LETTERS ITEM)."
  (handler-case (letter-count (second command))
    (room-not-found ()
      (invoke-restart 'guess-zero))))
#+end_src

Read the flow carefully, because this is the condition system's
round trip: =letter-count= runs inside =count-letters='s
=handler-case= body. When the item isn't in the alist,
=letter-count= signals =room-not-found= from inside its own
=restart-case=. The handler in =count-letters= runs — /and it sits
inside/ the =restart-case='s dynamic extent, because =letter-count=
is the function that established the =restart-case=. So the handler
can invoke the =guess-zero= restart, which resumes =letter-count=
from the =restart-case= — not from =count-letters='s point, but
from =letter-count='s. That's the whole condition system in one
example: the signalling function offers choices, the caller decides,
and neither needs to know the other's code.

Add the command:

#+begin_src lisp
(push '(count-letters . count-letters) *commands*)

(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((key . kitchen) (letter . bedroom)))
(list (run-command '(count-letters key))
      (run-command '(count-letters sword)))
#+end_src

Expected: ="key has 3 letters."= and ="sword has 0 letters."= — the
second through the restart path.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 15.1

Predict what each listing returns or prints, then check:

#+begin_example
(ignore-errors (+ 1 2))
