=defpackage= form:

#+begin_src lisp
(eval-when (:compile-toplevel :load-toplevel :execute)
  (unless (find-package :cave-of-noise)
    (make-package :cave-of-noise :use '(:cl))))
#+end_src

The =unless= guard means "safe in every situation": a second
compile in the same image doesn't warn about redefining a package
that's already there, and a first compile in a fresh image
creates it. That's the shape =eval-when= is for — code that
must run /in all three modes/ and is smart enough to be
idempotent.

*** Compilation units and compiler output

=compile-file= prints warnings — undefined functions, wrong
argument counts, style notes — /after/ processing the file,
grouped as one "compilation unit". =with-compilation-unit=
gives the same grouping to interactive work:

#+begin_src lisp
(with-compilation-unit ()
  (compile-file "packages.lisp")
  (compile-file "game.lisp"))
#+end_src

All warnings collect and print at the end — which matters when
warnings cascade (an early undefined function causes a dozen
follow-on ones). SBCL's warnings deserve respect: they catch,
at compile time, errors other implementations let through to run
time. A Lisp file that compiles silently is a real achievement,
and the redefinition-loop discipline of Chapter 22 is what gets
you there.

*** Common mistakes

**** Trusting the REPL to predict file compilation

Code that works line by line at the REPL can fail to compile as
a file: macro definitions after use, =defconstant= values
computed by functions not yet available, top-level code assuming
a package another file makes. The fix is always the same thought:
what does this form need /at compile time/, and is it there?

**** eval-when as a magic spell

Sprinkling =(:compile-toplevel :load-toplevel :execute)= on
everything "to be safe" wraps ordinary functions in pointless
complexity and can cause double evaluation surprises. The
situation list should be a /decision/: which times does this
form need, and why?

**** Load-time values baked as constants

#+begin_example
(defconstant *save-dir* (merge-pathnames "saves/"))
#+end_example

=defconstant= demands the same value every evaluation; a
pathname with a defaulted host may differ across images or
platforms, redefining a constant signals a warning, and the
right form is =defparameter= (recomputable, rebindable) or a
plain =defun= computing it on demand. Constants are for
mathematical truths, not for environment facts.

**** Forgetting *load-pathname* vs *default-pathname-defaults*

Code that opens ="saves/game.sav"= relative to the /current
directory/ works in your session and breaks when loaded from
elsewhere. Anchor file access on =*load-truename*=, not on the
process's accidental working directory.

*** Project step: a make-like loader

The game's files (Chapter 16) load in order by hand. This step
adds a tiny build step: compile the system's files and load the
compiled versions, with compile-time warnings grouped and the
load anchored on true pathnames.

The list of files matches Chapter 16's =.asd= for the game. If
you haven't split the game into files yet, do Chapter 16's
Exercise 16.7 first — the =:shop= package exercise is enough to
give you two files to work with.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *game-files*
  '("packages.lisp" "classes.lisp" "commands.lisp" "main.lisp"))

(defun build-game ()
  "Compile each file, then load the compiled result."
  (with-compilation-unit ()
    (dolist (src *game-files*)
      (let ((fasl (compile-file src)))
        (when fasl (load fasl))))))
#+end_src

=compile-file= returns the fasl pathname, or =nil= if
compilation failed hard — the =when= guards loading a failure.
Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(build-game)
(play)
#+end_src

And one =eval-when= in the real code: =packages.lisp= now
declares itself compile-time-available:

#+begin_src lisp
;; packages.lisp — now safe to compile as part of the batch
(eval-when (:compile-toplevel :load-toplevel :execute)
  (unless (find-package :cave-of-noise)
    (make-package :cave-of-noise :use '(:cl))))
#+end_src

Without it, compiling =classes.lisp= (whose =:cave-of-noise=
symbols need the package at compile time) warns "package
CAVE-OF-NOISE not found" the first time; with it, =build-game=
runs silent. That one form is the chapter in miniature: a
compile-time need, named and satisfied.

One refinement worth having before you move on: =build-game=
recompiles everything every time. A load-if-stale check makes it
a real make:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun fasl-for (src)
  (compile-file-pathname src))

(defun stale-p (src)
  (let ((fasl (fasl-for src)))
    (or (null (probe-file fasl))
        (> (file-write-date src)
           (file-write-date fasl)))))

(defun load-compile-if-stale ()
  (with-compilation-unit ()
    (dolist (src *game-files*)
      (when (stale-p src)
        (compile-file src))
      (load (fasl-for src)))))
#+end_src

This is a toy dependency check, and it lies in interesting ways
— see Exercise 20.6 — but for a single-file project with no
intra-file dependency tangles, it's the right amount of
machinery. When the system grows real dependencies, ASDF is
where the lying stops.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 20.1

For each form, say at which times it is evaluated when its file
is (a) compiled, (b) loaded, (c) evaluated at the REPL:

#+begin_example
(defparameter *x* (compute-x))
(defconstant +limit+ 10)
(defun f () (compute-y))
(eval-when (:compile-toplevel) (format t "hi"))
(eval-when (:load-toplevel :execute) (defun g () 1))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 20.2

A file contains, in this order: =(defmacro twice (x) (list ,x ,x))=,
then =(defun f (a) (twice a))=. Explain what can go wrong when
this file is compiled fresh, in a fresh image, and fix it two
ways: once with =eval-when=, once with file ordering and =:serial t=.

**** Exercise 20.3

Each snippet is wrong. Say what happens, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(defconstant *home* (user-homedir-pathname))

(defun self-path ()
  *default-pathname-defaults*)

(eval-when (:execute)
  (defmacro always (val) val))
(always 5)   ; used later in the same compiled file
#+end_example

**** Exercise 20.4

Write =compile-and-report=, which takes a list of filenames,
compiles each inside one =with-compilation-unit=, and returns an
alist mapping each source name to its fasl pathname — with
=nil= for files that failed to compile. What does grouping buy
that per-file compilation doesn't?

**** Exercise 20.5

Write =file-form-count=, a macro that expands to a constant
integer: the number of top-level forms in the file being
compiled — computed at compile time by reading the file with
=read= (Chapter 16 and Chapter 18). Why must the counting happen
at compile time, and what does the macro expand to at load time?

**** Exercise 20.6 (project)

=load-compile-if-stale= from the project step recompiles
=classes.lisp= when its source changes but not =main.lisp=,
which uses it. Add a naive "recompile everything after the
first stale file" rule: once any file in =*game-files*= is
stale, recompile all subsequent files too. Then say in a
comment what the rule still gets wrong that ASDF gets right.

** Chapter 21: Type Declarations and Performance

Every value you've handled has had a type all along — integers,
strings, lists, your own =room= structures — and Lisp has checked
what it needed to, when it needed to. What Lisp /doesn't/ do is
demand types up front. Declarations exist anyway, and this
chapter explains what they're for: telling the compiler what it
may assume, asking it to check, and — rarely, at measured spots —
asking it to go fast. This is also the promised full treatment of
=declare=, which you've met twice before (=(declare (ignore ...))=
in the reader chapter, =(declare (type ...))= glancingly).

*** Types are a property of values

In Java, a variable is declared with a type and can only hold
that type. In Lisp, types belong to /values/: the number 5 is of
type =(integer 0 10)= and =fixnum= and =number= and =t= all at
once, and one variable can hold values of different types at
different moments. =typep= asks, =type-of= reports:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (typep 5 'integer)          ; => T
      (typep 5 '(integer 0 10))   ; => T — a bounded type!
      (typep 5 'float)            ; => NIL
      (typep #'car 'function)     ; => T
      (type-of 5)                 ; => (INTEGER 0 4611686018427387903) on SBCL
      (type-of "hi"))             ; => (SIMPLE-ARRAY CHARACTER (2))
#+end_src

Type specifiers range from simple names (=integer=, =string=) to
parameterised ones — =(integer 0 10)=, =(string 10)=, =(vector
fixnum)=, =(member :on :off)=, =(or null room)=. =defstruct=
(Chapter 13) and =defclass= (Chapter 24) each define new type
specifiers too: =(typep r 'room)= has worked since you defined
rooms.

=subtypep= asks the type-system questions:

#+begin_src lisp
(subtypep 'fixnum 'integer)     ; => T, T
(subtypep 'integer 'fixnum)     ; => NIL, T — surely not a fixnum
(subtypep '(integer 0 10) 'fixnum)  ; => T, T
#+end_src

Two values, because some type questions have no settled answer;
the second value =T= means "the answer is certain". Compare
=typep= (a /value/ against a type) with =subtypep= (a /type/
against a type).

*** declare: the local promise

A =declare= is a /declaration/: information for the compiler,
placed at the head of a form that introduces bindings. It is not
an executable form, and it changes no values:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun add-fixnums (a b)
  (declare (type fixnum a b))
  (+ a b))
#+end_src

The declaration tells the compiler: =a= and =b= will always be
fixnums — assume it. What the compiler /does/ with the assumption
depends on the safety setting, coming below: at high safety it
verifies and signals a =type-error= on violation; at high speed
it trusts and optimises.

The declarations you'll actually use, collected:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun example (x list fn)
  (declare (type fixnum x)            ; x is a fixnum
           (type list list)           ; redundant but legal
           (function fn)              ; fn is a function
           (ignore fn)                ; deliberately unused
           (ignorable list)           ; may be unused — fine, don't warn
           (dynamic-extent list))     ; list won't outlive this call
  (+ x (length list)))
#+end_src

- =ignore= — you used this in the Reader chapter; the parameter
  must be unused, and using it is an error.
- =ignorable= — softer: unused is fine, used is fine, never warn.
- =dynamic-extent= — a promise the object dies with the call,
  letting implementations stack-allocate it; advanced, and
  mostly useful in hot loops.
- =type= — the star of this chapter.
- =special=, =ftype=, =inline=, =notinline=, =optimize= — met
  here or below.

=the= is the inline cousin — a type assertion on an expression,
no binding needed:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun sum-vector (v)
  (let ((total 0))
    (declare (type fixnum total))
    (dotimes (i (the fixnum (length v)) total)
      (incf total (the fixnum (aref v i))))))
#+end_src

And =check-type= is the runtime twin — interactive checking:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *frequency* 440)

(defun set-frequency (hz)
  (check-type hz (integer 20 20000) "a frequency in hertz")
  (setf *frequency* hz))
#+end_src

Violations signal a =type-error= with a restart that lets you fix
the variable and continue — the debugger as a live type checker.

*** declaim and proclaim: global declarations

=declaim=, at the top level, declares globally — for the rest of
the file or session:

#+begin_src lisp
(declaim (type fixnum *moves*))
(declaim (inline square))
(defun square (x) (* x x))
#+end_src

=proclaim= is the functional twin (an ordinary call, so usually
used dynamically); =declaim= is the compile-time-aware one and
the one you'll see in source. Global type declarations on
earmuffed variables are a common and cheap performance aid: the
compiler knows =*moves*= is a fixnum everywhere below.

=declaim (inline ...)= is also how inlining is requested —
a hint the compiler may take. Its opposite, =notinline=, forbids
inlining and is how you /stop/ the compiler from optimising away
a redefinition (the redefinition-loop discipline of Chapter 22
applies: after changing an inlined function, recompile its
callers or the change won't appear).

*** optimize: the quality dial

The =optimize= declaration sets compiler priorities:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun hot-inner-loop (v)
  (declare (optimize (speed 3) (safety 1) (debug 1))
           (type (simple-array double-float (*)) v))
  (loop for x across v sum (the double-float x)))
#+end_src

Five qualities, each 0–3: =speed=, =safety=, =debug=,
=compilation-speed=, =space=. Higher =safety= means more checking
(type errors, array bounds); higher =speed= means trusting
declarations and specialising; higher =debug= preserves the
stack and variable names for the debugger (Chapter 22's
backtraces are this setting in action). The qualities /trade
off/: =speed 3, safety 0= means the compiler will happily
generate code that corrupts memory on a type violation — a
wrong declaration at safety 0 is undefined behaviour, the one
place Lisp resembles C.

The SBCL defaults are =safety 1, debug 1, speed 1, space 1= —
a balanced profile that checks a good deal. The discipline this
chapter teaches, stated once: /profile first, then declare,
then measure/. SBCL ships =sb-profile=; the =time= macro from
Chapter 22 is the first instrument:

#+begin_src lisp
(time (sum-vector (make-array 1000000
                :element-type 'twice-float
                :initial-element 1.0d0)))
#+end_src

*** Why declarations matter to the compiler

The payoff, concretely. Without declarations, =(+ a b)= compiles
to a generic addition: fixnums? bignums? ratios? floats? Check
and dispatch at run time. With =(declare (type fixnum a b))=, it
compiles to one machine add — plus an overflow check the
compiler can often prove unnecessary. Arrays specialise the
same way: =(aref v i)= on a =(simple-array double-float (*))=
is a pointer read; on a plain =vector= it's a type dispatch per
access. This is why numeric Lisp code that's fast declares
heavily and uses typed arrays:

#+begin_src lisp
(make-array 4 :element-type 'twice-float :initial-element 0.0d0)
#+end_src

The =:element-type= from Chapter 13's arrays chapter now has its
performance meaning: an array that /holds only doubles/ is one
the compiler can treat as raw memory.

*** Common mistakes

**** Declaring wrong, at low safety

A wrong =type= declaration is not a checked annotation — under
=(safety 0)= it's a lie the compiler believes, and the result is
corruption, not an error. Keep =safety 1= or higher wherever
declarations are new or unproven; SBCL's compiler notes will
flag contradictions at compile time if you listen to them.

**** Declaring everything

Sprinkling =the= and =type= through ordinary code adds noise
with no measurable gain. Declaring pays in tight numeric loops
and hot data structures — profile, find the 5%, declare there.

**** Reading optimization qualities as switches

=(speed 3)= is a /weighting/, not a mode; implementations differ
in what they do with it. And =(safety 0)= is not "less checking,
more speed" — it's "no checking, possibly wrong answers". In
application code, =(speed 3) (safety 1) (debug 1)= is the
responsible hot-spot setting.

**** Forgetting declare placement

=declare= must come /immediately after/ the binding-introducing
form's header — right after the variable list in =defun=, right
after the bindings in =let=, right after =loop='s keywords. A
declaration after an executable form is an error, and the
compiler will tell you.

*** Project step: a fast item count

The game is pure symbol work — declarations buy it nothing, and
this step says so honestly while adding one genuinely typed
component: a heat map of room visits, as a fixnum vector indexed
by room number.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *room-list* '(bedroom hallway kitchen garden cellar))

(defun room-index (name)
  (or (position name *room-list*)
      (error "No room ~(~A~) registered." name)))

(defparameter *visits*
  (make-array (length *room-list*)
              :element-type 'fixnum
              :initial-element 0))

(defun note-visit (name)
  "Count a visit to room NAME."
  (let ((i (room-index name)))
    (declare (type (integer 0 #.array-dimension-limit) i))
    (incf (aref *visits* i))))

(defun visit-report ()
  "Rooms by visit count, busiest first."
  (sort (loop for r in *room-list*
              for n across *visits*
              collect (list n r))
        #'> :key #'first))
#+end_src

=#.array-dimension-limit= — the sharp-dot read-time
evaluation from the Reader chapter, giving the biggest legal
array index as a type bound. Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *visits* (make-array 5 :element-type 'fixnum
                             :initial-element 0))
(note-visit 'kitchen)
(note-visit 'kitchen)
(note-visit 'cellar)
(visit-report)
;; => ((2 KITCHEN) (1 CELLAR) (0 BEDROOM) (0 HALLWAY) (0 GARDEN))
#+end_src

And one measured experiment, the chapter's real habit:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-bits-undeclared (n)
  (loop for i below n count (evenp i)))

(defun count-bits-declared (n)
  (declare (type fixnum n) (optimize (speed 3) (safety 1)))
  (loop for i of-type fixnum below n count (evenp i)))

(time (count-bits-undeclared 10000000))
(time (count-bits-declared 10000000))
#+end_src

On a typical machine the declared version runs roughly twice as
fast — =loop='s =of-type= is a declaration shorthand. Your
numbers will differ; that's the point of =time=.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 21.1

Predict the results, then check:

#+begin_example
(typep 3 '(integer 0 2))
(typep 3 '(or null integer))
(typep :red '(member :red :green :blue))
(typep "abc" '(simple-array character (3)))
(typep '(1 2) '(cons integer (cons integer null)))
(subtypep 'null 'list)
(subtypep 'null 'symbol)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 21.2

Each snippet is wrong. Say what happens, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(defun f (x)
  (format t "x is ~A~%" x)
  (declare (type fixnum x))
  (* x 2))

(declaim (type string *player-name*))
(defparameter *player-name* 'anon)

(defun g (v)
  (declare (optimize (speed 3) (safety 0))
           (type fixnum v))
  (1+ v))
(g "oops")
#+end_example

**** Exercise 21.3

Write =safe-elt=, which takes a sequence and an index, uses
=check-type= to confirm the index is a non-negative fixnum
before touching the sequence, and returns the element. Then
write =fast-elt=, identical but with =(safety 0)= and a
fixnum declaration instead of =check-type=. In a comment:
which one belongs in the game, and why is the answer "safe-elt
almost everywhere"?

**** Exercise 21.4

Using =subtypep=, write =type-chain-p=, which takes a value and
a list of types and answers whether the value's =type-of= is a
subtype of /every/ listed type. What does
=(type-chain-p 5 '(integer fixnum number))= return, and what
does that tell you about how types overlap?

**** Exercise 21.5

Time three versions of summing a million-element
double-float array: (a) plain =loop= with =sum=, no
declarations; (b) with =(the double-float ...)= on the element
and a declared accumulator; (c) with the array declared
=(simple-array double-float (*))= as well and =(speed 3)=.
Report your three timings in a comment and say which
declaration bought the most.

**** Exercise 21.6 (project)

Add =(declaim (type fixnum *moves*))= globally with =declaim=,
make =*moves*= start as a fixnum, and run the game's =play=
reliably with a fresh save. Then write =drift-check=, which
walks every earmuffed game variable, =check-type=s it against a
small table of expected types (alist: variable name to type
specifier), and returns the list of variables that fail. What
kind of bug does =drift-check= catch that a wrong =declaim=
would turn into corruption at =(safety 0)=?

The chapter covers:

    The REPL as a workbench (=*, ==, =*=, =+=, =++=, =+++=, describe, documentation)

That's honest about what the game's data supports at this point.

** Chapter 22: The REPL and Debugging Workflow

You've met the debugger as a thing that interrupts you. This
chapter turns it around: the debugger, the inspector, the
backtrace and the REPL are one connected toolkit, and using it
well is what "thinking in Lisp" feels like in practice — you
don't write programs so much as /grow/ them, with the program
running the whole time.

*** The REPL as a workbench

The REPL is not a toy console for trying one-liners. It is a
window onto a running program, and the running program is where
your work happens. The habits that make it a workbench:

- Keep the /image/ alive. Unlike the write-compile-run cycle, a
  Lisp session holds state across edits: the game you're
  debugging stays loaded while you fix a function and try it
  again.
- Define things where you'll call them. A =defparameter= typed
  at the REPL is test data; the same line in a file is part of
  the program. Both are legitimate — but know which one you're
  doing.
- =*=, =**= and =***= are the last three /values/ the REPL
  printed; =+=, =++= and =+++= are the last three /forms/ you
  typed:

  #+begin_src lisp
  (+ 1 2)      ; => 3
  (* * 10)     ; => 30   — ten times the last value
  (+ * 1)      ; => 31   — three times the last form's value
  #+end_src

  Useful in the moment, dangerous as a habit: the next
  expression you evaluate silently changes what =*= means. For
  anything worth keeping, =defparameter= it with a real name.

- =describe= prints what Lisp knows about anything — a
  function's signature and documentation, a symbol's package,
  a variable's value:

  #+begin_src lisp :results output
  (describe #'run-command)
  (describe *current-room*)
  #+end_src

- =documentation= reads just the docstring:
  =(documentation 'take 'function)=. This is why Chapter 5
  insisted on docstrings: they are queryable at runtime, by
  you and by tools.

*** The inspector

=inspect= opens an interactive view of any object — in SLIME
or SLY, an Emacs buffer with one line per slot:

#+begin_src lisp
(inspect *current-room*)
#+end_src

You see the structure, each slot with its name and value, and
for the game's =room= structures that's exactly the view you
want: name, description, exits, locked, items. From there you
can descend: follow a slot into another object, evaluate
expressions /in the context of the object/, and walk back out.
The inspector is how you answer "what does this room actually
contain right now?" without writing printing code — Chapter
13's =:print-function= makes the top-level view readable, and
the inspector makes it navigable.

Use it on: an alist whose shape looks wrong, a condition
object caught in a handler, a structure whose slots hold
surprises. In SLIME/SLY, =C-c C-v C-o= (or =M-x
slime-inspect=) inspects the expression at point.

*** =break=, =error=, and deliberate stops

=error= says something is wrong. =break= says /stop here so I
can look around/ — it enters the debugger without signalling an
error, and =continue= from there resumes as if nothing
happened:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun suspicious-part (lst)
  (when (and lst (null (rest lst)))
    (break "Suspicious: one-element list ~S" lst))
  (reverse lst))
#+end_src

The difference is intent and recovery: an error unwinds looking
for handlers; a break just opens the debugger with a
=CONTINUE= restart that puts everything back. Use =break= while
hunting, then delete it — or turn it into =warn= if the
condition is worth reporting in the finished program.

=trace= (Chapter 8) shows calls and returns; =step= walks a
form one subform at a time, in SLIME/SLY showing each step in
Emacs with keys to descend, skip or evaluate. Stepping is slow
but exact — when you can't see /why= a function returns what it
returns, =step= shows the path; when you can't see /what= its
inputs were, =trace= shows the calls.

*** Reading a backtrace

When an unhandled error opens the debugger, below the condition
and the restarts is the stack: one frame per call in progress,
most recent first. Reading it is a skill with three steps:

1. /Read the condition first./ The message usually names the
   trouble: "The value KEY is not of type LIST" already tells
   you a list was expected and a symbol arrived. The stack only
   says where.
2. /Find the first frame that's yours./ Below it are
   implementation internals; above it, your callers. The
   boundary — the topmost frame whose name you recognise — is
   where your code and the failure meet.
3. /Look at the frame's arguments./ In SLIME/SLY, pressing
   =v= on a frame jumps to its source line; pressing =t= or
   clicking expands its arguments. "The function got the wrong
   thing" plus "here is the wrong thing" plus "here is the
   line" is most bugs solved.

A worked example. =(length 5)= gives:

#+begin_example
The value 5 is not of type LIST
   [Condition of type TYPE-ERROR]
...
0: (LENGTH 5)
1: (SOME-SUMMARY-FUNCTION 'KEY ...)
2: (RUN-COMMAND (INVENTORY))
#+end_example

Read from the top: =length= was given =5=. Frame 1 shows your
function passed a symbol where a list was expected — the bug is
in frame 1's own call to =length=. Frame 2 tells you who was
running. You didn't need to guess any of it; the stack said it.

*** Editing running code: redefinition

Chapter 16 showed the file-to-session loop; here's its
debugging form. When the game misbehaves:

1. Try the failing command at the REPL with the same arguments
   the game gave it. The debugger you get is /your/ debugger,
   with the arguments in front of you.
2. Find and fix the definition. =C-c C-c= it — the running game
   now calls the new version; nothing restarts.
3. Try the command again. If it works, done — and the fix is
   already in the file.

This loop is seconds long, and its speed changes how you debug:
instead of reasoning your way to the answer before touching
anything, you can test hypotheses directly — change one thing,
push it live, try again. Keep the loop honest by changing /one
thing/ at a time; a session where five definitions were
redefined since the last success is a session you can't reason
about.

Two safety nets:

  #+begin_src lisp
  (time (loop for i below 1000000 sum i))
  #+end_src

  Reports bytes allocated and milliseconds spent. Reach for
  =time= when something feels slow, not before.

*** When the image is sick

Rarely, the session itself is the problem: a variable redefined
to something mad, a package switched and forgotten, a method
that breaks printing (so even =print= errors — a nasty loop).
The ladder out, mildest first:

- =(in-package :cl-user)= fixes forgotten package switches.
- =makunbound= (Chapter 9) unbinds a variable you've broken.
- =fmakunbound= does it for functions; redefine afterwards.
- =(untrace)= removes all traces; =trace= with no arguments
  lists them.
- As a last resort, quit and =asdf:load-system= again —
  Chapter 16's design exists so this costs seconds, not work.
  Losing REPL scratch state is the price; that's why real
  findings get written to files.

*** Common mistakes

**** Debugging in the file instead of the session

Reading code and reasoning is slow and often wrong. The session
is right there: call the function with the failing input,
=inspect= the value, =trace= the calls. Reasoning confirms;
the REPL discovers.

**** Trusting =*= across other evaluations

=*= means "last value printed", and anything evaluated since —
a test, a stray Enter — changed it. Bind results to names the
moment they matter.

**** Fixing five things between tests

The redefinition loop's power comes from its granularity. One
change, one try. If you must change several, write them all in
the file first, then =C-c C-c= them one at a time in dependency
order — classes, then methods, then callers.

**** Leaving =break= and =trace= in finished code

=break= in shipped code stops the program for the user, not for
you. Trace output pollutes logs. Remove them when the hunt is
over, or convert =break= to =warn= where a report is genuinely
useful.

**** Reading the stack bottom-up

The bottom frame is the program's start; the top is where it
broke. Reading bottom-up is reading history before the crime.
Read the condition, then the top frames, downward only until
you find yourself.

*** Project step: a debug command for the game

The game gets a =debug= command that opens the inspector on the
current state — because the toolkit should be reachable from
inside the game too, and because writing it exercises the
chapter's pieces:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun debug-state ()
  "Enter the inspector on the game state."
  (inspect *current-room*))

(push '(debug . debug-state) *commands*)
#+end_src

Check it: =(run-command '(debug))= opens the inspector buffer in
Emacs (or the text inspector at the REPL), showing the current
room's slots. Quitting the inspector returns to where you were
— the =inspect= call simply returns.

And one that exercises the condition system and the backtrace
together, a self-diagnosis command:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun doctor ()
  "Report on the game's internal consistency."
  (let ((problems nil))
    (dolist (pair *item-locations*)
      (unless (find-room (cdr pair))
        (push (format nil "~(~A~) is in unknown room ~(~A~)"
                      (car pair) (cdr pair))
              problems)))
    (unless (or (null *current-room*)
                (member *current-room* *rooms* :test #'eq))
      (push "Current room is not in *rooms*" problems))
    (if problems
        (format nil "~{~A~^; ~}." problems)
        "All clear.")))

(push '(doctor . doctor) *commands*)
#+end_src

Check both:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((key . kitchen) (sword . ballroom)))
(doctor)
#+end_src

Expected: a report naming =sword='s unknown room — one check
catching one kind of drift. The =*current-room*= check catches
the other kind: a room symbol the game's state still references
after the room was removed from =*rooms*=. Two predicates, two
failure modes, one sentence of output.

This is the pattern for all debugging aids: cheap to write
(it's just the predicates you already have), and they turn "the
game feels wrong" into a sentence.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 22.1

Predict, then check each in a fresh order to prove to yourself
how =*= works:

#+begin_example
(+ 2 3)
(* * 4)
(list * ** ***)
#+end_example

What are the three elements? Evaluate one harmless form (like
=nil=) between the second and third lines and re-run: what
changes?

**** Exercise 22.2

Use =describe= on =#'append= and on =*commands*=. From the output
only (cover your memory of the chapter): what arguments does
=append= take, and what kind of structure is =*commands*=?

**** Exercise 22.3

Evaluate =(inspect (make-condition 'type-error :datum 5
:expected-type 'list))= and report, from what you see: what slots
does a =type-error= have, and what are their values here? Then
write a =handler-case= that catches a =type-error= and returns a
string built from those two slots.

**** Exercise 22.4

Each of these debugging situations has a best tool. Name it —
=trace=, =step=, =break=, =inspect=, =apropos=, =time=, =describe=
— and say in one sentence why the others fit worse.

#+begin_example
a) A function returns the right shape but wrong contents.
b) You remember "a function about prices" but not its name.
c) The game runs, but a command takes visibly half a second.
d) An alist has the right keys but one value is NIL, and you
   want to see the whole thing at once.
e) A loop seems to never end, and you want to watch each
   subexpression evaluate.
f) A predicate is firing when it shouldn't, and you want the
   program to stop right there with the data in front of you.
#+end_example

**** Exercise 22.5

Write =safe-run=, which takes a function and returns either
=(list :ok value)= when it returns, or =(list :error message)=
when it signals — using =handler-case=, and using =princ-to-string=
on the condition for the message. Check it with =#'= and a
lambda that divides by zero.

**** Exercise 22.6 (project)

Add a =whereis= command: =(whereis key)= prints every room the
item has ever been seen in, using =*history*= (Chapter 14's
undo snapshots). What condition does it need to handle when the
item is unknown, and where should that handling go — in the
command function or in =run-command=? Defend your answer in a
comment.

**** Exercise 22.7 (project)

Break the game on purpose, three ways: redefine =item-names=
to return =nil=; rebind =*current-room*= to a room symbol not in
=*rooms*=; delete a room from =*rooms*= while items are in it.
For each, run =(doctor)= and =inventory=, note what breaks and
what merely misbehaves, then repair with the redefinition loop.
Write the three symptoms as comments — this is your personal
bug catalogue for this codebase.

** Chapter 23: Symbols and the Property List

You've been using symbols since Chapter 2 — ='kitchen=,
=:name=, =*moves*= — and Chapter 9 added the two-slots picture:
every symbol has a value slot and a function slot. That picture
was incomplete on purpose. A symbol is a small record with five
parts, and one of them — the property list — is a data structure
you haven't used yet. This chapter completes the picture, and
with it a fair amount of "why is Lisp like this" falls into
place.

*** The five parts of a symbol

Every symbol has:

- a *name* — the string it was read from: ="KITCHEN"=.
- a *package* — which package's symbol table holds it (Chapter
  16): the =CL-USER= package for =kitchen=, =KEYWORD= for
  =:name=.
- a *value slot* — what =*moves*= evaluates to.
- a *function slot* — what =#'look= names.
- a *property list* — a hidden list of key/value pairs, empty by
  default.

=symbol-name=, =symbol-package= and =symbol-value= read the
first three; =symbol-function= is the function slot accessor
from Chapter 9's discussion of =#'=. All are setf-able, which is
what =setf= has been doing all along:

#+begin_src lisp
(symbol-name 'kitchen)          ; => "KITCHEN"
(symbol-package 'car)           ; => #<PACKAGE "COMMON-LISP">
(symbol-package :look)          ; => #<PACKAGE "KEYWORD">
(symbol-value '*moves*)         ; => nil (if *moves* is undefined)
(symbol-function 'car)          ; the function object for CAR
#+end_src

The last line's oddity: =symbol-function= takes a /symbol/, so
=(symbol-function 'car)=, not =(symbol-function #'car)=.
Writing =#'car= there would evaluate the function object first,
and =symbol-function= wants the name, not the value. The five
parts make concrete what Chapter 9 said abstractly: one name,
several slots.

*** The property list

A symbol's plist is a list of alternating keys and values,
stored /on the symbol itself/:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf (get 'kitchen 'description) "Where the noise begins")
(get 'kitchen 'description)     ; => "Where the noise begins"
(get 'hall 'description)        ; => NIL — never set
(setf (get 'hall 'visited) t)
(remprop 'kitchen 'description) ; removes the pair, returns non-nil
#+end_src

=get= reads; =(setf get)= writes; =remprop= removes. The default
for a missing property is =nil=, changeable with a third
argument:

#+begin_src lisp
(get 'hall 'description 'unknown)  ; => UNKNOWN when absent
#+end_src

The keys are symbols — usually keywords, by convention — and the
plist is what =get= walks: a list like
=(description "..." visited t)=. You can see it directly:

#+begin_src lisp
(symbol-plist 'hall)  ; => (VISITED T) — with KITCHEN's removed above
#+end_src

=symbol-plist= exposes the raw list. You'll rarely use it
directly — =get=, =remprop= and =get-properties= are the
interface.

*** =get= vs =getf=

These two functions are confused often, and the difference is
worth stating precisely:

- =get= takes a /symbol/ and a property indicator, and looks up
  the property /on that symbol's plist/.
- =getf= takes an /arbitrary plist/ as its first argument, and
  a key as its second, and looks up the key in that plist.

=get= is for per-symbol attributes. =getf= is for plist-shaped
data anywhere — a keyword-argument plist, a configuration
structure, anything that happens to be an alternating
key/value list:

#+begin_src lisp
(getf '(:name "kex" :exits nil) :name)   ; => "kex"
(getf '(:speed 3 :depth 2) :depth)       ; => 2
#+end_src

The signature tells you which you want: =(get symbol indicator)=
when the symbol /is/ the thing; =(getf plist key)= when the plist
/is/ the thing.

*** Plists vs alists vs hash tables

You now hold all three dictionary shapes Lisp offers natively:
the alist (Chapter 7), the hash table (Chapter 13), and the
plist. The differences are about /where the pairs live/ and
what operations are natural:

| Structure | Pairs live on           | Natural operations        | Costs             |
|-----------+-------------------------+---------------------------+-------------------|
| plist     | the symbols themselves  | =get=, =remprop=          | linear scan per lookup |
| alist     | one list you own        | =assoc=, =acons=, =push=  | linear scan       |
| hash table| a table object you own  | =gethash=, =remhash=, =maphash= | O(1) average |

The real distinction: a plist /smears/ the association across
all symbols that carry the property — =(get name 'location)=
across every room symbol is the data, and no single object owns
it. An alist is /one list/ that can be passed, rebound, saved
and rebuilt. That's why the game used alists for
=*item-locations*= — state you save and restore — and why
plists fit /attributes that belong to named things/:

- Symbols as /names of things/ (rooms, commands, items): a
  plist is the thing's attributes, reachable by name alone.
- One shared table of /mappings/: alist or hash table.
- Data to /save, pass, or have several versions of/: never a
  plist — you can't snapshot a symbol's plist short of copying
  it pair by pair, and there's one plist per symbol, not one
  per context.

=get-properties= is the plist utility worth knowing beyond
=get=:

#+begin_src lisp
(get-properties '(description "#| ... |#" visited t) '(visited description))
;; => (VISITED T) — first property present, with its value
#+end_src

The distinction from =getf=: =get-properties= reports which
properties are /present/, and its first return value is the
suffix of the plist starting at the first match — different from
=getf='s value-only answer. Rare in application code; worth
recognising in libraries.

*** Plists and keyword arguments

=format= isn't the only place a plist-shaped value appears in
disguise. Many libraries document "accepts a plist of options";
modern code uses real =&key= parameters (Chapter 5), but the
plist style survives in older APIs:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun draw-window (title &rest options)
  (let ((width  (getf options :width 80))
        (height (getf options :height 24)))
    (list title width height)))
#+end_src

When you read =(:name "kitchen" :exits ...)= in old code, you're
seeing a plist-shaped option list — a flat list of alternating
keywords and values, handled with =getf=. Chapter 5's =&key=
parameters are the modern spelling of the same idea; the plist
is the data shape underneath.

*** gensym revisited

Chapter 14's gensym created "fresh symbols" — now you can say
what that meant precisely. A gensym's product is a symbol with a
generated name — ="G123"=, say — and no home package:
=symbol-package= returns =nil=, which is the tell that it is not
an interned symbol.

Its read syntax =#:g123= exists so the printer can print it
back; reading =#:g123= produces a /different/ uninterned symbol
with the same name — which is why uninterned symbols can't
round-trip (Chapter 16's print/read round trip for save
files needed interned names).

#+begin_src lisp
(symbol-package (gensym)) ; => NIL
(symbol-name (gensym)) ; => "G456" — no colon, just the name
#+end_src

*** Common mistakes

**** Plists as primary state

Putting game state on plists and then trying to =save-game=:
there's no object to hand to print — you'd have to walk every
symbol and collect properties first, and restored state is
spread across the package. State you save belongs in lists,
structures and hash tables you own; plists for per-symbol
attributes, if at all.

**** remprop on shared symbols

remprop and (setf get) mutate /the symbol/, which is shared
by everyone using that name. Removing description from
'kitchen removes it for the whole image — including other code
that set it. Property lists are global by nature; treat them as
such.

**** get on non-symbol keys

(get 5 'x) signals an error — get requires symbol keys,
because the plist lives /on symbols/. For string or number keys,
use an alist with :test, or a hash table with the right test,
or — nothing Lisp-standard, because plist keys are symbols by
design.

**** Believing type declarations protect plists

*** Project step: per-command metadata

The *commands* table (Chapter 9) maps command names to
functions. This step adds per-command metadata — help text and
usage — where the plist genuinely fits: attributes belonging to
named things, consulted by name alone.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun define-command (name fn help usage)
  "Register a command with its metadata for a help system."
  (push (cons name fn) commands)
  (setf (get name 'help) help)
  (setf (get name 'usage) usage))

(defun command-doc (name)
  "Help text and usage for NAME, or a fallback."
  (format nil "~(~A~): ~A~% usage: ~A"
    name
    (or (get name 'help) "No help available.")
    (or (get name 'usage) "unknown")))

(defun help-command ()
  "List commands with one-line help, alphabetically."
  (format t "~:{~&~(~A~)~12T~A~}~%"
    (sort (loop for pair in commands
        for name = (car pair)
        collect (list name (or (get name 'help) "")))
      #'string< :key #'first)))
#+end_src

The ~:{~} directive walks a list of lists (Chapter 12); the
sort with :key is Chapter 10's =sort= doing book-listing
duty. Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf commands nil) ; start clean, for the demo
(define-command 'look #'look "Describe your surroundings." "(look)")
(define-command 'take #'take "Pick something up." "(take ITEM)")
(define-command 'go #'go-to "Move in a direction." "(go NAME)")

(help-command)
;; go Move in a direction.
;; look Describe your surroundings.
;; take Pick something up.

(command-doc 'take)
;; => "take: Pick something up.
;; usage: (take ITEM)"
#+end_src

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 23.1

Predict, then check:

#+begin_example
(symbol-name 'kitchen)
(symbol-package :kitchen)
(symbol-name :kitchen)
(eq 'kitchen 'KITCHEN)
(eq (intern "KITCHEN") 'kitchen)
(symbol-package (gensym))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 23.2

Each snippet is wrong or surprising. Say what happens, then fix
it.

#+begin_example
(get "kitchen" 'description)

(setf (get 'kitchen 'exits) '(hall cellar))
(remprop 'kitchen 'exits)
;; ...later, elsewhere:
(get 'kitchen 'exits)

(let ((props (symbol-plist 'kitchen)))
(remprop 'kitchen 'description)
props)

(getf '(name "hall" name "cellar") :name)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 23.3

Write =plist-p=, which answers whether a list is a valid plist
(an even number of elements). Then write =plist->alist=, which
converts =(a 1 b 2)= to =((a . 1) (b . 2))=, and
=alist->plist= the other way. Which direction loses
information, and why can that be acceptable?

**** Exercise 23.4

Write =plist-keys=, which returns the distinct keys of a plist.
Then using =get-properties=, write =plist-subset-p=, which
answers whether a plist contains every key/value pair of
another plist.

**** Exercise 23.5

Write =swap=, a macro expanding to code that exchanges two
variables' values, using =gensym= for the temporary. Then
demonstrate — with =macroexpand-1= and a variable actually
named =tmp= — why the naive version (with a plain =let= on
=tmp=) is a bug.

**** Exercise 23.6 (project)

Add per-item flavour text as plist properties on the item
symbols — =(setf (get 'key 'flavour) ...)= — and write a
=describe-flavour= function that returns the flavour or a
default. Then answer in a comment: why is flavour text (never
saved, fixed at definition) a defensible plist use where item
/locations/ (Chapter 7) were not?

Flavour text is (a) an attribute of a named thing, consulted by
name alone — the plist's natural shape; (b) fixed at definition
time, never needs snapshotting, versioning, or save-game; (c) global
by nature — the flavour of the key is the same every session, which
is exactly the intent. Item *locations*, by contrast, are mutable
state with a lifetime: they change during play, get saved, restored,
and would be spread unownably across symbols on a plist — which is
why Chapter 7 gave them an alist and Chapter 23's common-mistakes
list forbade plists for them. The dividing line, in one sentence:
plists for constants about names, owned structures for state about
things.

** Chapter 24: Multiple Values

A handful of functions you've already met have been returning
/two/ things, and the code has been quietly catching only one:
=floor= returned a quotient and a remainder, =gethash= returned
a value and a found-flag, =parse-integer= returned a number and
where reading stopped, =read-from-string= returned an object and
a position, =get-properties= returned a key, a value and a tail.
Lisp has /multiple values/ — a function result can be several
objects at once — and this chapter explains the mechanism
properly, because it is not what you'd guess coming from other
languages, and the guessing causes real bugs.

*** Values are not lists

The instinct — return a list of two things — is wrong twice
over. A list must be allocated, deconstructed and (for =nil=
and false values) made ambiguous: a list of =(nil nil)= can't
distinguish "two nils" from "a list whose car is nil" without
care. Multiple values are none of that: several objects handed
over in one result, with no structure built and no ambiguity.
The rule that makes them work:

/In any context that expects one value, extra values vanish./

#+begin_src lisp
(floor 7 2)            ; two values: 3 and 1
(+ (floor 7 2) 10)     ; => 13 — + saw only the 3; the 1 vanished
(* 2 (floor 7 2))      ; => 6 — same
(list (floor 7 2))     ; => (3) — the remainder dropped
#+end_src

This is why ordinary code can call =floor= inside arithmetic
without dismantling anything: the primary value — the first
one — is what every ordinary context sees. The extra values
exist only for the forms that ask for them.

=values= produces multiple values:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun divide (a b)
  "Return the quotient and remainder of A divided by B."
  (values (floor a b) (mod a b)))

(divide 7 2)        ; REPL shows both: 3, 1
(+ (divide 7 2) 0)  ; => 3 — the mod vanished
#+end_src

The REPL, which wants to show you everything, prints all the
values, each on its own line. Other contexts see one.

The truncation isn't a bug in your code; it's the /contract/ of
"function call arguments are single-valued". =(list (floor 7 2))=
doesn't fail to catch =1=; it deliberately discards it, because
=list='s arguments are single-valued slots. Only the forms
designed for values catch them all, and the rest of this
chapter names those forms.

*** Receiving values

=multiple-value-bind= catches values into variables:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (q r)
    (floor 7 2)
  (list q r))          ; => (3 1)
#+end_src

The syntax mirrors =let=: a list of variables, a form, a body.
You don't have to name the values you don't want — bind only
the first:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (q)
    (floor 7 2)
  q)                   ; => 3
#+end_src

=multiple-value-list= collects /everything/ into a list — the
general-purpose net:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-list (floor 7 2))  ; => (3 1)
(multiple-value-list (+ 1 2))      ; => (3) — one value, one-element list
(multiple-value-list (values))     ; => NIL — zero values, empty list
(multiple-value-list (values nil)) ; => (NIL) — one nil value, one-element
#+end_src

That last pair is the distinction to keep straight. =(values)=
produces /zero/ values; =(values nil)= produces /one/ value,
which happens to be nil. Both look like =nil= in a single-value
context, but =multiple-value-list= tells them apart — nil
(empty) versus =(nil)= (one element). The exercise section
puts this to work; for now, notice that "no value" and "the
value nil" are different things, and the empty list is the
signal for "no value."

Chapter 13 used =multiple-value-list= for =gethash= without
explaining it; now it's explained — the found-flag was kept
because the function returns two values:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *visited* (make-hash-table))

(multiple-value-bind (value found-p)
    (gethash 'kitchen *visited*)
  (if found-p
      value
      "first visit"))
#+end_src

The found-flag is the single most useful extra value in
Common Lisp, because it resolves the ambiguity =gethash= would
otherwise have: a stored =nil= and an absent key both read as
=nil= without it.

There's also =nth-value=, which extracts a specific value
without binding the others:

#+begin_src lisp
(nth-value 0 (floor 7 2))   ; => 3
(nth-value 1 (floor 7 2))   ; => 1
(nth-value 2 (floor 7 2))   ; => NIL — no third value
#+end_src

It's useful when you know exactly which value you want and
the others would just clutter the binding. (nth-value 1
(gethash k table))= is "just the found-flag, please."

=values-list= is =values= turned inside out — a list in,
values out:

#+begin_src lisp
(values-list '(3 1))                          ; same as (values 3 1)
(multiple-value-list (values-list '(1 2 3)))  ; => (1 2 3)
(multiple-value-list (values-list nil))       ; => NIL — zero values
#+end_src

=values= and =values-list= are duals: (values-list (multiple-
value-list form)) reconstructs =form='s values, and
=(multiple-value-list (values-list list))= reconstructs =list=.
They don't round-trip through any intermediate — each
produces what the other consumes, in the appropriate context.

*** Single-value and multiple-value contexts

The vocabulary for the vanishing rule: a /single-value context/
keeps the primary value; a /multiple-value context/ keeps all.
The full table, which is the reference to come back to:

| Context                                     | Values kept |
|---------------------------------------------+-------------|
| Argument of a function call                 | 1 (primary) |
| =let=/=let*= binding value form              | 1 (primary) |
| =if=/=when=/=cond= test form                 | 1 (primary) |
| =progn= non-final form (value discarded)    | 1 (primary) |
| Inner =(values ...)= in =values=' args        | 1 (primary) |
| Body's last form of =defun=/=let=/=progn=    | all         |
| =multiple-value-bind='s form                | all         |
| =multiple-value-list='s form                | all         |
| =multiple-value-call='s argument forms      | all (each)  |
| =multiple-value-prog1='s first form         | all         |
| =return=/=return-from= value form           | all         |

The table's shape is the lesson: /default behavior is
truncation/. Any form that uses its argument's values all
does so by explicit design, and the design is visible in
the name (=multiple-value-...=) or the position (a body's
last form, an exit form).

Two corners worth pointing at:

*The body's last form.* =(defun f () (values 1 2))= returns
two values, because the function body's last form is a
multiple-value context. Every function you write returns
/all values of its last expression/, whether you thought
about multiple values or not. That's why =(defun g () (f))=
passes both values through — =(f)= is the body's last form,
hence multiple-value.

=return-from=. =(return-from block (values 1 2))= exits
the block with /both/ values, and =(multiple-value-bind
(a b) (block ...) ...)= catches them. =return= uses the
same mechanism (it's =return-from nil=), and =dolist=,
=dotimes= and =loop= all establish =nil= blocks, so a
=return= with a values form delivers them all:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (a b)
    (dolist (x '(1 2 3) (values :done 'three))
      (declare (ignore x)))
  (list a b))    ; => (:DONE THREE)
#+end_src

And with =return= mid-loop:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (a b)
    (dolist (x '(1 2 3))
      (when (= x 2) (return (values :found x))))
  (list a b))    ; => (:FOUND 2)
#+end_src

The =(declare (ignore x))= in the first example is Chapter
21's declaration — the body is empty, =x= is unused,
silence the warning.

*** =multiple-value-call= and =multiple-value-prog1=

=multiple-value-call= calls a function with /all/ values of
every argument form appended together:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-call #'+ (values 1 2) 3 (values 4 5))
;; => 15 — arguments were 1, 2, 3, 4, 5
#+end_src

It is the only ordinary way to /combine/ values instead of
truncating them, and its commonest use is passing a
multiple-valued result onward untouched:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-call #'list (floor 7 2))
;; => (3 1) — same as (multiple-value-list (floor 7 2))
#+end_src

That identity is worth stating: (multiple-value-list form)=
is equivalent to (multiple-value-call #'list form)=. Once you
see it, "what does =multiple-value-call= do" stops needing a
special explanation.

=multiple-value-prog1= evaluates forms and returns the /first
one's/ values — all of them — unlike =prog1=, which truncates:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *trace* nil)

(defun floor-and-log (a b)
  "Return FLOOR's two values, logging a trace entry."
  (multiple-value-prog1
      (floor a b)                       ; all its values preserved
    (push (list 'floor a b) *trace*)))  ; side effect only

(multiple-value-list (floor-and-log 7 2))
;; => (3 1)
*trace*   ; => ((FLOOR 7 2))
#+end_src

prog1= would have kept only =floor='s primary value 3; the
remainder would be lost, and the caller catching both values
would see =nil= for the second. When the first form might
return multiple values and the result matters,
=multiple-value-prog1= is the correct spelling.

The pattern =multiple-value-prog1= serves: /do something
before returning a multi-valued result, without touching the
result/. Logging, tracing, snapshotting — the side effect
runs, the values come through untouched.

*** Writing functions that return values

Two styles, and choosing well is most of the craft here:

/Values for cheap secondary information./ =floor='s remainder,
=gethash='s flag, =read-from-string='s position: things the
caller /sometimes/ wants, cheap to ignore otherwise. If the
caller almost always wants it, make it a primary value or a
structure; if almost never, print it or store it.

/Values instead of ambiguity./ The strongest use:
=(gethash key table)= distinguishes "stored nil" from
"absent", and your own functions can too:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun lookup-room (name)
  "Two values: the room, and whether it was found."
  (let ((r (find name *rooms* :key #'room-name)))
    (values r (not (null r)))))
#+end_src

Callers who don't care about =found-p= write
=(lookup-room 'kitchen)= and use the room; careful callers
bind both. The function serves both without a keyword
argument.

Note what =values= /doesn't/ do: it isn't a tuple type.
=(values 1 2)= is not an object — there is no way to store
"the two values" in a variable; you can only catch them
immediately (=multiple-value-bind=) or solidify them
(=multiple-value-list=). Passing values through intermediate
functions needs the multiple-value forms:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun floor-with-log (a b)
  (multiple-value-prog1 (floor a b)
    (format *trace-output* "floor ~D ~D~%" a b)))

(defun twice-remainder (a b)
  (multiple-value-bind (q r)
      (floor a b)
    (declare (ignore q))
    (values q (* 2 r))))
#+end_src

The first function preserves floor='s two values while
logging. The second catches both values and emits two of its
own, the remainder doubled.

*** Common mistakes

**** Assuming (values a b) returns a list

#+begin_example
(length (values 1 2))       ; error: 1 is not a list
(append (values 1 2) '(3))  ; error: 1 is not a list
#+end_example

Values aren't a datum; =multiple-value-list= is the bridge
when you actually want the list. The error message tells you
the primary value escaped (as a number) and the list operation
got confused — the diagnosis, once you know values exist.

**** Losing values through innocuous forms

=(let ((q (floor 7 2))) ...)= — =q= gets 3; the remainder is
gone, silently. Any single-value binding truncates. The fix is
=multiple-value-bind= at the point of the call, and the
discipline is knowing where values live: only in the form that
produced them, until caught or dropped. Once caught into a
list or bound into variables, they're ordinary data.

**** return inside dolist that you meant as a single value

#+begin_example
(multiple-value-bind (a b)
    (dolist (x '(1 2))
      (return x))
  (list a b))   ; => (1 NIL)
#+end_example

The trap isn't the truncation — that's expected, return='
delivers x='s one value, and b= gets nil. The trap is
believing the opposite: some readers think =return=
truncates, and test (return (values 1 2))=, expecting
=(1 NIL)=, and are surprised by =(1 2)=. In fact =return=
preserves multiple values (as return-from= does). Knowing
this /prevents/ a class of bug where you avoid =return=
because you think it truncates, when it doesn't.

The genuine trap: =let= binding value forms truncate, and
they're common in the code that catches =return=:

#+begin_example
(let ((pair (return-from some-block (values 1 2))))
  pair)   ; pair binds 1, not (1 2)
#+end_example

=return-from= delivers both values, but =let= keeps one.
Use multiple-value-bind= if you want both.

**** Ignoring the found-flag where it matters

=(gethash 'key table)= returning =nil= means /either/
absent /or/ stored-nil. Code that treats it as "absent"
corrupts tables that legitimately store =nil= values.
Wherever =nil= is a meaningful stored value, the two-valued
call is not optional.

**** Confusing (values) with (values nil)

#+begin_example
(if (multiple-value-list (values)) :present :absent)      ; => :ABSENT
(if (multiple-value-list (values nil)) :present :absent)  ; => :PRESENT
#+end_example

Both look like "nothing" in a single-value context — both are
=nil= when passed to a function that expects one value. But
in a multiple-value context, one returns zero values, the
other returns one. The difference is only detectable by the
forms designed to detect it — =multiple-value-list=, the
count of a bind, and so on.

**** Expecting a toplevel to catch two values

=(defun f () (floor 7 2))=. At the REPL, =(f)= shows 3, 1.
But =(mapcar #'f ...)= sees only =3= per call. =mapcar='s
function-call arguments (and results) are single-valued. If
you want a mapcar-shaped operation that preserves values, you
have to write it (=multiple-value-call= with careful argument
plumbing) — there's no "mapcar for values" in the standard.

*** Project step: two-valued command dispatch

Chapter 15's =run-command= returns one value: the reply
string. When the player types a real command, the reply is
"you take the key"; when they type nonsense, the reply is
"I don't know how to do that". A caller can't tell those
cases apart from the string alone without matching on the
text. Multiple values fix this cleanly — every command
returns two things: the reply, and whether the command was
recognised.

First =call-command=, which knows the difference:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun call-command (command)
  "Call COMMAND's handler.  Two values: reply, known-p."
  (let ((entry (assoc (first command) *commands*)))
    (if (null entry)
        (values (format nil "No such command: ~(~A~)."
                        (first command))
                nil)
        (values (apply (cdr entry) (rest command))
                t))))
#+end_src

Then =run-command=, which wraps it with the failsafe from
Chapter 15:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun run-command (command)
  "Execute COMMAND.  Two values: reply, known-p."
  (restart-case
      (handler-case (call-command command)
        (program-error ()
          (values "Wrong number of arguments for that command." t)))
    (retry-with (fixed)
      :report "Run a corrected command instead."
      (run-command fixed))))
#+end_src

The =restart-case= preserves the two values — when the body
returns normally, =restart-case= returns all of its values.
When =program-error= is caught, the handler returns (values
"..." t)=: the command was known, but the arguments were
wrong, so it's flagged as known. That distinction is
genuinely useful: "known but failed" and "unknown command"
are different failures, and the caller can distinguish them
by the second value.

Now =play=, which was =(mapcar #'run-command commands)=
returning a list of replies. Updated for two values:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun play (commands)
  "Run COMMANDS.  Two values: replies, unknown-command list."
  (let ((replies nil)
        (unknown nil))
    (dolist (c commands (values (nreverse replies)
                                (nreverse unknown)))
      (multiple-value-bind (reply known-p)
          (run-command c)
        (push reply replies)
        (unless known-p (push (first c) unknown))))))
#+end_src

dolist='s result form is (values ...)=, which returns both
lists. Inside the body, =multiple-value-bind= catches each
command's two values; the =unless= decides where to file the
unknown ones. The =nreverse= preserves order, as usual.

Check it:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(setf *item-locations* '((key . kitchen)))

(multiple-value-bind (replies unknown)
    (play '((take key) (dance) (look)))
  (list replies unknown))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
(("You take the key."
  "No such command: DANCE."
  "You are in the Kitchen. A warm kitchen that smells of bread. You can see: key.")
 (DANCE))
#+end_example

The reply list carries the "No such command: DANCE." string
(the player sees it); the unknown list carries DANCE= (the
developer audits it). Both come from the same command, and
=play='s single function call returns both.

Now Exercise 11.12's count-unknown=, which compared reply
strings with =string== to detect unknowns, becomes honest:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-unknown (commands)
  "How many commands are unknown?"
  (multiple-value-bind (replies unknown)
      (play commands)
    (declare (ignore replies))
    (length unknown)))
#+end_src

The string-matching was always a hack — if you changed the
message text, the count broke. The two-value contract is what
the game meant all along: "was this command known?" is a
question the dispatcher can answer, and now it does.

The (declare (ignore replies))= is Chapter 21's declaration
doing its job: the binding exists to consume a value the
function doesn't need, and the declaration documents that the
non-use is deliberate.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 24.1

Predict, then check:

#+begin_example
(multiple-value-list (values))
(multiple-value-list (values 1))
(multiple-value-list (values 1 2 3))
(+ (values 1 2) (values 3 4))
(list (values 1 2) (values 3 4))
(values (values 1 2) (values 3 4))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 24.2

Each snippet is wrong or surprising. Say what happens, then
fix it.

#+begin_example
(defun two-things () (values :a :b))
(let ((x (two-things))) x)

(multiple-value-bind (a b)
    (loop for i below 3
          finally (return (values :done i)))
  (list a b))

(let ((table (make-hash-table)))
  (setf (gethash :k table) nil)
  (gethash :k table))

(defun pass-through (form-values)
  form-values)
(multiple-value-list (pass-through (values 1 2)))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 24.3

Write =safe-divide=, which returns two values — the quotient,
and =nil= on success or =:division-by-zero= when the divisor
is zero (primary value =nil= in that case) — and =safe-sum=,
which sums a list of (numerator denominator) pairs using
=safe-divide=, and returns =nil= and =t= as two values when
any division failed. No conditions: the exercise is doing
=gethash='s flag trick by hand.

**** Exercise 24.4

Write =compose-values=, which takes two functions =f= and =g=
(=f= runs first, then =g= gets all of =f='s values), and
returns the composed function. =(funcall (compose-values
#'floor #'list) 7 2)= should give =(3 1)=. Which primitive is
=compose-values= just =multiple-value-call= for?

**** Exercise 24.5

Write =first-or-default=, which takes a thunk and a default,
calls the thunk, and returns its primary value or the default
when it returned /zero/ values. What does
=(multiple-value-list (values))= return, and how does your
function detect that case?

**** Exercise 24.6 (project)

The =save-game=/=load-game= pair (Chapters 16 and 18)
currently return one value. Make both two-valued: =save-game=
returns =t= and the pathname written (or =nil= and an error
description on failure); =load-game= returns the state and a
loaded-p flag. Then update the game's =save= and =load=
commands to use the second value — no string matching on the
error message.

** Chapter 25: Closures as Objects

Chapter 9 introduced closures with =make-adder= and
=make-counter=: functions that remember the =let= variables
they were born next to. That was a first pass, enough to use
=lambdas= as callbacks. This chapter develops closures into
what they really are — /Lisp's objects, before CLOS objects/ —
with three new ideas: what exactly is captured, how several
closures can share one environment, and where closure state
beats =defparameter= state.

*** What is captured, precisely

When a =lambda= references a variable from an enclosing scope,
it captures the /binding/ — the variable itself, not the value
at creation time:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-counter ()
  (let ((count 0))
    (lambda ()
      (incf count))))

(defparameter *c* (make-counter))
(funcall *c*) ; => 1
(funcall *c*) ; => 2
(funcall *c*) ; => 3
#+end_src

The counter keeps counting because =count= is a live binding
that outlived the =let= that created it. This is the defining
property of lexical closures: the binding's extent extends as
long as any closure can reach it. Contrast the value-capture
instinct:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-counters-wrong ()
  (let ((count 0))
    (list (lambda () count)
      (lambda () (incf count)))))

(defparameter pair (make-counters-wrong))
(funcall (first pair)) ; => 1 — sees the increment...
(funcall (second pair)) ; => 2
(funcall (first pair)) ; => 2 — the SAME binding
#+end_src

Both closures share one =count=, so the "reader" sees every
increment. If you wanted independent copies, each closure
needs its own binding — which is the next section's subject.

*** Constructors: closures born in a loop

=dotimes= and =dolist= introduce fresh bindings /per
iteration/, so closures created inside a loop each capture
their own variable:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-bumpers (limit)
  "A list of closures; the i-th adds i."
  (loop for i from 1 to limit
    collect (lambda (x) (+ x i))))

(defparameter bumpers (make-bumpers 3))
(mapcar #'funcall bumpers (list 100 100 100))
;; => (101 102 103) — three separate bindings
#+end_src

No capture-in-a-loop bug in Common Lisp's case here — each
iteration binds a new =i=. (In languages with per-function
variable binding, this example is the classic trap; in Lisp
the loop's binding is per-iteration by specification, and
=loop='s =for= clauses too.) The corresponding /real/ trap in
Lisp is different, and covered in Common mistakes below:
capturing the /loop variable of a surrounding function/
instead of the loop's own binding.

*** Shared environments: objects from closures

The step beyond =make-counter=: return /several/ closures that
share one set of bindings. That shared set is private state
with no name anyone else can touch — in one word,
/encapsulation/, achieved with nothing but =let= and =lambda=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-account (balance)
  "A bank account as three closures over one binding."
  (values
    (lambda (amount) ; deposit
      (setf balance (+ balance amount)))
    (lambda (amount) ; withdraw
      (if (> amount balance)
        (error "Insufficient funds: ~A < ~A" amount balance)
        (setf balance (- balance amount))))
    (lambda () balance))) ; balance

(multiple-value-bind (deposit withdraw balance)
  (make-account 100)
  (funcall deposit 50)
  (funcall withdraw 30)
  (funcall balance)) ; => 120
#+end_src

Nothing outside the three closures can read or write
=balance= — not by =symbol-value=, not by any accessor, because
it isn't a slot anywhere; it's a variable that exists only in
their shared lexical world. Compare the CLOS version you'll
write in Chapter 26: a class with a =balance= slot is the same
design, with the closures replaced by methods and the =let=
replaced by the instance. Neither is better; the closure
version has no class to define, the class version has
=print-object=, inheritance and redefinition support. For
small, fixed, use-and-forget state, closures are lighter.

A dispatch-table variant — one closure, behaviour chosen by
message — is the style that grew into "generic functions" in
other languages:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-registry ()
  (let ((table (make-hash-table)))
    (lambda (message &optional key value)
      (ecase message
        (:put (setf (gethash key table) value))
        (:get (gethash key table))
        (:keys (loop for k being the hash-keys of table
            collect k))))))

(defparameter reg (make-registry))
(funcall reg :put 'key "brass")
(funcall reg :get 'key) ; => "brass"
(funcall reg :keys) ; => (KEY)
#+end_src

The hash table is invisible to callers; the closure is the
only door. Add a =:count= message later and no caller changes.

*** Closures vs defparameter state

The earmuffed globals you've used since Chapter 4 are
/process-wide/ state. Closure state is /instance/ state: call
=make-counter= twice and you have two independent counters,
whereas two uses of a =defparameter= counter are one counter.

The rule of thumb:

    One of something for the whole program (the game's =rooms=,
    =commands=): a =defparameter=. Global by intent.

A third use, small and constant in real code: closures as
/parameterised behaviour/. Chapter 9's =make-adder= is the
seed; =remove-if= takes a predicate, and building that
predicate on the fly is the daily use:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun name-matcher (target)
  (lambda (room) (eq (room-name room) target)))

(remove-if (name-matcher 'kitchen) rooms)
#+end_src

The =lambda= captures =target=; the predicate is data and
behaviour in one argument. No class, no global, no coupling.

*** Common mistakes

**** Capturing the wrong variable in a loop

#+begin_example
(let ((fns nil))
(dolist (x '(a b c) (nreverse fns))
(push (lambda () x) fns)))

(mapcar #'funcall **) ; => (A B C) — fine, dolist binds per iteration
#+end_example

The genuine trap is when the =lambda= references an
/outer/ function's parameter instead of the loop's variable —
then every closure sees the same (final or only) argument
value. When closures over loop variables misbehave, check
which binding each free variable names.

**** Closure state and save files

Closure bindings are invisible to =print=, =read= and
=save-game=: there is no object to serialise. State that must
survive belongs in structures, alists and hash tables reachable
from named variables. Closures for /session-lifetime/ state —
counters, iterators, per-connection handles.

**** funcall amnesia

A closure is a function object; =(make-counter)= alone creates
it and throws it away. =setf= it, pass it, store it in a table
— but if nobody holds it, its bindings die with it. The
=make-account= example only works because =multiple-value-bind=
kept all three closures; keeping one and dropping the others
orphaned their shared world.

**** Overusing closures for big state

Once a closure-based "object" grows past four or five messages,
or needs =print-object=, inheritance, or redefinition during
development (Chapter 22's workflow), move it to =defclass=.
Closures excel small; classes scale.

*** Project step: a command-history object

Chapter 14's =history= is a plain list of alist snapshots,
managed by the global =snapshot= and =undo= functions. This
step wraps history in a closure object — private state, three
operations, no global except the single handle.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-history ()
  "A bounded command history: :add, :recent, :clear, :undo-to."
  (let ((snapshots nil)
      (limit 20))
    (lambda (message &optional arg)
      (ecase message
        (:add
          (push arg snapshots)
          (when (> (length snapshots) limit)
            (setf snapshots (subseq snapshots 0 limit)))
          t)
        (:recent
          (subseq snapshots 0 (min (or arg limit)
              (length snapshots))))
        (:undo-to
          (when snapshots
            (let ((s (first snapshots)))
              (pop snapshots)
              s)))
        (:clear
          (setf snapshots nil)
          t)))))
#+end_src

Replace the global-history versions from Chapter 14:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter history (make-history))

(defun snapshot ()
  "Record the current item locations for undo."
  (funcall history :add (copy-alist item-locations)))

(defun undo ()
  "Restore the item locations from before the last move."
  (let ((s (funcall history :undo-to)))
    (if s
      (progn
        (setf item-locations s)
        "Undone.")
      "Nothing to undo.")))
#+end_src

The signatures of =snapshot= and =undo= are unchanged from
Chapter 14 — callers of these functions don't need to know
that the storage moved from a global list to a closure. That
is the /encapsulation/ the chapter promised: private state,
public interface, no change to the interface.

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf current-room (find-room 'kitchen))
(setf item-locations '((key . kitchen)))

(snapshot)
(setf item-locations (set-location item-locations 'key 'player))
(undo)
(item-location 'key) ; => KITCHEN
#+end_src

The =limit= trimming keeps the history bounded without any
caller knowing. Note what the design avoids: =history=
entries never reach =save-game= (they're closure state, by
design — a fresh session starts with empty history), and no
other code can reach in and mangle the list, because there is
no list to reach — only the four messages. If a future
chapter wants history to /survive/ saves, that's the signal to
convert this object to a =defclass= with a serialisable slot —
and the class version's save-game support would need to expose
the snapshots list, which is exactly the trade the chapter
warned about.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 25.1

Predict, then check:

#+begin_example
(let ((fns (loop for i from 1 to 3
collect (lambda () i))))
(mapcar #'funcall fns))

(defun make-two ()
(let ((n 0))
(values (lambda () (incf n))
(lambda () (decf n)))))
(multiple-value-bind (up down) (make-two)
(list (funcall up) (funcall up) (funcall down) (funcall up)))

(let ((x 10))
(let ((f (lambda () x)))
(setf x 99)
(funcall f)))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 25.2

Each snippet is wrong or surprising. Say what happens, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(defun make-accumulator ()
(lambda (n) (setf total (+ total n))))
(funcall (make-accumulator) 5)

(defparameter counters
(loop for name in '(a b c)
collect (list name (let ((count 0))
(lambda () (incf count))))))
(funcall (second (first counters)))

(let ((fns nil))
(dotimes (i 3)
(push (lambda () i) fns))
(mapcar #'funcall fns))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 25.3

Write =make-average=, returning a single closure that takes a
number and returns, as two values, the running average and the
running count. Then extend =make-registry= (from the chapter)
with a =:count= message — how many keys it holds — without
changing any existing caller.

**** Exercise 25.4

Write =make-stack=, returning four closures (=push!=, =pop!=,
=empty-p=, =contents=) sharing one list binding. =pop!= should
signal an error on underflow, and =contents= should return a
/copy= of the list. What does returning a copy teach about
encapsulation vs leaking?

**** Exercise 25.5

Chapter 10's =remove-duplicates= uses =eql= by default. Write
=make-key-matcher=, which takes a =:key= function and a target
value and returns a predicate suitable for =find-if=. Then use
it to find a room by name without writing an inline =lambda=.
Compare with the chapter's =name-matcher=: which one
generalises, and why?

**** Exercise 25.6 (project)

Wrap the game's item-taking in a closure-based /cooldown/: a
=make-cooldown= object with =:block= (records a command and
answers whether the same command came within the last two
calls) and =:reset=. Wire it into =run-command= so repeated
identical commands get the reply "Not again." instead of
executing. In a comment: is cooldown state (a) session state
like history, or (b) persistent state like item locations —
and what decides the answer?

** Chapter 26: CLOS

You've built the game with lists, alists, structures and
closures. That works at small size, but it has two strains you
may have felt: =room-name=, =room-exits= and friends are named
by convention (Chapter 13), and every new kind of thing means
more parallel functions. The Common Lisp Object System, CLOS,
offers a different arrangement: /classes/ give data named
parts, and /generic functions/ give behaviour a way to vary by
the data it works on.

CLOS is not a bolted-on layer. It is part of the language
standard, written in Lisp, and powerful enough that people have
built other object systems on top of it. Two ideas set it apart
from Java's model from the start, and the chapter keeps
returning to them:

- /Methods belong to generic functions, not to classes./ A
  class never "owns" a method; a generic function collects
  methods from wherever they're defined.
- /Methods specialise on all their arguments/, not just the
  first. Java's =this= picks one distinguished argument; CLOS
  picks among all of them.

The chapter also positions CLOS against the two alternatives
you already have: structures (Chapter 13) for internal records,
closures (Chapter 25) for small stateful objects. CLOS is the
tool for types at the /edges/ of your program, where other code
adds behaviour and other people extend.

*** Classes: =defclass=

A class is defined with =defclass=, and reads like a
=defstruct= with ambitions:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass thing ()
  ((name :initarg :name
         :reader thing-name)
   (description :initarg :description
                :reader thing-description
                :initform "an ordinary thing"))
  (:documentation "Something the player can carry or examine."))
#+end_src

The parts:

- The second slot of =defclass= — here =()= — lists
  /superclasses/. =thing= has none beyond the default.
- Each slot has a name and /slot options/:
  =:initarg= names the keyword =make-instance= accepts,
  =:reader= generates an accessor function,
  =:initform= gives a default.
- Unlike =defstruct=, nothing is exported or auto-generated
  beyond what you ask for. =thing-name= exists because you
  asked.

The class is named =thing=, not =item=, to avoid two collisions
with earlier chapters: =item= is a value the game passes around
(the symbol ='item= in command arguments), and =item-name= is
Chapter 7's function that lowercases a symbol for messages.
=thing= has neither conflict.

Instances are made with =make-instance=, and slots are read
with the readers:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *key-thing*
  (make-instance 'thing
                 :name 'key
                 :description "a small brass key"))

(thing-name *key-thing*)          ; => KEY
(thing-description *key-thing*)   ; => "a small brass key"
(thing-description (make-instance 'thing :name 'rock))
;; => "an ordinary thing"
#+end_src

=:initform= runs each time an instance is made, so defaults
like =:initform (list)= would give each instance its /own/
fresh list — the problem =defstruct='s shared defaults dodge
by luck. If you want a default evaluated once for all
instances, say so deliberately.

Slots are writable with =:accessor= instead of =:reader=,
which generates both the reader and a =setf= place:
