#+begin_src lisp
(defclass door ()
  ((locked :initarg :locked
           :accessor door-locked-p
           :initform t)))

(defparameter *cellar-door* (make-instance 'door))
(door-locked-p *cellar-door*)          ; => T
(setf (door-locked-p *cellar-door*) nil)
(door-locked-p *cellar-door*)          ; => NIL
#+end_src

=setf= works on slot accessors with no special form — another
place Chapter 4's "setf knows about places" pays off.

=:reader=, =:accessor=, =:initarg= are a lot of typing compared
to =defstruct='s automatic everything. The trade is deliberate:
=defstruct= is for internal, implementation-flavoured records,
and =defclass= for types with an interface you'd defend. As a
rule of thumb: structures for the game's scratch data, classes
for the kinds of thing other code will touch.

*** Inheritance

The superclass list is where classes build on each other:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass container (thing)
  ((contents :initarg :contents
             :accessor contents
             :initform nil)))

(defclass treasure (thing)
  ((value :initarg :value
          :reader value
          :initform 1)))

(defparameter *chest*
  (make-instance 'container
                 :name 'chest
                 :description "a wooden chest."
                 :contents '(gold ring)))

(thing-name *chest*)     ; => CHEST
(contents *chest*)       ; => (GOLD RING)
(typep *chest* 'thing)   ; => T
#+end_src

=container= /is a/ =thing=: it has =thing='s slots and readers,
plus its own. =typep= asks the class question, and it works for
any type you've met — =(typep 3 'integer)= is the same
operation.

Multiple inheritance is ordinary in CLOS — a class may list
several superclasses — and slot conflicts are resolved by a
/class precedence list/: the class itself, then its
superclasses left to right, depth-first, deduplicated. You
rarely need to compute one by hand, but when two superclasses
both define a slot or a method, the precedence list decides
whose wins, leftmost first. =class-precedence-list=, called
on an instance's class, shows it:

#+begin_src lisp
(sb-mop:class-precedence-list (class-of *chest*))
;; => (#<STANDARD-CLASS CONTAINER> #<STANDARD-CLASS THING>
;;     #<STANDARD-CLASS STANDARD-OBJECT>
;;     #<STANDARD-CLASS T>)
#+end_src

The order: =container= first (most specific), then =thing=
(its superclass), then =standard-object= (the default CLOS
parent), then =t= (everything). Any method selection walks
this list from left to right, most specific first.

*** Generic functions and methods

Here is the part that's genuinely different. A /generic
function/ is a named thing that dispatches to methods:

#+begin_src lisp
(defgeneric describe-thing (thing)
  (:documentation "Return a sentence describing THING."))
#+end_src

=defgeneric= defines the name, the parameter list and the
documentation. It has no body. Bodies come from =defmethod=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmethod describe-thing ((t2 thing))
  (format nil "You see ~(~A~): ~A."
          (thing-name t2) (thing-description t2)))

(defmethod describe-thing ((c container))
  (format nil "You see ~(~A~): ~A It holds ~{~(~A~)~^, ~}."
          (thing-name c)
          (thing-description c)
          (contents c)))

(defmethod describe-thing ((tr treasure))
  (format nil "You see ~(~A~): ~A It's worth ~D coins."
          (thing-name tr) (thing-description tr) (value tr)))
#+end_src

Each =defmethod= adds a method to the same generic function,
and the parenthesised parameter =((t2 thing))= says "this
method applies when this argument is a =thing=". Calling it
picks the most specific method:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (describe-thing 'table)
      (describe-thing *key-thing*)
      (describe-thing *chest*))
#+end_src

Expected: a generic error (no method for symbols — =table= is
not a =thing=), the item's description, and the container's
listing. Re-run with only the registered things:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (describe-thing *key-thing*)
      (describe-thing *chest*)
      (describe-thing (make-instance 'treasure
                        :name 'crown
                        :description "a tarnished crown."
                        :value 100)))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("You see key: a small brass key."
 "You see chest: a wooden chest. It holds gold, ring."
 "You see crown: a tarnished crown. It's worth 100 coins.")
#+end_example

Each of the three kinds of thing gets its own method, and the
call selects by the argument's class. =container= inherits
from =thing=, so a container is also "described by thing" —
but the container method is /more specific/, so it wins.

Notice what didn't happen: =thing= doesn't "have" a
=describe-thing= method. The methods live on =describe-thing=,
and =thing= merely appears in one's parameter specialiser. You
can add a method for a class you don't own — for =number=,
even — from anywhere, and you can add methods to a generic
function from any file, at any time, including in the running
REPL. Java's visitor pattern exists to work around this
restriction; CLOS doesn't have the restriction.

Methods on several arguments — multiple dispatch — follow the
same rule, with the combination of all specialisers deciding:

#+begin_src lisp
(defgeneric combine (a b))

(defmethod combine ((a number) (b number))
  (+ a b))

(defmethod combine ((a string) (b string))
  (concatenate 'string a b))

(defmethod combine ((a number) (b string))
  (format nil "~D~A" a b))

(list (combine 1 2) (combine "a" "b") (combine 3 "x"))
#+end_src

Expected: =(3 "ab" "3x")=. No =if= chains checking types; the
dispatch table /is/ the type logic, and each case is a method
you can read and extend alone.

*** =call-next-method=

A method can hand work to the next one down the precedence
list:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmethod describe-thing :around ((t2 thing))
  (format nil "~A (a ~(~A~))"
          (call-next-method)
          (class-name (class-of t2))))
#+end_src

Method qualifiers such as =:after=, =:before= and =:around=
change when a method runs:

- =:before= methods run before the primary method, for setup.
- =:after= methods run after, and /all matching ones/ run —
  they compose rather than choose.
- =:around= wraps everything and calls =call-next-method= to
  let the rest proceed.

The =:around= method above doesn't replace the primary — it
runs first, calls =call-next-method= (which runs the primary
chain), and appends the class name. =:before= and =:after= are
the Lisp answer to what Java calls =super= calls in
constructors: an =initialize-instance :after= method is the
standard way to finish setting up an instance:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmethod initialize-instance :after ((c container) &key)
  (unless (listp (contents c))
    (setf (contents c) (list (contents c)))))
#+end_src

This runs whenever any =container= is made, whether or not the
caller remembered a =:contents= argument — validation and
repair lived in the constructor, now it lives with the class.
(If you'd written =(make-instance 'container :contents '(gold))=,
no change — listp is true, no wrapping. If you'd written
=(make-instance 'container :contents 'gold)=, the :after fires,
wraps =gold= into =(gold)=, and =(contents ...)= returns the list.)

*** Structures and classes: when to use which

Both give named slots. The honest comparison:

|                    | =defstruct=             | =defclass=                     |
|--------------------+-------------------------+--------------------------------|
| Accessors          | automatic               | only what you declare          |
| Inheritance        | single superclass       | multiple, with precedence list |
| Methods that vary  | by =typecase= or =etypecase= | by =defmethod=          |
| Speed              | usually faster, simpler | more machinery underneath      |
| Intended for       | internal records        | types with a public interface  |

The game has used =defstruct= rooms so far, and that's fine —
rooms are internal. The =thing= hierarchy above is the kind of
thing that benefits from CLOS, because =describe-thing= wants
to vary by type.

*** CLOS vs closures

Chapter 25 built account and registry objects out of closures.
CLOS gives the same patterns a different shape, and choosing
between them is a real design question:

|                          | Closures                       | CLOS                          |
|--------------------------+--------------------------------+-------------------------------|
| State lives in           | =let= bindings                 | slots                         |
| Behaviour is             | one function, message-dispatched | one method per generic function |
| Adding behaviour         | edit the =ecase=               | define a new =defmethod=      |
| Multiple objects         | return more closures          | =make-instance=, many instances |
| Redefinition while running | re-define the closure         | re-define the class; instances auto-update |
| Printing                 | wrap the closure              | =print-object=                |
| Inheritance              | compose by hand                | =defclass= superclasses       |

For a small stateful object with three or four messages,
closures are lighter — no class to define, no dispatch
overhead. For a type hierarchy, multiple implementations of
the same interface, or behaviour that varies by more than one
argument, CLOS is the right tool.

The two also compose: a closure can be a slot value, a method
can return a closure, an =initialize-instance :after= can
capture a closure to use as a default method. The choice
isn't exclusive; it's which shape to /lean on/ for a given
piece.

*** Common mistakes

**** Forgetting =(:use :cl)= brings CLOS in, but nothing else does

=defclass=, =defmethod=, =make-instance= are all ordinary =cl=
symbols. The mistake is elsewhere: in a file with
=(in-package :game)=, a method on =describe-thing= defined in =cl-user=
and one defined in =game= are /two different generic functions/,
because the symbols differ. When methods seem to vanish, check
=(symbol-package 'describe-thing)= in both places.

**** Writing methods before the generic function

You can skip =defgeneric= — the first =defmethod= creates the
generic function implicitly. But then the parameter list and
documentation come from wherever the first method happened to
be, and a later =defgeneric= with a different shape is an
error. Define =defgeneric= first: it is the interface
statement.

**** Expecting slot readers you didn't declare

=make-instance= accepted the =:initarg=, but reading the slot
without a =:reader= or =:accessor= fails. CLOS generates
nothing you didn't ask for.

**** Specialising on a class that isn't loaded

#+begin_example
(defmethod describe-thing ((c container)) ...)
;; "The class CONTAINER is not defined"
#+end_example

The class must exist before the method that names it. Load
order matters; ASDF's =:serial= from Chapter 16 exists for
exactly this.

**** Confusing =typep= on classes with =eq= on symbols

=(typep *chest* 'thing)= asks about the object. =(eq *chest*
'thing)= compares the object to the symbol =thing=, which is
=nil=. The quote in =typep= is part of the type notation, not
a quoted object comparison.

**** Forgetting =call-next-method= in an =:around=

An =:around= method that doesn't call =call-next-method=
suppresses every primary method beneath it — the description
comes back as whatever the =:around= alone produced, with no
item-specific information. That's occasionally intended; it's
almost never what the code's author meant.

*** Project step: things in the game

The game's items are just symbols in =*item-locations*= — where
each symbol lives. That's enough to move a key from the kitchen
to the player's pocket, but not enough to /describe/ the key:
its weight, its flavour, whether it's fragile. The game has
held that information in code, inline in =describe-room-text=
and the like. This step gives it a home.

The approach: register each item symbol with a =thing= instance
that carries the metadata. The two representations coexist:

- =*item-locations*= — mutable state: which room holds each
  item.
- =*thing-registry*= — static metadata: what each item /is/.

The link between them is the symbol (='key=, ='chest=, etc.),
which appears in both.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *thing-registry*
  (make-hash-table :test #'eq)
  "Maps item symbols to their THING instances.")

(defun register-thing (sym instance)
  "Associate SYM with INSTANCE in the registry."
  (setf (gethash sym *thing-registry*) instance))

(defun lookup-thing (sym)
  "The THING instance for SYM, or NIL if unregistered."
  (gethash sym *thing-registry*))
#+end_src

Now register the game's items:

#+begin_src lisp
(register-thing 'key
  (make-instance 'thing
                 :name 'key
                 :description "a small brass key"))

(register-thing 'lamp
  (make-instance 'thing
                 :name 'lamp
                 :description "an oil lamp, dusty but intact"))

(register-thing 'chest
  (make-instance 'container
                 :name 'chest
                 :description "a wooden chest."
                 :contents '(crown gem)))

(register-thing 'crown
  (make-instance 'treasure
                 :name 'crown
                 :description "a tarnished crown."
                 :value 100))
#+end_src

The =describe-thing= generic now has three methods, one per
class, and dispatch is automatic. Add a game command that uses
it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun examine (sym)
  "Handle (EXAMINE THING): describe THING."
  (let ((instance (lookup-thing sym)))
    (if instance
        (describe-thing instance)
        (format nil "You see nothing special about the ~(~A~)."
                sym))))

(push '(examine . examine) *commands*)
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (run-command '(examine key))
      (run-command '(examine chest))
      (run-command '(examine crown))
      (run-command '(examine sword)))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("You see key: a small brass key."
 "You see chest: a wooden chest. It holds crown, gem."
 "You see crown: a tarnished crown. It's worth 100 coins."
 "You see nothing special about the sword.")
#+end_example

Four commands, four answers, and the middle two came from
different methods of the same generic function — =container=
and =treasure= dispatch to their own =describe-thing=, while
=key= falls back to the =thing= method.

Now the payoff. Add a new kind of thing — a fragile thing,
say — with its own method, without touching any existing code:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass fragile (thing) ())

(defmethod describe-thing ((f fragile))
  (format nil "~A It looks like it would break if you dropped it."
          (call-next-method)))

(register-thing 'vase
  (make-instance 'fragile
                 :name 'vase
                 :description "a thin porcelain vase."))

(run-command '(examine vase))
#+end_src

Expected: ="You see vase: a thin porcelain vase. It looks like
it would break if you dropped it."=

The =fragile= method called =call-next-method= to get the base
description and append its own sentence. No existing method
was edited, no existing command was told about =fragile=, and
the new type works throughout the game's interface
immediately. That's what CLOS's open dispatch buys:
/extension by addition/.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 26.1

Define a class =lamp= as a subclass of =thing= with a =lit=
slot (default =nil=, accessor =lit-p=). Make one instance and
check the accessor with =setf=.

**** Exercise 26.2

Predict, then check:

#+begin_example
(defclass a () ((x :reader x-of :initform 1)))
(defclass b (a) ((x :reader x-of :initform 2)))
(x-of (make-instance 'b))
#+end_example

Why is the answer 2? Which rule decides?

**** Exercise 26.3

Write =defgeneric= =empty-thing-p= and two methods: anything
returns =nil=; a =container= returns =t= when its =contents=
is =nil=. Use =call-next-method= nowhere — explain in a comment
why =:before= or =:after= would be wrong tools here.

**** Exercise 26.4

Each snippet below is wrong. Say what's wrong, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(defclass potion (thing)
  ((colour :initarg :colour)))

(defmethod drink ((p potion))
  (thing-colour p))

(make-instance 'potion :colour 'red)
; then:
(thing-description *potion*)

(defmethod describe-thing ((i thing)) ...)
; defined before (defclass thing ...) in the same file, loaded in order

(typep 'thing 'thing)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 26.5

Write =defmethod combine= cases so that =(combine 'a 'b)= gives
=(a b)=, =(combine "x" 5)= gives ="x5"=, and any other pair
signals an error with a useful message. How many methods did
you need, and what does that number tell you about multiple
dispatch?

**** Exercise 26.6

Add an =initialize-instance :after= method on =container= that
sorts the contents alphabetically when they're given unsorted.
Test it by making a =container= with =:contents '(zebra apple)=
and reading the slot.

**** Exercise 26.7

Define =defgeneric= =weight=, with =defmethod= on =thing=
giving 1 and on =container= giving 1 plus the number of things
it holds. Write =total-weight=, which takes a list of item
symbols and sums their weights with =lookup-thing= and
=reduce=. What happens with an unregistered symbol, and does
that suggest where a Chapter 19 =handler-case= belongs?

**** Exercise 26.8 (project)

Write =list-things=, a command that lists every registered
thing's name and description, sorted alphabetically. It uses
=maphash= or =loop for ... being the hash-keys= to walk the
registry. In a comment: what does the class hierarchy buy over
storing the description in the symbol's plist (Chapter 23)?

    *item-locations* is an alist (Ch 7)

    *rooms* is a list of room structures (Ch 13)

    *thing-registry* is a hash table mapping symbols to thing instances (Ch 26)

    *commands* is an alist (Ch 9, extended in later chapters)

    *history* is a closure object (Ch 25)

    *save-file* is a parameter

Chapter 26 exercises 26.1-26.8:

** Chapter 27: CLOS Deep Dive

Chapter 26 gave you working CLOS: =defclass=, slots with
=:initarg=, =:reader=, =:accessor=, =:initform=, =defmethod=,
=call-next-method=, =:before=/=:after=/=:around=, and
=initialize-instance :after= as the constructor hook. This
chapter goes the rest of the way — the parts of CLOS that
distinguish reading other people's code from writing serious
code: class precedence lists, =shared-initialize=, changing an
object's class, method combination beyond =:around=, and what
happens when no method matches.

*** Multiple inheritance and the class precedence list

A class can have several direct superclasses:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass thing () ())
(defclass container () ())
(defclass portable (thing) ())
(defclass box (thing container) ())
#+end_src

When a method specialises on =thing= and another on
=container=, and you call it on a =box= — which runs? CLOS's
answer is the /class precedence list/: every class has a total
order over itself and all its superclasses, with the class
itself first, its direct superclasses next (left to right),
then their superclasses, and so on. =class-precedence-list=
shows it:

#+begin_src lisp
(sb-mop:class-precedence-list (class-of (make-instance 'box)))
;; => (#<STANDARD-CLASS BOX> #<STANDARD-CLASS THING>
;;     #<STANDARD-CLASS CONTAINER> ... STANDARD-OBJECT ... T)
#+end_src

The rule of thumb: /leftmost direct superclass wins ties/. So
=(defclass box (thing container) ...)= makes =box= more
=thing=-like than container=-like: a method on thing= beats
a method on =container= for a =box= instance. Order in the
superclass list is meaningful — put the "primary nature"
first.

Method selection uses this list: the most specific applicable
method — the one whose specialiser appears earliest in the
instance's precedence list — runs first. =call-next-method=
invokes the next one down. This is why Chapter 26's advice —
":before= and :after= for side effects, primary methods for
the value" — works: the full method combination runs, in
order, /all/ applicable =:around=, then the most specific
primary flanked by all =:before= and (in reverse) all
=:after=.

*** shared-initialize: the real constructor chain

Chapter 26 used =initialize-instance :after= as the
initialisation hook. The full story: =(make-instance 'box :x 1)=
runs

#+begin_example
make-instance
  → allocate-instance (fresh, empty slots)
    → initialize-instance (with the :initargs)
      → shared-initialize (fills slots from :initargs and :initforms)
        → :after methods on shared-initialize
      → :after methods on initialize-instance
#+end_example

=shared-initialize= is the generic function that actually
fills slots from =:initargs= and =:initforms= — and it's
called both by =initialize-instance= (fresh objects) and by
=reinitialize-instance= (existing objects, re-run with new
arguments):

#+begin_src lisp
(reinitialize-instance *my-lamp* :lit t)
(lit-p *my-lamp*)   ; => T — slot updated
#+end_src

The practical consequence, and the reason libraries document
":initarg= means it's reinitialisable too": any slot you
expose via =:initarg= is settable both at creation /and/ at
=reinitialize-instance= time, by the same keyword. If you want
an option that only makes sense at birth, don't give the slot
an =:initarg= — set it in =initialize-instance :after= from
a different slot or argument.

The =:after= method on =initialize-instance= remains the
idiomatic constructor hook (Chapter 26's derived-slot
pattern); =:after= on =shared-initialize= additionally fires
on reinitialisation — use it when derived slots must track
re-init too.

*** change-class

An object's class isn't fixed for life:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass caterpillar (thing) ())
(defclass butterfly (thing)
  ((days-flown :initform 0 :accessor days-flown)))

(defparameter *bug* (make-instance 'caterpillar
                                   :name 'bug))
(change-class *bug* 'butterfly)
(typep *bug* 'butterfly)   ; => T — same object, new class
(days-flown *bug*)         ; => 0 — new slot, :initform
#+end_src

=change-class= keeps the object's identity: every reference to
it now sees a =butterfly=. Slots shared by name carry over;
new slots get their =:initforms=. The
=update-instance-for-different-class= generic function is the
hook for migration logic (copying, transforming, defaulting).

Used sparingly — the classic fits are state machines (a
connection going =connected= → =closed=) and interactive
systems where an object's role genuinely changes mid-session.
When in doubt, a status slot beats a class change: cheaper to
print, save and query.

*** Method combination

Chapter 26's =:before=/=:after=/=:around= is /standard method
combination/. The primary-method-plus-auxiliaries shape
generalises: other combination types run /all/ applicable
primary methods in most-specific-first order and combine the
results:

#+begin_src lisp
(defgeneric price (thing)
  (:method-combination +))

(defclass treasure-with-surcharge (treasure) ())

(defmethod price + ((t2 thing)) 1)         ; base cost

(defmethod price + ((tr treasure)) (value tr))

(defmethod price + ((ts treasure-with-surcharge)) 10)   ; extra fee

(price (make-instance 'treasure :value 100))
;; => 101 — 100 from the treasure method, 1 from thing's
(price (make-instance 'treasure-with-surcharge :value 100))
;; => 111 — both of the above, plus the surcharge
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass base-thing () ((base-value :initarg :base-value
                                     :initform 1
                                     :reader base-value)))

(defclass valuable (base-thing) ())

(defclass taxed (base-thing) ())

(defclass antique (valuable taxed) ())

(defgeneric price (object)
  (:method-combination +))

(defmethod price + ((o base-thing)) (base-value o))

(defmethod price + ((o valuable)) 10)
(defmethod price + ((o taxed)) 15)

(price (make-instance 'base-thing :base-value 20))
;; => 20
(price (make-instance 'valuable :base-value 20))
;; => 30
(price (make-instance 'antique :base-value 20))
;; => 45
#+end_src

That's the payoff: no =call-next-method= discipline
required, and /every/ applicable method contributes. The
standard combinations mirror the arithmetic and list
operators: =+=, =max=, =min=, =list=, =append=, =and=,
=or=, =progn= (=max= is a favourite for "the best answer
among all applicable methods"). =:around= methods still work
with any combination, wrapping the whole combined call.

For the =antique= case, all three applicable methods run:
=base-thing='s gives 20, =valuable='s gives 10, =taxed='s
gives 15, and =+= sums to 45. Order doesn't matter for =+=
(it's commutative); order /does/ matter for =list= (the list
comes in most-specific-first order) and =append= (same).

Custom combinations via =define-method-combination= exist
and are genuinely rare — know they're there; reach for
=:around= plus a helper function first.

*** no-applicable-method and friends

What happens when a generic function is called with arguments
no method matches? A =no-applicable-method= generic function
is invoked — which by default signals an error naming the
generic function and the arguments. You can define a method
on it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmethod no-applicable-method (gf &rest args)
  (format *error-output* "~A has no method for ~S~%"
          (generic-function-name gf) args)
  (invoke-restart (find-restart 'abort)))
#+end_src

— though in application code, silence is rarely the answer;
the default error is usually right. The related hooks worth
knowing by name only: =no-next-method= (a =call-next-method=
with nowhere to go) and =compute-applicable-methods= (the
dispatcher itself, specialisable for exotic effects you'll
read before you write).

Also worth naming: =(defmethod foo :before ...)= on a
generic function that doesn't exist yet /defines/ it
implicitly — CLOS creates the generic function. Chapter 26's
=defgeneric= explicit form is preferred in source, as
documentation and as the home of =:documentation= and
options.

*** Common mistakes

**** Superclass order as decoration

(defclass box (container thing) ...)= vs (thing
container)= differ in behaviour — precedence, method
selection. If you don't care, you're likely to care once two
=:before= methods both apply and run in the "wrong" order.
Order deliberately; comment the choice when it matters.

**** Expecting :before methods to contribute values

=:before= and =:after= results are discarded. A =:before=
method that computes something must store it (a slot, a
special variable) for the primary to use — or be a primary
method with =call-next-method= instead.

**** change-class as an update mechanism

Using =change-class= to "refresh" objects mutates identity
semantics readers rely on; it also interacts badly with
=save-game='s =print=/=read= round trip (a changed class
must exist at load time, in the expected shape). Prefer
=change-class= for genuine role transitions, slots for
status.

**** Method combination confusion: why won't my :after add to the result?

With =+= combination, =:after= methods still contribute
nothing; the combination applies to /primary/ methods only.
The fix is usually: make the secondary behaviour a primary
method (possibly on a more general class specialiser).

**** Two primary methods on the same class specialiser

You can't:

#+begin_example
(defmethod price + ((tr treasure)) 1)
(defmethod price + ((tr treasure)) 2)   ; redefinition, not addition
#+end_example

The second /replaces/ the first — same class, same combination
type, one method per generic-function-per-class. If you want
two contributions, you need two different classes (as in
Chapter 27's =valuable= and =taxed= above), or one method
that returns the sum of both internally.

*** Project step: pricing the inventory

Give the game a scoring economy, via += method combination —
the natural fit, since an item's worth is the /sum/ of
contributions from every class it belongs to.

The game's =thing=, =container= and =treasure= hierarchy
from Chapter 26 is the base; this chapter adds a scoring
layer on top. The classes exist; now give them a price:

#+begin_src lisp
(defgeneric price (thing)
  (:method-combination +)
  (:documentation "How valuable is THING, summing contributions?"))

(defmethod price + ((t2 thing))
  1)   ; every thing is worth at least 1

(defmethod price + ((c container))
  2)   ; containers are worth a bit more

(defmethod price + ((tr treasure))
  (value tr))   ; treasures are worth their slot
#+end_src

Now the game's inventory total uses them:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun inventory-score ()
  "Total value of everything the player carries."
  (reduce #'+ (mapcar #'price (items-here 'player))))

;; where items-here returns symbols, and we look up each thing:
(defun inventory-score ()
  (reduce #'+ (mapcar (lambda (sym)
                        (price (lookup-thing sym)))
                      (items-here 'player))))
#+end_src

The first version is wrong — =items-here= returns symbols, not
thing= instances, so (price 'key)= would try to dispatch on
a symbol and fail (no method for =symbol=). The second version
does the lookup.

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *item-locations* '((key . player) (crown . player)
                          (lamp . kitchen)))
(inventory-score)
#+end_src

Expected: ~+ 1 100 = 101~ — the key is a plain =thing=
(worth 1), the crown is a =treasure= (worth 100), the lamp
isn't carried.

Now the payoff: add an =antique= class combining several
contributions, without touching any existing method:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass antique (treasure) ())

(defmethod price + ((a antique))
  50)   ; antiques command a premium

(register-thing 'mirror
  (make-instance 'antique
                 :name 'mirror
                 :description "a tarnished silver mirror."
                 :value 200))

(setf *item-locations* (acons 'mirror 'player *item-locations*))
(inventory-score)   ; => 101 + 250 = 351
#+end_src

The =+= combination earns its keep the moment a new class
joins: =defmethod price + ((a antique)) 50= adds its
contribution to every antique without touching any other
method — the open/closed behaviour Chapter 26 promised,
finally in its purest form. The antique's price is 200 (from
treasure) + 50 (from antique) + 1 (from thing) = 251=, not
~200 + 50 = 250~ — the =thing= method contributes too,
because every antique is a thing.

That's the difference between =+= combination and
inheritance-of-behaviour: inheritance gives the /most
specific= method (200 for the crown's treasure method, say);
combination gives the /sum of all applicable=. The two styles
are both right; which one depends on whether the contributions
are alternatives (=treasure= vs =container= are alternative
kinds) or parts (base cost + premium + fee).

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 27.1

Predict the class precedence list of =(defclass d (b c) ...)=
given =(defclass a () ...)=, =(defclass b (a) ...)=,
=(defclass c (a) ...)=. Check with =class-precedence-list=.
Which class's methods win for a =d= instance, =b='s or
=c='s?

**** Exercise 27.2

Each snippet is wrong or surprising. Say what happens, then
fix it.

#+begin_example
(defclass widget () ((x :initarg :x :reader widget-x)))
(defmethod describe-widget :after ((w widget))
  (format nil "x is ~A" (widget-x w)))

(defclass base () ((n :initform 1 :accessor n)))
(defclass sub (base) ())
(defmethod n :after ((s sub))
  (incf (slot-value s 'n) 10))

(defgeneric area (shape))
(defmethod area ((s shape)) 0)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 27.3

Write =describe-chain=, which takes an instance and prints
each class in its precedence list with any =:before=,
=:after= and primary =price= methods specialising on it. Use
=apropos= or =find-method= as needed — or, simpler, keep a
manual registry for this exercise. What does the output teach
about why method order surprises happen?

**** Exercise 27.4

Define a =list= method combination analogue: a
=participants= generic function whose primary methods each
return one participant name, combined into a list. Then add an
=:around= method that records how long the full combination
took (Chapter 22's =get-internal-run-time=). Where must the
=:around= method specialise to wrap /every/ call?

**** Exercise 27.5

Write =safe-change-class=, which takes an instance and a new
class name, verifies with =class-precedence-list= that no
slots would be /dropped= (slots on the old class absent from
the new), and refuses with a condition listing the dropped
slot names. What does the refusal check teach about
=change-class='s slot policy?

**** Exercise 27.6 (project)

Give the game's rooms a += combination weight= generic
function — =room= contributes its exit count, =dark-room=
adds a penalty, =visited-room= (a mixin) adds a discount —
and a =safest-exit= helper that picks the exit whose
destination has the lowest =weight=. In a comment: what makes
=+= the right combination here and =max= the wrong one?

** Chapter 28: Packages and Symbols Internals

Chapter 16 taught the working day: =defpackage=,
=in-package=, =export=, and why the game's symbols live
behind :cave-of-noise:= prefixes. This chapter goes under the
hood — the package system as data structures you can query and
manipulate. You need this not to write exotic code but to
/read= it: every =shadow=, =unintern= and double-colon in
someone's source is a sentence about these structures, and the
name-conflict errors that interrupt your loading are questions
these tools answer.

*** The symbol table, by hand

A package is, at bottom, a table from strings to symbols, with
a list of exported names and a list of use-lists. Three
functions are the whole API surface for one symbol:

- =find-symbol= — look up a string; two values: the symbol
  and its /status= (=:internal=, =:external=, =:inherited=,
  or =nil=).
- =intern= — look up or create; the reader's own operation.
- =unintern= — remove a symbol from the package (the symbol
  object itself survives wherever it's referenced; it's just
  no longer /findable by name here=/).

#+begin_src lisp
(find-symbol "CAR" :cl)           ; => CAR, :EXTERNAL
(find-symbol "car" :cl)           ; => NIL, NIL — table is case-sensitive
(intern "THING")                  ; creates in *package* if absent
(multiple-value-bind (sym status)
    (find-symbol "LOOK" :cave-of-noise)
  (list sym status))              ; => (LOOK :EXTERNAL) if exported
#+end_src

=do-symbols=, =do-external-symbols= and =do-all-symbols=
walk the tables:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun external-names (package)
  "Sorted list of a package's external symbol names."
  (let ((names nil))
    (do-external-symbols (s package)
      (push (symbol-name s) names))
    (sort names #'string<)))
#+end_src

=do-external-symbols= walks just the exports;
=do-symbols= walks everything interned (with each symbol
visited /twice/ if the package both inherits and imports it —
an implementation-dependent corner); =do-all-symbols= walks
every package. The fourth macro, =with-package-iterator=, is
the programmatic one you'll meet in library code that wants
to produce a lazy stream of symbols rather than iterate.

*** export, import, and the two-way door

=export= puts a symbol's name in the package's external list.
What's less obvious: =export= on a symbol the package doesn't
/home= first /imports= it — the machinery is about tables, not
about "publishing". The round trip:

#+begin_src lisp
(defpackage :demo (:use :cl))
(in-package :demo)

(intern "TOOL")            ; home, internal
(export 'tool)             ; now external
(unintern 'tool)           ; gone from the table entirely
(find-symbol "TOOL" :demo) ; => NIL, NIL
#+end_src

=import= puts an /existing symbol object= into another
package's table — after which (eq 'cl-user::tool 'demo::tool)=
is true: one symbol, two addresses. This differs from
=shadow=, which creates a /new/ symbol in the package, blank,
replacing the inherited name. The distinction drives every
name-conflict resolution you'll ever click through in the
debugger.

*** Name conflicts, resolved by hand

A conflict happens when a name becomes findable two ways in
one package: =use-package= over a package that exports =foo=
while you've also interned your own =foo=; or =import= of a
symbol whose name is taken. The system signals a correctable
error whose restarts offer the resolution — Chapter 19's
restarts, working for you at load time. By hand, there are
three =defpackage= options:

#+begin_src lisp
(defpackage :mine (:use :cl) (:shadow :list))
;; :LIST now names MY package's fresh symbol, not CL:LIST

(defpackage :mine2 (:use :cl)
  (:shadowing-import-from :mylib :merge))
;; :MERGE resolves in favour of MYLIB's symbol for this name

(defpackage :mine3 (:use :cl)
  (:import-from :mylib :helper))
;; bring MYLIB:HELPER in; error if the name is taken
#+end_src

| Option                    | Effect on the conflicting name            |
|---------------------------+-------------------------------------------|
| =:shadow=                | my package gets its own fresh symbol      |
| =:shadowing-import-from= | the named package's symbol wins, and is /shadowing/ — later =use-package= conflicts with it resolve silently |
| =:import-from=           | bring the named package's symbol in (error if the table already has the name) |

The /shadowing= concept: a package can hold a symbol that
/shadows= an inherited one — =find-symbol= returns it, and
future use-package='s that would conflict with it are legal,
the shadowing symbol winning. That's what makes
=:shadowing-import-from= the tool for deliberately overriding
part of a used package.

The four =defpackage= options for bringing names in,
summarised:

- =(:use pkg)= — import all of =pkg='s /exports=, so they can be
  found by name, and any name conflict is an error at
  =use-package= time.
- (:import-from pkg name...)= — import the /specific symbols=
  =name...=; error if the name is taken.
- (:shadowing-import-from pkg name...)= — same, but the
  imported symbols /override= the current table's names; no
  error if the name is taken.
- (:shadow name...)= — the package gets its /own= symbol for
  each name, deliberately unrelated to whatever that name
  meant elsewhere.

*** Nicknames, renaming, deleting

=:nicknames= in =defpackage= gives short handles —
(defpackage :cave-of-noise (:nicknames #:con) ...)= makes
=con:look= legal. =rename-package= renames or re-nicknames
in place (data and compiled code referencing the old name keep
working — they hold the /package object=, not the string).
=delete-package= removes one that's empty (no other package
uses it); symbols home in it become garbage unless referenced
elsewhere.

Nicknames are for typing comfort at the REPL and in qualified
names — code inside the package never uses them. Renaming is
for a library that changed its name and wants to keep the old
one working.

*** Reading foreign symbols, and keyword reality

The remaining oddity is the bare =:sym= — that's not package
syntax at all. =:sym= reads as the symbol named =SYM= /in
the KEYWORD package/ — keywords are simply the symbols home
in =:keyword=, all externally exported by definition,
self-evaluating because their value slot holds themselves.
The whole story:

#+begin_src lisp
(eq :look (intern "LOOK" :keyword))   ; => T
(symbol-package :look)                ; => #<PACKAGE "KEYWORD">
:look                                 ; self-evaluating
#+end_src

And the inverse notations, rarely needed but met in macros
and printouts: =#:sym= (uninterned, Chapter 23) and
=cl-user::foo= with the =::= reaching internals.

*** Designing a package layout for a system

Chapter 16 split the game into files; the deeper design
question is /packages per file vs one package/. The practical
wisdom:

- One package per /interface=, not per file. =packages.lisp=
  defining one =:cave-of-noise= package is right for an
  application; a library exposing =:mylib= and
  =:mylib.driver= splits when the parts serve different
  readers.
- =(:use :cl)= always; =use= of your own other packages only
  where the dependency is real — every used package's exports
  become part of your package's unqualified namespace, which
  is coupling.
- Export deliberately: the export list is your API contract,
  and =find-symbol= can test it. A symbol exported by
  accident is a commitment to support it.

ASDF's =:package-inferred-system= (Chapter 20) takes the
per-file-package style to its conclusion: one package per
file, named after the file, used by explicit =:use= or
=:import-from= lines at the top. You'll meet it reading
modern libraries; the skill of /reading/ =:import-from=
lists as a dependency declaration is what this chapter buys.

*** Common mistakes

**** Fighting case in package names

(find-symbol "car" :cl)= fails — tables hold upcased names.
(find-symbol (string-upcase "car") :cl)= is the working form;
the same for =intern= and every package function taking a
string designator. The reader upcases names; the table stores
them upcased; string comparisons must too.

**** :use lists as decoration

Long =:use= lists invite conflicts and hide provenance: when
you read =merge= in the code, which package's =merge= is it?
The modern default is (:use :cl)= plus explicit
=:import-from= for the handful of foreign names you want
unqualified — provenance readable at the top of the file.

**** unintern in live code

=unintern= of a symbol that functions still reference leaves
those references holding a symbol findable nowhere by name —
redefinitions then create a /different= symbol with the same
name, and the old code silently diverges from the new.
Unintern only in package-fiddling sessions, never in program
logic.

**** Treating keywords as "just strings"

Keywords are symbols: they intern (a table grows with every
=new= keyword), they never garbage-collect in practice, and
building keyword names from user input and interning them
leaks and invites conflicts. Fixed vocabularies only:
keywords for options, not for data.

**** Confusing single vs double colon

=:pkg:sym= means "external symbol =sym= in =pkg=" — and
signals a reader error if =sym= is internal (not exported).
=:pkg::sym= means "any symbol =sym= in =pkg=, exported or
not". The single colon is the strict form; the double colon
is the escape hatch. Writing =:pkg::look= when =look= is
exported works, but it's a lie: the reader can't tell you
cared, and readers of the code can't tell the symbol's status
without checking. =:pkg:look= documents "this is API"; =:pkg::look=
documents "I'm reaching into internals".

*** Project step: the game's API surface

The game exports whatever Chapter 16 felt like exporting. This
step makes the export list deliberate and testable — an API
you can verify.

The package definition, refined:

#+begin_src lisp
(defpackage :cave-of-noise
  (:use :cl)
  (:shadow #:room)
  (:nicknames #:con)
  (:export :play
           :look :take :drop :inventory
           :save-game :load-game
           :run-command))
#+end_src

Nine exports, all functions a caller would reasonably want.
Deliberately not exported: =*rooms*=, =*item-locations*=,
=*commands*=, =find-room=, =call-command=, =describe-thing=,
=*thing-registry*= — the machinery the exports rest on.
Callers use the API; internals stay internal.

The query that turns "what does my package expose?" from
memory into a REPL operation:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun unexported-api ()
  "Symbols the package defines but does not export."
  (let ((internal nil))
    (do-symbols (s :cave-of-noise)
      (multiple-value-bind (found status)
          (find-symbol (symbol-name s) :cave-of-noise)
        (when (and found (eq status :internal))
          (pushnew s internal))))
    (sort internal #'string< :key #'symbol-name)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(in-package :cl-user)
(con:look)
;; => the current room's description — the nickname works

(length (unexported-api))   ; => a number, probably 30-something
(member 'run-command (unexported-api))   ; => NIL — it's exported
(member '*rooms* (unexported-api))       ; => (*ROOMS*) — internal
#+end_src

Running =unexported-api= after a long development session
routinely surfaces three or four symbols you meant to keep
private but accidentally exported, or symbols you've been
referring to by =::= that should be part of the API. That's
the audit: names that /should= be public are in =:export=;
names that shouldn't be are internal and reachable only by
=::=.

Two more tests worth adding to the audit habit:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun export-documented-p ()
  "Exported symbols lacking documentation — an audit subset."
  (let ((undocumented nil))
    (do-external-symbols (s :cave-of-noise)
      (when (and (fboundp s)
                 (null (documentation s 'function)))
        (push s undocumented)))
    (nreverse undocumented)))
#+end_src

Every exported function should have a docstring — the export
list is the promise, and the docstring is the promise's terms.

And one deliberate import, the shape modern packages prefer:

#+begin_src lisp
;; hypothetical: a future cave-of-noise that uses cl-ppcre
;; for one helper
(defpackage :cave-of-noise
  (:use :cl)
  (:shadow #:room)
  (:import-from :cl-ppcre :scan-to-strings)
  (:nicknames #:con)
  (:export :play
           :look :take :drop :inventory
           :save-game :load-game
           :run-command))
#+end_src

One name imported, by name, at the top — readable as a
dependency, not as a =:use= that would pull the whole of
=cl-ppcre= into the namespace.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 28.1

Predict, then check:

#+begin_example
(find-symbol "LIST" :cl)
(find-symbol "list" :cl)
(eq 'cl:car (intern "CAR" :cl))
(symbol-package 'cl:car)
(symbol-package 'car)              ; evaluated in CL-USER
(multiple-value-list (find-symbol "RUN-COMMAND" :con))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 28.2

Each snippet is wrong or surprising. Say what happens, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(defpackage :p1 (:use :cl) (:export :run))
(in-package :p1)
(defun run () :p1s)
(in-package :cl-user)
(use-package :p1)
run

(defpackage :p2 (:use :cl :p1))
(in-package :p2)
(defun run () :p2s)

(intern "TEMP" :p1)
(unintern 'temp :p1)
p1::temp
#+end_example

**** Exercise 28.3

Write =where-does-name-live=, which takes a string and a
package and returns, as a keyword or =nil=: =:external=,
=:internal=, =:inherited= or =:absent=. Then write
=find-anywhere=, which searches a package /and/ its use-lists
in precedence order and returns the first package in which the
name is findable. What does the order tell you about how the
reader resolves unqualified names?

**** Exercise 28.4

Write =package-diff=, which takes two packages and returns
three values: names external to both, names external to only
the first, names external to only the second. Use it on
=:cl= and =:sb-ext= (or another implementation package you
have) and report the counts in a comment.

**** Exercise 28.5

Write =with-temp-package=, a macro that creates a fresh
package with a =gensym= name, evaluates a body with =*package*=
bound to it, and deletes the package after — body forms intern
there. Demonstrate with a body that defines a function and
calls it. What does this exercise assume about
=delete-package='s requirements, and how does your macro
satisfy them?

**** Exercise 28.6 (project)

Split the game's single package into two:
=:cave-of-noise.data= (for =*rooms*=, =*item-locations*=,
=save-game=/=load-game=) and =:cave-of-noise= (commands,
=play=), with the main package =:use=-ing =:cl= and
=:import-from=-ing only the data names it needs. Which names
did the main package actually need, and what does the final
=:import-from= list tell you about where the real coupling
between the halves lies?

** Part IV review

Part IV was the part that looked inside. Every chapter took
something you had been using on trust and showed you the
mechanism underneath:

- Chapter 17 split *where a name lives in the text* from *when
  it lives at all*: lexical scope, dynamic binding, and the
  earmuffs that announce which one you are looking at.
- Chapter 18 turned the reader from a black box into a
  configurable stage — read syntax, case conversion,
  readtables, and =with-standard-io-syntax= as the rule that
  makes a save file mean the same thing tomorrow.
- Chapter 19 took the one-shot deal of =handler-case= apart and
  rebuilt error handling as =handler-bind=, the condition
  hierarchy, and restarts you choose from rather than catch.
- Chapter 20 named the three times — read, compiled, loaded —
  and made =eval-when= the ordinary consequence it is.
- Chapter 21 turned declarations into promises: a local
  =declare=, a global =declaim=, =(optimize ...)= as a dial,
  and the measurements that show what they buy.
- Chapter 22 made the REPL a workbench: the inspector,
  deliberate =break=s, backtraces read from the bottom,
  redefinition of running code, and what to do when the image
  itself is sick.
- Chapter 23 took a symbol apart into its five parts and drew
  the line that matters: plists for constants about names,
  owned structures for state about things.
- Chapter 24 showed that =values= are not lists —
  =multiple-value-bind= and =multiple-value-call=, the
  single-value contexts, and the =return= trap that silently
  eats the second value.
- Chapter 25 gave closures a job: constructors that run once,
  shared environments as objects, and the habit of asking
  whether state belongs in a closure or in a =defparameter=.
- Chapters 26 and 27 did CLOS twice — the model, then the
  corners: =call-next-method=, the class precedence list,
  =shared-initialize=, =change-class=, and method combination
  as the alternative to inheriting behaviour.
- Chapter 28 opened the symbol table by hand, so that a package
  became an interface you design rather than a =defpackage=
  you copy.

The game, at the end of Part IV, is no longer a handful of
rooms and a list of symbols. Items are =thing= instances
registered under their symbols in =*thing-registry*=, while
=*item-locations*= still says where each one is; =price= is a
generic function whose =+= combination adds base value, kind
and premium without touching the methods already written;
=run-command= returns two values; the command history is a
closure; and the package exports an eight-name API — =play=,
the five commands, =save-game=, =load-game=, =run-command= —
that the rest of the world is allowed to touch. Everything
else is private on purpose.

If any of this feels shaky, the fix is REPL time, not
re-reading: take one chapter's exercises and redo two of them
from scratch. Part V takes this program out of the image —
talking to other systems, generating data instead of
collecting it, reading code you did not write, an executable
you can hand to someone, and the tests that keep it honest.

* Part V: Building Programs

Part IV gave you the mechanisms. When something in Lisp
surprises you now, you have a model to reason from.

Part V puts the pieces together into a real program. It is
shorter than Part IV because the concepts are already in
place; what's new is the practice of /shipping/: talking to
the outside world, processing data streams lazily, reading
code you didn't write, wrapping the game into an executable,
testing it, and knowing where the culture goes next.

The chapters:

- *Chapter 29: Interoperability* — JSON, CSV, shell commands,
  C via CFFI. How the game exchanges data with the world
  outside the image, and why the seam is thin on purpose.
- *Chapter 30: Iterators and Generators* — the closure
  pattern from Chapter 25 applied to streams, with the
  design trade-offs made explicit.
- *Chapter 31: Reading Other People's Lisp* — the audit
  habits, applied first to your own game, then to any
  Quicklisp library.
- *Chapter 32: Building Executables* — SBCL's
  =save-lisp-and-die=, the build script, the cross-platform
  story.
- *Chapter 33: Testing* — what to test, how to wire it
  into the build, and why the flag-vs-condition rule from
  Chapters 24 and 19 shapes what's worth testing.
- *Chapter 34: The Bigger Picture* — libraries, community,
  where to go after this book.

Every chapter uses the game as its running example. By the
end, the game is a binary that runs on a machine that has no
Lisp installed, exports its state to JSON and CSV, and has a
test suite that refuses to let a broken build ship.

** Chapter 29: Interoperability

Lisp has run your whole game so far without leaving the
image — but real programs talk to the world: config files in
JSON, data in CSV, command-line tools, C libraries. This
chapter is the passport control: shelling out, reading and
writing the two data formats you'll meet most, and calling C
— with an honest account of where the walls are.

The chapter's guiding principle: /the seam is thin on
purpose/. Your Lisp code stays Lisp; the boundary converts
between the outside world's shapes and yours in as few
places as possible. Every interop layer, if it's large, is
a sign the boundary wasn't thought through. Done right, the
interop is one file with a handful of functions; done
wrong, it's sprinkled through the codebase.

*** Shelling out: uiop:run-program

=uiop= (bundled with ASDF, loaded everywhere) is the
portable shell:

#+begin_src lisp
(uiop:run-program '("ls" "-l") :output :string)
;; => the ls output, as one string

(uiop:run-program '("date") :output :string)
#+end_src

The argument list is /not/ passed through a shell — no
quoting games, no injection: each string is one argument,
word for word. That's safer than the shell and it's the
first thing to know. Options that matter:

#+begin_src lisp
(uiop:run-program '("grep" "noise" "log.txt")
                  :output :string
                  :error-output :string
                  :ignore-error-status t)

(multiple-value-bind (out err code)
    (uiop:run-program '("false") :output :string
                                 :ignore-error-status t)
  (list out err code))   ; exit status code is the third value
#+end_src

Without :ignore-error-status t=, a non-zero exit signals an
error — Chapter 15's =subprocess-error=, catchable like any
other. For streaming output line by line instead of
collecting it all, the :output :string= version collects;
for large output, stream to a file or use the callback form:

#+begin_src lisp
(uiop:run-program '("tail" "-n" "100" "cave.log")
                  :output :string)
;; just the last 100 lines, collected
#+end_src

The exact keyword for line-by-line processing has drifted
across =uiop= versions — =M-.= on =uiop:run-program= and
read its docstring; this is the reading skill of Chapter 31
applied within the hour. For input, :input "string"= pipes
it to the process's stdin.

*** Reading and writing CSV

CSV looks trivial and has exactly one trap: quoted fields
containing commas. A quick-and-dirty split on commas breaks
on real data; the library exists for the general case. For
/well-behaved/ CSV you produce yourself, Chapter 27's tools
suffice:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun write-csv (rows path)
  "Write ROWS (lists of strings) as naive CSV."
  (with-open-file (out path :direction :output
                            :if-exists :supersede)
    (dolist (row rows)
      (format out "~{~A~^,~}~%" row))))

(write-csv '(("room" "visits")
             ("kitchen" "3")
             ("cellar" "1"))
           "/tmp/visits.csv")
#+end_src

=~{~A~^,~}= is Chapter 12's iteration with =~^= cutting the
final comma. Reading well-behaved CSV back:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun read-csv (path)
  "Read naive CSV — no quoted commas — as a list of lists."
  (with-open-file (in path)
    (loop for line = (read-line in nil nil)
          while line
          collect (uiop:split-string line :separator ","))))
#+end_src

For CSV from the outside world, use the library:
=(ql:quickload :cl-csv)=, whose =(cl-csv:read-csv file)=
handles quoting, embedded newlines, and the rest. The lesson
generalises: /parse formats you produce; library-parse
formats you consume./

The one shared concern worth noting: both naive CSV and cl-csv
write strings, not Lisp objects. If a field can be a number
or a symbol or a list, the writer must decide how to render it
(=princ-to-string=? =write-to-string=? something schema-
specific?) and the reader must decide how to parse it back.
That's the schema problem, and CSV doesn't solve it for you.

*** Reading and writing JSON

JSON's shapes map onto Lisp's — objects to alists (or plists,
or hash tables, library-configurable), arrays to lists,
strings/numbers/booleans to their Lisp twins. The main library
is =jonathan= or =cl-json=; both degrade JSON's =true=/=false=
into =t=/=nil= or keywords, and =null= into =nil= — the
ambiguity you should expect at the edges:

#+begin_src lisp
(ql:quickload :cl-json)

(cl-json:decode-json-from-string
 "{\"room\": \"kitchen\", \"visits\": 3, \"items\": [\"key\"]}")
;; => ((:ROOM . "kitchen") (:VISITS . 3) (:ITEMS "key"))
#+end_src

Note the shape: an /alist with keyword keys/ — Chapter 7's
assoc= with Chapter 23's keyword conventions, ready-made:

#+begin_src lisp
(assoc :visits **)   ; => (:VISITS . 3) — cdr gives 3
#+end_src

Encoding:

#+begin_src lisp
(cl-json:encode-json-to-string
 '((:room . "hall") (:lit . t)))
;; => {"room":"hall","lit":true}
#+end_src

Watch two walls. First, null= vs absence: JSON distinguishes
="k": null= from missing =k=; alist decode makes both =nil= —
if your consumer cares, the library has options, or you handle
the edge yourself. Second, keyword interning: JSON keys become
keywords, so keys from /user data/ intern symbols (Chapter
23's keyword warning — fixed vocabularies only). For a save
file talking to a web client, define the schema first, decode
against it.

jonathan= is the modern alternative and worth knowing:

#+begin_src lisp
(ql:quickload :jonathan)
(jonathan:parse "{\"a\": 1, \"b\": [true, null]}")
;; => #<HASH-TABLE :TEST EQUAL ...>
#+end_src

Jonathan decodes objects to hash tables by default (which
preserves null= distinctly as +null+= — a sentinel — rather
than collapsing it to =nil=), and has options for alist or
plist output. The choice between the two libraries is
habit and use-site; both are good.

*** Calling C: CFFI

The C foreign-function interface is the one Quicklisp
dependency every implementation supports:

#+begin_src lisp
(ql:quickload :cffi)
#+end_src

The pattern, complete for a real (POSIX) function:

#+begin_src lisp
(cffi:define-foreign-library libc
  (:unix (:or "libc.so.6" "libc.so"))
  (t (:default "libc")))

(cffi:use-foreign-library libc)

(cffi:defcfun ("getpid" c-getpid) :int)

(c-getpid)   ; => the process id, as a Lisp integer
#+end_src

Three forms, one each for: /where the library is=
(define-foreign-library=, with per-platform alternatives —
Chapter 18's =#+= done by the library), /load it/
(=use-foreign-library=), and /one C function's signature=
(=defcfun=: C name, Lisp name, return type). Types map the
languages: =:int=, =:double=, =:string= (converted
automatically, copy in each direction), =:pointer= for the
rest.

Structs and pointers, one step deeper:

#+begin_src lisp
(cffi:defcstruct timeval
  (tv-sec :long)
  (tv-usec :long))

(cffi:with-foreign-object (tv '(:struct timeval))
  (setf (cffi:foreign-slot-value tv '(:struct timeval) 'tv-sec) 0)
  (cffi:foreign-slot-value tv '(:struct timeval) 'tv-sec))
;; => 0
#+end_src

=with-foreign-object= is =with-open-file='s shape for raw
memory: allocate on the stack for the body's duration, free
automatically. Memory you allocate yourself with
=cffi:foreign-alloc= you must =foreign-free= — the resource
discipline of every =with-= form, minus the automation.

An honest boundary note: CFFI is /readable= after this
chapter but /serious= C interop (callbacks, memory management
across the boundary, threads) is its own book chapter in other
books. The realistic division of labour: call a C library for
something Lisp lacks (a hardware binding, a fast codec), keep
your logic in Lisp, and treat the FFI layer as a thin, tested
seam — a dozen =defcfun= lines in one =ffi.lisp= file, never
scattered.

*** When to leave the image, and when not to

The game's needs so far have been satisfied in-image, and most
programs' are. The decision guide:

- /Text and data formats/ — Lisp-native for producing;
  libraries for consuming (CSV, JSON above).
- /Other programs' functionality/ — =uiop:run-program= for
  batch tools, but remember every shell-out is a fork and a
  parse boundary: fine for gzip=, wrong for a per-item loop.
- /C libraries/ — only for capability Lisp lacks, and via
  Quicklisp-wrapped bindings first: check Quicklisp for
  =cl-sqlite=, =cl-postgres=, =cffi= bindings before
  writing your own.
- /Performance/ — Chapter 21 first; the FFI is not the escape
  hatch beginners hope: crossing the boundary costs
  conversion, and SBCL compiles Lisp to machine code already.

*** The three failure modes of an interop layer

If you take one mental model from this chapter, take this
one: interop fails in three distinct ways, and each wants a
different defensive shape.

/Format mismatch./ The outside world produces data in a shape
your Lisp didn't expect — a CSV row with the wrong number of
fields, a JSON object missing a required key, a shell command
returning empty stdout. These are /expected= failures in the
sense of Chapter 24: the caller usually knows what to do
(fill defaults, skip the record, try the next file). Defend
them with validate-then-use — the shape =game-state-json=
uses in the project step below.

/Environment failure./ The file doesn't exist, the command
isn't installed, the library isn't on the PATH. These are
Chapter 15 conditions: catchable, and the right answer is
almost always to surface them with a specific condition type
so the caller can distinguish "missing save file" from
"corrupt save file".

/World inconsistency./ The bytes on disk or on the wire
aren't what the format says they should be — a truncated JSON
document, a CSV with an unterminated quote, a response that
times out mid-stream. These are the hardest, because the
format's own parser usually signals a very generic error
that doesn't say /where/ it gave up. The reader's
=reader-error= is the canonical example. Defensive interop
catches these at the seam with a translated, informative
condition — never lets them escape as a bare
=end-of-file= in the middle of some business logic.

The project step's =import-state-json= (Exercise 29.6)
shows all three: it checks field presence (format mismatch),
catches file absence (environment failure), and if the JSON
is corrupt, the cl-json= parser signals a jonathan.error:-
invalid-json= or cl-json-equivalent condition (world
inconsistency) that the caller translates.

*** Common mistakes

**** Building shell command strings

#+begin_example
(uiop:run-program (format nil "grep ~A log.txt" term)
                  :output :string)     ; WRONG: through the shell,
                                       ; injection, quoting bugs
#+end_example

The list form — ='("grep" term "log.txt")= with the term as
/one element= — is the safe call: no shell, no re-parsing.
If your term contains spaces or special characters, the list
form does the right thing; the string form does whatever the
shell's parsing decides.

**** Assuming JSON round-trips

Decode then encode does not always return the original:
=null= collapses, key order changes, =1.0= may become =1=,
whitespace and Unicode escapes may differ. Treat JSON as an
interface format with a schema, not as a serialization of
Lisp data — Chapter 16's =print=/=read= round trip is the
Lisp-native serialization, and it stays inside the image for
a reason.

**** Forgetting exit codes

=run-program= signals on non-zero exit by default, but a
tool that prints an error /and exits zero= will pass
silently. Check the output, not just the absence of an
error, when the tool's failure mode is quiet.

**** CFFI string conversions in loops

Each =:string= conversion copies. Passing a Lisp string into
C per iteration of a million-item loop is a million copies —
restructure to pass buffers, or do the loop in Lisp.

**** Trusting input from outside the image

Every byte read from =read=, =parse-integer=, or a JSON
parser is untrusted until you've validated it. The reader is
particularly dangerous — Chapter 18's =#.= executes arbitrary
Lisp at read time. Bind *read-eval*= to nil= before reading
anything you didn't write yourself.

*** Project step: the game exports its data

A real interop seam, small and useful: the visit report from
Chapter 21 leaves the image as CSV, and the game's current
state exchanges with a hypothetical web front end as JSON.

The CSV export: visit-report= returns a list of (count
room-symbol) lists. That's the data to write.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun export-visits-csv (path)
  "The visit report as CSV."
  (write-csv
   (cons '("room" "visits")
         (mapcar (lambda (entry)
                   (list (string-downcase
                          (symbol-name (second entry)))
                         (write-to-string (first entry))))
                 (visit-report)))
   path))
#+end_src

Note the conversions: the room's /symbol= is downcased to a
string for readability; the count is =write-to-string='d so
the CSV writer receives strings as it expects. CSV is a
string-in, string-out format; the conversion lives here, at
the seam.

The JSON export — and its first draft that the chapter warns
against:

#+begin_src lisp
;; Wrong: tries to serialise the whole state, including
;; non-JSON-able parts.
(defun game-state-json-WRONG ()
  (cl-json:encode-json-to-string
   (game-state)))    ; game-state includes *history*, which
                     ; is a closure — JSON encoding fails
#+end_src

The problem: =game-state= (Chapters 16 and 24) returns a
list containing *item-locations*=, *current-room*='s name,
and =*history*= — a /closure object/. JSON can't encode
closures, symbols, or hash tables directly. Even before it
fails, the shape of =game-state= is Lisp's serialisation
format (Chapter 18's =print=/=read=), not JSON's.

The correct shape — define a schema at the boundary:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun game-state-json ()
  "The exchangeable slice of state, as a JSON string."
  (cl-json:encode-json-to-string
   `((:room . ,(symbol-name (room-name *current-room*)))
     (:items . ,(mapcar #'symbol-name
                        (items-in *item-locations*
                                  (room-name *current-room*))))
     (:carrying . ,(mapcar #'symbol-name
                           (items-here 'player))))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(setf *item-locations* '((lamp . cellar)
                          (key . kitchen)
                          (letter . bedroom)))

(game-state-json)
;; => "{\"room\":\"kitchen\",\"items\":[\"key\"],\"carrying\":[]}"
#+end_src

Notice what the schema /includes/: the room's name as a
string, the items in that room as strings, the player's
carried items as strings. Notice what it /omits/: =*history*=
(closure state, not meaningfully exchangeable), the
=*thing-registry*= (structured metadata, not player state),
=*visits*= (session-local heat map, not relevant to a fresh
consumer), and the /internal representation/ of anything
(symbols become strings; the room's structure is flattened
to its name).

The schema is the seam's contract. Every field in it is a
promise: "this is what the game exports". Every field not in
it is a deliberate omission, not an oversight. When a web
front end arrives, it consumes exactly these three fields;
when a second version of the game arrives, the schema is
what stays compatible.

And the import seam, which the exercises build out:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun import-state-json (json-string)
  "Restore state from a JSON exchange.  Signals on bad input."
  (let* ((alist (cl-json:decode-json-from-string json-string))
         (room-name (cdr (assoc :room alist)))
         (item-names (cdr (assoc :items alist)))
         (carrying (cdr (assoc :carrying alist))))
    ;; validate before touching state:
    (unless (and room-name (stringp room-name))
      (error "Bad import: :room missing or not a string"))
    (unless (listp item-names)
      (error "Bad import: :items missing or not a list"))
    (unless (listp carrying)
      (error "Bad import: :carrying missing or not a list"))
    (let ((room (find-room (intern (string-upcase room-name)))))
      (unless room
        (error "Bad import: room ~S does not exist." room-name))
      ;; validation complete — only now touch state:
      (setf *current-room* room)
      (setf *item-locations*
            (mapcar (lambda (pair)
                      (cons (intern (string-upcase (first pair)))
                            (intern (string-upcase (second pair)))))
                    *item-locations*))
      (dolist (name item-names)
        (setf *item-locations*
              (set-location *item-locations*
                            (intern (string-upcase name))
                            room-name-sym-of room)))
      (t t))))
#+end_src

That draft got tangled in the item-location setting. The
honest version separates the schema fields from the storage:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun import-state-json (json-string)
  "Restore state from a JSON exchange.  Signals on bad input."
  (let* ((alist (cl-json:decode-json-from-string json-string))
         (room-name (cdr (assoc :room alist)))
         (item-names (cdr (assoc :items alist)))
         (carrying (cdr (assoc :carrying alist))))
    ;; validation first — see Exercise 29.6
    (unless (and room-name (stringp room-name))
      (error "Bad import: :room missing or not a string"))
    (unless (and (listp item-names)
                 (every #'stringp item-names))
      (error "Bad import: :items missing or malformed"))
    (unless (and (listp carrying)
                 (every #'stringp carrying))
      (error "Bad import: :carrying missing or malformed"))
    (let ((room (find-room
                 (intern (string-upcase room-name)))))
      (unless room
        (error "Bad import: room ~S does not exist." room-name))
      ;; mutation phase:
      (setf *current-room* room)
      (dolist (name item-names)
        (setf *item-locations*
              (set-location *item-locations*
                            (intern (string-upcase name))
                            (room-name room))))
      (dolist (name carrying)
        (setf *item-locations*
              (set-location *item-locations*
                            (intern (string-upcase name))
                            'player)))
      t)))
#+end_src

The shape is what matters: /validate every field before
mutating any state/. A partially-applied import (state
half-changed, then a validation error) leaves the game in an
inconsistent state that no =handler-case= can recover from
cleanly. Validation-then-mutation makes the mutation phase
atomic in practice: every =error= fires before the first
=setf=.

The intern (string-upcase ...)= pairs are the boundary's
case-and-package conversion, stated plainly where they
belong: in the seam, once. If a future version of the game
changes the symbol names (a rename, a pluralization), this
is the one place to update.

Round-trip check:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun json-roundtrip-test ()
  (let ((json (game-state-json)))
    (setf *current-room* (find-room 'bedroom))
    (setf *item-locations* '((letter . bedroom)))
    (import-state-json json)
    (list (room-name *current-room*)
          (items-in *item-locations* 'kitchen))))
#+end_src

Expected: (KITCHEN (KEY))= — the room and the item back where
the JSON said they were.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 29.1

Predict, then check:

#+begin_example
(uiop:run-program '("echo" "hello world") :output :string)
(uiop:run-program '("echo" "a;b|c") :output :string)

(cl-json:decode-json-from-string "[1, 2, [3]]")
(cl-json:decode-json-from-string "{\"a\": null, \"b\": 0}")
#+end_example

**** Exercise 29.2

Each snippet is wrong or surprising. Say what happens, then fix
it.

#+begin_example
(uiop:run-program (format nil "ls ~A" dir) :output :string)

(cl-json:decode-json-from-string
 "{\"key\": \"x\"}")
(assoc "key" *)

(uiop:run-program '("gzip" "missing.txt") :output :string)

(defun json->state (json)
  (setf *current-room*
        (find-room (cdr (assoc :room json)))))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 29.3

Write =csv->json=, which reads a CSV file (first row headers)
and returns its JSON string form: each row an object keyed
by the headers, numeric-looking fields as numbers. Say in a
comment what your naive number test misses, and when
=cl-csv='s parser becomes necessary.

**** Exercise 29.4

Using =cffi:defcfun= against libc: bind =abs= (one =:int=,
returns =:int=) and =getenv= (one =:string=, returns
=:string=), call both, and explain what =getenv= returning a
=:null-pointer= means and how =cffi:null-pointer-p= guards
it. Why does =abs= need no memory management while a
hypothetical =malloc= binding would?

**** Exercise 29.5

Write =run-and-parse=, which shells out to a command of your
choice that emits one JSON object per line (or simulate one
with a helper script), decodes each line, and returns a list
of the decoded alists. What does the line-at-a-time design
buy over decoding one giant array — and when does it lie?

**** Exercise 29.6 (project)

Add =import-state-json=, the inverse seam to
=game-state-json=: decode, validate each field (room exists
in =*rooms*=, items exist in the registry), and apply it to
the live state — signalling a =bad-import= condition
(Chapter 19) naming the first invalid field when validation
fails, /before/ any state is touched. In a comment: why is
validate-then-apply the only safe order here, and which
Chapter 24 pattern does the "first invalid field" return
value use?

** Chapter 30: Iterators and Generators

Chapter 25 built closures with private state. Chapter 24 made
functions return several values. Put the two together and you
get an idea Lisp handles without any library: a /generator/ —
a function that produces a stream of values one at a time,
remembering where it left off. This chapter builds them by
hand, uses them for lazy processing, and looks at the one
widespread library approach (=series=) — with an honest word
about when ordinary eager lists remain the right answer.

*** Why generators exist

Chapter 10's sequence functions all build complete results.
=mapcar= over a million-element list allocates a million-element
list before returning. =remove-if= walks the whole input
before returning. =reduce= is better — it collapses to one
value — but it still needs the input to exist.

For many problems that's fine: the data is already in memory,
the collection fits, and eager processing reads clearly. But
for three categories of problem, eager processing either
doesn't fit or wastes work:

The generator pattern is the closure-based answer. It isn't
the only answer (SBCL has full call/cc via =cl-cont=, and the
=series= library compiles generator chains to loops), but it's
the one you can build with Chapters 24 and 25 and the one every
Lisp programmer should be able to write from memory.

*** A generator from closures and values

The simplest generator counts:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-counter-gen (start)
  "A generator of successive integers, starting at START."
  (let ((n start))
    (lambda ()
      (values (incf n) t))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter next (make-counter-gen 0))
(multiple-value-list (funcall next)) ; => (1 T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall next)) ; => (2 T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall next)) ; => (3 T)
#+end_src

Two values, following Chapter 24's flag pattern: the item, and
"there is one". The flag is always =t= here — this generator
is infinite — but the protocol requires it, because generators
can end, and the caller must be able to tell.

The protocol, stated precisely:

    Calling it returns /two values/: the next item, and a flag.

    Flag =t= means "an item was produced".

    Flag =nil= means "the generator is exhausted" — the item
    value is undefined (usually =nil=, but callers shouldn't
    read it).

    Once exhausted, a generator stays exhausted — calling it
    again returns =(nil nil)=.

A generator over a list:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-list-gen (list)
  (lambda ()
    (if list
      (values (pop list) t)
      (values nil nil))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *g* (make-list-gen '(a b c)))
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (A T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (B T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (C T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (NIL NIL) — exhausted
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (NIL NIL) — stays exhausted
#+end_src

=pop= on the captured binding — the generator /consumes/ the
list it holds, one =funcall= per step, state held nowhere but
in the closure (Chapter 25's encapsulation, again). The fifth
call is the protocol's stickiness: exhausted generators don't
come back to life.

*** map-gen: processing lazily

Chapter 10's =mapcar= builds the whole result list before you
look at any of it. A generator version transforms one step at
a time:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun map-gen (fn gen)
  "A generator of (FUNCALL FN item) for each item of GEN."
  (lambda ()
    (multiple-value-bind (item ok)
      (funcall gen)
      (if ok
        (values (funcall fn item) t)
        (values nil nil)))))
#+end_src

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *g*
  (map-gen #'1+ (make-list-gen '(1 2 3))))
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (2 T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (3 T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (4 T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*)) ; => (NIL NIL) — exhausted
#+end_src

Composition works because generators are functions returning
functions — the closure-composition habit of Chapter 25, now
with a stream flowing through. Filter too:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun filter-gen (pred gen)
  "A generator of the items of GEN satisfying PRED."
  (lambda ()
    (loop
      (multiple-value-bind (item ok)
        (funcall gen)
        (cond ((not ok) (return (values nil nil)))
          ((funcall pred item) (return (values item t))))))))
#+end_src

The =loop= inside =filter-gen= keeps pulling until it finds a
match or the source runs dry — laziness needs this "pull until
satisfied" shape, and it's the first generator that consumes
/variable/ amounts of its source per step. A source of
1000 odd numbers produces zero output from this filter, and
one call to the filter is 1000 calls to the source.

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter evens
  (filter-gen #'evenp (make-counter-gen 0)))
(multiple-value-list (funcall evens)) ; => (2 T)
(multiple-value-list (funcall evens)) ; => (4 T)
#+end_src

Note the counter starts at 0, =incf= makes the first value 1,
1 is odd (filtered), 2 is even (kept) — so the first =funcall=
on =evens= walks the counter through 1 and 2 before returning.
If the counter were a million-element loop, that first =funcall=
would do two steps. It's doing exactly the work asked.

*** The consumer side: for-each and collect

A generator is useless without consumers. Three, and that's the
complete vocabulary:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun for-each (fn gen)
  "Call FN on every remaining item of GEN; return the count."
  (let ((n 0))
    (loop
      (multiple-value-bind (item ok)
        (funcall gen)
        (if (not ok)
          (return n)
          (progn (funcall fn item) (incf n)))))))

(defun collect-gen (gen)
  "Every remaining item of GEN, as a fresh list."
  (let ((items nil))
    (for-each (lambda (item) (push item items)) gen)
    (nreverse items)))

(defun take-gen (n gen)
  "A generator of at most N items of GEN, then exhausted."
  (lambda ()
    (if (<= n 0)
      (values nil nil)
      (multiple-value-bind (item ok)
        (funcall gen)
        (if ok
          (progn (decf n) (values item t))
          (values nil nil))))))
#+end_src

=for-each= consumes the whole generator, calling =fn= on each
item. =collect-gen= is the bridge back to eager lists — process
