lazily through a pipeline of =map-gen=/=filter-gen=, then
=collect-gen= at the end when you genuinely want everything.
=take-gen= is the opposite of =filter-gen='s "consume until
satisfied" — it stops asking after =n= items, closing the
pipeline from the consumer side.

The pipeline:

#+begin_src lisp
(collect-gen
  (filter-gen (lambda (c) (member c '(#\a #\e #\i #\o #\u)))
    (map-gen #'identity
      (make-list-gen
        (coerce "caveofnoise" 'list)))))
;; => (#\a #\e #\o #\o #\i #\e)
#+end_src

Nothing but one element ever exists at a time between the
stages — for a 12-character string that's a nicety; for a
stream of a million lines it's the whole design.

*** Generators from thin air: infinite sources

=make-counter-gen= is already infinite. The point of lazy
processing is that infinite sources are usable when consumers
can stop early:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun first-few (gen n)
  (collect-gen (take-gen n gen)))

(first-few (filter-gen #'evenp (make-counter-gen 0)) 5)
;; => (2 4 6 8 10) — from an infinite stream, finite work
#+end_src

The =take-gen= closure shares =n= with itself only — a private
countdown. Combined, these small functions give you /streams/
in the full sense: sources (from lists, counters, or eventually
I/O), transformers, takers, collectors.

The test of understanding: trace =(first-few ... 5)=.
=take-gen= produces up to 5. Each =funcall= on =take-gen=
=funcall='s =filter-gen=, which walks =make-counter-gen=
until it finds an even number. The counter goes 1 (skip),
2 (keep), 3 (skip), 4 (keep), ..., through 10. Ten =funcall='s
on the counter, five on the take, one =collect-gen= at the
end. No more work is done than the answer requires.

*** Generators over the game: a file source

The natural game source is the transcript — Chapter 11's
command log — processed as a stream rather than held as a
list. And the natural extension is a generator over a /file/,
streaming lines without holding the file in memory:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-line-gen (path)
  "A generator of lines from the file at PATH, with :close."
  (let ((in (open path :if-does-not-exist nil)))
    (lambda (message)
      (ecase message
        (:next
          (if (null in)
            (values nil nil)
            (multiple-value-bind (line eof-p)
              (read-line in nil nil)
              (if eof-p
                (progn (close in) (setf in nil)
                  (values nil nil))
                (values line t)))))
        (:close
          (when in (close in) (setf in nil)))))))
#+end_src

The generator takes a message — =:next= or =:close= — instead
of zero arguments. That's a protocol extension: most generators
are zero-arg, but resource-owning generators need an explicit
cleanup path. The =:next= message returns the protocol's two
values; =:close= returns nothing useful, but it frees the
resource.

The weakness of the zero-arg version is now visible: a
generator that opened a file and was never driven to exhaustion
would leak its stream until the image died. The =:close=
message is the fix — callers who abandon a generator should
=:close= it. The discipline is the same as =with-open-file='s
(=unwind-protect= guarantees), stated in protocol form
instead of macro form.

The consumer that uses it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun replay-streamed (path)
  "Replay a transcript, stopping at the first unknown command."
  (let ((gen (make-line-gen path)))
    (unwind-protect
      (loop
        (multiple-value-bind (line ok)
          (funcall gen :next)
          (if (not ok)
            (return t)
            (let ((cmd (read-from-string line)))
              (multiple-value-bind (reply known-p)
                (run-command cmd)
                (unless known-p
                  (error "Unknown command: ~A" cmd))
                (format t "~&~A" reply))))))
      (funcall gen :close))))
#+end_src

=unwind-protect= (Chapter 15) guarantees the =:close= on any
exit, error included. The consumer stops early on unknown
commands, and the =:close= still runs. That's the whole
resource discipline in one function.

*** series, briefly

The =series= library (installable via Quicklisp:
=(ql:quickload "series")=) is the compiled-to-eager
alternative: you write /series expressions/ that look like lazy
pipelines, and a code-writing macro phase fuses them into a
single loop with no intermediate structures at all:

#+begin_src lisp
(ql:quickload "series")
(use-package :series)

(collect
  (choose-if #'evenp (scan '(1 2 3 4 5 6))))
;; => (2 4 6) — compiled into one loop, zero intermediate lists
#+end_src

=scan= turns a sequence into a series, =choose-if= is
=filter=, =collect= is the eager sink; the remarkable part is
that =(collect (choose-if ... (scan ...)))= is a single loop
after macroexpansion — the compiler never sees an intermediate
allocation. Series is elegant, standards-adjacent (an appendix
to the standard's process, never ratified), and — honestly —
not where a newcomer should start.

The hand-built generators above teach the model; series
optimises it; ordinary =loop= with =for ... being the elements
of= covers most eager needs. Reach for series when profiling
shows intermediate-list allocation mattering, or when the
pipeline is complex enough that the fused-loop performance
matters. Reach for hand-built generators when the source is
genuinely streaming (files, sockets, incremental computation)
and the pipeline is small enough to reason about directly.

*** Common mistakes

**** funcalling a generator expecting one value

#+begin_example
(mapcar #'funcall (list g1 g2)) ; keeps primaries only
;; any exhaustion flags are dropped — and worse, one funcall
;; per generator advances it once, hidden inside mapcar
#+end_example

Generators have /effect/: every call consumes. Call them in
contexts where the two values are caught, and never
"innocently" (for instance inside =mapcar= over a list of
them).

**** Abandoned generators holding resources

The =make-line-gen= warning restated: a generator that opened
a file and is never driven to exhaustion never closes it.
Either design exhaustion-guaranteed use, add an explicit
=:close= message, or keep the =with-open-file= outside and
generate from an in-memory chunk.

**** Laziness as decoration

Wrapping a 12-element list in =make-list-gen= then
=collect-gen= adds machinery for zero benefit. Lazy processing
pays when: the source is unbounded or expensive, the consumer
may stop early, or intermediate allocation is measured to
matter. Otherwise eager lists are the simpler, correct tool —
and Chapter 10's sequence functions remain the default
vocabulary.

**** Shared generators

One generator handed to two consumers =takes turns= — each
=funcall= advances the single cursor. Two independent views
need two generators (re-call the constructor), or an explicit
=tee= that buffers — which is a real design task, not a freebie.

**** Mixing the flag into the value

A consumer that ignores the flag — =(let ((item (funcall
gen))) ...)= — sees =nil= when the generator is exhausted,
and treats that as "an item whose value is nil". The bug shows
up as =nil= leaking into data that should have ended the
stream. /Every/ =funcall= on a generator should be inside a
=multiple-value-bind= with the flag checked, or inside one of
the standard consumers (=for-each=, =collect-gen=, =take-gen=).

*** Project step: a streaming transcript replay

Chapter 11's =transcript= ran a fixed command list eagerly.
This step streams the log file: read the saved transcript
(one command per line), execute lazily, stop at the first
unknown command — so a long replay can be aborted without
running everything.

First write a transcript file to replay:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun write-transcript (path commands)
  "Write COMMANDS to PATH as a replayable transcript."
  (with-open-file (out path :direction :output
      :if-exists :supersede)
    (dolist (cmd commands)
      (with-standard-io-syntax
        (print cmd out)))))
#+end_src

Then the source:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-transcript-gen (path)
  "A generator of commands from the transcript at PATH."
  (let ((in (open path :if-does-not-exist nil)))
    (lambda (message)
      (ecase message
        (:next
          (if (null in)
            (values nil nil)
            (multiple-value-bind (line eof-p)
              (read-line in nil nil)
              (if (or eof-p (zerop (length line)))
                (progn (close in) (setf in nil)
                  (values nil nil))
                (values (read-from-string line) t)))))
        (:close
          (when in (close in) (setf in nil)))))))
#+end_src

And the consumer:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun replay-streamed (path)
  "Replay a transcript, stopping at the first unknown command."
  (let ((gen (make-transcript-gen path)))
    (unwind-protect
      (loop
        (multiple-value-bind (cmd ok)
          (funcall gen :next)
          (if (not ok)
            (return t)
            (multiple-value-bind (reply known-p)
              (run-command cmd)
              (unless known-p
                (error "Unknown command in transcript: ~A"
                  cmd))
              (format t "~&~A" reply)))))
      (funcall gen :close))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(write-transcript "/tmp/cave-replay.lisp"
  '((look) (take key) (inventory)))
(replay-streamed "/tmp/cave-replay.lisp")
#+end_src

Expected: three replies printed, and the =:close= runs at the
end (whether via the loop's natural exit, an error, or an
interrupt).

The design point the step teaches: once a source is a
generator, /stopping early is free/ — a loop that returns
before exhaustion costs nothing for the unread remainder, and
adding a "replay only the first N" option is a =take-gen=
away. Chapter 11's eager =transcript= had to run every command
because it took a list; the streaming version has no such
obligation.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 30.1

Predict, then check:

#+begin_example
(defparameter *g* (make-list-gen '(a b)))
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*))
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*))
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*))
(multiple-value-list (funcall *g*))

(collect-gen (take-gen 3 (map-gen #'* (make-counter-gen 1))))

(collect-gen (filter-gen (lambda (x) (> x 3))
(take-gen 2 (make-counter-gen 0))))
#+end_example

**** Exercise 30.2

Each snippet is wrong or surprising. Say what happens, then
fix it.

#+begin_example
(defparameter src (make-list-gen '(1 2 3 4)))
(list (collect-gen src) (collect-gen src))

(defun lazy-all-p (pred gen)
(loop
(multiple-value-bind (item ok) (funcall gen)
(if (not ok) (return t)
(unless (funcall pred item) (return nil))))))
;; caller wants to keep checking gen afterwards

(let ((g (make-line-gen "/tmp/big.txt")))
(funcall g)) ; one line read, then g abandoned
#+end_example

**** Exercise 30.3

Write =gen-take-while=, which yields items while =pred= holds
and stops (exhausted) at the first failure — the lazy cousin of
Chapter 10's =remove-if-not= truncation. Then write
=gen-append=, which yields all of one generator, then all of
another. What does =gen-append= of two infinite generators
produce, and what does that tell you about where laziness
ends?

**** Exercise 30.4

Write =zip-gens=, which takes two generators and yields, as
three values per call, their next items together with the ok
flag — the protocol stretch for two-item zips. Then use it
with =zip-gens= over two counters to generate the squares
1, 4, 9, 16, ... /without/ computing each square directly.
(That's the diagonal of a multiplication table.)

**** Exercise 30.5

Write =make-fib-gen=, an infinite generator of Fibonacci
numbers, using two closure bindings and no recursion. Then
write =fib-sum-below=, which sums Fibonacci numbers below a
limit /without collecting any list/ — the sum accumulated
inside a loop that stops early. Which side of the protocol
does the stopping belong to, and why?

**** Exercise 30.6 (project)

Write =make-room-viz-gen=: a generator that yields each room
reachable from the current one, breadth-first, without
repeats, using the closure-owned visited list. Walk it with
=for-each= to print a map — stopping after five rooms with
=take-gen=. In a comment: what does the generator version buy
over Chapter 8's recursive traversal, and what does the visited
state being closure-private make /hard/ (a later caller wanting
to know how far the walk went)?

** Chapter 31: Reading Other People's Lisp

You can now write everything this guide has covered — but a
great deal of your Lisp life will be /reading/: Quicklisp
libraries, other people's systems, your own code from eight
months ago. Library source has conventions this guide hasn't
named, and a shape that's invisible until you've seen it
once. This chapter is the guided tour: how real systems are
laid out, what the comment conventions mean, what that
=declaim= prologue is doing, and how to evaluate an
unfamiliar library before trusting it.

*** Get a real library and open it

The tour needs a specimen. Pick a small, well-regarded one:

#+begin_src lisp
(ql:quickload :split-sequence)
(asdf:system-source-directory :split-sequence)
#+end_src

That last call returns the directory where Quicklisp unpacked
the library. Open it in Emacs (=C-x C-f= then the path, or
=C-x d= for dired — Appendix C covers both). Reading source in
Emacs, =M-.= on any symbol jumps to its definition (with
=slime= or =sly= running, and it works across the whole image
— library code included); =M-,= jumps back. This one key is
most of "reading Lisp in Emacs".

The reason this works: the Lisp image holds the /definitions/
of every function, not just the text. =M-.= asks the running
Lisp where =split-sequence= was defined, and the answer points
at the file and line. That's a completely different lookup
from Emacs's tag-based systems in other languages — the image
is the authority.

*** The shape of a system

Most Quicklisp libraries look like this on disk:

#+begin_example
split-sequence/
├── README.md
├── LICENSE
├── split-sequence.asd
├── package.lisp
├── split-sequence.lisp
└── tests/
└── split-sequence-tests.lisp
#+end_example

The conventions, top to bottom:

    /README/ — one screen: what it does, a usage example. If
    the README has no example, the tests are the example.

    /.asd/ — the =defsystem= from Chapter 16. Read it first:
    the =:components= list /is/ the table of contents, and
    =:depends-on= /is/ what you're pulling in.

    /package.lisp/ — the API contract: one =defpackage=, and
    the =(:export ...)= list is literally every name the
    library promises you.

    /one to three source files/ — most libraries are small.
    split-sequence is one file of ~150 lines. cl-ppcre is
    large; most are not.

The proportion to internalise: /a Common Lisp library is
often smaller than its documentation/. A feature you'd
schedule a sprint for in Java is frequently 200 readable
lines here.

*** Comment conventions: the four (and five) semicolons

The comment heading conventions, standardised by tradition
and used across Quicklisp:

#+begin_src lisp
;;;; split-sequence.lisp
;;;
;;; Chapter-level banner: file title and purpose. Four
;;; semicolons, alone at the top (and section separations).

;;; Section heading — three semicolons, for a group of
;;; definitions that belong together.

(defun split-sequence (delimiter seq &key #| ... |#)
  ;; Two semicolons: comment on the code at the same
  ;; indentation as the form it describes.
  (let ((start 0))
    ;; ... sometimes a paragraph of rationale here
    (do ((i 0 (1+ i)))
      (#| ... |#)
      ;; One semicolon: a note on one line, running text,
      ;; often at the end of the line it explains. ; like this
      #| ... |#)))
#+end_src

So: =;;;;= file banners, =;;;= section headings, =;;= block
comments beside code, =;= end-of-line asides. Emacs's
auto-fill indents them by count; that's why the counts
matter — =M-;= inserts the right one at point. Docstrings
(Chapter 5) carry the /interface/ documentation; comments
carry the /why/, and the four-level convention tells you how
deep in the structure you are.

*** The typical file prologue

Open a random library file and the first twenty lines are
usually a prologue you can now read completely:

#+begin_src lisp
;;;; package.lisp
(defpackage :split-sequence
  (:use :cl)
  (:export :split-sequence :split-sequence-if
    :split-sequence-if-not))

;;;; split-sequence.lisp
(in-package :split-sequence)

(declaim (inline split-sequence-from-end))
(defun split-sequence-from-end (#| ... |#)
  #| ... |#)

(declaim (ftype (function (t t &key (:start index)
        (:test function))
      list)
    split-sequence))
#+end_src

Reading it with this guide's chapters: the =defpackage=
contract (Ch 28), =in-package= (Ch 16), =declaim (inline)=
(Ch 21), =declaim (ftype ...)= declaring a function's /type/ —
the =ftype= declaration you saw listed in Chapter 21 and have
now met in the wild: it tells the compiler and the reader the
function's signature; the compiler checks calls below against
it. None of it is exotic anymore — that is the point of the
tour.

Also common in prologues, now readable:

#+begin_src lisp
(eval-when (:compile-toplevel :load-toplevel :execute)
  #| ... |#) ; Ch 20

(setf (documentation 'split-sequence 'function)
  "The real docstring, set programmatically.") ; Ch 22
#+end_src

*** Docstring and naming idioms

Library code follows habits you can adopt:

    /Exported symbols all have docstrings/, written as complete
    sentences: ="Return a list of subsequences..."=.
    =(documentation 'split-sequence 'function)= reads them.

    /Internal names carry a marker=: =%compute-table=,
    =%parse=, or =nread-= — the percent prefix is the
    community's spelling of "private, don't call". You'll also
    see =package-name-= prefixes on internals from older
    code.

    /Predicates end in =-p= or =-p= of one word= (=emptyp=,
    =boundary-p=); /destructive/ functions are =n=-prefixed or
    warned in the docstring ("may destroy LIST").

    /Keywords for options/, =&key= for everything tweakable,
    defaults in the signature — the interface style this
    guide's =&key= chapter became.

*** Reading macros you'll meet

Chapter 14 covered =defmacro=, but libraries use a small set
of established macro names. Recognising them on sight is the
reading skill:

    =with-...= — a dynamic-scope resource or context (Ch 15's
    =unwind-protect= pattern). =with-open-file= is the model;
    =with-standard-io-syntax= (Ch 18), and every library's
    =with-connection=, =with-database= — same shape.

    =do-...= — an iteration protocol walking something
    (=do-symbols= Ch 28, =do-hash-table= in libraries).

    =define-...= — adds definitions to the system
    (=define-condition=, and libraries' =define-command=,
    =define-hook=) — usually a macro writing =defun= or
    =defmethod= calls for you.

    =assert= family and =check-type= (Ch 21) inline.

    =loop= keywords you haven't used: =minimize=, =maximize=,
    =sum=, =count=, =thereis=, =never=, =always= — Chapter
    11's =loop= clause set, extended; all one vocabulary.

When a library macro is unfamiliar, the reflex is now
mechanical: =macroexpand-1= it at the REPL with sample
arguments (Ch 14) — the expansion is usually plain code you
can read straight.

*** Evaluating a library before trusting it

A checklist, in order of cheapness:

    /License/ — usable in your context?

    /Dependencies/ — =:depends-on= in the =.asd=. Each is
    something you also trust and ship. (uiop and alexandria
    are nearly universal and effectively standard.)

    /Tests/ — does =tests/= exist? Can you run them?
    =(asdf:test-system :split-sequence)= works for systems
    that define an =:test-operation=.

    /Maintenance/ — the dists directory's dates, or the repo:
    stale is not dead in Lisp (stable standard, stable code),
    but a decade of silent issues is a signal.

    /Read the source/ — the final and most Lisp-native step.
    150 lines, the whole story, and =M-.= makes it walkable.

The cultural note to carry: in Lisp, /the source is the
documentation of last resort, and reading it is normal/.
Hyperspec lookup (Appendix B) for the standard, =M-.= for
the library — the reading loop is the same skill at both
layers.

*** Common mistakes

**** Calling internal symbols

Reaching =(library::%parse ...)= because it does what you
need couples you to an implementation detail the author may
rearrange. If the function is genuinely useful, it's a
missing export — fork, patch and ask upstream, or wrap
defensively, but don't =::= in committed code.

**** Copying library code as if it were yours

The LICENSE file governs; MIT/BSD need the copyright notice
preserved in copies. Beyond law: a small copied function
becomes /your/ maintenance burden; a dependency stays the
upstream's.

**** Assuming stasis means abandonment

A library unchanged for years on a stable standard is often
/done/, not dead. The signal to fear isn't silence — it's a
tracker full of ignored runtime bugs.

*** Project step: reading the game as a stranger

The discipline of this chapter, applied to your own system:
open =cave-of-noise= as if you'd never seen it, and audit it
against the tour's checklist:

    Does the =.asd= read as a table of contents?

    Does =packages.lisp= hold the full API contract, and does
    every exported symbol have a docstring?

    Are the comment conventions used consistently — =;;;;=
    file banners present?

    Are internals marked (=%= prefix or naming convention)
    and reachable only via =::=?

    Does =(asdf:test-system :cave-of-noise)= exist? If the
    guide's project has no tests file yet, write the first one:

#+begin_src lisp
;;;; tests.lisp
(defpackage :cave-of-noise/tests
  (:use :cl :cave-of-noise)
  (:import-from :cave-of-noise
                ;; state the tests read and rebind
                #:*item-locations* #:*current-room* #:*history*
                #:*save-file* #:*game-output*
                ;; functions and classes they call
                #:find-room #:item-location #:register-thing
                #:describe-thing #:make-history #:save-game
                #:load-game #:restore-state #:price #:treasure #:thing
                ;; command and data symbols: identity matters
                #:undo #:key #:kitchen #:player)
  (:export :run-tests))

(in-package :cave-of-noise/tests)

(defun run-tests ()
  "A handful of invariants, reported as a list of (name . ok)."
  (let ((results nil))
    (flet ((check (name got want)
          (push (cons name (equal got want)) results)))
      (check "item-location-roundtrip"
        (progn
          (setf item-locations '((key . kitchen)))
          (set-location item-locations 'key 'hallway)
          (item-location 'key))
        'hallway)
      (check "empty-history-safe"
        (progn (funcall history :clear)
          (funcall history :recent))
        nil))
    (nreverse results)))
#+end_src

And wire it into the =.asd=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defsystem :cave-of-noise
  #| ... |#)

(defsystem :cave-of-noise/tests
  :depends-on (:cave-of-noise)
  :components ((:file "tests")))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(load (merge-pathnames "tests.lisp"
    (asdf:system-source-directory :cave-of-noise)))
(cave-of-noise/tests::run-tests)
;; => (("item-location-roundtrip" . T)
;; ("empty-history-safe" . T))
#+end_src

The audit's real deliverable is the habit: after this chapter,
you can't unsee a missing file banner or an undocumented
export — in anyone's code, including the person who wrote it
eight months ago.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 31.1

Load a small library from Quicklisp and, from the source
directory alone, answer: how many source files, what does the
=:components= list order, and what does the export list
promise? Write three sentences — the library's contract —
without reading any implementation file.

**** Exercise 31.2

Each of these prologue lines is met in the wild. Say what each
does and which chapter's machinery it is:

#+begin_example
(declaim (ftype (function (string) list) parse-header))
(eval-when (:compile-toplevel) (pushnew :mylib features))
(declaim (declaration my-pragma))
(setf (documentation 'run 'function) "Run it.")
(in-package :cl-user)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 31.3

Write =system-report=, which takes an ASDF system name and
returns an alist: =:directory=, =:files= (source component
names in order), =:depends-on=, =:license= if the system
object carries it. Which of these does
=asdf:system-source-directory= give you directly, and which
does =asdf:system-registered-p= vs =asdf:find-system=
distinguish?

**** Exercise 31.4

Pick three exported functions from the library of Exercise
31.1 and read their docstrings with =documentation=. Rate
each: example present? argument roles named? failure mode
stated? Then write the docstring your =run-command= /should/
have had, and compare with what it has.

**** Exercise 31.5

Find one macro in the library you don't know.
=macroexpand-1= it with plausible arguments, and read the
expansion. Write three sentences on what it does, and say
whether the expansion used machinery you already know
(with-style cleanup, gensym, ecase dispatch) — and which.

**** Exercise 31.6 (project)

Finish the audit: list every symbol the game defines with
neither a docstring nor an internal-name marker. For each,
either write the docstring or rename to the =%= convention.
Then write =undocumented-exports=, which walks the game's
package and returns the exported symbols lacking function
or variable documentation — the audit as a reusable query,
in the spirit of Chapter 28's =unexported-api=.

** Chapter 32: Building Executables

Everything so far lived inside a Lisp session. For sharing —
or just for the satisfaction of double-clicking — the game
can become a standalone program: a file that runs with no
REPL, no Emacs, no Lisp installed on the other machine (well,
almost — read on).

*** Images: what a Lisp session actually is

A running SBCL session is called an /image/: the whole heap
of definitions, packages, data and state, alive in memory.
Everything the game has been since Chapter 1 is in that
image: the functions, the structures, the closure-captured
state, the package layout, the loaded libraries, the
current value of every earmuffed variable. That's what makes
Lisp development feel like editing a living thing — you
change a function and the running program uses the new
version — and it's what makes deployment an interesting
question. What do you /ship/=?

Two answers, both from the same function: save-lisp-and-die=.

*** Saving an image to disk

sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die= writes the image to disk and
exits the current process. Two forms of the result:

#+begin_src lisp
(sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die "/tmp/game.core"
:toplevel #'main)
#+end_src

This writes a /core file/ — a raw dump of the image, to be
loaded by an SBCL that's already installed:

#+begin_example
sbcl --core /tmp/game.core
#+end_example

The core file is small-ish (a few megabytes for a stripped
image, tens for one with all Quicklisp loaded) but /useless
without SBCL=. Whoever runs it needs to have SBCL installed.

The other form:

#+begin_src lisp
(sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die "game"
:executable t
:toplevel #'main)
#+end_src

This writes an /executable= — an image with SBCL's runtime
bundled in, self-contained:

#+begin_example
./game # on Linux
game.exe # on Windows — SBCL writes the right kind
#+end_example

The executable is large — typically 40 to 80 megabytes —
because the runtime is included. But it runs on any machine
of the same architecture with no Lisp installed. That's the
deployment story: /a Lisp program deployed to a user is an
executable, and the executable is the whole language runtime
plus your code./

Two more save-lisp-and-die= options worth knowing:

    :compression t= — compresses the image with zstd. The
    executable halves in size, at the cost of slightly slower
    startup. On SBCL builds with sb-core-compression= (most
    modern ones), this is worth it.

    :save-runtime-options t= — remembers the runtime options
    SBCL was started with. Useful for tuning the executable's
    runtime (stack size, GC behaviour). Skip it unless you know
    you need it.

*** The entry point: main=

The :toplevel= function is the executable's first mover.
Its job, in order:

    Handle --help=, --version=, and any other early exits.

For the game, that's:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun main ()
  "Entry point for the deployed executable."
  (handler-case
    (progn
      (multiple-value-bind (state loaded-p)
        (load-game)
        (if loaded-p
          (restore-state state)
          (progn
            ;; no save yet — start fresh
            (setf current-room (find-room 'kitchen))
            (setf item-locations nil)
            (setf history (make-history)))))
      (play)
      (save-game) ; best-effort save on clean exit
      (sb-ext:exit :code 0))
    (error (c)
      (format error-output "cave: ~A~%" c)
      (sb-ext:exit :code 1))))
#+end_src

Read it carefully; each piece is doing something.

The multiple-value-bind= on load-game= is Chapter 24's
two-value contract, put to work: loaded-p= tells us whether
there was a save to adopt. When there isn't (first run), we
set up fresh state.

The sb-ext:exit :code 0= at the end is essential. Without
it, SBCL would fall through into its default REPL after
main= returns, and the executable would hang waiting for
input. sb-ext:exit= closes the process immediately.

The two exit codes matter for scripting and CI: 0 means "ran
to completion", non-zero means "something went wrong". A shell
script that launches ./game= can test $?= and know
whether the run succeeded.

*** Building from a script

save-lisp-and-die= is a single Lisp call, but it shouldn't
be typed at the REPL each release. It lives in a build script
that the shell runs. A file build.sh= next to the =.asd=:

#+begin_example
#!/bin/sh
sbcl --noinform --non-interactive \
  --eval '(asdf:load-system "cave-of-noise")' \
  --eval '(sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die "game"
           :executable t :compression t
           :toplevel (function cave-of-noise:main))'
#+end_example

Reading the flags:

    --noinform= — skip SBCL's startup banner.

    --non-interactive= — exit after the --eval= forms
    rather than dropping into a REPL.

The script's structure is a template: load the system, then
save. Every release of every SBCL program looks like this in
its build script. The variations are in what the load=
does (test suite before build? version bump? cleanup?) and
in what options the save= takes (:compression=,
:toplevel=, :executable=).

A minimal Makefile wrapper, for projects that prefer it:

#+begin_example
game: build.sh
sh build.sh

clean:
rm -f game
#+end_example

That's the whole release process: make= builds, make clean= wipes.

One build-script discipline worth stating: build from a
/fresh image=, not from the session you've been developing
in. The build.sh above starts a new SBCL, which has none of
your REPL experiments, redefinitions, or in-progress debugging
state. That's exactly what you want — the executable should
reflect the files, not the session.

*** Command-line arguments

A real program takes arguments: a save file path, a
--version= flag, a command to run without starting the game
loop. SBCL exposes the process's arguments in
sb-ext:*posix-argv*=, a list of strings:

#+begin_src lisp
sb-ext:*posix-argv*
;; => ("/home/you/src/game/game" "--version")
#+end_src

The first element is the program's name (as invoked); the
rest are the arguments, in order. A typical main=
inspection:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun main ()
  "Entry point, with argument handling."
  (let ((args (rest sb-ext:*posix-argv*)))
    (cond
      ((member "--help" args :test #'string=)
        (format t "Usage: game [--help] [--version] [--save-file PATH]~%")
        (sb-ext:exit :code 0))
      ((member "--version" args :test #'string=)
        (format t "The Cave of Noise, version 1.0~%")
        (sb-ext:exit :code 0))
      ((member "--save-file" args :test #'string=)
        (let ((path (second (member "--save-file" args
                  :test #'string=))))
          (when path
            (setf save-file path))))
      ;; fall through to game
      (t nil))
    (run-game))) ; the play/save/exit wrapper from before
#+end_src

Two things:

    :test #'string== on the member= calls — arguments are
    strings, member= defaults to eql=, and "help"= is
    not eql= to "--help"=.

The dispatch shape (search for the flag, act, exit or fall
through) is standard; it doesn't need a library. If the
argument set gets complex — dozens of options, subcommands,
required/optional distinctions — reach for unix-opts= or
clingon= from Quicklisp. For a game with a save-file
option and a version flag, the cond= above is the whole
argument parser.

*** Cross-platform notes

sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die= :executable t= produces a native
executable for the platform it runs on. Build on Linux, get a
Linux binary; build on Windows, get a Windows executable; to
support both, build on both. The build is not cross-platform;
the /source= is.

The Windows-specific concerns:

    The generated =.exe= opens a console window by default.
    That's what you want for a game that reads typed commands;
    for a GUI application, sb-ext:*save-lisp-and-die*= has a
    :windows-subsystem= option (or you use a different
    launcher).

    Absolute paths in the code should use forward slashes
    ("C:/Users/you/save.lisp"=), not backslashes, because
    backslash is the escape character in Lisp strings. The
    filesystem accepts both.

For implementations other than SBCL, sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die=
doesn't exist. CCL has ccl:save-application= (similar
shape), ECL uses ecl:build-program=, CLISP has
ext:saveinitmem=. If you want portability, isolate the
call behind a thin function (Chapter 31's seam discipline):

#+begin_src lisp
(defun save-executable (path toplevel)
  "Save the current image as an executable at PATH."
  #+sbcl (sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die path
    :executable t
    :compression t
    :toplevel toplevel)
  #+ccl (ccl:save-application path :toplevel-function toplevel)
  ;; etc. — one branch per implementation
)
#+end_src

The #+= conditionals (Chapter 18) select at read time, so
only the branch matching your implementation is even a
program in the executable. The other branches never exist as
code.

*** Common mistakes

**** Saving from the wrong image

save-lisp-and-die= dumps /the current process/. If you
call it from your development session — the one you've been
debugging in, with experimental functions, half-loaded
libraries, and a mutated game state — the executable
contains all of that. The build script's fresh SBCL is the
antidote: it starts clean, loads the files, saves. The
executable reflects the source, not your session.

**** Forgetting sb-ext:exit=

An executable whose main= returns falls through to SBCL's
default REPL. The program "hangs" (from the user's
perspective) because it's waiting for input from a REPL they
don't know exists. Every exit path in main= needs
sb-ext:exit=, and every handler needs it too:

#+begin_example
(defun main ()
(play)) ; WRONG — play returns, then REPL
#+end_example

versus:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun main ()
  (play)
  (sb-ext:exit :code 0)) ; right — play returns, program exits
#+end_src

**** Exit code 0 for failure

An executable that exits 0 after a crash is a script's worst
nightmare: the shell thinks everything worked. The
handler-case= in main= exits 1 on error — that's the
distinction scripts rely on. Return 0 for "did what it
should", non-zero for "didn't", and let the caller decide
what to do.

**** Losing the debugger

The handler-case= in main= catches all errors and
translates them to stderr + exit 1. That's right for a
deployed executable — the user isn't a Lisp programmer, and a
full debugger would be noise. But during development, you
/want= the debugger. Two ways to have both:

    Build two executables, one with handler-case=, one
    without, from two =toplevel= functions. main-production=
    wraps, main-development= doesn't.

    Gate on an environment variable or a command-line flag
    (--debug=). main= checks it and chooses.

For the game, main-production= is what ships; during
development, the REPL is more useful than the executable.

**** Forgetting :compression

An uncompressed executable is 60–100 MB; compressed is
30–50. The startup cost of decompression is a fraction of a
second. For distribution, compress; the only reason not to
is if startup latency really matters (it doesn't, for a
game).

*** Project step: release the game

The release, end to end. The build script:

#+begin_example
#!/bin/sh
build.sh — the whole build

set -e # stop on first error
sbcl --noinform --non-interactive
--eval "(asdf:load-system "cave-of-noise")"
--eval "(sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die "game"
:executable t
:compression t
:toplevel #'cave-of-noise:main)"
#+end_example

The main= from earlier — with the argument handling, the
load-game check, the play-save-exit sequence — is what
:toplevel= points at.

The release steps, in order:

    sh build.sh= builds ./game=.

That's a minimal release test: the executable exists, plays,
saves, reloads, handles flags. The full test suite
(Chapter 33) checks more, but this sequence catches the
common deployment failures — wrong toplevel, missing save
directory, unhandled error — before the executable ships.

The final polish — a version string and a welcome message:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter version "1.0")

(defun welcome ()
  "Print the game's opening banner."
  (format t "The Cave of Noise, version ~A.~%" version)
  (format t "Type (look) to begin, (help) for commands,~%")
  (format t "(quit) to exit.~%"))
#+end_src

And play= calls welcome= at its start. Version in one
place, bumped per release, visible to players: the small
discipline that separates programs from projects. Chapter 33's
tests will read version= too, so the version the game
reports and the version the tests exercise are the same
string.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 32.1

Build the smallest possible executable: a toplevel that prints
"hello from a saved image" and exits 0. Build it with a script
(or the two forms from a running SBCL), run it from a shell,
and record its file size in a comment. Compare with
(defparameter *payload* (make-array 1000000 :initial-element 0))=
defined before saving: how much did the payload change the
size, and why so little?

**** Exercise 32.2

Write a main= that takes --help=, --version= and

instead of the kitchen. Test all three from a shell. What
does --room attic= do when attic= doesn't exist, and
which chapter's tool handles it?

**** Exercise 32.3

Each snippet is wrong. Say what happens, then fix it.

#+begin_example
(sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die "game" :toplevel #'play)

(defun main ()
(format t "usage: ...")
;; and nothing else, invoked with --help
)

(sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die "x" :executable t :toplevel #'main)
(quit)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 32.4

Write a =build.sh= that also runs the test suite before
building. Add a flag to skip tests for a quick build
(--skip-tests= argument). Write the shell script and the
main= changes needed to test it.

**** Exercise 32.5

Add a =--replay FILE= flag to main= that reads a transcript
from FILE and replays it through replay-streamed= (Chapter

    instead of starting the interactive loop. Exit 0 on
    success, 1 on the first unknown command. Why does this flag
    belong in main= and not in play=?

**** Exercise 32.6 (project)

Wrap save-lisp-and-die= in a function =build-executable= that
takes the output path, the toplevel function, and a keyword
:compress= (default =t=). Show how the function can be called
from a fresh SBCL (=sbcl --eval '(build-executable "game"
'"'cave-of-noise:main)=) so build.sh= can be reduced to one
--eval=. What does this buy over the direct
save-lisp-and-die= call in the script, and what does it cost?

    *version* is "1.0"

    *history* is a closure (Chapter 25)

    *rooms* is a list of room= structures (Chapter 13)

    *thing-registry* is a hash table (Chapter 26)

** Chapter 33: Testing

A program you can't test is a program you can't change. The
game has grown for thirty-two chapters — features added,
bugs fixed, structures converted from alists to structs to
closures — and every one of those changes was an opportunity
for a regression. This chapter is about catching them before
your players do.

Lisp's testing story is unusually simple, and unusually
native. Tests are functions; assertions are conditions;
running tests is calling functions. No JUnit-style
framework ceremony, no annotations, no build-tool
integration needed to start. The two most-used libraries —
=fiveam= and =prove= — add structure for large test suites,
but the hand-rolled version from Chapter 31's project step
is already a test framework in miniature, and for the game's
size it's enough to build on.

*** Why test at all

The question a beginner asks — "why write a test when I can
just run the code?" — has a clear answer once the program
grows past a page: /you can run the code once, but not a
thousand times/. Tests are the answer to "does this still
work?" asked without re-running everything manually.

The game has three specific reasons to test:

- /State transitions./ take= modifies *item-locations*=;
  =undo= restores from history. Every change to either
  could break the other. A test that takes an item, undoes,
  and checks the item is back in the room is cheap and
  catches a whole class of regressions.

- /Format round-trips./ =save-game= and =load-game= are
  inverse operations; =game-state-json= and
  =import-state-json= are another pair. Testing that
  round trip in isolation catches most persistence bugs.

- /Cross-chapter invariants./ The =thing-registry= from
  Chapter 26 holds descriptions; the =item-locations= from
  Chapter 7 holds positions. A regression in one — an item
  registered but not tracked, or tracked but not registered
  — is the kind of bug that passes casual play but shows up
  in-game eventually. A test that lists registered things
  and asserts each has a location is a one-line check of
  the invariant.

The three reasons have a common shape: /any invariant you
can state in a sentence is a test waiting to be written/.

*** The minimal test framework

Chapter 31's =run-tests= is already the pattern:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun run-tests ()
  "A handful of invariants, reported as a list of (name . ok)."
  (let ((results nil))
    (flet ((check (name got want)
             (push (cons name (equal got want)) results)))
      (check "item-location-roundtrip"
             (progn
               (setf *item-locations* '((key . kitchen)))
               (set-location *item-locations* 'key 'hallway)
               (item-location 'key))
             'hallway)
      (check "empty-history-safe"
             (progn (funcall *history* :clear)
                    (funcall *history* :recent))
             nil))
    (nreverse results)))
#+end_src

Two design choices in the =check= helper are worth
unpacking, because they determine what the suite can do:

/Checks return =(name . ok)= pairs, not signalling errors.=
A failing check is data, not an exception. That lets
=run-tests= run every check regardless of failures and
report them all, instead of stopping at the first — the
behaviour you want when a change broke three things at once
and you want to see the whole list.

/The =check= helper is a =flet= local, not a global.=
Test assertions are local to the test function; making them
global means test code and library code share a namespace,
and a bad test helper can shadow the very functions it's
testing. =flet= keeps the scope tight.

Both choices scale: =fiveam= adds assertion types (=is-true=,
=is-false=, =is-equal=, =is-error=), assertion messages,
fixtures, and a runner, but the underlying model — a test is
a function; a check is a comparison; results are collected
— is exactly what the hand-rolled version shows.

*** What to test

The first discipline: /test behaviour through the public
interface, not internals/. Tests that call =items-here=
directly are testing the machinery; tests that call
run-command '(take key)= and check the reply are testing
the /behaviour= players see. The two kinds of tests have
different stability profiles:

- Internal tests (call =items-here=, =set-location=) break
  when the internals change. They're cheap to write, useful
  during development of one function, and should be
  discarded or moved into a "unit test" file when the
  function stabilises.
- Behaviour tests (call run-command '(take key)=,
  =save-game=, =look=) are what players exercise. They
  break when the /contract= changes, which is exactly when
  you want to be notified.

For the game, the behaviour test set is short:

#+begin_src lisp
;; Behaviour: taking an item moves it from the room to the player.
;; Behaviour: undo restores the item to the room.
;; Behaviour: save/load round-trips the state.
;; Behaviour: unknown commands return a reply without signalling.
;; Behaviour: examine of a registered thing returns its description.
;; Behaviour: examine of an unregistered thing returns a generic line.
;; Behaviour: price of a treasure returns its value.
;; Behaviour: inventory lists carried items alphabetically.
#+end_src

Eight tests, each one a sentence. Run them after every
change; if all eight pass, the game's basics are intact.
That's the /regression gate/= — not the full specification
of the game, but the subset of it whose breakage would
surprise you.

*** The flag-vs-condition rule applied to tests

The flag-vs-condition distinction from Chapters 24 and 19
determines which behaviours /can/= be tested cleanly and
which need special handling:

- /Flag-returning functions/ — =run-command= (two values:
  reply, known-p), =save-game= (success-p, path),
  =load-game= (state, loaded-p) — are tested by checking
  both values. (multiple-value-bind (reply known-p)
  (run-command '(dance)) ...)= lets the test assert "reply
  is a string, known-p is nil" in one go.

- /Condition-signalling functions/ — =safe-divide= (if you
  wired the Chapter 19 version), =class-change-drops-slots=
  — are tested with handler-case= inside the test:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun expect-condition (condition-type thunk)
  "Run THUNK, expecting CONDITION-TYPE.  Returns T if signalled."
  (handler-case (progn (funcall thunk) nil)
    (condition-type (c) (declare (ignore c)) t)))
#+end_src

The (progn ... nil)= is the trick: if the thunk returns
normally, the progn= returns nil= and the test fails
because the expected condition never fired. If the thunk
signals the expected type, the handler runs and returns
=t=. If the thunk signals a /different= type, the handler
doesn't match, and the condition propagates — the test
fails with the /wrong= condition visible, not with a
misleading "expected condition, got value".

- /Value-returning functions/ — =item-names=, =item-phrase=,
  comma-list= — are the easy case: call with an input,
  compare with =equal=.

The rule the three cases encode: /test the function's own
contract, not what a caller does with it/. A flag-tester
asserts the flag; a condition-tester asserts the
condition; a value-tester asserts the value. The
mismatch — testing a flag-returning function by expecting a
condition — is a common beginner error, and the test's
diagnostic is baffling when it fires.

*** Test doubles: closures over state

Some tests need to control what a function sees without
changing the game's actual state. The *game-output*= variable
from Chapter 17 is exactly this kind of seam:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun run-command-output (command)
  "Run COMMAND with output captured; return the string."
  (with-output-to-string (s)
    (let ((*game-output* s))
      (run-command command))))
#+end_src

=run-command-output= is a test helper — not part of the
game's API, just a convenience for tests that care about
/what was printed= rather than /what was returned=. Testing
=look= through it:

#+begin_src lisp
(check "look-mentions-room"
       (search "kitchen"
               (string-downcase
                (run-command-output '(look))))
       0)   ; SEARCH returns 0 when the substring is at the start
#+end_src

The =search= check is interesting: it returns the index of
the match, so any result other than =nil= means "found".
The test asserts =0= specifically because =look= should
/start= with the room name; a =search= returning 5 would
mean the name appears but not where the player expects. The
assertion is stricter than "contains", which is the point —
tests can encode formatting expectations that a
=stringp= check can't.

The general pattern: closures (Chapter 25) make test doubles
free. A function that reads from *game-output*= can be
tested by binding it to a string stream; a function that
reads from *save-file*= can be tested by binding it to
=/tmp/test-save=. No mocking framework needed; the dynamic
variables are the seams, and =let= is the tool.

*** Wiring tests into the build

Chapter 31's project step wired a small suite into the
game's ASDF system. This chapter strengthens it to a real
gate:

#+begin_src lisp
;; tests.asd
(defsystem "cave-of-noise/tests"
  :depends-on ("cave-of-noise")
  :components ((:file "tests"))
  :perform (asdf:test-op (op system)
             (declare (ignore op system))
             (unless (every #'cdr (cave-of-noise/tests::run-tests))
               (error "Test suite failed."))))
#+end_src

The =:perform= clause on =asdf:test-op= is what =asdf:test-system=
calls. Running it:

#+begin_src lisp
(asdf:test-system "cave-of-noise/tests")
;; => either silent success, or an error naming the failed tests
#+end_src

The build script's test step becomes:

#+begin_example
sbcl --noinform --non-interactive \
     --eval "(asdf:test-system \"cave-of-noise/tests\")" \
     --eval "(asdf:load-system \"cave-of-noise\")" \
     --eval "(cave-of-noise::build-executable ...)"
#+end_example

Three =--eval= forms: test, load, build. A failed test
signals from =test-system=, SBCL's =--non-interactive= mode
propagates the error as a non-zero exit, and =set -e= in the
shell stops the script before the build runs. Three layers
of gate, one intent: /no release with a red test/.

The three-layer structure is worth noting as a pattern. Each
layer is independent (the shell's =set -e= doesn't know
about the tests; the tests don't know about the build), and
each is necessary — take any one away and the whole
sequence can ship a broken binary. That's the normal shape
for build automation: many small checks, each doing its
part.

*** Growing the suite across chapters

The natural discipline is /one test per bug fixed/. When
play reveals a bug — a command that returns the wrong
string, a save that loses a field, an undo that leaves
state half-restored — the fix is two changes:

1. Fix the code.
2. Add a test that would have caught the bug.

The second change is what makes the test suite grow into
something useful. It's also the hardest habit to form,
because the bug is already fixed and the test feels like
afterthought. But the test's purpose isn't to prove the bug
exists /now/= — it's to ensure the bug /doesn't exist
again/=, after some future change. And every future change
to that part of the code is now protected.

The game's test suite grows the same way. If Chapter 24's
two-valued =run-command= broke something that a caller
relied on, the test that catches it would be added then.
The suite isn't a chapter's deliverable; it's the shape
the codebase takes as it grows.

*** When to reach for fiveam

The hand-rolled framework from Chapter 31 is enough for
under about thirty tests. Beyond that, three things start
to matter:

- /Better assertion messages/. fiveam='s is-equal= prints
  a diff when it fails; the =check= helper above prints
  "got vs want" only if you wrote it that way. The
  ergonomics of failure matter when a test suite has fifty
  tests and you're reading the failing one.

- /Test discovery and organisation/. =fiveam= groups tests
  into suites, runs subsets (=run! 'my-suite=), and
  reports summaries. The hand-rolled version is one list of
  (name . ok)= pairs; you organise manually.

- /Fixtures/. "Set up this state before every test in this
  suite" is a common need once tests start sharing setup
  code. =fiveam= has fixtures; the hand-rolled version
  would grow a macro for it, which is the sign you're
  building fiveam badly.

The transition point is when the test suite has its own
structure — multiple files, shared setup, several suites.
For the game at thirty-two tests, hand-rolled is fine. For
the game at fifty, reach for the library.

The run-tests= shape survives the transition: fiveam='s
run!= returns a similar result, and the build script's
check is the same. The framework is a /tool=; the discipline
(test the contract, one test per bug, green before
shipping) is the /habit=, and it transfers.

*** Common mistakes

**** Testing internals

A test that calls (items-here 'kitchen)= directly is testing
items-here=; a test that calls run-command '(look)= and
searches the reply is testing =look=. The second survives
=items-here= being renamed, rewritten, or moved to another
package. The first breaks every time. Test through the
interface; save internal tests for /during= the development
of one function, then delete them.

**** Tests that depend on order

#+begin_example
(check "take-key" ...)     ; mutates *item-locations*
(check "undo-take" ...)    ; assumes the previous check ran
#+end_example

If =take-key= is skipped or =undo-take= is run alone,
=undo-take= fails. The fix is to set up the state each
test needs at the start of each check — a =let= or
=setf= reset — so each check is independent. Tests that
depend on order are tests that pass in the suite and fail
when debugged individually, which is the worst kind of
debugging.

**** Silent test failures

A check that returns (name . t)= when the value is wrong —
because the comparison used =eq= where =equal= was needed,
or because the "value" was a side-effect-producing form
that always returns =t= — is worse than no test. The
symptom: the suite says "green" and the bug ships anyway.
check='s (equal got want)= is doing the right
comparison; using =eq= on strings or lists is the classic
mistake (Chapter 10's four equalities, in a test context).

The disproof: deliberately break the code a test covers,
run the test, watch it fail. If it doesn't fail, the test
isn't testing what you thought. Every test suite should
have at least one deliberate-break session where the
developer /sees= the tests do their job.

**** Trusting the debugger during tests

Running a test in the REPL, watching it enter the debugger
on failure, and manually inspecting state — that's normal
during test development. But the suite as run by
=asdf:test-system= must not enter the debugger. A test that
signals an error should be /caught= by the framework or by
an explicit handler-case= in the test; letting the debugger
open turns "test suite" into "interactive test session", and
the build script hangs waiting for input.

The =check=-based framework is debugger-safe: assertions
compare values and return pairs, no error signalling. Tests
that use =expect-condition= are debugger-safe for the
/expected= condition; an /unexpected= condition is the
problem the framework must catch. Wiring =run-tests= into
a handler-case= in test-op=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun run-tests-safely ()
  "Run the test suite; signal on any unexpected condition."
  (handler-case (run-tests)
    (error (c)
      (list (cons "test-runner-error"
                  (format nil "Test runner crashed: ~A" c))))))
#+end_src

— so the framework catches its own failures and reports them
as (name . nil)= pairs, the same as test failures.

**** Over-testing

Tests have a cost: writing them, maintaining them, waiting
for them. A test that asserts "string-upcase of "a" is "A""
is testing the language, not your code. A test that asserts
(1+ 2)= is 3= is testing SBCL. Tests are for /your=
decisions — the invariants you chose — not for the
language's. Delete any test you can't imagine ever failing.

**** Over-mocking

The game's tests use the game's actual functions with
=let=-bound variables. That's the right amount of
substitution: the functions are the game; the variables are
the configuration. A test that replaces =run-command= with
a stub to test =play= isn't testing =play= — it's testing
the stub. Replace configuration (variables) when you need
control; never replace the code under test.

*** Project step: the game's test suite

Write the eight behaviour tests, wire them into the ASDF
system, and make the build script refuse to ship without
them. The whole file:

#+begin_src lisp
;;;; tests.lisp
;;;;
;;;; The game's behaviour test suite.  Eight tests, each
;;;; asserting one sentence of the game's contract.

(defpackage :cave-of-noise/tests
  (:use :cl :cave-of-noise)
  (:import-from :cave-of-noise
                ;; state the tests read and rebind
                #:*item-locations* #:*current-room* #:*history*
                #:*save-file* #:*game-output*
                ;; functions and classes they call
                #:find-room #:item-location #:register-thing
                #:describe-thing #:make-history #:save-game
                #:load-game #:restore-state #:price #:treasure #:thing
                ;; command and data symbols: identity matters
                #:undo #:key #:kitchen #:player)
  (:export :run-tests))

(in-package :cave-of-noise/tests)

(defun run-tests ()
  "Run every check.  Returns (name . ok) pairs."
  (let ((results nil))
    (flet ((check (name got want)
             (push (cons name (equal got want)) results)))

      (check "take-moves-item"
             (progn
               (setf *item-locations* '((key . kitchen)))
               (setf *current-room*
                     (find-room 'kitchen))
               (let ((*game-output* (make-string-output-stream)))
                 (run-command '(take key))
                 (item-location 'key)))
             'player)

      (check "undo-restores-item"
             (progn
               (setf *item-locations* '((key . kitchen)))
               (setf *current-room*
                     (find-room 'kitchen))
               (let ((*history* (make-history)))
                 (run-command '(take key))
                 (run-command '(undo))
                 (item-location 'key)))
             'kitchen)

      (check "save-load-roundtrip"
             (let ((*save-file* "/tmp/test-roundtrip.lisp")
                   (*item-locations*
                    '((key . kitchen))))
               (setf *current-room*
                     (find-room 'kitchen))
               (save-game)
               (setf *item-locations* nil)
               (multiple-value-bind (state loaded-p)
                   (load-game)
                 (and loaded-p
                      (progn
                        (restore-state state)
                        (equal *item-locations*
                               '((key . kitchen)))))))
             t)

      (check "unknown-command-returns"
             (multiple-value-bind (reply known-p)
                 (run-command '(xyzzy))
               (declare (ignore reply))
               known-p)
             nil)

      (check "examine-registered-thing"
             (progn
               (register-thing
                'probe (make-instance 'thing
                                      :name 'probe
                                      :description "a test probe"))
               (search "test probe"
                       (multiple-value-bind (reply known-p)
                           (run-command '(examine probe))
                         (declare (ignore known-p))
                         reply)))
             (search "test probe"
                     (describe-thing
                      (make-instance 'thing
                                     :name 'probe
                                     :description "a test probe"))))

      (check "examine-unregistered-thing"
             (multiple-value-bind (reply known-p)
                 (run-command '(examine nonexistent))
               (declare (ignore known-p))
               (not (null (search "nothing special"
                                  (string-downcase reply)))))
             t)

      (check "price-of-treasure"
             ;; 42 from the treasure method, plus 1 from thing's
             (price (make-instance 'treasure
                                   :name 'test
                                   :value 42))
             43)

      (check "inventory-alphabetical"
             (progn
               (setf *item-locations*
                     '((zebra . player)
                       (apple . player)
                       (mango . player)))
               (multiple-value-bind (reply known-p)
                   (run-command '(inventory))
                 (declare (ignore known-p))
                 (not (null (search "apple, mango and zebra"
                                    (string-downcase reply))))))
             t))

    (nreverse results)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(cave-of-noise/tests::run-tests)
;; => (("take-moves-item" . T)
;;     ("undo-restores-item" . T)
;;     ("save-load-roundtrip" . T)
;;     ("unknown-command-returns" . T)
;;     ("examine-registered-thing" . T)
;;     ("examine-unregistered-thing" . T)
;;     ("price-of-treasure" . T)
;;     ("inventory-alphabetical" . T))
#+end_src

The eight checks, and what each is doing:

- =take-moves-item= — Chapter 7's invariant, tested through
  Chapter 9's command dispatch.
- =undo-restores-item= — Chapter 14's invariant, using the
  Chapter 25 closure-based history.
- =save-load-roundtrip= — Chapters 16/18/24's round trip,
  tested with the Chapter 24 two-valued =load-game=.
- =unknown-command-returns= — Chapter 24's contract: the
  second value is =nil= for unknown commands.
- =examine-registered-thing= — Chapter 26's CLOS dispatch,
  tested through Chapter 9's command interface.
- =examine-unregistered-thing= — the fallback path in
  =examine=.
- =price-of-treasure= — Chapter 27's method combination,
  tested directly at the generic function.
- =inventory-alphabetical= — Chapter 10's sort, tested at
  the player-visible output.

Each =progn= inside the check /sets up= the state the
check needs, so no check depends on another. That's the
"independent tests" discipline from the common mistakes
list, applied.

The ASDF system:

#+begin_src lisp
(defsystem "cave-of-noise/tests"
  :depends-on ("cave-of-noise")
  :components ((:file "tests"))
  :perform (asdf:test-op (op system)
             (declare (ignore op system))
             (let ((results
                     (cave-of-noise/tests::run-tests)))
               (unless (every #'cdr results)
                 (error "Test suite failed: ~{~A~^, ~}"
                        (remove-if #'cdr results))))))
#+end_src

The :perform= clause on test-op= is what asdf:test-system=
invokes. The check: if any =cdr= is nil, the failure message
lists /which= tests failed — same shape as =check='s
=(name . ok)= pairs, one line of code, and the caller sees
the failure names, not just "tests failed".

And the build script (already shown in Chapter 32's Exercise
32.4, but for completeness):

#+begin_example
#!/bin/sh
set -e
sbcl --noinform --non-interactive \
     --eval "(asdf:test-system \"cave-of-noise/tests\")" \
     --eval "(asdf:load-system \"cave-of-noise\")" \
     --eval "(cave-of-noise::build-executable \
              \"game\" #'cave-of-noise:main)"
#+end_example

Test, load, build. Every step is a gate; the script stops on
the first failure. That's the release discipline: a build
that can't fail the test suite isn't a build you can trust.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 33.1

Add three more checks to =run-tests=, one per category from
the chapter: a state transition (=drop=), a format
round-trip (=game-state-json= / =import-state-json=, if
you did Chapter 29's project step), and a cross-chapter
invariant (every registered thing is also in
*item-locations*= somewhere, OR carried OR both, OR is a
'defunct' thing kept for later — pick the invariant that
holds for your game, and defend the choice in a comment).

**** Exercise 33.2

Each of these tests is broken. Say what's wrong, then fix
it.

#+begin_example
(defun check (name got want)
  (push (cons name (eq got want)) results))

(defun run-tests ()
  (let ((results nil))
    (flet ((check (name got want)
             (push (cons name (equal got want)) results)))
      (check "string-upcase-of-a"
             (string-upcase "a") "A")
      (check "plus-is-associative"
             (+ 1 (+ 2 3)) (+ (+ 1 2) 3))
      (check "test-failure-detected"
             (handler-case (error "should fail")
               (error () :caught))
             :caught))))

(check "test-failure-detected" (error "should fail") nil)
#+end_example

**** Exercise 33.3

Write =fiveam=-style =is-equal=, =is-true=, =is-error=, and
=is-values= macros (or functions, for the first two) that a
=fiveam=-style test can call. The macro versions add
assertion messages; the function versions just return
(name . ok) pairs. What does the macro version buy, and
what does it cost?

**** Exercise 33.4

Write =run-tests-with-filter=, which takes a list of test
names and runs only those. What does this let you do that
the full suite doesn't, and when does the answer change (as
the suite grows, as the code matures)?

**** Exercise 33.5

Take one of the game's bugs — either a real one you've found,
or the deliberate =item-names= bug from Chapter 22's
Exercise 22.7 — and add a test that catches it. Deliberately
break the code, run the test, confirm it fails. Then fix the
code, run again, confirm it passes. That's the discipline
the chapter describes: /the test proves itself by failing/.

**** Exercise 33.6 (project)

Convert the game's test suite to =fiveam= (or =rove=, or
=prove= — pick one). The conversion should be mechanical:
each =check= becomes an =is=/=is-equal= form, each test
becomes a (test NAME "docstring" ...)= form, and the
test-op= calls (fiveam:run! 'cave-of-noise/tests)= instead
of =run-tests=. What did the framework buy you — be honest:
if the answer is "nothing" for a suite this small, that's a
useful finding too.

** Chapter 34: The Bigger Picture

The game is built, tested, and shippable. This chapter is the
map of what comes next — the ecosystem you can draw from, the
references you'll consult, the community you'll join, and the
final honest assessment of what "thinking in Lisp" actually
means.

*** Quicklisp, properly

You've loaded libraries throughout — =fiveam=, =cl-json=,
=cl-csv=, =cffi=. Quicklisp's job is larger than "download
libraries": it's the curated distribution, updated monthly,
with a stability guarantee that makes the ecosystem work.
The commands:

#+begin_src lisp
(ql:system-apropos "json")   ; find systems by substring
(ql:quickload :cl-json)      ; load (and download) one
(ql:who-depends-on :cl-json) ; who uses it?
(ql:update-dist "quicklisp")  ; update the distro index
(ql:update-all-dists)        ; update installed libraries
#+end_src

Two habits worth adopting early:

- /Pin your dependencies./ =quickload= fetches the current
  distro; months later, the same call fetches newer
  versions. When a project matters, record which distro
  version it used — Quicklisp's =ql-dist= can lock to a
  specific distro, and for shared work the heavy-duty tool
  is =Qlot=, which pins per-project versions in a file.
  Start by simply noting the distro version in your
  project's README.
- /Prefer many small libraries over one framework./ Lisp
  culture composes focused libraries rather than adopting
  large all-embracing ones — the same instinct as the Unix
  tools, and the reason Quicklisp has a dozen JSON
  libraries instead of one.

*** The ecosystem, by category

A short field guide, by task. All load with =ql:quickload=.
The list isn't exhaustive — it's the "first choice" by
category, the libraries you'd find in most modern Lisp
projects.

| Task                  | Library        | Note                                    |
|-----------------------+----------------+-----------------------------------------|
| Files and paths       | uiop           | ships with ASDF; portable path & OS ops |
| Command-line args     | unix-opts, clingon | typed options, subcommands, help    |
| JSON                  | jonathan, cl-json | read and write JSON                  |
| CSV                   | cl-csv         | quoted fields, embedded newlines        |
| Regular expressions   | cl-ppcre       | compiled to Lisp code; fast             |
| Web servers           | hunchentoot, clack | the veteran / the server abstraction |
| Web framework         | caveman2       | routing, templates, sessions on clack   |
| HTTP client           | dexador, drakma | requests with headers, forms, SSL      |
| HTML templating       | djula, cl-who  | Django-style / s-expressions            |
| Databases (Postgres)  | postmodern, mito | native protocol / ORM                 |
| Databases (SQLite)    | cl-sqlite      | embedded, file-based                    |
| Threads               | bordeaux-threads, lparallel | thin portable / parallel map-car |
| Dates and times       | local-time     | timestamps, formatting, arithmetic      |
| Iteration             | iterate, series | loop alternative / fused pipelines     |
| Pattern matching      | trivia, optima | destructuring =match=                   |
| Common data structures | fset, cl-containers | immutable sets / heaps, queues   |
| Utility belt          | alexandria     | small correct helpers, everywhere       |

The pattern across all categories: small, composable,
standard-loving libraries rather than frameworks that own
your program. You assemble; nothing assembles you.

Two of these deserve a longer look, because they show the
culture's shape.

=alexandria= is the foundation of the modern Lisp ecosystem
— not because it's powerful, but because it's the opposite.
A couple of hundred small functions, each correcting some
tiny corner of the standard that the standard doesn't reach.
=read-file-into-string=, =ensure-list=, =plist-alist=,
=hash-table-keys= — none of these is a big idea; each is a
small, correct tool that saves you a line. Every project
starts with (ql:quickload :alexandria)= because the cost of
depending on it is zero and the savings are steady.

=cl-ppcre= is the opposite: a substantial, ambitious library
that compiles regular expressions to Lisp code. You'll meet
it in any Lisp program that touches text, and its API shows
what the "compile to Lisp" idiom looks like in practice:

#+begin_src lisp
(ql:quickload :cl-ppcre)

(cl-ppcre:scan-to-strings "\\d+" "abc123def")
;; => "123", #("123")

(cl-ppcre:split "\\s+" "hello world of lisp")
;; => ("hello" "world" "of" "lisp")

(cl-ppcre:regex-replace "noise" "the cave of noise" "silence")
;; => "the cave of silence"
#+end_src

The =scan-to-strings= returns the matched substring and a
vector of capture groups; =split= takes a regex and a
string; =regex-replace= returns a new string with the
matched portion replaced. Three functions that cover most
text-processing needs, with no framework to learn.

*** The Hyperspec habit

The /Common Lisp HyperSpec= is the language standard,
hypertext-browsed: every operator's full specification,
every detail you've seen hedged as "implementation-defined"
or "unspecified" settled in print. The habit to build: when
any question survives the REPL's answers, read the Hyperspec
entry.

Appendix B installed the lookup: =C-c C-d h= on any symbol
opens the right page. Use it for three kinds of question:

1. /"What does this operator actually do in edge case X?"= —
   the Hyperspec's Description and Examples sections. What
   (sort nil #'<)= does, what (parse-integer "12 ") does
   with trailing whitespace, what (values)= returns as
   distinct from (values nil)= — all specified, all
   discoverable.
2. /"Is this standard, or implementation-specific?"= — the
   Definition section says. If you're using something whose
   spec page you can't find, it isn't standard; you're
   relying on a specific implementation's extension.
   Chapter 18's =#+sbcl= conditionals exist for this
   reason.
3. /"What conditions can this signal?"= — the Exceptional
   Situations section names them, which connects every
   Hyperspec entry back to Chapter 19's condition system.
   Knowing that =parse-integer= signals =parse-error= and
   not =end-of-file= is what makes a =handler-case=
   correct.

Reading the Hyperspec is a skill. The entries have a fixed
shape — Syntax, Arguments and Values, Description, Examples,
Exceptional Situations, Notes — and the "Side Effects"
section, when present, is the one that surprises people.
"Sort modifies its argument in place" is in the Side
Effects of the =sort= page, which is why Chapter 10's rule
"always use the return value" exists.

The Glossary section is the standard's own index of ideas:
/proper name/, /fresh/, /literal/, /stable/, /home package/.
Reading those definitions once, at the point when the game
has given you the context to understand them, is worth an
afternoon. They are the standard's own answer to "what does
it mean that Lisp works this way".

*** Where the reading goes

Books, in the order this guide leaves you ready for them:

- /Practical Common Lisp/= (Seibel) — the classic second
  book, free online. Real systems (a spam filter, an HTML
  generator, a streaming MP3 server) built chapter by
  chapter. Half of it will feel like reviews of what
  you've just done; the other half will be new. Its
  introduction to macros and CLOS is gentler than this
  guide's; its projects are more substantial.
- /Paradigms of Artificial Intelligence Programming/=
  (Norvig) — the classic demonstration of Lisp's style.
  Norvig writes Lisp the way good Lisp reads: dense, clear,
  idiomatic. Read the first half (through the pattern
  matcher), skip the AI chapters if you're not interested,
  come back to them if you are. The exercises are
  exceptional.
- /Let Over Lambda/= (Hoyte) — macros at full depth. This
  is a hard book, and deliberately so: it treats macro
  writing as a discipline with its own idioms and its own
  pitfalls. Worth reading after you've written a dozen
  macros of your own and have felt where they hurt.
- /Object-Oriented Programming in Common Lisp/= (Keene) —
  CLOS as a design language. After Chapter 26's and
  Chapter 27's working knowledge, this is the book that
  turns CLOS into a design tool rather than a feature.
- /The Art of the Metaobject Protocol/= (Kiczales et al.)
  — how CLOS is built, and how to build like it. The book
  that changed how a generation of programmers thought
  about object systems. Readable when CLOS feels like
  home and you want to know what's underneath.

Online references worth bookmarking:

- /The Common Lisp Cookbook= — task recipes that stay
  current with the libraries above. When you know what
  you want to do and not what to call, this is the
  fastest lookup.
- /The CLiki= — the community wiki, with library listings
  and how-tos. Older and less reliable than the Cookbook,
  but sometimes the only place a specific library's quirks
  are documented.
- /The Common Lisp HyperSpec= — already installed; the
  habit is worth building before any book, because it's
  the reference you'll use for years.

*** The community

Lisp's community is small and old, in the way a language
that's been stable for forty years is. That has
consequences: documentation is patchier than Python's or
Java's (because the language doesn't change, no one's
racing to write the new-version tutorial), community
discussions move slower (because there's less to discuss),
and library maintenance can be idiosyncratic (because the
libraries work, and their authors have moved on to other
projects).

The consequences the other way are just as real: the
libraries are stable. The language is stable. Solutions
published in 1995 still work in 2025. A library you adopt
today won't be abandoned by a maintainer who's "moved on
to Rust" — it'll be here, tested against every Lisp
implementation, in another decade.

Where to find the community:

- The =#lisp= and =#commonlisp= channels on Libera.Chat
  (IRC, still, after all these years — the Lisp community
  never left).
- =r/lisp= and =r/Common_Lisp= on Reddit, which have
  different cultures worth visiting both of.
- The =#lisp= Discord server (more active with
  newcomers).
- Various Planet Lisp-style blog aggregators, still
  active.
- The monthly Quicklisp release announcement (subscribe to
  see what's new).

The culture rewards people who read the standard, write
docstrings, publish small systems, and don't make noise.
You are now equipped to do all three. The first is what
this chapter's Hyperspec section is for; the second
Chapter 22's rules; the third is a =ql:quickload= and a
README away.

*** What "thinking in Lisp" means, honestly

The book's title promised that you'd learn to /think in
Common Lisp=. That's a phrase that deserves unpacking,
because it's easy to teach syntax and hard to teach the
thing under the syntax. Here's what this guide means by it.

/Thinking in Lisp isn't fluency in writing Lisp.= A
programmer fluent in Java, given a specification and a
week, writes Java. A programmer fluent in Lisp, given the
same, writes Lisp the way Java is written, with =loop=
where Java has for-loops and =defclass= where Java has
class declarations. The code compiles; the tests pass; the
programmer is fluent. And the code isn't Lisp.

/Thinking in Lisp is fluency in the shapes Lisp thinks in.=
When you're stuck on a problem, what you reach for — the
first idea that comes to mind about how to structure the
solution — that's the test. For a Java programmer it's
"what class do I write?". For a Lisp programmer it's one
of these:

- "What data structure represents this?" — the alist, the
  plist, the hash table, the structure, the class, the
  closure — the choice determines the function that walks
  it, and Lisp makes all six available.
- "What's the function that produces this from the data?"
  — Lisp's functional core means most problems are
  "transform this sequence into that one", and the sequence
  functions from Chapter 10 handle most cases without a
  loop.
- "What could go wrong, and what should the caller be able
  to do about it?" — the condition system's question, and
  its answer shapes the function's signature, its
  signalling, and its restarts.
- "What's the macro that would make this read better?" —
  the notation question, which only Lisp even asks,
  because only Lisp lets you answer it.
- "What's the REPL experiment that tells me what's
  actually happening?" — the discovery question, which
  replaces "reasoning about the code" with "asking the
  running program".

The chapters in this book taught these questions by
demonstrating them: when Chapter 7 chose an alist for
=*rooms*=, when Chapter 8's =set-location= copy-and-
rebuild beat =push='s aliasing, when Chapter 17's
=*game-output*= dynamic variable replaced an
argument-threading problem, when Chapter 26's CLOS
hierarchy arrived at the shape where =describe-thing=
varied by class without an =if=. Each of those is a
choice from the shape-vocabulary, made because the shape
was the right one for the problem. That's the thinking the
book was after.

You won't know whether it "took" immediately. The test
comes in the next program you start, or the next non-Lisp
program you read — do you reach for a class when a closure
would do? Do you write a for-loop when a =mapcar= reads
more clearly? Do you let a bug hide behind a comment when a
