condition would surface it? If yes, the shapes are
becoming yours. If not, the shapes are still
Java-shaped, and the language is a dialect of Java that
happens to look different.

The book's whole structure was in service of this: Part I
gave you the atoms (numbers, symbols, lists); Part II gave
you the shapes those atoms build (conses, recursion,
functions-as-values); Part III gave you the tools (loops,
=format=, structures, macros, conditions, packages);
Part IV gave you the /why= of those tools (binding,
reading, compiling, types, symbols-as-data, closures,
classes, packages-as-data); Part V gave you the
discipline of shipping (interoperability, streaming,
reading strangers' code, executables, tests).

You can now read, understand, write, and extend Common
Lisp. The "grok" the guide promised in its introduction is
the shapes part — the vocabulary that comes to mind when a
new problem arrives. That comes with practice, not with
the book.

The best thing the book can do now is suggest what to
practice on.

*** Three suggestions, in order

1. *Extend the game past the book.* The game you've built
   is small — five rooms, four item types, one player. It's
   also /yours=/ in a way a book's exercise never is: you
   chose the room descriptions, you chose which items go
   where, you chose how =describe-thing= reads. Add a shop
   where buying and selling happen; add a monster that
   moves between rooms; add a save format that survives a
   schema change; add a room that only opens when the
   player carries the key. Every addition is a
   chapter's tools re-applied. And every one teaches
   something the chapters couldn't, because they had to be
   ordered.

2. *Write something small and new.* A backup tool, a log
   analyser, a text adventure generator, a budget
   calculator. Start with =ql:quickproject= (which
   scaffolds an =.asd=, a =package.lisp=, a =tests/=
   directory, and a README), one package, one =.asd=, one
   file — the Chapter 28 shape, fresh. Do the whole
   discipline: docstrings, tests, a build script, a
   README. The game taught you how; a new project teaches
   you /why= — because the shapes feel natural this time,
   and the code reads like Lisp instead of like
   Lisp-looking Java.

3. *Read one good codebase.* Pick a small Quicklisp
   library you use — =split-sequence=, =iterate=,
   =cl-ppcre= if you have patience — and read it end to
   end. Keep the Hyperspec open beside it. Notice how the
   author solves the problems you've solved, and where the
   solutions differ. Notice the docstrings, the comment
   conventions, the test suite. Reading with the standard
   at hand is how the remaining rough edges — yours and the
   language's — get polished off. Chapter 31 was a tour;
   this is the practice.

The three suggestions have a shape: /more game/, /new
game/, /someone else's game/. That's deliberate. The first
teaches extension, the second teaches inception, the third
teaches continuation. Together they're the full life of a
programmer in any language, and they're what the book's
whole structure was pointing at.

The running image, the file, and the REPL: they were the
whole method. Every chapter ended in one of them — a
=C-c C-c= into a live game, a =with-open-file= to a
disk, a REPL experiment that answered a question no amount
of reading could. That method is Lisp's method, and it is
what the book was teaching all along. Enjoy the language —
it rewards exactly the curiosity that got you this far.

*** Exercises

**** Exercise 34.1

Explore the distro: run (ql:system-apropos "test")= and
pick one system you've never heard of. Quickload it and
skim its README (find it with =(ql:where-is-system
"name")=, then look in that directory). Write one
sentence on what it does and whether you'd reach for it.

**** Exercise 34.2

Read the Hyperspec entry for a function you use but don't
fully understand — =loop= or =format= or =decode-json=
if you like adventure. Write down one sentence you didn't
know before the reading, and say which chapter of this
guide it corrects or sharpens.

**** Exercise 34.3

Pick a library from the ecosystem table by category, load
it with =ql:quickload=, and write three sentences on what
it does and whether you'd reach for it in a real project.
Then answer: what does the library's README /lack= that a
project's README should have? (The answer is usually the
same thing Chapter 31's audit checklist calls out.)

**** Exercise 34.4

Convert =item-names= from Chapter 8 to a generator that
yields one name at a time. Compare with the eager version:
when would you prefer each? Then convert the generator
/back= to eager processing — what did the conversion cost
in code complexity, and what did it buy?

**** Exercise 34.5

Add a =--version= flag to the game's =main= (Chapter 32)
that reports /both= the game's version and the Quicklisp
distro version, so =./game --version= tells a user both.
The distro version comes from =ql-dist:=; =M-.= on it in
your Lisp, or (asdf:system-version= (asdf:find-system
:quicklisp))= — find the right accessor and use it.

**** Exercise 34.6 (project)

Write the game's =README.md=. A one-page file that a
stranger can open and run the game from. Use Chapter 31's
audit checklist as the requirement list: what the game
does, a usage example, how to build it, how to run the
tests. Then answer — in a comment in the README — which
parts you had to re-read a chapter to write. Those are the
parts of the game you don't yet understand, and they're
your own "next thing to learn" list.

** Part V review

Part V put the game into the world. The chapters:

- Chapter 29 built the interop seam: JSON and CSV for
  exchanging data, =uiop:run-program= for shelling out,
  CFFI for calling C — and the discipline of keeping the
  seam /thin on purpose=, with three failure modes
  (format mismatch, environment failure, world
  inconsistency) each defended differently.
- Chapter 30 built generators from closures and
  multiple values: the two-value protocol, the
  source/transform/consume vocabulary, the resource
  discipline of =:close=, and the honest boundary where
  laziness ends (append of two infinite sources, the
  value of eager lists).
- Chapter 31 taught reading as a skill: the shape of a
  system, the comment conventions, the prologue idioms,
  and the audit checklist for evaluating unfamiliar code.
- Chapter 32 shipped the executable: =save-lisp-and-die=,
  the build script, the entry point, the argument handling,
  the release test.
- Chapter 33 made the discipline concrete: test the
  behaviour not the internals, one test per bug, green
  tests gate the build.
- Chapter 34 (this chapter) is the map of what comes
  next: the ecosystem, the references, the community, and
  the closing statement about what "thinking in Lisp"
  means.

The game, at the end of Part V, is a standalone binary
that runs on a machine with no Lisp installed, exports
its state to JSON and CSV, has a test suite that refuses
to ship a broken build, and is built by a script that
tests before it builds. That's a program, not a
collection of functions.

The appendices that follow are the reference apparatus:
the installation and Emacs setup guides, the answers to
the exercises, a consolidated table of the project's
redefinitions across chapters, the SBCL error messages you will
meet, the full source of the finished game, a glossary of the
standard's own terms, and a concept index in chapter order.

* Appendix A: Installing SBCL

SBCL (Steel Bank Common Lisp) is the program that runs Lisp code. You
type Lisp, and SBCL runs it.

** Linux

Use your package manager:

#+begin_src sh
# Debian / Ubuntu / Mint
sudo apt install sbcl

# Fedora
sudo dnf install sbcl

# Arch
sudo pacman -S sbcl
#+end_src

Check it worked:

#+begin_src sh
sbcl --version
#+end_src

You should see something like =SBCL 2.x.x=. Any recent version is fine
for this guide.

** macOS

macOS needs Homebrew for the compiler, then is identical to Linux:

#+begin_src sh
brew install sbcl
curl -O https://beta.quicklisp.org/quicklisp.lisp
sbcl --load quicklisp.lisp
#+end_src

At the =(quicklisp-quickstart:install)= prompt, answer as the Linux
section says; the =ql:add-to-init-file= step writes to =~/.sbclrc=
exactly as on Linux. Two macOS notes: the Homebrew SBCL is current —
skip any web page telling you to build from source — and Gatekeeper
may warn the *first* time you run a =sb-ext:save-lisp-and-die=
executable; right-click → Open once clears it permanently.

** Windows

1. Go to https://www.sbcl.org/platform-table.html and download the
   Windows installer (=.msi=) for x86-64.
2. Run it and accept the defaults.
3. Open a *new* Command Prompt (Win key, type =cmd=, Enter) and run:

#+begin_src sh
sbcl --version
#+end_src

If you get "'sbcl' is not recognized", SBCL isn't on your PATH. You can
still use it from Emacs by giving the full path (see Appendix B). Its
location is usually something like
=C:\Program Files\Steel Bank Common Lisp\sbcl.exe=. Check that folder
to confirm.

** Trying SBCL on its own

Type =sbcl= in a terminal. You get a prompt =*=. Type:

#+begin_example
,* (+ 1 2)
3
,* (exit)
#+end_example

That's a REPL (Read, Eval, Print, Loop). Like Python's =>>>= prompt,
but you'll mostly use it from inside Emacs.

** Quicklisp (the library installer)

You won't need libraries until Part V. Installing now saves you a
detour later.

1. Download https://beta.quicklisp.org/quicklisp.lisp into your home
   folder.
   - Linux: =curl -O https://beta.quicklisp.org/quicklisp.lisp=
   - Windows: save it from the browser into =C:\Users\YourName\=
2. In a terminal, from that folder, run =sbcl --load quicklisp.lisp=
3. At the =*= prompt, type these one at a time:

#+begin_example
,* (quicklisp-quickstart:install)
,* (ql:add-to-init-file)
#+end_example

When it asks, press Enter. Then type =(exit)=.

From now on, SBCL loads Quicklisp automatically every time it starts.

** Common mistakes

- *"sbcl: command not found" right after installing on Windows:* close
  and reopen the Command Prompt. Old windows don't see the new PATH.
- *Typing =exit= without parentheses:* SBCL thinks you mean a variable
  called =exit= and opens the debugger. Type =0= or =(abort)= and press
  Enter to get out, then type =(exit)=.
- *Running the Quicklisp install twice:* it complains that Quicklisp is
  already installed. That's harmless. It means the first run worked.

* Appendix B: Setting Up Emacs for Common Lisp

Your Emacs config was written for you, so step 1 finds out what you
have. Then you add a small block of settings.

** Step 1: Find your config file

In Emacs, press ~C-h v~, type =user-init-file= and press Enter.

A help window shows a file path. Note it down:

- Path contains =doom= → you use *Doom Emacs*. See "If you use Doom"
  below.
- Path contains =spacemacs= or the file is =.spacemacs= → see "If you
  use Spacemacs" below.
- Anything else (often =~/.emacs.d/init.el=, =~/.config/emacs/init.el=
  or =~/.emacs=) → plain Emacs. Follow step 2.

Close the help window with ~q~.

** Step 2: Check whether a Lisp mode is already installed

Your config might already include SLIME or SLY. These are the two
packages that connect Emacs to Lisp. Only one should be active.

Press ~M-x~ and type =sly=. If a command called exactly =sly= shows up,
SLY is installed. Press ~C-g~ to cancel.

- *SLY is there:* use the SLY version in step 3.
- *SLY isn't there:* use the SLIME version (the guide's default).

** Step 3: Add the settings

Open your config file (~C-x C-f~, then type the path from step 1). Go
to the very end (~M->~) and paste *one* of these settings blocks.

*** SLIME version (default)

#+begin_src emacs-lisp
;;; ---- Common Lisp setup ----
(require 'package)
(add-to-list 'package-archives
             '("melpa" . "https://melpa.org/packages/") t)
(package-initialize)
(unless package-archive-contents
  (package-refresh-contents))
(unless (package-installed-p 'use-package)
  (package-install 'use-package))
(require 'use-package)

(use-package slime
  :ensure t
  :init
  (setq inferior-lisp-program "sbcl")
  :config
  (slime-setup '(slime-fancy)))
#+end_src

*** SLY version (only if step 2 found SLY)

Same as above, but replace the =use-package slime= part with this:

#+begin_src emacs-lisp
(use-package sly
  :ensure t
  :init
  (setq inferior-lisp-program "sbcl"))
#+end_src

With SLY, wherever this guide says ~M-x slime~, type ~M-x sly~ instead.

*** Windows: if =sbcl= isn't on your PATH

Replace ="sbcl"= with the full path, using forward slashes:

#+begin_src emacs-lisp
(setq inferior-lisp-program
      "C:/Program Files/Steel Bank Common Lisp/sbcl.exe")
#+end_src

** Step 4: Restart and test

1. Save the config file (~C-x C-s~) and quit Emacs (~C-x C-c~).
2. Start Emacs again. The first start after this change downloads
   SLIME (or SLY), so it may take up to a minute. Wait until it
   finishes.
3. Type ~M-x slime~ (or ~M-x sly~) and press Enter. A window titled
   =*slime-repl sbcl*= (or =*sly-mrepl*=) opens with a prompt like
   =CL-USER>=. Lisp is now running.
4. At the =CL-USER>= prompt, type =(+ 1 2)= and press ~RET~. Lisp
   answers =3=.

** Step 5: Run code from a buffer

Typing at the prompt is one half of the loop. The other half is
sending an expression from a file to Lisp.

1. Open a new file ending in =.lisp= (~C-x C-f~, then =hello.lisp=).
   Type =(+ 1 2)= and save (~C-x C-s~).
2. Put the cursor after the closing parenthesis and press ~C-c C-c~.
   The first time, Emacs starts Lisp for that buffer. You should see
   the value =3= in the echo area at the bottom of the frame — not in
   the buffer itself.

If both work, you're set. Go back to "How to Use This Guide" and
start Part I. If not, read "Common mistakes" at the end of this
appendix.

** Hyperspec lookup

The Common Lisp HyperSpec is the standard, browsed like a manual.
Install it so =C-c C-d h= works from any symbol:

1. Download the HyperSpec (the Common Lisp HyperSpec mirror of your
   choice — the landing page offers a tarball) and unpack it
   somewhere permanent, e.g. =~/docs/HyperSpec/=.
2. Tell Slime where it lives, in your init file:

#+begin_src emacs-lisp
(setq common-lisp-hyperspec-root
      "file:///home/you/docs/HyperSpec/"
      common-lisp-hyperspec-symbol-table
      "/home/you/docs/HyperSpec/Data/Symbol-Table.text")
#+end_src

(Windows users: forward slashes work in these paths even on Windows —
=C:/docs/HyperSpec/=.)

Now, with Slime connected: point at any symbol, =C-c C-d h= opens the
Hyperspec page; =C-c C-d d= does the same for spec dict entries. A
later chapter builds the *habit*; this is the *keypress*.

** If you use Doom Emacs

Doom keeps your settings in =~/.config/doom/= (or =~/.doom.d/=). If
either folder exists, you use Doom, even if step 1 showed a different
path. Don't add the settings from step 3. Do this instead:

1. Open =~/.config/doom/init.el=. Find the =:lang= section and remove
   the =;;= in front of =common-lisp=, so the line reads:

#+begin_src emacs-lisp
       common-lisp       ; if you've seen one lisp, you've seen them all
#+end_src

2. In a terminal, run =doom sync= (on Windows it may be
   =~/.config/emacs/bin/doom sync=), then restart Emacs.

Doom uses *SLY*, so use ~M-x sly~ wherever this guide says
~M-x slime~. Doom also uses Vim-style keys by default. Read "If your
keys behave like Vim" in Appendix C.

** If you use Spacemacs

1. Open =~/.spacemacs= (press ~SPC f e d~ as a shortcut).
2. Find the list called =dotspacemacs-configuration-layers= and add
   =common-lisp= to it if it's missing:

#+begin_src emacs-lisp
   dotspacemacs-configuration-layers
   '(
     common-lisp
     ;; ... whatever is already here ...
     )
#+end_src

3. Restart Emacs (~SPC q R~). Spacemacs uses SLIME, so ~M-x slime~
   works as written.

** Common mistakes

- *"No SLIME connection" or "Not connected" when pressing ~C-c C-c~:*
  Lisp isn't running yet. Type ~M-x slime~ (or ~M-x sly~) and try
  again. You need this once per Emacs session.
- *"Searching for program: No such file or directory, sbcl":* Emacs
  can't find SBCL. On Windows, use the full-path setting from step 3.
  On Linux, check that =sbcl --version= works in a terminal.
- *SLY says SLIME is also loaded and asks to disable it:* your config
  has both. Answer yes, and keep only one of them in the config.
- *The result is =nil= but you expected printed text:* the printing
  went to the output; the value at the end is what came back. See "Two
  kinds of results" in "How to Use This Guide".

* Appendix C: Emacs Survival Guide

This is the smallest set of keys that lets you work comfortably.
Learn the first section today. The rest will come with use.

** Reading key names

- ~C-x~ means: hold *Control*, press =x=.
- ~M-x~ means: hold *Alt*, press =x=. (M stands for "Meta".) If Alt
  doesn't work, press and release *Esc*, then press =x=.
- ~C-x C-f~ means: Control-x, then Control-f. You can keep Control held
  down for both.
- ~C-x b~ means: Control-x, release, then plain =b=.
- ~RET~ is Enter, ~SPC~ is the space bar, ~TAB~ is Tab.

** The keys you need today

| Key       | What it does                                    |
|-----------+-------------------------------------------------|
| ~C-g~     | Cancel. Use it whenever you're confused or stuck |
| ~C-x C-f~ | Open a file (or create a new one)               |
| ~C-x C-s~ | Save                                            |
| ~C-/~     | Undo                                            |
| ~C-x C-c~ | Quit Emacs                                      |
| ~M-x~     | Run any command by name                         |

~C-g~ is the most important key in Emacs. If a prompt appears at the
bottom of the screen and you don't know what it wants, press ~C-g~
until it goes away.

** Buffers and windows

A *buffer* is anything Emacs has open: a file, the Lisp REPL, a help
page. A *window* is a section of the screen that shows a buffer.

| Key       | What it does                                  |
|-----------+-----------------------------------------------|
| ~C-x b~   | Switch to another buffer (type part of its name) |
| ~C-x o~   | Move the cursor to the other window           |
| ~C-x 1~   | Make the current window fill the screen       |
| ~C-x 0~   | Close the current window (the buffer stays open) |
| ~C-x 2~   | Split the screen top and bottom               |
| ~C-x 3~   | Split the screen left and right               |
| ~C-x k~   | Close a buffer                                |

A good layout for this guide: ~C-x 3~, your =.lisp= file on the left,
the REPL on the right.

** Moving around

Arrow keys, Page Up/Down, Home and End all work. These are faster
once you know them:

| Key   | What it does               |
|-------+----------------------------|
| ~C-a~ | Start of line              |
| ~C-e~ | End of line                |
| ~M-<~ | Start of file              |
| ~M->~ | End of file                |
| ~C-s~ | Search forward (repeat ~C-s~ for the next match, ~RET~ to stop) |
| ~C-r~ | Search backward            |

** Copy, cut and paste

Emacs uses its own keys for these:

| Key     | What it does                                     |
|---------+--------------------------------------------------|
| ~C-SPC~ | Start selecting (then move the cursor)           |
| ~M-w~   | Copy the selection                               |
| ~C-w~   | Cut the selection                                |
| ~C-y~   | Paste                                            |

Selecting with the mouse also works, followed by ~M-w~ or ~C-w~.

If you'd rather use =C-c=, =C-x= and =C-v= like other programs, type
~M-x cua-mode~. It stays on until you restart Emacs.

** Lisp keys

These work in the REPL and in =.lisp= files.

| Key         | What it does                                        |
|-------------+-----------------------------------------------------|
| ~RET~       | In the REPL: run what you typed                     |
| ~M-p~       | In the REPL: bring back the previous input          |
| ~M-n~       | In the REPL: the next input                         |
| ~C-x C-e~   | Run the expression just before the cursor           |
| ~C-c C-c~   | In a Lisp buffer: compile the definition under the cursor |
| ~C-c C-k~   | Compile and load the whole =.lisp= file             |
| ~C-M-f~     | Jump forward over one whole expression              |
| ~C-M-b~     | Jump backward over one whole expression             |
| ~C-M-k~     | Delete the next whole expression                    |

When the cursor is on a parenthesis, Emacs highlights its partner.
Watch for this. It's how you check that your parentheses match.

** The debugger window

When your code has an error, a window called =*sldb ...*= (SLIME) or
=*sly-db ...*= (SLY) opens. It shows the error message and a list of
numbered choices called /restarts/.

For now:

- Read the first line. It tells you what went wrong.
- Press ~q~ to close the window and go back to work.

A later chapter teaches you what the other choices do. They're one of
the best features of Lisp.

** Debugger keys

When an error opens the SLDB buffer (the debugger of a later chapter),
the keys that matter:

| Key    | Action                                        |
|--------+-----------------------------------------------|
| =v=    | view the current frame's source               |
| =n= / =p= | next / previous frame                       |
| =t=    | toggle details of the current frame           |
| =1–9=  | invoke the numbered restart from the menu     |
| =a=    | invoke ABORT (the standard escape restart)    |
| =q=    | quit — abort out of the debugger              |
| =i=    | inspect the current frame's condition         |

The Inspector buffer: =TAB= moves between parts, =RET= descends
into one, =l= goes back up. And the trace buffer from =trace=:
=g= refreshes. Two habits cover 80% of use: =n= down the frames
reading the backtrace, =1= on the restart you decided on — everything
else is exploration.

** If your keys behave like Vim

Doom, Spacemacs and many AI-written configs turn on *Evil*, which
makes Emacs work like the Vim editor. Signs: typing letters doesn't
insert text, or the cursor is a solid block and the bottom bar shows
=<N>= or =NORMAL=.

The basics:

- Press =i= to start typing text (insert mode).
- Press ~Esc~ to stop typing and go back to moving around (normal mode).
- Press =:w= then ~RET~ to save.
- The ~C-~ and ~M-~ keys in this guide mostly still work, including
  ~C-c C-c~.
- Doom and Spacemacs also use ~SPC~ in normal mode as a menu key.
  Press it and wait: a menu of choices appears.

If you'd rather not use Vim keys, type ~M-x evil-mode~ to turn them
off until the next restart.

** If Lisp gets stuck

If an expression never finishes (for example, a loop that never
ends):

1. Switch to the REPL buffer (~C-x b~, then type =repl=).
2. Press ~C-c C-c~ there to interrupt Lisp. The debugger opens.
3. Press ~q~.

If that doesn't help, run ~M-x slime-restart-inferior-lisp~ (SLY:
~M-x sly-restart-inferior-lisp~). This starts a fresh Lisp. Everything
you defined is gone, so re-run the earlier definitions.

** Common mistakes

- *Pressing ~C-z~ by habit (undo in other programs):* it minimises
  Emacs, or with Evil switches key styles. Undo is ~C-/~. If the
  keys suddenly feel different, press ~C-z~ again.
- *Pressing ~C-x~ and then waiting:* Emacs is waiting for the second
  key. Finish the combination or press ~C-g~.
- *Closing the REPL buffer with ~C-x k~:* Lisp may still be running in
  the background. Run ~M-x slime~ again; if it asks whether to create
  an additional connection, answer no.
- *Losing your place in a large file:* ~M-<~ and ~M->~ jump to the ends,
  ~C-s~ searches forward, and ~C-M-f~ moves over whole expressions.

* Appendix D: Answers to Exercises

Each answer has a solution, test lines to run after your own answer,
and the result you should see.

** How to use these answers

Each answer gives a solution, a *check* to run, and the *expected*
result. To check your own answer:

1. Run your definition.
2. Run the check lines after it.
3. Compare with the expected result.

For exercises where the answer is a single expression, the check is
simply running your expression. Your code may look different from
the solution and still be right. Only the result has to match.

** Chapter 1

*** Exercise 1.1

#+begin_src lisp
(* 7 24 60)
#+end_src

Expected: =10080=

=*= takes any number of arguments, so you don't need to nest
=(* 7 (* 24 60))=. That version works too.

*** Exercise 1.2

#+begin_src lisp
(* (- 10 4) (+ 3 2))
#+end_src

Expected: =30=

*** Exercise 1.3

#+begin_src lisp
(- (/ 100 (* 2 5)) 3)
#+end_src

Expected: =7=

Work from the inside out: =(* 2 5)= is =10=, =(/ 100 10)= is =10=,
and =(- 10 3)= is =7=.

*** Exercise 1.4

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(princ "Welcome, traveller.")
#+end_src

Expected: =Welcome, traveller.=

The line is what =princ= writes. Its value is the string itself, and
Lisp shows a string with quotes around it.

*** Exercise 1.5

| Wrong          | Problem                                       | Fixed           |
|----------------+-----------------------------------------------+-----------------|
| =(5 * 3)=      | The operator must come first                  | =(* 5 3)=       |
| =(+2 3)=       | =+2= reads as the number 2. A space is needed | =(+ 2 3)=       |
| =(+ 1 (* 2 3)= | Missing a closing parenthesis                 | =(+ 1 (* 2 3))= |
| =(* 2, 4)=     | Commas don't separate arguments               | =(* 2 4)=       |

** Chapter 2

*** Exercise 2.1

| Expression      | Result    | Why                                              |
|-----------------+-----------+--------------------------------------------------|
| ='(+ 2 3)=      | =(+ 2 3)= | Quoted, so not evaluated                         |
| =(list '+ 2 3)= | =(+ 2 3)= | =list= builds the same list from its arguments   |
| =(+ 2 3)=       | =5=       | A normal call                                    |

The first two give the same list in two different ways. This is the
point of the exercise: code and data look the same in Lisp.

*** Exercise 2.2

#+begin_src lisp
(list (third '(a b c d))
      (nth 2 '(a b c d))
      (first (rest (rest '(a b c d)))))
#+end_src

Expected: =(C C C)=

Any three working ways are fine. =(second (rest '(a b c d)))= is
another.

*** Exercise 2.3

#+begin_src lisp
(first (last '(red green blue)))
#+end_src

Expected: =BLUE=

=(third '(red green blue))= also works. =(last ...)= alone gives
=(BLUE)=, a list, which is the trap this exercise sets.

*** Exercise 2.4

#+begin_src lisp
(reverse (append '(1 2 3) '(4 5 6)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(6 5 4 3 2 1)=

Another way: =(append (reverse '(4 5 6)) (reverse '(1 2 3)))=.

*** Exercise 2.5

| Expression                  | Result | Why                                           |
|-----------------------------+--------+-----------------------------------------------|
| =(listp nil)=               | =T=    | =nil= is the empty list                       |
| =(null '(nil))=             | =NIL=  | A list holding one element is not empty       |
| =(length '((a b) (c d e)))= | =2=    | Two elements, each of which is a list         |
| =(rest '(only))=            | =NIL=  | Nothing after the first element               |

The second one catches many people. =(nil)= is a list with one
element, which happens to be =nil=. It isn't empty.

*** Exercise 2.6

#+begin_src lisp
(second (second '(garden (kitchen shed))))
#+end_src

Expected: =SHED=

The inner =second= gives the exits, =(KITCHEN SHED)=. The outer one
picks the second exit.

** Chapter 3

*** Exercise 3.1

#+begin_src lisp
(+ 2/3 1/4)
#+end_src

Expected: =11/12=

#+begin_src lisp
(float (+ 2/3 1/4))
#+end_src

Expected: =0.9166667=

*** Exercise 3.2

| Expression   | Result | Why                                                   |
|--------------+--------+-------------------------------------------------------|
| =(/ 10 4)=   | =5/2=  | Integer division is exact and reduces 10/4 to 5/2     |
| =(/ 10 4.0)= | =2.5=  | One float makes the result a float                    |
| =(mod 10 4)= | =2=    | 10 = 2 × 4 + 2                                        |
| =(isqrt 50)= | =7=    | 7 × 7 = 49 fits in 50. 8 × 8 = 64 doesn't             |

*** Exercise 3.3

#+begin_src lisp
(subseq "I love Lisp" 7)
#+end_src

Expected: ="Lisp"=

Count from zero: =I= is 0, the space is 1, =love= is 2 to 5, the
second space is 6, and =L= is 7. =(subseq "I love Lisp" 7 11)= also
works.

*** Exercise 3.4

#+begin_src lisp
(concatenate 'string
             (string-upcase "hello")
             ", "
             (string-upcase "world")
             "!")
#+end_src

Expected: ="HELLO, WORLD!"=

This is shorter: upper-case once, after joining.

#+begin_src lisp
(string-upcase (concatenate 'string "hello" ", " "world" "!"))
#+end_src

*** Exercise 3.5

#+begin_src lisp
(list (digit-char-p #\7)
      (char-code (char-upcase #\b)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(7 66)=

=(char-upcase #\b)= gives =#\B=, and the code of =B= is =66=, one
more than =A= (65).

*** Exercise 3.6

| Expression                       | Result    | Why                                         |
|----------------------------------+-----------+---------------------------------------------|
| =(eq 'Apple 'APPLE)=             | =T=       | Symbol names become upper case when read    |
| =(string= "Apple" "APPLE")=      | =NIL=     | Strings keep their case. =string== checks it |
| =(string-equal "Apple" "APPLE")= | =T=       | =string-equal= ignores case                 |
| =(symbol-name :north)=           | ="NORTH"= | The colon isn't part of the name            |

*** Exercise 3.7

#+begin_src lisp
(concatenate 'string
             "You are in the "
             (string-capitalize
              (symbol-name
               (first '(garden "An overgrown garden." (kitchen shed)))))
             ". "
             (second '(garden "An overgrown garden." (kitchen shed))))
#+end_src

Expected: ="You are in the Garden. An overgrown garden."=

It's the same shape as the kitchen example in the chapter, with the
garden list in place of the kitchen list. Typing the room twice is
still clumsy. Chapter 4 fixes that.

** Chapter 4

*** Exercise 4.1

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *apples* 12)
(defparameter *pears* 8)
(+ *apples* *pears*)
#+end_src

Expected: =20=

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *pears* 20)
(+ *apples* *pears*)
#+end_src

Expected: =32=

*** Exercise 4.2

Expected: =NORMAL=

| Line                            | =*mode*= after it | Why                                  |
|---------------------------------+-------------------+--------------------------------------|
| =(defvar *mode* 'easy)=         | =EASY=            | First definition                     |
| =(setf *mode* 'hard)=           | =HARD=            | =setf= changes it                    |
| =(defvar *mode* 'easy)=         | =HARD=            | Already defined, so =defvar= skips it |
| =(defparameter *mode* 'normal)= | =NORMAL=          | =defparameter= always sets it        |
| =(defvar *mode* 'easy)=         | =NORMAL=          | Already defined again                |

If you ran these in a session where =*mode*= already existed, the
first line also does nothing. Restart Lisp to see the table exactly.

*** Exercise 4.3

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((width 8)
      (height 5))
  (list (* width height)
        (* 2 (+ width height))))
#+end_src

Expected: =(40 26)=

*** Exercise 4.4

#+begin_src lisp
(let* ((celsius 30)
       (fahrenheit (+ (* celsius 9/5) 32)))
  (list celsius fahrenheit))
#+end_src

Expected: =(30 86)=

=9/5= is a ratio, so the result stays exact: 30 × 9/5 is 54, plus 32
is 86. A plain =let= fails here, because =fahrenheit= uses =celsius=.

*** Exercise 4.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *steps* 0)
(incf *steps*)
(incf *steps*)
(incf *steps*)
(incf *steps* 10)
#+end_src

Expected: =13=

The last =incf= returns the new value, so that is what you see. Adding
=*steps*= as a final line gives the same result.

*** Exercise 4.6

| Wrong                         | Problem                                           | Fixed                          |
|-------------------------------+---------------------------------------------------+--------------------------------|
| =(let (total 10) total)=      | Each binding needs its own parentheses            | =(let ((total 10)) total)=     |
| =(let ((a 3) (b (* a a))) b)= | =b= uses =a= from the same =let=                  | =(let* ((a 3) (b (* a a))) b)= |
| =(* '*apples* 2)=             | The quote gives the name, not the value           | =(* *apples* 2)=               |

Check the fixed versions:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (let ((total 10)) total)
      (let* ((a 3) (b (* a a))) b)
      (* *apples* 2))
#+end_src

Expected: =(10 9 24)= (with =*apples*= at 12 from Exercise 4.1)

*** Exercise 4.7

Expected: =11=

The outer =x= is 5. The inner =let= binds a new =x= to 5 × 2 = 10.
=setf= changes the inner =x= to 11, and the body returns it.

With =(list x)= after the inner =let=:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((x 5))
  (let ((x (* x 2)))
    (setf x (+ x 1)))
  (list x))
#+end_src

Expected: =(5)=

The =setf= changed only the inner =x=. Once the inner =let= ends,
the outer =x= is visible again, and it was never touched. It is
still =5=.

*** Exercise 4.8

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* *kitchen*)

(let ((name (first *current-room*))
      (exits (third *current-room*)))
  (list name (length exits)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(KITCHEN 2)=

The exits are the third element of a room, =(HALLWAY GARDEN)=, and
=length= counts them.

** Chapter 5

*** Exercise 5.1

#+begin_src lisp
(defun square (n)
  (* n n))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (square 4) (square -3) (square 1/2))
#+end_src

Expected: =(16 9 1/4)=

*** Exercise 5.2

#+begin_src lisp
(defun average3 (a b c)
  "Return the average of A, B and C."
  (/ (+ a b c) 3))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (average3 1 2 3) (average3 1 2 4) (float (average3 1 2 4)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(2 7/3 2.3333333)=

The result is exact. 7/3 isn't an error, it's the true average.

*** Exercise 5.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defun greet (name)
  (concatenate 'string "Hello, " (string-upcase name) "!"))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(greet "ada")
#+end_src

Expected: ="Hello, ADA!"=

*** Exercise 5.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun power-of (base &optional (exponent 2))
  (expt base exponent))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (power-of 5) (power-of 2 10) (power-of 3 0))
#+end_src

Expected: =(25 1024 1)=

*** Exercise 5.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-greeting (name &key (greeting "Hello") (punctuation "!"))
  (concatenate 'string greeting ", " name punctuation))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (make-greeting "Ada")
      (make-greeting "Ada" :greeting "Welcome")
      (make-greeting "Ada" :punctuation "?" :greeting "Ready"))
#+end_src

Expected: =("Hello, Ada!" "Welcome, Ada!" "Ready, Ada?")=

The keywords can come in any order. The third call gives
=:punctuation= first and still works.

*** Exercise 5.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-args (&rest things)
  (length things))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (count-args) (count-args 'a) (count-args 1 2 3 4 5))
#+end_src

Expected: =(0 1 5)=

With no arguments, =things= is the empty list, so its length is 0.

*** Exercise 5.7

| Wrong                        | Problem                                              | Fixed                       |
|------------------------------+------------------------------------------------------+-----------------------------|
| =(defun add (a b) (+ a b c))= | =c= isn't a parameter. SBCL warns "undefined variable: C" | =(defun add (a b) (+ a b))= |
| =(defun (twice n) (* 2 n))=  | The name and the parameter list are separate parts   | =(defun twice (n) (* 2 n))= |
| =(add 1)=                    | =add= needs two arguments: "invalid number of arguments: 1" | =(add 1 2)=          |

Check the fixed versions:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun add (a b) (+ a b))
(defun twice (n) (* 2 n))
(list (add 1 2) (twice 21))
#+end_src

Expected: =(3 42)=

*** Exercise 5.8

#+begin_src lisp
(defun exit-count (place)
  (length (room-exits place)))

(defun room-title (place)
  (string-capitalize (symbol-name (room-name place))))
#+end_src

Check (run the Chapter 5 project code first):

#+begin_src lisp
(list (exit-count *hallway*)
      (exit-count *garden*)
      (room-title *kitchen*))
#+end_src

Expected: =(2 1 "Kitchen")=

You can now use =room-title= inside =describe-room=, which makes it
shorter:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun describe-room (place)
  "Return the text shown when the player is in PLACE."
  (concatenate 'string
               "You are in the " (room-title place) ". "
               (room-description place)))
#+end_src

Small functions that each do one thing, built on each other, are how
Lisp programs are written.

** Chapter 6

*** Exercise 6.1

#+begin_src lisp
(defun sign (n)
  (cond ((< n 0) 'negative)
        ((= n 0) 'zero)
        (t 'positive)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (sign -4) (sign 0) (sign 9))
#+end_src

Expected: =(NEGATIVE ZERO POSITIVE)=

=(zerop n)= works in place of =(= n 0)=.

*** Exercise 6.2

| Expression                                 | Result  | Why                                     |
|--------------------------------------------+---------+-----------------------------------------|
| =(if nil 1 2)=                             | =2=     | =nil= is false                          |
| =(if 0 1 2)=                               | =1=     | =0= is not =nil=, so it's true          |
| =(when (> 1 2) 'yes)=                      | =NIL=   | The test fails, so the body doesn't run |
| =(unless (> 1 2) 'yes)=                    | =YES=   | =unless= runs when the test fails       |
| =(if (member 3 '(1 2 3)) 'found 'missing)= | =FOUND= | =member= returns =(3)=, which is true   |

*** Exercise 6.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defun my-abs (n)
  (if (< n 0)
      (- n)
      n))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (my-abs -7) (my-abs 0) (my-abs 3/2))
#+end_src

Expected: =(7 0 3/2)=

=(- n)= with one argument negates, as in the Chapter 3 table.

*** Exercise 6.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun grade (score)
  (cond ((>= score 90) 'a)
        ((>= score 80) 'b)
        ((>= score 70) 'c)
        (t 'f)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (grade 95) (grade 90) (grade 85) (grade 72) (grade 40))
#+end_src

Expected: =(A A B C F)=

The order does the work. A score of 85 fails the first test and
passes the second, so you never need to write "between 80 and 89".

*** Exercise 6.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defun day-type (day)
  (case day
    ((saturday sunday) 'weekend)
    ((monday tuesday wednesday thursday friday) 'weekday)
    (otherwise 'unknown)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (day-type 'sunday) (day-type 'wednesday) (day-type 'funday))
#+end_src

Expected: =(WEEKEND WEEKDAY UNKNOWN)=

*** Exercise 6.6

First listing. =if= got four arguments after the test, so SBCL
complains that there are too many. Group the two prints with =progn=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(let ((x 5))
  (if (> x 0)
      (progn
        (print "positive")
        (print "big"))
      (print "negative")))
#+end_src

Expected output:

#+begin_example
"positive" 
"big" 
#+end_example

Second listing. Without the extra parentheses, =cond= takes =>= as the
test and tries to look up a variable called =>=: "The variable > is
unbound". Each clause needs its own pair:

#+begin_src lisp
(cond ((> 5 0) 'yes))
#+end_src

Expected: =YES=

Third listing. It returns =0=, not an error. =case= can't match
strings, so the ="yes"= clause never matches. Use =cond= with
=string==:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((answer "yes"))
  (cond ((string= answer "yes") 1)
        (t 0)))
#+end_src

Expected: =1=

*** Exercise 6.7

Check all five at once:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (or nil "" 5)
      (and 1 0 nil)
      (or (member 'x '(a b)) 'none)
      (and 'a 'b)
      (not 0))
#+end_src

Expected: =("" NIL NONE B NIL)=

| Expression                      | Result | Why                                              |
|---------------------------------+--------+--------------------------------------------------|
| =(or nil "" 5)=                 | =""=   | =""= is the first true value, so =or= stops there |
| =(and 1 0 nil)=                 | =NIL=  | One argument is =nil=                            |
| =(or (member 'x '(a b)) 'none)= | =NONE= | =member= gives =nil=, so =or= moves on           |
| =(and 'a 'b)=                   | =B=    | All true, so =and= returns the last value        |
| =(not 0)=                       | =NIL=  | =0= is true, so =not= gives =nil=                |

*** Exercise 6.8

#+begin_src lisp
(defun fizzbuzz-word (n)
  (cond ((zerop (mod n 15)) "FizzBuzz")
        ((zerop (mod n 3)) "Fizz")
        ((zerop (mod n 5)) "Buzz")
        (t (princ-to-string n))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (fizzbuzz-word 1)
      (fizzbuzz-word 3)
      (fizzbuzz-word 5)
      (fizzbuzz-word 15)
      (fizzbuzz-word 7))
#+end_src

Expected: =("1" "Fizz" "Buzz" "FizzBuzz" "7")=

The test for 15 must come first. If the test for 3 came first, 15
would give ="Fizz"=. =(and (zerop (mod n 3)) (zerop (mod n 5)))= also
works as the first test.

*** Exercise 6.9

#+begin_src lisp
(defun can-go-p (name)
  "Return T if NAME is an exit of the current room, NIL otherwise."
  (if (member name (room-exits *current-room*))
      t
      nil))
#+end_src

Check (run the Chapter 4 to 6 project code first):

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* *hallway*)
(list (can-go-p 'kitchen) (can-go-p 'garden))
#+end_src

Expected: =(T NIL)=

A shorter version is =(not (null (member ...)))=: =null= turns the
result into =t= or =nil= the wrong way round, and =not= flips it
back.

*** Exercise 6.10

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *garden-unlocked* nil)

(defun go-to (name)
  "Move the player to the room called NAME, if it is an exit."
  (cond ((eq name (room-name *current-room*))
         "You are already there.")
        ((and (eq name 'garden) (not *garden-unlocked*))
         "The garden door is locked.")
        ((member name (room-exits *current-room*))
         (setf *current-room* (find-room name))
         (look))
        (t
         "You can't go there from here.")))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* *kitchen*)
(setf *garden-unlocked* nil)
(list (go-to 'garden)
      (progn
        (setf *garden-unlocked* t)
        (go-to 'garden)))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("The garden door is locked."
 "You are in the Garden. An overgrown garden.")
#+end_example

The new clause goes *before* the =member= clause. If it came after,
the =member= clause would match first and the lock would never be
checked.

Arguments to =list= are evaluated left to right, so the first
=go-to= runs while the door is still locked, and the =progn= unlocks
it before the second one.

Part II rebuilds =go-to= from scratch, so it doesn't matter whether
you keep this version or the chapter's.

** Chapter 7

*** Exercise 7.1

#+begin_src lisp
(list (cons 1 (cons 2 (cons 3 nil)))
      (cons 'a (cons (cons 'b (cons 'c nil)) nil)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((1 2 3) (A (B C)))=

The diagram for =(A (B C))=:

#+begin_example
+---+---+   +---+---+
| A | *-+-->| * |NIL|
+---+---+   +-+-+---+
              |
              v
            +---+---+   +---+---+
            | B | *-+-->| C |NIL|
            +---+---+   +---+---+
#+end_example

The outer list has two conses, so two elements. The car of the second
one points to the inner list =(B C)=.

*** Exercise 7.2

#+begin_src lisp
(list (cons 'a 'b)
      (cons '(a) '(b))
      (cons nil nil)
      (cdr '(a))
      (car '((x y) z)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((A . B) ((A) B) (NIL) NIL (X Y))=

| Expression         | Result    | Why                                              |
|--------------------+-----------+--------------------------------------------------|
| =(cons 'a 'b)=     | =(A . B)= | The cdr isn't a list, so it's a dotted pair      |
| =(cons '(a) '(b))= | =((A) B)= | The element =(A)= goes on the front of =(B)=     |
| =(cons nil nil)=   | =(NIL)=   | A one-element list whose element is =nil=        |
| =(cdr '(a))=       | =NIL=     | Only one cons, and its cdr is the end            |
| =(car '((x y) z))= | =(X Y)=   | The first element is itself a list               |

*** Exercise 7.3

#+begin_src lisp
(list (caddr '(a b c d))
      (cddr '(a b c d))
      (cdar '((a . 1) (b . 2))))
#+end_src

Expected: =(C (C D) 1)=

Read =cdar= right to left: the car gives =(A . 1)=, and its cdr is
=1=.

*** Exercise 7.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun swap-pair (pair)
  (cons (cdr pair) (car pair)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (swap-pair '(1 . 2)) (swap-pair '(key . kitchen)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((2 . 1) (KITCHEN . KEY))=

*** Exercise 7.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *s* '(x))
(push 'y *s*)
(push 'z *s*)
(list (pop *s*) *s*)
#+end_src

Expected: =(Z (Y X))=

After both pushes, =*s*= is =(Z Y X)=. =pop= returns =Z= and leaves
=(Y X)=. The arguments of =list= run left to right, so =*s*= is read
after the =pop=.

*** Exercise 7.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *capitals*
  '((tunisia . tunis)
    (france . paris)
    (japan . tokyo)))

(defun capital-of (country)
  (or (cdr (assoc country *capitals*))
      'unknown))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (capital-of 'japan) (capital-of 'tunisia) (capital-of 'peru))
#+end_src

Expected: =(TOKYO TUNIS UNKNOWN)=

*** Exercise 7.7

| Wrong                                    | Problem                                      | Fixed                                  |
|------------------------------------------+----------------------------------------------+----------------------------------------|
| =(cons '(1 2) '(3 4))=                   | =cons= adds =(1 2)= as one element           | =(append '(1 2) '(3 4))=               |
| =(cdr (assoc "b" '(("a" . 1) ("b" . 2))))= | =assoc= can't compare strings              | =(cdr (assoc 'b '((a . 1) (b . 2))))=  |
| =(push 'x '(a b))=                       | =push= needs a variable to store the result  | =(cons 'x '(a b))=                     |

Check the fixed versions:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (append '(1 2) '(3 4))
      (cdr (assoc 'b '((a . 1) (b . 2))))
      (cons 'x '(a b)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((1 2 3 4) 2 (X A B))=

If you really want =push=, put the list in a variable first:
=(let ((l '(a b))) (push 'x l))= also gives =(X A B)=.

*** Exercise 7.8

#+begin_src lisp
(defun drop (item)
  "Put down ITEM in the current room, if the player carries it."
  (if (eq (item-location item) 'player)
      (progn
        (push (cons item (room-name *current-room*)) *item-locations*)
        (concatenate 'string "You drop the " (item-name item) "."))
      "You don't have that."))
#+end_src

Check. This resets the items, so run it in one go:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(list (drop 'key)
      (take 'key)
      (progn
        (go-to 'hallway)
        (drop 'key))
      (item-location 'key))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("You don't have that." "You take the key." "You drop the key." HALLWAY)
#+end_example

The key was taken in the kitchen and dropped in the hallway. The
=progn= moves the player first, then drops the key, and returns the
drop message.

*** Exercise 7.9

#+begin_src lisp
(push '(shed "A wooden shed full of old tools." (garden)) *rooms*)
(push '(garden "An overgrown garden." (kitchen shed)) *rooms*)
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'garden))
(go-to 'shed)
#+end_src

Expected: ="You are in the Shed. A wooden shed full of old tools."=

The new garden replaces the old one because =assoc= returns the
/first/ pair with a matching key. The new garden is at the front of
=*rooms*=, so =find-room= finds it before the old one. The old garden
is still in the list, hidden.

The =setf= must come /after/ the pushes. If =*current-room*= was set
to the garden earlier, it still holds the old garden list, whose
exits don't include the shed, and =go-to= answers "You can't go there
from here."

Each push adds to =*rooms*= again, so running this twice puts
two copies at the front. That's harmless, because only the first one
is ever found. Re-run the =defparameter= for =*rooms*= from the
project step to start clean.

** Chapter 8

*** Exercise 8.1

#+begin_src lisp
(defun my-length (lst)
  "Return the number of elements in LST."
  (if (null lst)
      0
      (+ 1 (my-length (rest lst)))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (my-length nil) (my-length '(a)) (my-length '(a b c d)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(0 1 4)=

The empty list is the base case: zero elements. Otherwise, one for
the first element plus the length of the rest.

*** Exercise 8.2

#+begin_src lisp
(defun my-sum (lst)
  "Return the sum of the numbers in LST."
  (if (null lst)
      0
      (+ (first lst) (my-sum (rest lst)))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (my-sum nil) (my-sum '(5)) (my-sum '(1 2 3 4)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(0 5 10)=

The base case is =0=, because adding nothing gives zero. Compare that
with =my-length=: the same shape, a different "nothing" value.

*** Exercise 8.3

#+begin_src lisp
(list (count-atoms '(a (b) c))
      (countdown 3)
      (factorial 4)
      (items-in '((lamp . cellar) (key . kitchen)) 'kitchen)
      (double-all-into '(1 2) nil))
#+end_src

Expected: =(3 (3 2 1) 24 (KEY) (4 2))=

| Expression                              | Result    | Why                                                       |
|-----------------------------------------+-----------+-----------------------------------------------------------|
| =(count-atoms '(a (b) c))=              | =3=       | =a=, =b= and =c=: three atoms, at any depth                |
| =(countdown 3)=                         | =(3 2 1)= | Counts down and stops at zero                              |
| =(factorial 4)=                         | =24=      | 4 × 3 × 2 × 1                                              |
| =(items-in ... 'kitchen)=               | =(KEY)=   | Only the pair whose value is =kitchen=                     |
| =(double-all-into '(1 2) nil)=          | =(4 2)=   | The accumulator is built backwards, so no =reverse= here   |

The last one is the accumulator pattern without its =reverse=. Running
=double-all= instead gives =(2 4)=, because =double-all= reverses the
result of the helper.

*** Exercise 8.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun my-reverse-into (lst result)
  "Cons each element of LST onto RESULT."
  (if (null lst)
      result
      (my-reverse-into (rest lst)
                       (cons (first lst) result))))

(defun my-reverse (lst)
  "Return LST in the opposite order."
  (my-reverse-into lst nil))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (my-reverse nil) (my-reverse '(1 2 3)) (my-reverse '(a)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(NIL (3 2 1) (A))=

No =reverse= is needed at the end, because walking from the front and
consing onto the accumulator already reverses the list. That's the
whole job here, so the helper's result *is* the answer.

*** Exercise 8.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defun my-find (item lst)
  "Return ITEM if it is in LST, NIL otherwise."
  (cond ((null lst) nil)
        ((eq (first lst) item) item)
        (t (my-find item (rest lst)))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (my-find 'b '(a b c))
      (my-find 'a '(a b c))
      (my-find 'z '(a b c))
      (my-find 'x nil))
#+end_src

Expected: =(B A NIL NIL)=

Compare this with the built-in =member= from Chapter 6: =member=
returns the rest of the list from the match onward, and =my-find=
returns just the item. Both are useful.

*** Exercise 8.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-symbol (sym tree)
  "Return how often SYM appears in TREE, at any depth."
  (cond ((null tree) 0)
        ((consp tree) (+ (count-symbol sym (car tree))
                         (count-symbol sym (cdr tree))))
        ((eq tree sym) 1)
        (t 0)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (count-symbol 'a '(a (b a) c a))
      (count-symbol 'a '(x y))
      (count-symbol 'a 'a)
      (count-symbol 'a nil))
#+end_src

Expected: =(3 0 1 0)=

*** Exercise 8.7

#+begin_src lisp
(defun flatten (tree)
  "Return the atoms of TREE as one flat list."
  (cond ((null tree) nil)
        ((consp tree) (append (flatten (car tree))
                              (flatten (cdr tree))))
        (t (list tree))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (flatten '(a (b (c)) d))
      (flatten '())
      (flatten '(1 (2 (3 (4))))))
#+end_src

Expected: =((A B C D) NIL (1 2 3 4))=

Two recursive calls again, one for each half of the cons. The last
clause wraps the atom in a list, because the answer must always be a
list: =(flatten 'a)= would otherwise return the atom =A=, and
=append= would refuse it.

=append= copies every cons of the lists it is given (Chapter 7), so
this version is fine for the sizes in this guide, and slow on very
large trees.

*** Exercise 8.8

*Wrong:* =bad-length= throws away the recursive result, so it returns
=0= for every list. The recursive call has to add one:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun fix-length (lst)
  (if (null lst)
      0
      (+ 1 (fix-length (rest lst)))))
#+end_src

*Wrong:* =bad-sum= has the wrong base case. Adding a number to =nil=
gives "The value NIL is not a NUMBER" as soon as the recursion ends:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun fix-sum (lst)
  (if (null lst)
      0
      (+ (first lst) (fix-sum (rest lst)))))
#+end_src

*Wrong:* =bad-countdown= uses =(1+ n)=, so it walks away from zero and
never reaches the base case. SBCL prints "Control stack exhausted".
The input must get smaller:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun fix-countdown (n)
  (if (zerop n)
      nil
      (cons n (fix-countdown (1- n)))))
#+end_src

Check all three:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (fix-length '(a b c))
      (fix-sum '(1 2 3))
      (fix-countdown 3))
#+end_src

Expected: =(3 6 (3 2 1))=

With =(1- n)= in place, the third one walks down from =3= to =0=, which
is four calls. The broken version needs a million calls to fill the
stack, so never run it to see what happens.

*** Exercise 8.9

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-conses (tree)
  "Return the number of cons cells in TREE."
  (cond ((null tree) 0)
        ((consp tree) (+ 1 (count-conses (car tree))
                          (count-conses (cdr tree))))
        (t 0)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (count-conses '(a b))
      (count-conses '((a) b))
      (count-conses 5)
      (count-conses nil))
#+end_src

Expected: =(2 3 0 0)=

=(a b)= is two conses, as Chapter 7's box diagram shows. =((a) b)= has
one cons for the outer pair, one for the inner =(a)=, and one for the
chain =(b)=: three. An atom has none, and =nil= isn't a cons either.

*** Exercise 8.10

#+begin_src lisp
(defun item-report (pairs)
  "Return one string per item in PAIRS, saying where it is."
  (cond ((null pairs) nil)
        ((eq (cdar pairs) 'player)
         (cons (concatenate 'string
                            (item-name (caar pairs))
                            " is carried")
               (item-report (cdr pairs))))
        (t
         (cons (concatenate 'string
                            (item-name (caar pairs))
                            " is in the "
                            (string-downcase
                             (symbol-name (cdar pairs))))
               (item-report (cdr pairs))))))
#+end_src

Check. This resets the items and marks one as carried, so run it in
one go:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(push '(coin . player) *item-locations*)
(item-report *item-locations*)
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("coin is carried"
 "lamp is in the cellar"
 "key is in the kitchen"
 "letter is in the bedroom")
#+end_example

Three clauses, three jobs: stop at the end, one wording for items the
player carries, another for items in a room. The order of the result
follows the order of the alist, because the recursion walks it from
the front.

*** Exercise 8.11

#+begin_src lisp
(defun item-names (items)
  "Return a string listing ITEMS by name."
  (cond ((null items) "")
        ((null (cdr items))
         (item-name (first items)))
        ((null (cddr items))
         (concatenate 'string
                      (item-name (first items))
                      " and "
                      (item-name (second items))))
        (t
         (concatenate 'string
                      (item-name (first items))
                      ", "
                      (item-names (rest items))))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (item-names nil)
      (item-names '(key))
      (item-names '(key lamp))
      (item-names '(key lamp coin))
      (item-names '(key lamp coin box)))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("" "key" "key and lamp" "key, lamp and coin" "key, lamp, coin and box")
#+end_example

=cddr= is "the rest of the rest", from Chapter 7. When it is =nil=,
the list has exactly two elements left, and that's the moment to write
"and" instead of a comma. The last two elements are always handled by
that clause, whichever call reaches them.

*** Exercise 8.12

#+begin_src lisp
(defun take-all (items)
  "Take every item in ITEMS."
  (cond ((null items) nil)
        (t (take (first items))
           (take-all (rest items)))))

(defun loot ()
  "Take everything lying in the current room."
  (let ((found (items-here (room-name *current-room*))))
    (if (null found)
        "There is nothing here to take."
        (progn
          (take-all found)
          (concatenate 'string
                       "You take "
                       (item-names found)
                       ".")))))
#+end_src

Check. Reset the items and walk to the kitchen first:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(list (loot) (loot) (inventory))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("You take key."
 "There is nothing here to take."
 "You are carrying: key.")
#+end_example

The second =loot= finds nothing, because the first one moved the key
into the player's hands.

=take-all= walks =found=, the list that =items-here= built before
anything was taken. =take= replaces =*item-locations*= with a new
list, but =found= is a separate list, so the walk isn't affected.

This answer needs the fixed =take= from the end of the Chapter 8
project step. With the old =take=, the second =loot= still finds the
hidden =(KEY . KITCHEN)= pair and says "You take key." again.

** Chapter 9

*** Exercise 9.1

#+begin_src lisp
(list (funcall #'list 1 2)
      (apply #'* 2 '(3 4))
      (mapcar #'length '((a) (b c) ()))
      (mapcar (lambda (x) (cons x x)) '(a b))
      (apply-twice (lambda (s) (concatenate 'string s "!")) "hi"))
#+end_src

Expected: =((1 2) 24 (1 2 0) ((A . A) (B . B)) "hi!!")=

| Expression                        | Result              | Why                                   |
|-----------------------------------+---------------------+---------------------------------------|
| =(funcall #'list 1 2)=            | =(1 2)=             | Same as =(list 1 2)=                  |
| =(apply #'* 2 '(3 4))=            | =24=                | Same as =(* 2 3 4)=                   |
| =(mapcar #'length ...)=           | =(1 2 0)=           | One length per inner list             |
| =(mapcar (lambda (x) ...) ...)=   | =((A . A) (B . B))= | Each symbol consed onto itself        |
| =(apply-twice ...)=               | ="hi!!"=            | One ="!"= per call, two calls         |

*** Exercise 9.6

*Wrong:* =twice= without =#'= is read as a variable, and there is no
such variable. Write =(mapcar #'twice '(1 2))=.

*Wrong:* =(f 10)= looks for a function named =f=. =f= is a variable
holding a function, so call it with =funcall=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun call-with-ten (f)
  (funcall f 10))
#+end_src

*Wrong:* the quote turns the =lambda= into a plain list. Remove it:
=(funcall (lambda (x) (+ x 1)) 5)=.

*Wrong:* the last argument of =apply= must be a list. Either
=(apply #'max '(3 7))= or =(funcall #'max 3 7)=.

Check the fixes:

#+begin_src lisp

      (call-with-ten #'twice)

      (apply #'max '(3 7)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((2 4) 20 6 7)=

*** Exercise 9.7

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-stepper (start step)
  "Return a function that returns START, then START+STEP, and so on."
  (let ((next start))
    (lambda ()
      (let ((current next))
        (incf next step)
        current))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((s (make-stepper 10 5)))
  (list (funcall s) (funcall s) (funcall s)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(10 15 20)=

The inner =let= saves the value to return before =incf= moves =next=
forward. Without it, the first call would return =15=.

#+begin_src lisp
(mapcar #'cons '(a b c) '(1 2 3))
#+end_src

Expected: =((A . 1) (B . 2) (C . 3))=

With two lists, =mapcar= passes one element from each to =cons=, which
builds one dotted pair per position.

*** Exercise 9.9

Expected: =("HEY" "HEY!")=

=*table-a*= stored the function object from the first definition, and
redefining =shout= doesn't touch it. =*table-b*= stored the name, so
=funcall= finds the new definition at the moment of the call. That's
why the game's =*commands*= table stores names.

(push '(i . inventory) *commands*)
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp

#+end_src

Expected: =T=

Both short forms point at existing functions, so =run-command= needs
no change. Run the =push= lines once only: running them again adds
duplicate pairs. They do no harm here, because =assoc= stops at the
first match, but the =help= command of the next exercise would list
them twice. To start clean, rerun the =defparameter= of =*commands*=
from the project step.

*** Exercise 9.11

#+begin_src lisp
(defun help ()
  "List the command words."
  (concatenate 'string
               "Commands: "
               (item-names (mapcar #'car *commands*))
               "."))

(defparameter *commands*
  '((help . help)
    (look . look)
    (inventory . inventory)
    (take . take)
    (go . go-to)
    (loot . loot))
  "Map each command word to the function that handles it.")
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(run-command '(help))
#+end_src

Expected: ="Commands: help, look, inventory, take, go and loot."=

=(mapcar #'car *commands*)= gives the list of command words, and
=item-names= turns any list of symbols into a string, so it works on
commands as well as on items. If you added =drop= or the short forms
from Exercise 9.10, they appear in the list too.

The table is rebuilt with =defparameter= here so that =help= appears
first. A =push= would also work.

** Chapter 10

*** Exercise 10.1

#+begin_src lisp
(list (remove-if-not #'evenp '(5 6 7 8))
      (find-if #'numberp '(a b 3 c 4))
      (count-if #'symbolp '(a 1 b 2 c))
      (position #\n "banana")
      (reduce #'* '(1 2 3 4))
      (every #'symbolp '())
      (remove-duplicates '(1 2 1 3 2)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((6 8) 3 3 2 24 T (1 3 2))=

| Expression                    | Result    | Why                                          |
|-------------------------------+-----------+----------------------------------------------|
| =(remove-if-not #'evenp ...)= | =(6 8)=   | Keeps the even numbers                       |
| =(find-if #'numberp ...)=     | =3=       | The first number, not all of them            |
| =(count-if #'symbolp ...)=    | =3=       | =a=, =b= and =c=                             |
| =(position #\n "banana")=     | =2=       | Counting from zero: b, a, n                  |
| =(reduce #'* ...)=            | =24=      | 1 × 2 × 3 × 4                                |
| =(every #'symbolp '())=       | =T=       | No element says no                           |
| =(remove-duplicates ...)=     | =(1 3 2)= | Keeps the last copy of each by default       |

*** Exercise 10.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-vowels (s)
  "Return the number of vowels in the string S."
  (count-if (lambda (c) (find c "aeiou"))
            (string-downcase s)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (count-vowels "Common Lisp")
      (count-vowels "AEIOU")
      (count-vowels "xyz"))
#+end_src

Expected: =(3 5 0)=

=string-downcase= deals with upper case once, so the list of vowels
needs only lower-case letters. =find= returns the character when it is
a vowel and =nil= otherwise, which is all =count-if= needs.

*** Exercise 10.7

#+begin_src lisp
(list (eql 2 2)
      (eql 2 2.0)
      (equal "a" "a")
      (equal '(1 (2)) '(1 (2)))
      (equalp "Lisp" "LISP")
      (eql (list 1) (list 1)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(T NIL T T T NIL)=

=(eql 2 2.0)= is =NIL= because one is an integer and the other a
float. The last one is =NIL= because each =list= call builds its own
cons, and =eql= asks whether they are the same object.

*** Exercise 10.8

*Wrong:* =sort= modifies the quoted constant ='(3 1 2)=. Write
=(sort (list 3 1 2) #'<)=.

*Wrong:* =reduce= on an empty list calls =(max)=, which needs at least
one argument. Give a starting value:
=(reduce #'max '() :initial-value 0)=.

*Wrong:* =find= compares with =eql=, and two strings are different
objects. Write =(find "b" '("a" "b") :test #'string=)=.

*Wrong:* =remove-if= returns a new list and leaves =*numbers*= alone.
Store the result:
=(setf *numbers* (remove-if #'evenp *numbers*))=.

Check the fixes:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *numbers* (list 1 2 3 4))
(list (sort (list 3 1 2) #'<)
      (reduce #'max '() :initial-value 0)
      (find "b" '("a" "b") :test #'string=)
      (setf *numbers* (remove-if #'evenp *numbers*))
      *numbers*)
#+end_src

Expected: =((1 2 3) 0 "b" (1 3) (1 3))=

*** Exercise 10.9

#+begin_src lisp
(reduce (lambda (a b)
          (if (> (length b) (length a)) b a))
        '("hi" "hello" "hey"))
#+end_src

Expected: ="hello"=

=a= is the longest string so far, =b= the next one. The =lambda= keeps
whichever is longer. With =>=, a tie keeps the earlier string.

*** Exercise 10.10

#+begin_src lisp
(defun my-every (pred lst)
  "Return T if PRED says yes to every element of LST."
  (not (some (complement pred) lst)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (my-every #'plusp '(1 2 3))
      (my-every #'plusp '(1 -2 3))
      (my-every #'plusp '()))
#+end_src

Expected: =(T NIL T)=

"Every element says yes" means "no element says no". =(complement
pred)= is the "says no" test, =some= looks for one, and =not= flips
the answer. The empty list gives =T=, as =every= does.

*** Exercise 10.11

#+begin_src lisp
(defun empty-rooms ()
  "Return the names of the rooms with no items lying in them."
  (remove-if (lambda (name) (items-here name))
             (mapcar #'car *rooms*)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *item-locations*
  '((lamp . cellar)
    (key . kitchen)
    (letter . bedroom)))
(empty-rooms)
#+end_src

Expected: =(HALLWAY GARDEN)=, with the rooms in the order your
=*rooms*= lists them.

=(mapcar #'car *rooms*)= gives the room names, and =items-here=
returns =nil= for an empty room, so =remove-if= drops every room that
has something in it. The lamp is in the cellar, which isn't one of
the rooms, so it doesn't count.

If you added the short forms or =drop=, they appear in their

** Chapter 11

*** Exercise 11.1

#+begin_src lisp
(list (loop for i from 1 to 4 collect (* i i))
      (loop for i below 4 sum i)
      (loop for x in '(a b c) for i from 0 collect (list i x))
      (loop for tail on '(1 2 3) collect (length tail))
      (dolist (x '(1 2 3)) x)
      (loop for x in '(5 8 2 9) count (> x 4))
      (loop for c across "hello" count (char= c #\l)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((1 4 9 16) 6 ((0 A) (1 B) (2 C)) (3 2 1) NIL 3 2)=

| Expression          | Result              | Why                                        |
|---------------------+---------------------+--------------------------------------------|
| Squares, 1 to 4     | =(1 4 9 16)=        | =to= includes 4                            |
| =sum= below 4       | =6=                 | 0 + 1 + 2 + 3                              |
| Two =for= clauses   | =((0 A) (1 B) (2 C))= | Both step together                        |
| =for tail on=       | =(3 2 1)=           | Tails of lengths 3, 2, 1                   |
| =dolist=            | =NIL=               | =dolist= returns =nil= without a result form |
| =count=             | =3=                 | =5=, =8= and =9= are above =4=             |
| =across= + =count=  | =2=                 | Two =l= in "hello"                         |

*** Exercise 11.2

#+begin_src lisp
(defun sum-to (n)
  "Return the sum of the integers from 1 to N."
  (let ((total 0))
    (dotimes (i (1+ n) total)
      (incf total i))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (sum-to 10) (sum-to 0))
#+end_src

Expected: =(55 0)=

=dotimes= counts from =0=, so the loop must run =n= plus one times to
include =n= itself: =(1+ n)=. The sum starts at =0=, and the first
pass adds =0=, which changes nothing. With =loop= the same job is
=(loop for i from 0 to n sum i)=, or from 1, since adding 0 is idle
work.

*** Exercise 11.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defun count-vowels (s)
  "Return the number of vowels in the string S."
  (loop for c across (string-downcase s)
        count (find c "aeiou")))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (count-vowels "Common Lisp") (count-vowels "xyz"))
#+end_src

Expected: =(3 0)=

=across= walks the string one character at a time, and =count= adds
one for every character that =find= locates in ="aeiou"=. The =loop=
version is shorter than the =count-if= version and does the same work.

*** Exercise 11.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun range (start end)
  "Return the integers from START up to, not including, END."
  (loop for i from start below end
        collect i))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (range 2 5) (range 0 3) (range 5 5))
#+end_src

Expected: =((2 3 4) (0 1 2) NIL)=

=below end= is the whole job: it stops before =end=, which is what
"up to, but not including" means. When =start= is not below =end=, the
loop runs no passes and =collect= builds an empty list. =(range 5 5)=
shows that: no error, just =nil=.

*** Exercise 11.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defun first-repeat (lst)
  "Return the first element of LST that appears again later."
  (loop for tail on lst
        when (member (first tail) (rest tail))
          return (first tail)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (first-repeat '(a b c b a))
      (first-repeat '(a b c))
      (first-repeat '(x x y)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(A NIL X)=

Walking the tails, the question for each tail is "does the rest of the
list hold another copy of my first element?" =member= answers that,
returning the rest of the list from the match, and =return= as a
clause leaves the loop with the element itself.

The second test is worth reading closely. =(first-repeat '(a b c))=
checks =a= against =(b c)=, then =b= against =(c)=, then =c= against
=nil=. None match, the loop ends, and a =loop= with no =return= gives
=nil=.

*** Exercise 11.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun split (lst)
  "Return two lists: the numbers in LST, then everything else."
  (loop for x in lst
        if (numberp x) collect x into numbers
        else collect x into others
        finally (return (list numbers others))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (split '(a 1 b 2 3)) (split '(1 2)) (split nil))
#+end_src

Expected: =(((1 2 3) (A B)) ((1 2)) (NIL))=

With =into=, the two lists are named variables that =finally= hands
back. Elements that fail =numberp= go to =others= through the =else=.

*** Exercise 11.7

*Wrong:* plain forms after =for= need =do=:
=(loop for i from 1 to 3 do (print i))=.

*Wrong:* =dolist= throws its body's values away. Either
=(mapcar (lambda (x) (* x 2)) '(1 2 3))= or
=(loop for x in '(1 2 3) collect (* x 2))=.

*Wrong:* =in= walks lists, and ="abc"= is a string. Use =across=:
=(loop for c across "abc" collect (char-upcase c))=.

*Wrong:* =finally= without =return= gives =nil=. Write
=finally (return total)=.

Check the fixes:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(print (loop for i from 1 to 3 do (print i)))
(print (loop for x in '(1 2 3) collect (* x 2)))
(print (loop for c across "abc" collect (char-upcase c)))
(print (loop with total = 0
             for x in '(1 2 3)
             do (incf total x)
             finally (return total)))
#+end_src

Expected output:

#+begin_example
1
2
3
(1 2 3) 
(A B C) 
6 
#+end_example

Each =print= of a result ends with a space and no newline, which is
why the last three lines sit where they do.

*** Exercise 11.8

#+begin_src lisp
(defun take-while (pred lst)
  "Return the front elements of LST that PRED says yes to."
  (loop for x in lst
        while (funcall pred x)
        collect x))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (take-while #'plusp '(1 3 -1 2))
      (take-while #'plusp '(-1 1))
      (take-while #'plusp '(1 2 3)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((1 3) NIL (1 2 3))=

=while= tests before each pass. When it fails at =-1=, the loop stops
and never collects =2=, even though =2= is positive. That is what
distinguishes =take-while= from =remove-if-not=: one keeps the front
run, the other keeps everything that matches.

*** Exercise 11.9

#+begin_src lisp
(loop for name in '(ada alan)
      for score in '(90 72)
      collect (cons name score))
#+end_src

Expected: =((ADA . 90) (ALAN . 72))=

Two =for= clauses step in step, =cons= builds each pair, and
=collect= gathers them. The destructuring pattern of Exercise 11.9's
question goes the other way: it takes an alist apart, this one builds
one.

*** Exercise 11.10

#+begin_src lisp
(list (loop for x in '(2 4 6) always (evenp x))
      (loop for x in '(1 2 3) never (minusp x))
      (loop for x in '(a b 3 c) thereis (numberp x))
      (loop for x in '(2 4) always (evenp x) finally (return 'checked)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(T T 3 CHECKED)=

| Expression    | Result    | Why                                              |
|---------------+-----------+--------------------------------------------------|
| =always=      | =T=       | Every element is even                            |
| =never=       | =T=       | No element is negative                           |
| =thereis=     | =3=       | Returns the value, not just =T=                  |
| The last one  | =CHECKED= | The list ran out, so =always= never said no      |

The last one is the subtle case. On ='(2 4)=, =always= never fails,
the list ends, and =finally= runs as usual, returning =CHECKED=. On a
list containing an odd number, =always= leaves the loop early and
returns =nil= without running =finally=. So =finally= after =always=
only runs when the loop survived to the end.

*** Exercise 11.11

#+begin_src lisp
(defun describe-all ()
  "Print one line per room, saying what lies in it."
  (dolist (entry *rooms*)
    (let ((name (room-name entry))
          (here (items-here (room-name entry))))
      (princ name)
      (princ ": ")
      (if (null here)
          (princ "nothing")
          (princ (item-names here)))

#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp :results output

    (letter . bedroom)))
(describe-all)
#+end_src

Expected output, in the order =*rooms*= lists them:

KITCHEN: key
GARDEN: nothing

#+end_example

=(room-name entry)= is the function from Chapter 5, which pulls the
name out of a room entry, so =dolist= walks =*rooms*= and the =let=
names the two things each pass needs. =princ= prints the symbols in
