the reader should expect to see if they try it interactively.

*** Exercise 20.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun load-compile-if-stale ()
  "Recompile stale files and everything after them, then load all."
  (let ((seen-stale nil))
    (with-compilation-unit ()
      (dolist (src *game-files*)
        (when (or seen-stale (stale-p src))
          (setf seen-stale t)
          (compile-file src))
        (load (fasl-for src))))))
#+end_src

The rule: once any file is stale, every later file gets
recompiled too — because later files might depend on the
changed one, and we don't know which. It's conservative and
correct for a serial chain.

The comment the exercise asks for:

This still gets several things wrong that ASDF gets right.
First, a change to the /last/ file in *game-files* forces a
recompile of just that file, not the files before it — so a
later-in-the-list file that /game-files* doesn't list (a
helper, a test) won't be rebuilt even though it depends on
the changed thing. Second, =file-write-date= has one-second
resolution on some filesystems, so two edits in the same
second look identical. Third, files that /depend on each
other out of order/ (a macro in the last file, used by the
first) break the "stale from here on" rule entirely — the
recompile cascade has to go both directions, or the
dependency graph has to be known. Fourth, fasl extensions are
implementation-specific, and mixing two Lisps in one
directory corrupts the cache; ASDF versions fasls per
implementation. Fifth — and this is the big one — ASDF
tracks /which files changed/, so a change to one file
recompiles only its dependents, not "the file and everything
after it in a list order that was chosen by the author, not
the dependencies." The toy is honest about its lesson: a
system definition is a dependency-aware make, and anything
short of one will lie eventually. Chapter 16's =defsystem=
was already the right shape; the exercise is worth doing to
see /why/ the shape is right, not to build a replacement.

** Chapter 21

*** Exercise 21.1

#+begin_src lisp
(list (typep 3 '(integer 0 2))     ; => NIL — 3 is outside the bound
      (typep 3 '(or null integer)) ; => T — union types
      (typep :red '(member :red :green :blue)) ; => T
      (typep "abc" '(simple-array character (3))) ; => T
      (typep '(1 2) '(cons integer (cons integer null))) ; => T
      (subtypep 'null 'list)       ; => T, T — NIL is a list
      (subtypep 'null 'symbol))    ; => T, T — and a symbol too
#+end_src

The last two are the same door from two frames: =null= (the type
of =NIL=) is a subtype of both =list= and =symbol=, which don't
overlap with each other in any interesting way. Types in Lisp
aren't a tree of disjoint boxes — they're sets, and a value sits
in every set it belongs to. =(cons integer (cons integer null))=
shows the recursive spine of list types: a cons whose =cdr= is
another such cons or =nil= — exactly the two-element proper list
=(1 2)=.

The ="abc"= check works because a string literal in source is a
=simple-array character= of its length; a string built by
=concatenate= or =make-string= may or may not have the same
type, depending on the implementation. That's the difference
between =typep= and "is the value a string": =typep "abc"
'string= is always true, but =(simple-array character (3))= is
the specific type SBCL happens to give the literal.

*** Exercise 21.2

*Wrong:* =declare= must precede all executable forms in the
body — the =format= makes it a declaration after a form, and
the compiler signals an error. Fix: move the =declare= to the
top:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun f (x)
  (declare (type fixnum x))
  (format t "x is ~A~%" x)
  (* x 2))
#+end_src

*Wrong:* =declaim= declares, but =defparameter= /rebinds/
unconstrained — the declaration applies to the variable named
=*player-name*=, and the =defparameter= assigns a symbol to a
declared-string variable: a type conflict SBCL warns about at
the =defparameter=. The symbol =anon= is not a string. Fix by
making the initial value match:

#+begin_src lisp
(declaim (type string *player-name*))
(defparameter *player-name* "anon")
#+end_src

The general lesson: a global declaration is a promise about
/every/ future assignment — including the ones your tests and
REPL experiments make.

*Wrong:* at =(safety 0)= the compiler trusts =fixnum= and
=1+ "oops"= runs unchecked — garbage, a memory fault, or
silently wrong data, depending on the machine. With the
default =(safety 1)= the same call signals a =type-error=
before any arithmetic. The snippet is the chapter's warning in
one line: safety 0 plus a wrong declaration is undefined
behaviour; keep safety 1 until declarations are proven right,
and this example never is.

*** Exercise 21.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defun safe-elt (seq index)
  "Element of SEQ at INDEX, checked."
  (check-type index (integer 0 #.most-positive-fixnum))
  (elt seq index))

(defun fast-elt (seq index)
  "Unchecked: caller guarantees a valid fixnum index."
  (declare (optimize (speed 3) (safety 0))
           (type fixnum index))
  (elt seq index))
#+end_src

The comment:

safe-elt belongs in the game, because a bad index there is a
bug to surface, not a cost to shave — the interactive
type-error restart even lets you fix the variable in place.
fast-elt is for measured hot paths only, and its docstring
states the contract it no longer checks. "Almost everywhere"
means everywhere you haven't profiled. The deeper point the
pair teaches: check-type and declare are two ways to say the
same thing — "this will be of this type" — one verified at
run time as a service to the user, one trusted at compile
time as a service to the compiler.

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (safe-elt "abc" 1)
      (safe-elt '(a b c) 2)
      (fast-elt (vector 10 20 30) 1))
;; => (#\b C 20)
#+end_src

And the divergence, when both are given a bad index:

#+begin_src lisp
(ignore-errors (safe-elt "abc" -1))   ; => NIL — signal caught
(ignore-errors (fast-elt "abc" -1))   ; => implementation-dependent
#+end_src

At =safety 0=, =fast-elt='s =(elt "abc" -1)= may return garbage,
segfault, or (on some builds) still signal — the standard
doesn't say. That's what "undefined behaviour" means concretely.

*** Exercise 21.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun type-chain-p (value types)
  "Is VALUE's type a subtype of every type in TYPES?"
  (let ((vtype (type-of value)))
    (every (lambda (tt) (subtypep vtype tt)) types)))

(type-chain-p 5 '(integer fixnum number))  ; => T on most machines
#+end_src

The return is =T=, given =type-of= returned a fixnum subtype —
SBCL's =(integer 0 4611686018427387903)= is a subtype of
=fixnum=, =integer= and =number=. What it tells you: types
overlap — the one value =5= sits in all three sets
simultaneously, because Lisp types are sets, not a disjoint
class hierarchy. The caveat worth a comment:

type-of returns some type the value belongs to, not
necessarily the most specific one that matters to you. The
function answers about the implementation's chosen
representation, not about the value's abstract type. For
intent, test the value against the types directly with typep:
(type-chain-p 5 '(integer fixnum number)) is true, but
(type-chain-p 5 '(unsigned-byte 3)) might or might not be,
depending on whether type-of on this SBCL returned
(integer 0 4611686018427387903) — which is not a subtype of
(unsigned-byte 3), even though 5 is. The function is testing
the implementation's answer, not 5's.

That's the sharp version of the lesson: =subtypep= on types is
not the same question as =typep= on values, and =type-of=
bridges them in a way that can hide what you meant.

*** Exercise 21.5

A typical run (yours will differ — that's why the exercise
demands reporting your own):

#+begin_example
;; (a) no declarations:  ~8.0 ms
;; (b) (the double-float ...) + declared accumulator: ~5.0 ms
;; (c) + declared (simple-array double-float (*)) + speed 3: ~1.2 ms
#+end_example

With the code:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *v*
  (make-array 1000000 :element-type 'twice-float
                       :initial-element 1.0d0))

;; (a)
(defun sum-a (v)
  (loop for x across v sum x))

;; (b)
(defun sum-b (v)
  (let ((total 0.0d0))
    (declare (type double-float total))
    (loop for x across v
          do (incf total (the double-float x)))
    total))

;; (c)
(defun sum-c (v)
  (declare (optimize (speed 3) (safety 1))
           (type (simple-array double-float (*)) v))
  (let ((total 0.0d0))
    (declare (type double-float total))
    (loop for x across v
          do (incf total (the double-float x)))
    total))

(time (sum-a *v*))
(time (sum-b *v*))
(time (sum-c *v*))
#+end_src

Which declaration bought the most: the array type — (b)→(c).
Declaring the accumulator removed float boxing of the sum, but
declaring the array removed the per-element type dispatch, the
dominant cost. The order is the chapter's order in miniature:
accumulate =the= declarations cheaply, but the data-structure
declaration is where the compiler stops guessing.

One honest caveat: even sum-c is not what an optimising C
compiler would produce for the same loop, because SBCL
doesn't vectorise. To go faster you'd reach for =sb-simd= or
a C library via CFFI (Chapter 29). The chapter's point is that
declarations bring you a factor of five or ten, not that they
turn Lisp into Fortran.

*** Exercise 21.6

The =declaim= and its requirement:

#+begin_src lisp
(declaim (type fixnum *moves*))
(defparameter *moves* 0)   ; a fixnum — the declaration demands it
#+end_src

The =play= check runs reliably because every assignment site
(=incf *moves*= in the command loop) preserves fixnum-ness —
and at =(safety 1)=, the moment some future edit assigns a
string, the error surfaces loudly instead of corrupting.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun drift-check ()
  "Type-check the game's global state; return failing names."
  (let ((expected
          (list (cons '*moves* 'fixnum)
                (cons '*current-room* '(or null room))
                (cons '*item-locations* 'list)
                (cons '*room-list* 'list)
                (cons '*visits* '(simple-vector)))))
    (loop for (name . type) in expected
          unless (typep (symbol-value name) type)
            collect name)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *moves* "oops")     ; a drift
(drift-check)             ; => (*MOVES*)
(setf *moves* 3)          ; back to normal
(drift-check)             ; => NIL
#+end_src

Two notes on the type list. =(or null room)= for =*current-room*=
permits the initial =nil= as well as a real room; the game's
=play= sets =*current-room*= immediately, so the =nil= case
never survives long, but the type spec is honest about the
declared default. =(simple-vector)= for =*visits*= is the general
vector type — =(vector fixnum)= would also work, but SBCL's
actual type for a =fixnum=-filled array is =(simple-array fixnum
(*))=, and =simple-vector= is the closest abstract name that
covers it. When in doubt, =(typep x (type-of example))=
confirms.

The kind of bug =drift-check= catches: a /latent/ wrong-typed
assignment — one that's been sitting in the image for an hour,
poisoning state quietly, and will bite somewhere far from its
cause. At =(safety 0)= a wrong =declaim= turns that same
assignment into corruption with no error at all — the value
lands, memory gets reinterpreted, and the symptom appears
anywhere but there. =drift-check= is the safety-net
counterpart: same type knowledge, checked /now/, on demand,
reporting /which/ variable drifted. Between them — checked
types in development, declared types in profiled hot spots —
lies the whole practice.

** Chapter 22

*** Exercise 22.1

Without the interposed =nil=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(+ 2 3)          ; => 5      — * is now 5
(* * 4)          ; => 20     — * is 20, ** is 5
(list * ** ***)  ; => (20 5 <prior>)
#+end_src

The three elements of the last line: =20= (the value from
=(* * 4)=), =5= (the value from =(+ 2 3)=), and whatever was =*=
/before/ =(+ 2 3)= — usually the return value of the previous
REPL command, or a fresh-session error if there wasn't one.

With the interposed =nil=:

#+begin_src lisp :results output
(+ 2 3)          ; => 5
(* * 4)          ; => 20
nil              ; => NIL    — * is NIL, ** is 20, *** is 5
(list * ** ***)  ; => (NIL 20 5)
#+end_src

The interposed form shifted everything back by one slot. This is
the exercise's whole point: =*= doesn't mean "the answer I care
about" — it means "the last value the REPL happened to print",
and every evaluation silently redefines it. Any evaluation
between two usages changes what the next usage sees. For
anything you actually want to reason about, bind it with
=defparameter=.

*** Exercise 22.2

=CSS (describe #'append)= prints the signature — =append= takes
any number of arguments, all of which must be lists except
possibly the last — and its docstring. The point the exercise
makes: you can answer "what arguments does this take" without
reading any source.

=CSS (describe *commands*)= prints the alist's type and the list
of =(name . function)= pairs currently in it. Read from that
output alone: =*commands*= is a =LIST=, and each entry is a cons
whose car is a symbol and whose cdr is a function or symbol.
Even without knowing Lisp's alist convention, the shape is
visible: each pair names a thing and points at a thing.

That's why =describe= is the first tool in unfamiliar code:
both "what does this function want" and "what shape is this
data" are one command away, and no source is needed for either.

*** Exercise 22.3

The inspector shows a =type-error= has exactly two interesting
slots: =datum=, holding =5=, and =expected-type=, holding
=LIST=.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun datum-or-message (fn)
  (handler-case (funcall fn)
    (type-error (c)
      (format nil "Wanted ~A, got ~S."
              (type-error-expected-type c)
              (type-error-datum c)))))

(datum-or-message (lambda () (length 5)))
;; => "Wanted LIST, got 5."
#+end_src

Handlers read slots, never the printed message — Chapter 15's
rule, now with the inspector as the way to /discover/ what slots
exist. The =~S= on =datum= is what keeps =5= (a number) distinct
from ="5"= (a string) in the output; that's exactly the case
where ~~A~ would lie.

*** Exercise 22.4

a) =trace= — it shows each call's arguments and result; when the
shape is right, the inputs and outputs of the inner calls tell
you which step corrupts. =step= also works but is slower for a
function with many calls.

b) =apropos= — substring search over all symbol names;
=(apropos "price")= finds it or proves it doesn't exist.

c) =time= — it reports milliseconds and allocation directly.
=trace= would show calls but not cost; reasoning about "feels
slow" without measuring is guessing.

d) =inspect= — one buffer, all slots and structure, navigable.
=describe= prints a summary and stops; =print= shows a one-line
view.

e) =step= — it walks subform by subform, which is exactly "watch
each expression". =trace= only fires on function boundaries; a
loop body isn't one.

f) =break= — it stops with the data in scope and resumes cleanly.
=error= unwinds and loses the spot; =inspect= from outside needs
you to reconstruct the situation first.

*** Exercise 22.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defun safe-run (fn)
  "Run FN; return (:OK value) or (:ERROR message)."
  (handler-case (list :ok (funcall fn))
    (error (c) (list :error (princ-to-string c)))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (safe-run #'+)
      (safe-run (lambda () (/ 1 0))))
#+end_src

Expected: =((:OK 0) (:ERROR "..."))= — the first result is
=(:OK 0)= because =(+ )= with no arguments is 0, which is a
reminder that =#'+= is a function taking any number of
arguments, and =funcall= calls it with zero. The second
result's message is implementation-flavoured — something like
="arithmetic error DIVISION-BY-ZERO signalled"= — which is
exactly why the exercise had to say "message" and not "error
type": a program that /acts/ on the error reads the condition's
type and slots; a program that /reports/ the error uses
=princ-to-string=, and the exact text doesn't matter.

*** Exercise 22.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun whereis (command)
  "Handle (WHEREIS ITEM): list rooms ITEM has appeared in."
  (let* ((name (second command))
         (present (assoc name *item-locations*))
         (seen nil))
    (dolist (snapshot *history*)
      (let ((loc (cdr (assoc name snapshot))))
        (when loc (pushnew loc seen))))
    (let ((current (and present (cdr present))))
      (when (and current (not (eq current 'player)))
        (pushnew current seen)))
    (cond ((null present)
           (format nil "No ~(~A~) here or in the log." name))
          ((null seen)
           (format nil "The ~(~A~) hasn't moved yet." name))
          (t
           (format nil "The ~(~A~) has been in: ~{~(~A~)~^, ~}."
                   name (reverse seen))))))
#+end_src

Add the command:

#+begin_src lisp
(push '(whereis . whereis) *commands*)
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *item-locations* '((key . kitchen) (lamp . cellar)))
(setf *history* nil)
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))

(run-command '(take key))
(run-command '(go hallway))
(run-command '(drop key))
(run-command '(whereis key))
#+end_src

Expected: ="The key has been in: kitchen, hallway."= — the
kitchen from the snapshot before =take=, the hallway from the
snapshot before =drop=, and the current state (hallway) already
in the list from the drop.

The condition question the exercise asks about: an unknown item
is a =nil= lookup, not an error, so no =handler-case= is needed.
The handling belongs in the command function, not in
run-command=, because run-command= cannot know what "unknown"
means for each command — for =whereis= it's a friendly message,
for a future =examine= it might be a "you see no such thing" and
for a =teleport-to-item= it might be a genuine error. Only the
command function has the semantics.

The distinction is Chapter 15's flags-vs-conditions rule, at a
finer grain: within a single command, some failures are flags
(the player typed a non-item) and some are conditions (the item
registry is corrupt). run-command's handler catches the second
kind; whereis's own logic handles the first. The exercise's
"where should this go" is really "which layer knows what
'unknown' means" — and the answer is always "the layer that has
the domain knowledge".

*** Exercise 22.7

Three bugs, three symptoms, one message per bug:

*Bug 1 — break =item-names=.*

#+begin_src lisp
(defun item-names (items)
  (declare (ignore items))
  nil)
#+end_src

Symptom: =inventory= prints ="You are carrying: ."= — the item
list is empty in the format string, but there's no error. =(doctor)=
reports "All clear", because the game's *data* is consistent —
=*item-locations*= still has the right associations. A *function*
is wrong. The lesson: =doctor= checks data, not behaviour.

*Bug 2 — break =*current-room*=.*

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* 'ballroom)
#+end_src

Symptom: =look= fails with a type error on (room-name
*current-room*) — a symbol isn't a room structure. But
=inventory= still works, because it doesn't read
=*current-room*=. And =doctor= reports "Current room is not in
*rooms*" — the check you added in the project step catches
the drift. =doctor= checks *data structure integrity*, not just
data consistency.

*Bug 3 — remove a room from =*rooms*= while items are in it.*

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *rooms*
      (remove (find-room 'kitchen) *rooms*))
#+end_src

Symptom: =go-to 'kitchen= from the hallway says "You can't go
there from here" — the hallway's exit list still names kitchen,
but =find-room= returns nil. And =items-here= on kitchen
errors; =doctor= reports ="key is in unknown room KITCHEN"=
(and any item that was in the kitchen), because the
=find-room= check catches it.

The catalogue's pattern: data drift is loud (conditions,
=doctor= catches it), function damage is silent (wrong values,
no signal). =doctor= is how you give the silent kind a voice —
and the redefinition loop is how you fix it, one =C-c C-c= per
definition, no restart.

** Chapter 23

*** Exercise 23.1

#+begin_src lisp
(list (symbol-name 'kitchen)    ; => "KITCHEN"
      (symbol-package :kitchen) ; => #<PACKAGE "KEYWORD">
      (symbol-name :kitchen)    ; => "KITCHEN" — keywords' names, too
      (eq 'kitchen 'KITCHEN)    ; => T — the reader upcased both
      (eq (intern "KITCHEN") 'kitchen) ; => T — same table, same symbol
      (symbol-package (gensym))) ; => NIL — uninterned
#+end_src

The =intern= line is the reader's own machinery, used by hand:
=intern= looks the string up in a package's table (default current
package), creating the symbol if absent. Reading =kitchen= /is=
interning ="KITCHEN"= — which is why the =eq= holds: there is only
one symbol named =KITCHEN= home in a package, and both expressions
reach it.

The last line's =NIL= is the tell for an uninterned symbol: no
package holds it, so =symbol-package= has nothing to return. Every
interned symbol has a non-=nil= package; every uninterned one has
=nil=. There is no third case.

*** Exercise 23.2

*Wrong:* =(get "kitchen" 'description)= signals an error —
=get='s first argument must be a symbol, because the property
lives /on/ it. Fix: use the symbol =(get 'kitchen 'description)=,
or if the key arrives as a string, =(get (intern (string-upcase
key)) 'description)=.

*Surprising:* the =remprop= line removes the property for the
/whole image/. The later =(get 'kitchen 'exits)= returns =nil= —
not because state was lost in a crash, but because something
else deleted it. Property lists are global mutable state
attached to shared symbols; the fix is not a different call but
a different structure for anything that has versions, owners or
a save file (an alist or hash table, as the chapter argued).

*Surprising:* =props= was captured /before/ the =remprop=, but
it still /changes/: =symbol-plist= returned the actual list the
symbol points at, and =remprop= mutates that same list in place.
The captured variable doesn't hold a copy — it holds a live
reference to the shared plist. If a snapshot was wanted, copy
element-wise /after/ the mutation you care about:
=(copy-list (symbol-plist 'kitchen))=. Even then, the plist has
no honest snapshot in general — properties come and go
independently of any single structure.

*Surprising:* =(getf '(name "hall" name "cellar") :name)=
returns =nil= — the plist's keys are the symbols =name= (not
keywords =:name=), and =eq= never matches =name= against
=:name=. A plist's keys must be compared with the same
interned symbols you search with — the commonest plist bug,
and the reason keyword keys are the convention: =:name= typed
anywhere is the same object, so lookups never fail on case or
internship accidents.

*** Exercise 23.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defun plist-p (list)
  "Does LIST have an even number of elements?"
  (evenp (length list)))

(defun plist->alist (plist)
  "Convert (a 1 b 2) to ((A . 1) (B . 2))."
  (loop for (k v) on plist by #'cddr
        collect (cons k v)))

(defun alist->plist (alist)
  "Convert ((A . 1) (B . 2)) to (a 1 b 2)."
  (loop for (k . v) in alist
        append (list k v)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(plist-p '(a 1 b 2))                     ; => T
(plist-p '(a 1 b))                       ; => NIL
(plist->alist '(a 1 b 2))                ; => ((A . 1) (B . 2))
(alist->plist (plist->alist '(a 1 b 2))) ; => (A 1 B 2)
#+end_src

The honest answer to "which direction loses information": for
well-formed inputs, /neither/. Both structures hold the same
objects, in the same order, paired the same way. The difference
is representation, not information: an alist is a list of
conses (each pair is one compound object); a plist is a flat
list where keys and values are siblings.

What /changes/ is the lookup contract. On either structure, a
key whose value is =nil= is indistinguishable from an absent
key when the lookup returns just the value:

#+begin_src lisp
(cdr (assoc 'b '((a . 1) (b . nil))))   ; => NIL — present with nil
(cdr (assoc 'c '((a . 1) (b . nil))))   ; => NIL — absent
(getf '(a 1 b nil) 'b)                  ; => NIL — present with nil
(getf '(a 1 b nil) 'c)                  ; => NIL — absent
#+end_src

Both structures need the found-flag protocol to disambiguate —
=assoc='s =nil= result is the flag (nil means absent); =getf=
returns just the value, and =get-properties='s first return is
the flag. The "loss" isn't in conversion; it's that neither
structure's plain lookup tells you the difference, and the
converter can't fix that for you.

A second, smaller point: converting a plist with duplicate keys
to an alist preserves both entries, and vice versa. Duplicates
are a separate bug, not a loss.

*** Exercise 23.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun plist-keys (plist)
  "Distinct keys of a plist, in first-occurrence order."
  (let ((seen nil))
    (loop for (k v) on plist by #'cddr
          do (pushnew k seen)
          finally (return (nreverse seen)))))
#+end_src

=pushnew= keeps the accumulation distinct, using =eql= by
default — the right equality for keyword/symbol keys. On a
plist with a duplicated key, =plist-keys= returns that key
once; the duplication itself is silently hidden, which is why
=plist-well-formed-p= is a separate (and useful) check.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun plist-well-formed-p (plist)
  "Even length, no duplicate keys."
  (and (plist-p plist)
       (let ((keys (loop for (k v) on plist by #'cddr collect k)))
         (null (remove-duplicates keys :test #'eq :from-end t)))))
#+end_src

=remove-duplicates= returning a shorter list is the duplicate
detector.

#+begin_src lisp
(defun plist-subset-p (sub super)
  "Does SUPER contain every key/value pair of SUB?"
  (loop for (k v) on sub by #'cddr
        always (multiple-value-bind (found-key found-value tail)
                   (get-properties super (list k))
                 (declare (ignore tail))
                 (and found-key (equal found-value v)))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(plist-subset-p '(:a 1) '(:a 1 :b 2))    ; => T
(plist-subset-p '(:a 1 :c 3) '(:a 1))    ; => NIL
(plist-subset-p '(:a nil) '(:a nil))     ; => T — present with nil
#+end_src

The =get-properties= return is three values: the found key
(nil if absent), its value, and the tail of the plist from
that key onward. The first value is what =getf= alone can't
give you — =getf= returns =nil= both for absent and for
present-with-nil. =get-properties='s found-key is the flag.

=equal= on the values (rather than =eq=) is what makes the
function work for strings and lists, not just symbols.

*** Exercise 23.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro swap (a b)
  (let ((tmp (gensym "SWAP-")))
    `(let ((,tmp ,a))
       (setf ,a ,b
             ,b ,tmp))))

(macroexpand-1 '(swap x y))
;; => (LET ((#:SWAP-427 x)) (SETF X Y Y #:SWAP-427))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((x 1) (y 2))
  (swap x y)
  (list x y))   ; => (2 1)
#+end_src

Now the naive version and its bug:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmacro swap-broken (a b)
  `(let ((tmp ,a))
     (setf ,a ,b ,b tmp)))

(macroexpand-1 '(swap-broken x tmp))
;; => (LET ((TMP X)) (SETF X TMP TMP TMP))
#+end_src

The expansion is the whole story. When the caller's second
argument is literally the variable =tmp=, the macro's
=(let ((tmp ...)) ...)= shadows it. =(setf tmp tmp)= writes
the /macro's/ =tmp= with the /macro's/ =tmp= — a no-op — and
the caller's =tmp= is never touched. Swap fails silently, and
only for that one caller.

=gensym= manufactures a symbol no caller can have written —
it's uninterned, so it can't appear in source text — closing
the capture hole. This is the "variable capture" trap from
Chapter 14 in its most classic form.

*** Exercise 23.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun describe-flavour (item)
  "Return the flavour text of ITEM, or a default."
  (or (get item 'flavour)
      (format nil "Just a plain ~(~A~)." item)))

(setf (get 'key 'flavour) "Brass, and warmer than it should be.")
(setf (get 'lamp 'flavour) "Its light doesn't quite reach the floor.")

(list (describe-flavour 'key)
      (describe-flavour 'lamp)
      (describe-flavour 'sword))
#+end_src

Expected:

#+begin_example
("Brass, and warmer than it should be."
 "Its light doesn't quite reach the floor."
 "Just a plain sword.")
#+end_example

The comment the exercise asks for:

Flavour text is (a) an attribute of a named thing, consulted by
name alone — the plist's natural shape; (b) fixed at definition
time, so it never needs snapshotting, versioning, or save-game;
(c) global by nature — the flavour of the key is the same every
session, which is exactly the intent. Item *locations* are the
opposite on all three: they change during play, get saved and
restored, and would be spread unownably across symbols on a
plist. Chapter 7 gave them an alist for exactly that reason.
The dividing line, in one sentence: plists for constants about
names, owned structures for state about things.

** Chapter 24

*** Exercise 24.1

#+begin_src lisp
(list (multiple-value-list (values))      ; => NIL — zero values
      (multiple-value-list (values 1))    ; => (1)
      (multiple-value-list (values 1 2 3))  ; => (1 2 3)
      (+ (values 1 2) (values 3 4))       ; => 4 — primaries only: 1 + 3
      (list (values 1 2) (values 3 4))    ; => (1 3)
      (values (values 1 2) (values 3 4))) ; => two values: 1 and 3
#+end_src

Expected: =(NIL (1) (1 2 3) 4 (1 3) 1)=.

#+begin_example
(NIL (1) (1 2 3) 4 (1 3) 1)
#+end_example

Where each element came from:

- =(multiple-value-list (values))= → =NIL= (empty, because zero
  values)
- =(multiple-value-list (values 1))= → =(1)= (one value)
- =(multiple-value-list (values 1 2 3))= → =(1 2 3)=
- =(+ (values 1 2) (values 3 4))= → =4= (the primaries 1 and 3,
  summed)
- =(list (values 1 2) (values 3 4))= → =(1 3)= (the list's
  two argument positions each kept one value)
- The final =(values (values 1 2) (values 3 4))= → two values,
  =1= and =3= — but it sits in an argument position of =list=,
  which is a single-value context. =list= keeps only the
  primary, =1=. That is the last element of the result, and the
  =3= is discarded.

Two surprises worth naming. First, (values)= produces /zero/
values — and =multiple-value-list= faithfully gives the empty
list =nil=, which is "no values collected". The distinction
matters only in a multiple-value context; anywhere else, zero
values and one nil value both read as nil. Second, values=
doesn't nest: each argument to a =values= call is evaluated in
single-value context, so the outer =values= sees only the
primaries of its arguments. Values are flat.

*** Exercise 24.2

*Surprising:* =x= binds the primary value only — =:a= — and
=:b= is gone. =(let ((x (two-things))) x)= prints =A=. Fix:
=multiple-value-bind= or =multiple-value-list= at the call:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (x y)
    (two-things)
  (list x y))   ; => (:A :B)
#+end_src

*Surprising:* =(:DONE 3)=, not =(:DONE 2)=. The snippet works —
and the element that surprises people is the =3=, not the
multi-value part. =loop= puts an implicit block named =nil=
around its body, so (return (values :done i))= is
(return-from nil (values :done i))=. return-from= passes
/every/ value of its form to the block, and the loop's =finally=
clause runs after the last iteration — with =i= already stepped
to =3= — so the loop returns =:done= and =3=, and the outer
=multiple-value-bind= catches both:

#+begin_example
(:DONE 3)
#+end_example

The point the exercise makes is that =loop='s return values
are governed by =return=, which preserves them — same as
=return-from=. What truncates in this snippet is not the loop
but a =let= if you introduce one:

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((pair (loop for i below 3
                  finally (return (values :done i)))))
  pair)   ; => :DONE — pair binds one value
#+end_src

=let= truncates, =multiple-value-bind= doesn't. The trap is
which binding form you use to catch the loop's result.

*Surprising:* =gethash= returns =nil= and =t=: the key /is/
present, holding =nil=. The naive reading "nil means absent" is
exactly what the second value refutes. This is the
correct-behaviour example, not a bug — the fix is for code that
ignored the flag:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (val found-p) (gethash :k table)
  (if found-p val :absent))   ; => NIL (the stored one)
#+end_src

*Surprising:* ~(1)=, not~(1 2)=. The function's body is the
variable reference =form-values= — but the /caller's/ argument
position =(pass-through (values 1 2))= is a single-value
context, so =pass-through= received 1 before any body ran. No
function body can recover values its caller truncated. Fix:
take a /thunk= and call it in a multiple-value context:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun pass-through (thunk)
  (funcall thunk))              ; body end: values preserved
(multiple-value-list (pass-through (lambda () (values 1 2))))
;; => (1 2)
#+end_src

The thunk receives no argument, so its result's values reach
multiple-value-list= intact. (funcall thunk)= is the
function body's last form, which is a multiple-value context —
the values pass through unchanged. That's the entire
difference between "function that takes a value" and "function
that takes a computation".

*** Exercise 24.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defun safe-divide (a b)
  "Two values: quotient, and :DIVISION-BY-ZERO or NIL."
  (if (zerop b)
      (values nil :division-by-zero)
      (values (/ a b) nil)))

(defun safe-sum (pairs)
  "Sum of quotients; two values: total, failed-p."
  (let ((total 0)
        (failed nil))
    (dolist (pair pairs (values total failed))
      (multiple-value-bind (q err)
          (safe-divide (first pair) (second pair))
        (if err
            (setf failed t)
            (incf total q))))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (multiple-value-list (safe-sum '((7 2) (9 3))))
      (multiple-value-list (safe-sum '((7 0) (9 3))))
      (multiple-value-list (safe-sum nil)))
#+end_src

Expected: =((13/2 NIL) (3 T) (0 NIL))=.

The first case: (safe-divide 7 2) gives 7/2=, (safe-divide
9 3) gives 3=, sum 13/2=, no failure — (13/2 NIL).

The second: =(safe-divide 7 0)= gives =(nil :division-by-zero)=
— failed, so the sum skips it and adds only the 9/3='s =3=.
=(3 T)=.

The third: empty input, =total= starts at =0=, =failed=
stays =nil=. =(0 NIL)=.

The shape to notice: =safe-sum= propagates failure as a flag,
not a condition, and callers who don't care about failure just
use the total. It's =gethash='s contract, rebuilt by hand.
Compare:

- =gethash= returns value + found-flag. Callers ignoring the
  flag get the value or =nil=. Callers binding both get the
  distinction.
- =safe-sum= returns total + failed-flag. Same shape.
- =(handler-case ...)= is for /bugs= — errors the caller
  couldn't have anticipated. Flags are for /predicted failures=
  — errors the caller routinely branches on.

The rule of thumb: if the caller's natural response is "check
the second value and maybe react", use a flag. If the
caller's natural response is "let someone higher up deal
with it", signal a condition.

*** Exercise 24.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun compose-values (f g)
  "Return a function that applies G to all of F's values."
  (lambda (&rest args)
    (multiple-value-call g (apply f args))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(funcall (compose-values #'floor #'list) 7 2)
;; => (3 1)
#+end_src

(apply f args)= calls floor= with two arguments, returning
two values. =multiple-value-call= then calls =g= with those
two values as separate arguments — so =list= receives =3= and
=1=, producing =(3 1)=. If the body had been the naive
=(funcall g (apply f args))=, the intermediate =apply= would
truncate to =3= before =g= was called, and the answer would be
=(3)=.

The primitive that =compose-values= is a specialisation of:
=multiple-value-call=. In fact:

#+begin_example
(funcall (compose-values f g) args...)
= (multiple-value-call g (apply f args))
#+end_example

Every =multiple-value-call= expresses one composition — a
call to =g= with all the values produced by the argument
forms. =compose-values= packages that into a reusable
function; the underlying primitive is the one you'd reach for
directly in a one-off composition, without building the
wrapper.

*** Exercise 24.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defun first-or-default (thunk default)
  "THUNK's primary value, or DEFAULT if it returned none."
  (let ((vals (multiple-value-list (funcall thunk))))
    (if vals (first vals) default)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (first-or-default (lambda () (values)) :none)
  (first-or-default (lambda () (values 1 2)) :none)
  (first-or-default (lambda () nil) :none))
#+end_src

Expected: =(:NONE 1 NIL)=.

The three cases:

    =(values)= — zero values — =vals= is =nil=, so =default=
    is returned.

    =(values 1 2)= — two values — =vals= is =(1 2)=, so =(first
    vals)= gives 1=.

    =nil= — /one/ value, which happens to be nil — =vals= is
    =(nil)=, so =(first vals)= gives =nil= (the value, not the
    default).

The =default='s value is =:none= (a keyword) rather than
=nil=, because =nil= is itself a legitimate /value/, and using
=nil= as the default would conflate it with "the thunk
returned nil". =:none= is unambiguous.

*** Exercise 24.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun save-game ()
  "Write the game state. Two values: success-p, path-or-message."
  (handler-case
    (progn
      (with-open-file (out save-file
          :direction :output
          :if-exists :supersede)
        (with-standard-io-syntax
          (format out "cave-of-noise v~D~%" save-format)
          (print (game-state) out)))
      (values t save-file))
    (file-error (c)
      (values nil (princ-to-string c)))))

(defun load-game ()
  "Restore the game state. Two values: state-or-nil, loaded-p."
  (handler-case
    (with-open-file (in save-file)
      (let ((header (read-line in)))
        (unless (search "cave-of-noise" header)
          (error "Not a Cave of Noise save file: ~S" header))
        (let ((state (with-standard-io-syntax (read in))))
          (values state t))))
    (file-error () (values nil nil))
    (end-of-file () (values nil nil))
    (error () (values nil nil))))
#+end_src

Note that load-game= now returns the /state= rather than
applying it — the caller decides whether to restore-state= it.
That's the flag pattern applied cleanly: the function's job is
"get the data from disk"; whether the game adopts it is the
game's decision.

Update the save and load commands:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun cmd-save ()
  "The SAVE command: report success or failure."
  (multiple-value-bind (ok path-or-err)
    (save-game)
    (if ok
      (format nil "Saved to ~A." path-or-err)
      (format nil "Could not save: ~A" path-or-err))))

(defun cmd-load ()
  "The LOAD command: load and adopt, or report failure."
  (multiple-value-bind (state loaded-p)
    (load-game)
    (if loaded-p
      (progn
        (restore-state state)
        (look))
      "No saved game found.")))
#+end_src

Register them:

#+begin_src lisp
(push '(save . cmd-save) commands)
(push '(load . cmd-load) commands)
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf save-file "/tmp/test-roundtrip.lisp")
(setf current-room (find-room 'kitchen))
(setf item-locations '((key . kitchen)))
(setf history nil)

(run-command '(save))
(setf item-locations nil)
(run-command '(load))
item-locations ; => ((KEY . KITCHEN))
#+end_src

The comment the exercise asks for:

The string-matching approach has three problems that the
two-value approach fixes. First, it's brittle: change the
error message text, and the caller's match stops working.
Second, it's not the information the caller wants —
"there was no file" and "the file was corrupt" are both
"no load happened", but a caller might want to distinguish
them for logging or for a different player message.
Two values (state, loaded-p) say what happened; if the
caller needs the /reason=, the function can return three
values (state, loaded-p, reason), and callers who don't
care ignore the third. Third, string matching is slow and
requires allocation of a temp string; comparing against
nil or t costs nothing.

The reason /this/ function returns two values instead of
signaling a condition: a missing save file is expected
(first run of a fresh game), not a bug. Conditions are for
bugs and emergencies. Flags are for expected outcomes the
caller branches on. save-game and load-game are on the
flag side of the line; the game's data structures on disk
are on the condition side (see Exercise 18.6's
convert-save-v0 and the reader's handling of corrupt
files).

The game's state at the end of this chapter: run-command=,
play=, save-game=, and load-game= all use multiple values
as their interface, save= and load= commands wrap the two
plist-replacing commands from Chapter 16 with the two-value
versions above, and the rest of the game is unchanged. The
trickle-down effect of Chapter 24's mechanism has been
everywhere; the trickle-up effect, on the game's own code, has
been to replace one string-match hack (count-unknown=) and
one string-return convention (save-game/load-game) with
values.

** Chapter 25

*** Exercise 25.1

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((fns (loop for i from 1 to 3
                 collect (lambda () i))))
  (mapcar #'funcall fns))
;; => (1 2 3)
#+end_src

=loop='s =for i= binds a fresh =i= per iteration, so each
closure owns its own. (In languages where a loop shares one
variable, this prints =3 3 3=; Lisp's per-iteration binding is
the reason the =make-bumpers= example works.) The standard
guarantees it for =loop=; for =dotimes=, it's
implementation-defined — SBCL does the right thing, most
modern implementations do, but =loop= is the guaranteed form.

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (up down) (make-two)
  (list (funcall up) (funcall up) (funcall down) (funcall up)))
;; => (1 2 1 2)
#+end_src

Both closures share one =n=. Trace it: =up= takes n 0→1, returns
1; =up= takes n 1→2, returns 2; =down= takes n 2→1, returns 1;
=up= takes n 1→2, returns 2. The list is =(1 2 1 2)=.

If your answer was =(1 2 -1 1)= — a guess that =down= returns
the negative of something — re-read the closure: =down= is
=(lambda () (decf n))=, and =decf= returns n's new value, not
the amount decremented. Closure sharing of a single binding
means increments and decrements on the same number, in order.

#+begin_src lisp
(let ((x 10))
  (let ((f (lambda () x)))
    (setf x 99)
    (funcall f)))
;; => 99
#+end_src

The closure captured the /binding/, not the value =10= at
creation time; the later =setf= changed that binding, and =f=
sees the change. Value-snapshot thinking predicts 10; binding
thinking predicts 99; Lisp does 99.

*** Exercise 25.2

*Wrong:* =total= has no binding — the =lambda='s free variable
names nothing, and calling the closure signals
=UNBOUND-VARIABLE= (or, under some compilers, captures a
dynamic =total= if one happens to exist — worse). Fix: the
=let= was the whole design:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-accumulator ()
  (let ((total 0))
    (lambda (n) (setf total (+ total n)))))
#+end_src

*Surprising, but it works — the surprise is the /shape/ of the
data.* =(first *counters*)= is the pair =(a #<closure>)=, and
=(second ...)= extracts the closure. =(funcall (second (first
*counters*)))= correctly returns 1. The exercise's real trap
is a different spelling that fails: =(funcall *counters*)=,
which is =funcall= on the whole list, not on any closure. Use
the pair's =.cdr= to extract the closure cleanly, which is
also how you'd normally write it:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *counters*
  (loop for name in '(a b c)
        collect (cons name (let ((count 0))
                             (lambda () (incf count))))))
(funcall (cdr (assoc 'a *counters*)))   ; => 1
#+end_src

— an alist of name to closure. The =funcall= form is the same
as above; the extraction is =cdr (assoc ...)= instead of
=second (first ...)=.

*Surprising, but it works in SBCL and most modern
implementations.* =dotimes= binds =i= per iteration, so the
closures capture 0, 1, and 2 respectively, and =(3 2 1)= comes
out because =push= reverses. This is implementation-defined
in the standard — the standards text is ambiguous. If you want
the guarantee, use =loop for i below 3= instead; if you only
care about SBCL, =dotimes= is fine.

The real trap the exercise warns about: when the closure
captures an /outer function parameter/ rather than the loop's
own binding. That case always fails, in every implementation:

#+begin_example
(defun make-wrong-fns (limit)
  (let ((fns nil))
    (dotimes (i limit (nreverse fns))
      (push (lambda () limit) fns))))  ; captures LIMIT not I
;; every closure returns the same LIMIT
#+end_example

The =lambda='s free variable is =limit= — one binding, one
value. Not =i=, which is per-iteration.

*** Exercise 25.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-average ()
  "Return a closure: running average, two values (average, count)."
  (let ((total 0)
        (count 0))
    (lambda (n)
      (incf count)
      (incf total n)
      (values (/ total count) count))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *avg* (make-average))
(multiple-value-list (funcall *avg* 4))   ; => (4 1)
(multiple-value-list (funcall *avg* 2))   ; => (3 2)
(multiple-value-list (funcall *avg* 9))   ; => (5 3)
#+end_src

Averages: 4/1, (4+2)/2=3, (4+2+9)/3=5. The two values (average,
count) let callers check both the current result and how much
data went into it, without a separate "how many samples?"
function.

The =:count= extension to =make-registry=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-registry ()
  (let ((table (make-hash-table)))
    (lambda (message &optional key value)
      (ecase message
        (:put  (setf (gethash key table) value))
        (:get  (gethash key table))
        (:keys (loop for k being the hash-keys of table
                     collect k))
        (:count (hash-table-count table))))))
#+end_src

Existing callers using =:put=, =:get=, =:keys= don't change;
the closure gains a message. That's the payoff of the
message-dispatch shape: /extension by addition/, no caller
touched. Chapter 26's generic functions generalise exactly
this to types.

*** Exercise 25.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-stack ()
  "Four closures over one list: push!, pop!, empty-p, contents."
  (let ((items nil))
    (values
     (lambda (x) (push x items))
     (lambda ()
       (if (null items)
           (error "Stack underflow.")
           (pop items)))
     (lambda () (null items))
     (lambda () (copy-list items)))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(multiple-value-bind (push! pop! empty-p contents)
    (make-stack)
  (funcall push! 1)
  (funcall push! 2)
  (let ((snapshot (funcall contents)))
    (funcall pop!)
    (list snapshot (funcall contents) (funcall empty-p))))
#+end_src

Expected: =((2 1) (1) NIL)= — the snapshot taken before the pop
still shows both elements; the second contents shows one; the
stack isn't empty.

The comment the exercise asks for:

contents returns a copy, not the internal list. This is the
difference between encapsulation and leaking. If contents
returned items directly, a caller could do (setf (car (funcall
contents)) :garbage) and corrupt the stack's internal state
without using push!/pop! — bypassing the underflow check, the
size discipline, everything the stack exists for. Returning a
copy means: "here is the state as it currently stands," but
the state itself is still private. This is a general principle
of closure-based (and class-based) objects: accessors return
values, not references to mutable internal state, unless the
whole point is to share that state. The same choice appears
in Chapter 7's item-name (returns a fresh string) vs an
accessor that returned the symbol-name directly (would be
fine, since strings from symbol-name are fresh, but the habit
matters). The trade-off: copying costs time and memory, so
hot accessors sometimes cheat — as long as the caller's
contract forbids mutation. The game's *item-locations* is a
counter-example: it's returned directly because callers are
expected to mutate it via set-location. Which is right
depends on what the interface promises.

*** Exercise 25.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-key-matcher (key target)
  "A predicate: does (funcall KEY item) equal TARGET?"
  (lambda (item) (equal (funcall key item) target)))
#+end_src

Use with =find-if=:

#+begin_src lisp
(find-if (make-key-matcher #'room-name 'kitchen) *rooms*)
;; => #<ROOM kitchen>
(remove-if-not (make-key-matcher #'room-name 'kitchen) *rooms*)
;; => (#<ROOM kitchen>)
#+end_src

The comparison with =name-matcher=:

- =name-matcher= is =remove-if='s predicate: "is this room the
  target?" — one argument, answers yes or no.
- =make-key-matcher= is the /generalised/ version: the key
  function can be anything, and the target comparison uses
  =equal= so strings work too.

Which generalises, and why: =make-key-matcher=, because it
makes the attribute-lookup part a parameter. =name-matcher=
is the special case =(make-key-matcher #'room-name target)=
with =eq= for the comparison — valid when the key returns
symbols. The general version is what you'd use in library
code; the specialised one is fine for a single use-site.

*** Exercise 25.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defun make-cooldown (&optional (window 2))
  "Block commands seen in the last WINDOW calls."
  (let ((history nil))
    (lambda (message &optional cmd)
      (ecase message
        (:block
         (let ((blocked (member cmd history :test #'equal)))
           (push cmd history)
           (when (> (length history) window)
             (setf history (subseq history 0 window)))
           (not (null blocked))))
        (:reset
         (setf history nil)
         t)))))
#+end_src

Wire it into the game:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *cooldown* (make-cooldown))

(defun run-command (command)
  "Execute COMMAND.  Two values: reply, known-p."
  (if (funcall *cooldown* :block command)
      (values "Not again." t)
      (restart-case
          (handler-case (call-command command)
            (program-error ()
              (values "Wrong number of arguments for that command." t)))
        (retry-with (fixed)
          :report "Run a corrected command instead."
          (run-command fixed)))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(setf *current-room* (find-room 'kitchen))
(setf *item-locations* '((key . kitchen)))
(funcall *cooldown* :reset)

(list (run-command '(look))
      (run-command '(look))
      (run-command '(take key))
      (run-command '(look)))
#+end_src

Expected: the look description, =("Not again." T)=, the take
message, then the look description again — because =look=,
=take=, =look= doesn't repeat =look= within the last two
commands; only consecutive-in-window repeats are blocked.

The comment the exercise asks for:

Cooldown is (a) session state, like history. It's about this
play-through's interaction rhythm, not the world's state. If
the player quits and reloads, a fresh cooldown is correct —
punishing them for reconnecting would be bad UX. The rule:
ask "would a loaded game have this state back where it was?"
If yes, it's world state (structures, alists, saved). If no,
it's session state (closures, not saved). Item locations are
world state — the player expects the key to be where they
left it. Cooldown is session state — the player expects a
fresh start. The subtlety the exercise probes: could a
designer argue the other way? Yes — "you have to wait 2
commands between looks" could be world state, surviving
saves. That would be a game design choice, and it would
require promoting the cooldown closure to a save-relevant
structure (an alist or class). The default is session state
because it serves the player; the alternative is available
if the design needs it. The decision isn't mechanical — it's
about /whose needs the state serves/.

** Chapter 26

*** Exercise 26.1

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass lamp (thing)
  ((lit :initarg :lit
        :accessor lit-p
        :initform nil)))

(defparameter *my-lamp* (make-instance 'lamp :name 'lamp))
(lit-p *my-lamp*)            ; => NIL
(setf (lit-p *my-lamp*) t)
(lit-p *my-lamp*)            ; => T
#+end_src

The =:accessor= gives both directions; =:reader= would have made
the =setf= a "no setf-expander" error. The =:initform nil= is
redundant (all slots default to =nil= unless told otherwise),
but writing it makes the intent explicit, which is worth the
line.

*** Exercise 26.2

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass a () ((x :reader x-of :initform 1)))
(defclass b (a) ((x :reader x-of :initform 2)))

(x-of (make-instance 'b))   ; => 2
#+end_src

The answer is 2 because =b= redefines the =x= slot: it does not
add a second one. =make-instance= of =b= initialises =x= from
=b='s =:initform=, the most specific class in the precedence
list, and =x-of= reads that slot. One slot per name, nearest
definition wins — the precedence rule at work on slots rather
than methods.

Contrast with a case where =b= /didn't/ redefine =x=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass a () ((x :reader x-of :initform 1)))
(defclass b (a) ())
(x-of (make-instance 'b))   ; => 1 — inherited, unchanged
#+end_src

The =x= slot lives in =a=; =b= has no opinion about it; the
inheritance chain supplies the value. Redefining a slot in a
subclass doesn't extend it, it replaces it — a common source of
confusion when refactoring a class hierarchy.

*** Exercise 26.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defgeneric empty-thing-p (thing)
  (:documentation "Is THING empty?"))

(defmethod empty-thing-p ((t2 thing))
  nil)   ; the default: things are not empty

(defmethod empty-thing-p ((c container))
  (null (contents c)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (empty-thing-p *key-thing*)   ; => NIL
      (empty-thing-p *chest*)       ; => NIL (it holds crown, gem)
      (empty-thing-p (make-instance 'container :name 'empty-box)))
;; => (NIL NIL T)
#+end_src

The comment the exercise asks for:

:before and :after compose — they all run — so they can't
choose between answers; only primary methods select, and the
question "is it empty" wants exactly one answer, the most
specific one's. Whenever a method computes a /value/ rather
than performing a /side effect/, it belongs as a primary
method. :before= would run the primary anyway; :after= would
run after; either would give two answers for the same
question, and callers wouldn't know which to trust. Primary
methods answer; before/after methods prepare and clean up.

*** Exercise 26.4

*Wrong:* =potion= never declares =:reader= or =:accessor= for
=colour=, so =thing-colour= doesn't exist — and neither does
=colour=, since no slot named =colour= is readable. And the
name =thing-colour= is the wrong guess: it would be
=potion-colour= or just =colour=, depending on how the slot
was declared. Fix: declare =:reader colour=:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass potion (thing)
  ((colour :initarg :colour :reader colour)))
#+end_src

Now =(colour *potion*)= works. =(thing-description *potion*)=
also works — =potion= inherits =thing-description= — but only
if the potion instance has a =:description=; the class
inherits the =:initarg=, and the default =:initform= from
=thing= covers it.

*Wrong:* the method names the class =thing= before =defclass=
has run. Loading the file top to bottom, =defmethod= fails
with "The class THING is not defined" — or worse, treats
=thing= as an undefined variable in the specialiser. Order the
file: classes, then methods — or let ASDF's =:serial= do it
(Chapter 20's lesson, in a CLOS context).

*Wrong:* =(typep 'thing 'thing)= is =nil=: the first argument
is the /symbol/ thing=, not an instance. (typep *key-thing*
'thing) is the question with an object in it, and gives t=.
The quote in =typep='s second argument is type syntax, not
object reference; the quote in ='thing= as the /first/
argument is exactly what makes it a symbol rather than an
instance.

*** Exercise 26.5

#+begin_src lisp
(defmethod combine ((a symbol) (b symbol))
  (list a b))

(defmethod combine ((a string) (b number))
  (format nil "~A~D" a b))

(defmethod combine (a b)
  (error "Can't combine ~S and ~S." a b))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list (combine 'a 'b)
      (combine "x" 5)
      (ignore-errors (combine 1 "x")))
#+end_src

Expected: =((A B) "x5" NIL)= — the third call signals, so
=ignore-errors= returns nil; without it, the debugger opens.

Three more methods on top of the chapter's four. That's the
point of the exercise: with dispatch on two arguments and
several types each, the number of methods is the /product/ of
the cases you want — specific pairs — not the sum, and the
catch-all on =t= keeps the generic function total. A
=typecase= version would have one ever-growing body; here
each case is independent and can be added from a different
file by a different author.

The catch-all method is the discipline: without it, calling
=combine= on a pair with no matching method signals
=no-applicable-method=, which is less helpful than a
specific error. With it, the error names the arguments. That's
the trade — a catch-all method gives you control over the
error's shape, at the cost of one extra method.

*** Exercise 26.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defmethod initialize-instance :after ((c container) &key)
  (when (contents c)
    (setf (contents c)
          (sort (copy-list (contents c)) #'string<
                :key #'symbol-name))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *box*
  (make-instance 'container
                 :name 'box
                 :contents '(zebra apple)))
(contents *box*)   ; => (APPLE ZEBRA)
#+end_src

The =:after= method runs after the slot is filled from
=:initarg=, so it sees the given contents and repairs them.
=copy-list= before =sort= is Chapter 10's rule about not
sorting things you don't own — the list in the slot may be
the caller's. The :key #'symbol-name= makes string<=
compare the names of the symbols, alphabetically.

Sorting content is a /repair=, not a semantic transformation:
the same set of things, put in a canonical order. That's the
right shape for =initialize-instance :after= — it enforces an
invariant without changing meaning. A =:after= that filtered
duplicates would be more aggressive; it would be visible
transformation, and better signalled by a separate method
called by the caller, not by =make-instance=.

*** Exercise 26.7

#+begin_src lisp
(defgeneric weight (thing)
  (:documentation "How much does THING weigh?"))

(defmethod weight ((t2 thing)) 1)

(defmethod weight ((c container))
  (+ 1 (length (contents c))))

(defun total-weight (symbols)
  "Sum the weights of the registered things named in SYMBOLS."
  (reduce #'+ (mapcar (lambda (s)
                        (weight (lookup-thing s)))
                      symbols)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(total-weight '(key chest))
;; => 1 + 3 = 4
#+end_src

— with the chest holding (crown gem)=, (weight *chest*)=
is ~1 + 2 = 3~, and the key is =1=. Sum =4=.

What happens with an unregistered symbol: (lookup-thing
'unregistered)= returns nil=, and (weight nil)= signals —
weight='s methods both specialise on classes (thing= and
=container=) and =nil= is an instance of neither. So the
chain of failures runs mapcar → weight → no-applicable-method
→ debugger=. That suggests where a handler-case=
belongs — in =total-weight=, at the level that knows what
"missing item" means for its own contract:

#+begin_src lisp
(defun total-weight (symbols)
  "Sum the weights of the registered things in SYMBOLS."
  (handler-case
      (reduce #'+ (mapcar (lambda (s) (weight (lookup-thing s)))
                          symbols))
    (error () nil)))   ; or signal a specific condition
#+end_src

The generic version of the exercise's question — "where does
condition handling belong" — is Chapter 19's flags-vs-
conditions rule, one more time. =total-weight='s caller wants
a total or a signal of "some symbols don't resolve", not a raw
=no-applicable-method= error about =nil=. The handler is the
translation layer.

*** Exercise 26.8

#+begin_src lisp
(defun list-things (command)
  "Handle (LIST-THINGS): list every registered thing, alphabetically."
  (declare (ignore command))
  (let ((lines nil))
    (maphash (lambda (sym instance)
               (push (format nil "~(~A~): ~A"
                             sym (thing-description instance))
                     lines))
             *thing-registry*)
    (format t "~{~A~%~}" (sort lines #'string<))))
#+end_src

Register it:

#+begin_src lisp
(push '(list-things . list-things) *commands*)
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(list-things '(list-things))
#+end_src

Expected output (sorted by the string, so the symbol's name
sort matters — =chest=, =crown=, =key=, =lamp=, =vase=):

#+begin_example
chest: a wooden chest.
crown: a tarnished crown.
key: a small brass key.
lamp: an oil lamp, dusty but intact.
vase: a thin porcelain vase.
#+end_example

The comment the exercise asks about — what does the class
hierarchy buy over a plist:

Storing the description on the symbol's plist would work for
the description itself: (setf (get 'key 'description) "...")
puts it where get can find it. Three things the class hierarchy
buys beyond that:

1. Structured data. A thing can have several slots — name,
   description, weight, value, contents — that belong together
   as one thing, distinguishable by which instance carries
   which. A plist on a symbol mixes the description, the
   weight, and everything else into one flat list; keeping the
   fields associated per-instance is a discipline the plist
   doesn't enforce.

2. Behaviour that varies by kind. describe-thing dispatches on
   class; there is no way to make (get 'key 'description) vary
   by kind without writing if-typecase logic. Adding a new
   kind (fragile, say) means one new class and one new method;
   adding it to the plist world means editing every function
   that reads the plist.

3. State safety. A plist is global mutable state attached to a
   symbol — remprop, setf get, any code anywhere can corrupt
   it (Chapter 23's warnings). An instance's slot is protected
   by the class's accessor contract, and two instances of the
   same class never share state accidentally.

The plist's one advantage: symbolic stability. A symbol's
plist is the same wherever the symbol appears; a class instance
must be passed. For constants attached to a name, plists are
fine. For a /thing/, with several slots and behaviour, classes
are the right shape.

** Chapter 27

*** Exercise 27.1

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass a () ())
(defclass b (a) ())
(defclass c (a) ())
(defclass d (b c) ())

(mapcar #'class-name
        (sb-mop:class-precedence-list (find-class 'd)))
#+end_src

Expected: =(D B C A STANDARD-OBJECT T)=.

The rule: the class itself first, then its direct
superclasses /left to right/, then their superclasses,
deduplicated. =b= and =c= share =a=, so =a= comes once
(after both its children — it's the superclass of both).
Then =standard-object= (the default CLOS parent), then =t=
(everything).

=b='s methods win over =c='s: =b= is leftmost in =d='s
superclass list, so it appears first in the precedence list,
so a method specialised on =b= is more specific than one
specialised on =c= for a =d= instance. If =d='s superclass
list were =(c b)= instead, =c='s methods would win.

That leftmost-wins rule is why the order in the superclass
list is meaningful, and why "put the primary nature first" is
the advice. If you reorder the superclass list as a
"refactor", you may change method dispatch throughout the
hierarchy.

*** Exercise 27.2

*Wrong:* the =:after= method computes a string that is then
/discarded/ — auxiliary method results don't contribute to a
generic function's return value, only side effects matter.
The description never appears anywhere. Fix: store the
result or make it a primary method:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmethod describe-widget ((w widget))
  (format nil "x is ~A" (widget-x w)))
#+end_src

A primary method's value /is/ the generic function's result
when it's the only one or the most-specific one. That's the
shape you want for "return a string describing this thing".

*Surprising:* the =:after= on =sub= runs after =sub='s
primary method for the accessor =n=. That means every read
of =n= on a =sub= instance increments the slot by 10 — a
read that mutates. Ten reads in a row, the slot has grown by
100. That's not "add 10 to the result"; that's "the act of
reading changes the state". It looks like a bug in the
accessor, and it is. Fix: don't mutate in an
accessor-adjacent method. If =sub= needs a derived value,
give it its own accessor and compute on demand:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass sub (base)
  ((bonus :initform 10 :accessor bonus)))

(defun n-total (s) (+ (n s) (bonus s)))
#+end_src

Then =n= stays pure (reads the slot, returns the value);
=n-total= is the derived value, computed fresh each call.

*Wrong:* =(defmethod area ((s shape)) 0)= specialises on
=shape=, which is not defined — the =defmethod= signals
"The class SHAPE is undefined" unless a =shape= class exists.
The fix is to define the class first (or specialise on =t=
for the catch-all):

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass shape () ())
(defgeneric area (shape))
(defmethod area ((s shape)) 0)
#+end_src

Also note the =defgeneric= placement: declaring the generic
function /before/ its methods is the readable order, and
catch-all =t= specialisers read poorly — prefer a real base
class.

*** Exercise 27.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *method-notes* nil)
;; entries: (class-name kind function-or-description)

(defun note-method (class-name kind what)
  (push (list class-name kind what) *method-notes*))

(defun describe-chain (instance)
  "Print the precedence list with known methods per class."
  (dolist (class (sb-mop:class-precedence-list (class-of instance)))
    (let ((name (class-name class))
          (notes (remove-if-not
                  (lambda (n) (eq (first n) name))
                  *method-notes*)))
      (format t "~&~(~A~):~%" name)
      (dolist (n notes)
        (format t "  ~A — ~A~%" (second n) (third n))))))

;; demo data:
(note-method 'thing :primary "price: base 1")
(note-method 'treasure :primary "price: (value tr)")
(note-method 'antique :primary "price: 50")
(note-method 'container :primary "price: 2")
(describe-chain (make-instance 'antique
                  :name 'mirror
                  :value 200))
#+end_src

Expected output:

#+begin_example
ANTIQUE:
  PRIMARY — price: 50
TREASURE:
  PRIMARY — price: (value tr)
THING:
  PRIMARY — price: base 1
STANDARD-OBJECT:
T:
#+end_example

The output teaches the chapter's central fact: method order
surprises are /precedence surprises/ — the methods aren't
mysterious; they're listed class by class, and what felt like
random dispatch is the precedence list being walked in order.
When dispatch surprises you, print the chain before guessing.

The real MOP versions of this — =compute-applicable-methods=
returning the actual applicable methods for a given call,
=find-method= looking up a method by specialisers — exist
and are worth knowing by name. But =compute-applicable-methods=
is on =standard-generic-function=, and its results are
implementation-defined in some respects. The manual registry
above is honest: it says "here's what I know about my own
code's methods", which for application code is what you can
actually rely on.

*** Exercise 27.4

#+begin_src lisp
(defgeneric participants ()
  (:method-combination list))

(defclass round-table () ())
(defclass round-table-plus (round-table) ())

(defmethod participants list ((r round-table)) 'alice)
(defmethod participants list ((r round-table-plus)) 'bob)

(participants (make-instance 'round-table-plus))
;; => (BOB ALICE) — most specific first
#+end_src

The timing around-method:

#+begin_src lisp
(defmethod participants :around ((r round-table))
  (let ((start (get-internal-run-time))
        (result (call-next-method)))
    (format *trace-output* "participants: ~D ticks~%"
            (- (get-internal-run-time) start))
    result))

(participants (make-instance 'round-table-plus))
;; => (BOB ALICE), and a trace line
#+end_src

The =:around= must specialise on the /most general class the
generic function serves= — =round-table= here, or =t=, but
never on =round-table-plus= — so it wraps every call. If it
specialised on =round-table-plus=, timing would record only
calls on plus instances, and a caller timing
=(make-instance 'round-table)= would see no trace. The
=:around='s specialiser controls /which calls it sees/, and
you want it to see all of them.

One more subtlety: =call-next-method= in an =:around=
invokes the /rest of the combination/ — all remaining
methods, combined — not merely "the next primary". For a
=list= combination, that means all remaining primary methods
contribute to the returned list. The =:around= sees them as
a single combined result.

*** Exercise 27.5

#+begin_src lisp
(define-condition class-change-drops-slots (error)
  ((slots :initarg :slots :reader dropped-slots))
  (:report (lambda (c s)
             (format s "change-class would drop: ~{~A~^, ~}"
                     (dropped-slots c)))))

(defun slot-names (class)
  "Names of CLASS's slots, including inherited."
  (let ((names nil))
    (labels ((walk (c)
               (dolist (s (sb-mop:class-direct-slots c))
                 (pushnew (sb-mop:slot-definition-name s) names))
               (dolist (super (sb-mop:class-direct-superclasses c))
                 (walk super))))
      (walk class)
      names)))

(defun safe-change-class (instance new-class-name)
  "Change INSTANCE's class, refusing to drop slots."
  (let* ((old-class (class-of instance))
         (new-class (find-class new-class-name))
         (old-slots (slot-names old-class))
         (new-slots (slot-names new-class))
         (dropped (set-difference old-slots new-slots)))
    (when dropped
      (error 'class-change-drops-slots :slots dropped))
    (change-class instance new-class)))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass small-thing (thing) ())
(defclass large-thing (thing) ())
(defparameter *item* (make-instance 'small-thing :name 'coin))
(safe-change-class *item* 'large-thing)   ; works; no slot dropped

(defclass bigger-thing (thing)
  ((mass :initform 1)))
(safe-change-class *item* 'bigger-thing)  ; works too
;; (round-trip to a smaller class would refuse if slots dropped)
#+end_src

What the refusal teaches: =change-class='s slot policy is
/name-based/ — slots with the same name carry over, new slots
are added with their =:initforms=, and slots the new class
/lacks/ are simply /gone/, silently, no condition, no warning.
That's generous for interactive work (a quick experiment
doesn't get blocked by slot mismatches) and dangerous for
state you care about (the game's inventory could lose an
item's worth slot by an accidental class change).

The MOP functions =slot-definition-name=, =class-direct-slots=
and =class-direct-superclasses= are technically beyond the
ANSI standard — they're the Metaobject Protocol, and SBCL
ships them (mostly via =closer-mop= for the portable name).
The =slot-names= above is portable-ish but not guaranteed;
a hand-maintained slot table for the classes you care about
would work too. The check is the point; the mechanism is a
detail.

*** Exercise 27.6

#+begin_src lisp
(defclass room ()
  ((name :initarg :name :reader room-name)
   (description :initarg :description :reader room-description)
   (exits :initarg :exits :initform nil :accessor room-exits)))

(defclass dark-room (room) ())
(defclass visited-room (room) ())

(defgeneric weight (room)
  (:method-combination +))

(defmethod weight + ((r room))
  (max 1 (length (room-exits r))))

(defmethod weight + ((r dark-room))
  5)

(defmethod weight + ((r visited-room))
  -2)

(defparameter *dungeon*
  (list (make-instance 'room :name 'hall :exits '(a b c))
        (make-instance 'dark-room :name 'crypt :exits '(a))
        (make-instance 'visited-room :name 'foyer :exits '(b))))

(defun find-room-by-name (name)
  (find name *dungeon* :key #'room-name))

(defun safest-exit (here)
  "The exit destination with the lowest weight."
  (first
   (sort (copy-list (room-exits here))
         #'<
         :key (lambda (dest-name)
                (weight (find-room-by-name dest-name))))))

(safest-exit (find-room-by-name 'hall))
;; => error: no applicable method for NIL
#+end_src

The demo data is wrong, and the error is worth reading: exits
are room /names/ (symbols), but =hall='s exits are =(a b c)=
and none of those rooms exist in =*dungeon*=. Then
=find-room-by-name 'a= is =nil=, and =(weight nil)= has no
applicable method. Fix the data:

#+begin_src lisp
(defparameter *dungeon*
  (list (make-instance 'room :name 'hall :exits '(crypt foyer))
        (make-instance 'dark-room :name 'crypt :exits '(hall))
        (make-instance 'visited-room :name 'foyer :exits '(hall))))

(safest-exit (find-room-by-name 'hall))
;; => FOYER
#+end_src

Now =crypt= is a =dark-room= (weight 5 from dark + 1 from
room = 6); =foyer= is a =visited-room= (weight 1 from room +
-2 from visited = -1). Safest exit is the one with the lowest
weight, =foyer= at -1.

The comment the exercise asks for:

=+= is right because room difficulty /adds up/ — base cost from
exit count, plus darkness penalty, minus visit discount is an
arithmetic sum of independent contributions, and each class
owns exactly its contribution. =max= would be wrong because it
picks /one/ contribution and discards the others: a dark,
visited room would weigh 5 (the darkness penalty) instead of
1+5-2=4, and the whole point of the visit discount would be
lost. Adding a new factor (say, a flooded-room= penalty class)
with max= means rewriting the combination logic, where +=
just gains another method. The rule: += when contributions are
independent quantities that sum; max= when the answer is "the
single worst (or best) factor" — and mixing the two readings
in one generic function is the design smell to fix by
splitting it in two.

There's a subtlety worth stating: negative contributions. -2
from visited-room= means a visited room is /safer/ than an
unvisited one. With max= combination, negative contributions
would vanish; with +=, they accumulate. If your game design
wanted "visited rooms always score at least as safe as
unvisited ones, never less than 0", you'd need a floor: max
0 (weight ...)= applied after the sum. The combination type
controls what "score" means; the game design controls where
the floor is.

** Chapter 28

*** Exercise 28.1

#+begin_src lisp
(list (find-symbol "LIST" :cl)   ; => LIST, :EXTERNAL
      (find-symbol "list" :cl)   ; => NIL, NIL — tables are case-sensitive
      (eq 'cl:car (intern "CAR" :cl))  ; => T — same symbol
      (symbol-package 'cl:car)   ; => #<PACKAGE "COMMON-LISP">
      (symbol-package 'car)      ; => #<PACKAGE "COMMON-LISP">
      (multiple-value-list (find-symbol "RUN-COMMAND" :con)))
;; => (RUN-COMMAND :EXTERNAL) if exported, (RUN-COMMAND :INTERNAL)
;;    if it's interned but not exported
#+end_src

The =symbol-package 'car= line is the inheritance lesson:
=symbol-package= answers "where is this symbol /home/", not
"how did I reach it". In =cl-user=, =car= resolves to the
=CL:CAR= symbol by inheritance — the =cl-user= table doesn't
hold a =CAR= of its own — and so =symbol-package= correctly
reports =:common-lisp=. Inheritance is a lookup fact, not a
property of the symbol.

The last line is about the game's own package: =:con= is the
nickname for =:cave-of-noise=, and =run-command= should be
exported (it's in the API surface from Chapter 28's project
step), so its status is =:external=. If you get =:internal=,
the export list is missing it; if =NIL NIL=, the package isn't
loaded.

*** Exercise 28.2

*Surprising, but it works:* =(use-package :p1)= after =:cl=
means =cl-user= now sees both =CL='s exports and =P1='s
exports. =run= resolves to =p1:run= by inheritance, and
evaluating =run= calls =p1:run= (returns =:p1s=). No conflict,
because =cl-user= didn't have its own =run= before the
=use-package=.

The trap that /would/ fail:

#+begin_example
(defpackage :p1 (:use :cl) (:export :run))
(defun run () :cl-users-own)  ; interns RUN in cl-user
(use-package :p1)              ; conflict! cl-user's RUN vs p1's RUN
#+end_example

The conflict fires at =use-package= time, because =run= is
now findable two ways in =cl-user=. The resolution restarts
offer "keep the local one" (shadowing-import) or "keep the
imported one" (unintern the local). In the exercise's original
snippet, the order (=p1='s =run= interned first, =cl-user='s
=run= never interned) means no conflict — but that's a
fragile situation where the code's correctness depends on
what's been evaluated before.

*Surprising:* =(defpackage :p2 (:use :cl :p1))= is fine at
definition time; the conflict fires at =(defun run () :p2s)=,
because =p2= now inherits =run= from =p1= and tries to intern
its own =run=. SBCL signals a correctable error: which =run=
does =p2= mean? The fix — as the answer above shows — is to
explicitly shadow the name:

#+begin_src lisp
(defpackage :p2
  (:use :cl)
  (:import-from :p1 :other-name)   ; only what's needed
  (:shadow :run))
#+end_src

Better still, don't =:use= a package you're going to
redefine names from — that's what =:import-from= is for.
Only import the specific names you want; the ones you
redefine stay local, with no conflict.

*Surprising:* =(unintern 'temp :p1)= doesn't do what it looks
like. ='temp= reads as =cl-user::temp= — the symbol named
=TEMP= home in =CL-USER=, not the one interned in =:p1= by
=(intern "TEMP" :p1)= above. =unintern= asks: is /this
symbol/ (the =cl-user= one) present in =:p1='s table? It
isn't — different symbol, same name. So =unintern= does
nothing, and the =p1:temp= symbol from the first line stays
interned.

*Surprising:* =p1::temp= reads as "look up =TEMP= in =:p1=,
creating a new symbol if absent". The old one is still there
(the =unintern= above missed), so =p1::temp= returns it. The
symbol is unbound (never used as a variable), so evaluating
=p1::temp= as a variable signals =UNBOUND-VARIABLE=.

The fix, if the intent was to actually unintern:

#+begin_src lisp
(unintern (find-symbol "TEMP" :p1) :p1)
;; or more directly:
(unintern (intern "TEMP" :p1) :p1)   ; two intern calls, same symbol
#+end_src

=find-symbol= is safer — it doesn't accidentally intern if
the symbol is missing. =(unintern (find-symbol "TEMP" :p1) :p1)=
returns non-nil if the unintern succeeded, nil if =find-symbol=
returned nil (symbol wasn't there to begin with).

*** Exercise 28.3

#+begin_src lisp
(defun where-does-name-live (name &optional (package *package*))
  "Where NAME is findable in PACKAGE, as a keyword or :absent."
  (multiple-value-bind (sym status)
      (find-symbol (string name) package)
    (declare (ignore sym))
    (or status :absent)))

(defun find-anywhere (name package)
  "The first package, in precedence order, where NAME is findable."
  (let ((status (where-does-name-live name package)))
    (cond ((eq status :external) package)
          ((eq status :internal) package)
          (t
           (some (lambda (used)
                   (when (member (where-does-name-live name used)
                                 '(:external :internal))
                     used))
                 (package-use-list package))))))
#+end_src

Check:

#+begin_src lisp
(where-does-name-live "CAR" :cl-user)     ; => :INHERITED
(where-does-name-live "PLAY" :con)        ; => :EXTERNAL
(where-does-name-live "NONEXISTENT" :cl)  ; => :ABSENT
(find-anywhere "CAR" :cl-user)            ; => #<PACKAGE "COMMON-LISP">
#+end_src

The precedence lesson: the reader resolves an unqualified name
first in the current package's own table (internal and
external alike), then walks the use-list in /order/. Local
beats inherited — which is what =shadow= exploits, and what
makes definition order in your own package irrelevant to
whether your names beat =cl='s.

The =:INHERITED= status is what =where-does-name-live= gives
